The duty of the Legislative Council is: 

"(a) To investigate departments, agencies and officers of the State and to study their functions and problems; (b) To make studies for the use of the legislative branch of the 

State Government; (c) To gather information for the use of the Legislature; (d) To make such other investigations, studies, and reports as may be deemed useful to the Legislative branch of the State Government; (e) To sit and perform its duties in the interim between sessions; (f) To report to the Legislature its recommendations from time to time and to accompany its reports with such drafts of legislation as it deems proper. 11

The object of this staff research report is to assist the Legislative Council in carrying out this responsibility. Any recommendations concerning the subject of this research report that the Council may make will be transmitted to the 52d Legislature. 7 3 4 5 2 3

TRANSMITTAL NOTE 

This research report is submitted to provide background information and some general analysis of the assigned problem for the use of the Texas Legislative Council, its Study Committee on Taxation, and the Legislature of the State of Texas. This is a Staff research report for which only the Staff assumes responsibility. The Council Staff stands ready to assist the Council, the Study Committee, and the Legislature in any additional work on this subject. 

The Texas Legislative Council, at its meeting of March 23, 1950, directed its Staff to make a survey of taxation. Recognizing that the next Legislature would face a problem of taxation and that limitations of time and money precluded a thorough and detailed study, the Council directed the Staff to collect as much information as possible that would be useful to the Legislature. 

The Council specified that the tax survey was not to be designed to serve the purpose of developing a tax program nor of proposing rate changes. In carrying out these instructions, the Council Staff undertook a survey study of taxation in Texas. For a convenient presentation of the results, the material on the subject has been divided into two parts. Part I of this survey, entitled, "Comparative Tax Revenue Analysis--Texas and Selected States," was published as Staff Research Report No. 51-3. The Staff research report submitted herewith is the second and final part; initially, a third part was planned, but it had to be abandoned because of the limitations of time. 

Because of the limitations of time and the burden of other research products, the Staff had completed and ready for consideration by the Study Committee on Taxation and the Council only five of the nine taxes presented in this report. The Council considered the Cigarette, Cement, Motor Vehicle 

Sales, Carbon Black, and Chain Store taxes and made findings and recommendations to the Legislature. The Legislative Council's report on this subject may be found in Chapter III of "The Council Report to the 52nd Legislature." 

The Council directed its Staff to continue its work and complete the study of other individual taxes. After the 52nd Session began, the House Committee on Revenue and Taxation requested the Council Staff to present material designed to give the Committee a general background on Texas taxes. Acting under the directive of the Council to serve committees and members of the Legislature on the subjects studied, the Staff has presented the requested tax material to the Committee. Chapters of this report have been separately distributed in the process of serving the Revenue and Taxation Committee. Not all the studies of the taxes originally intended to be discussed have been completed, however, it is felt that the publication of this report can be delayed no longer. ii

This report is the product of the combined efforts of the Council Staff. 

Arthur J. Pehrkon, Staff Research Associate, was largely responsible for coordinating the study here presented, participated in its planning, and did much of the research and writing. He was assisted principally by Joe Grady 

Moore, Jr. and, in addition, received valuable assistance from Arnold A. 

Heyl and Thomas Clinton Smith, Jr., Staff Research Assistant. Also, we were fortunate in obtaining, on leave of absence, for work on this study, Robert G. Cherry, Assistant Professor, Department of Agricultural Eco nomics and Sociology, Texas Agricultural Experimental Station, the Texas 

Agricultural and Mechanical College, who has specialized in taxation. 

In view of the size of the other research tasks assigned to the Staff in response to requests by the Legislature, the Council concluded to limit the study by the Staff of taxation to a survey and not to attempt a comprehensive and intensive study.

A more detailed description of the methods and limitations of the material presented in this report is set out in the Introduction. Briefly, this report contains an examination of the history and workings of nine of the more significant, from the revenue point of view, State taxes. No attempt is made to determine who bears the burden of the taxes examined, what other taxes are imposed upon the persons affected by the individual tax, or the fairness of the tax rate.

In making this study, the Staff consulted the laws of Texas and other States, the available technological and tax data, the reports of State agencies, and a great deal of the other literature on the subject. Officials and employees of various State agencies, especially tax collecting agencies, were consulted. · 

The Staff wishes to express its appreciation to the Comptroller of Public Accounts and his staff and the various other State officials and employees consulted for their invaluable cooperation, information and help. It also wishes to thank Commerce Clearing House, Inc., for its aid and the use of some of its publications. This assistance greatly facilitated the preparation of this report.

This survey has emphasized to the Staff the importance of a thorough study of taxation and the fact that this two part survey falls short of the mark. 

It is hoped, however, that this survey may be of assistance to the Legislative Council, its Study Committee on Taxation and the Legislature of the State of Texas. 

Respectfully submitted, Mnoh Moseley 

Jo. D. Moseley 

Executive Director xecutive iii

INTRODUCTION 

1120 Part II, Analysis of Individual Taxes, of the report on A Survey⁹ of Taxation in Texas has been prepared to present an analysis of some of the individual State taxes currently levied in Texas. 19 ti

As mentioned in the Transmittal Note, the Legislative Council directed its Staff to collect as much information as possible which would be useful tỏ the Legislature in the field of taxation. The Staff employed the working assumption that it was expected to do more than merely describe the tax law and the mechanics of its administration. In conformance with the procedure used in the other Staff Research Reports and approved by the Council, this report also describes the problems discovered or reported and the possible approaches to these problems which the Staff found in its research and investigation. However, in view of the fact that this report involves a survey of the subject, these alternate approaches have not been fully investigated to determine their advantages and disadvantages and their validity and practicality. It was felt that if the Legislature, the Council, or any member became interested in any of the ideas reported, that then the Staff would be available to do any further work that might be desired. 

As a result of the limitations of time and those expressed in the instructions of the Council in the assignment of this topic, strict boundaries for the research carried out had to be established. No effort has been made to analyze the taxes from the viewpoint of the taxpayer. Analysis has been confined to the tax statutes, the records of and discussions with several tax administrators, and to printed and other written material available from library files and other sources. It is realized, of course, that a consideration of the viewpoints of interested taxpayer groups, and particularly of their knowledge of the trade and industry practices which bear on tax administration, would afford much valuable material. However, such consideration, even if it were done by sampling techniques, has been beyond the limits of time available for this survey.

The focus of the survey has been on the individual tax, with only incidental and occasional consideration of the relationship of each tax to the entire tax structure and to the other taxes now being levied. Thus, each chapter of this report is devoted to an individual tax and may be considered as complete within itself, not necessarily requiring reading within the context of the remainder of the report. In this way, it was felt that the report could serve as a convenient reference for those interested in one or another but not all of the taxes discussed. iv

genera

toarea

It is obvious that any conclusions that might be drawn from material using such an approach cannot be generalized, nor applied to an a area broader in scope than that under consideration. This point may be illustrated by an example. Note is made in the discussions of each tax whether it has a separate enforcement fund, or whether administrative costs are paid by the a by the enforcement fund of another tax. The observation that a particular tax has 

H-ular tax has er gro the discussio dis its administrative costs paid by monies collected from another group of 

The ob lex dvan taxpayers may leave the impression that such practice is not preferred. 

No such evaluation is intended. Integration of enforcement funds may well be desired on the basis of consideration of all taxes or of groups basis of consideration groups of taxes. 

However, consideration of only one tax may vindicate advantages of separate consideration of only funds. On the other hand, the question could be raised whether or not it is good state tax policy to establish separate enforcement funds at all. Such as policy to establish funds at al apparent contradictions are not true conflicts of thought, but the result of tadictions are not trug but the re different approaches to the same subject. The approach in this report has his been narrow, confined to the particular individual tax under consideration. 

On the other hand ame dividua cwhether As mentioned earlier each chapter of the report is devoted to one tax. 

The organization of the material within each chapter is the same. In each chapter the initial section devotes itself to the historical and legal background of the tax. The purpose of this section is two-fold. One is to permit the reader a brief orientation in regard to the environment in which the tax functions and in which it has developed. The other, to permit the reader a knowledge of the statutory changes which have preceded the current provisions of the law, and to indicate to a certain extent the effects on the current situation of earlier statutory provisions.

Following the initial section of each chapter are sections dealing specifically with the administrative organization which handles the tax, assessment methods and procedures, and collection and enforcement. The three sections discuss these phases of the administration of the present tax. 

Another section deals with the results of the operation of the tax from the point of view of revenues collected, comparability with similar taxes of other States, administrative costs, and the like. It is intended to afford the reader some basis for general comparisons which tend to be made between taxes, and to give some brief statistical data. It is realized, and it should be emphasized, that much of the comparative and statistical data available has only limited value for evaluative purposes of the taxes discussed. In fact, some of the data are not fully comparable. They are included primarily to afford a starting point for anyone interested in further study of these aspects of each particular tax.

Concluding the discussion of each tax is a section entitled "Summary and Problem Areas." In this are summarized those matters of policy or

practice which appear to warrant consideration either because of the problems of tax administration or application which they occasion, or because other and different methods of dealing with them than those used in this State have been advocated or tried elsewhere. 

In this section are presented the ideas mentioned earlier which have been found during the research and which can be used as possible approaches to each of the problem areas. None of these alternative methods is intended as a recommendation or proposal, but rather to point out some of the directions of approach which might be used. It has been assumed that an always-present possibility for the Legislature when considering these tax matters is the retention of the policy or method now being used. Therefore, no effort has been made to point out for each tax that continuance of the present plan is a possible method of dealing with the situation. This is intended to be obvious. 

By means of this organization of the material presented, the report has endeavored to orient the reader, discuss the present administration and its relation to the tax statute, point out some of the significant areas about which questions of policy or practice may exist, and mention some of the alternative methods available for dealing with the problem areas. 

Fifteen taxes, selected on the basis of their revenue significance, were originally planned for inclusion in this publication. However, the already formidable bulk of this volume after the completion of the discussion on nine of the taxes, and the lack of time, resulted in a decision to limit the content of the publication to the reports on nine taxes. Some research has been carried out in regard to the other taxes, but the writing, editing, and reproduction of these has not been completed. Be The enormity and complexity of the subject matter has made it necessary throughout this report to summarize, delete and attempt to simplify matters which are difficult so to handle. It is recognized that there may be oversimplification in parts, misleading brevity in others, and occasional omissions. cause of this, it must be emphasized that this report is not to be considered as exhaustive of the subject matter dealt with, but rather as an initial survey of some of the taxes in Texas for the primary purpose of affording the Legislature as much information as possible to aid it in its work. vi

SECTION 

Chapter I CIGARETTE TAX HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

The cigarette tax in Texas was set up as a depression finance measure in 1931. The act establishing the new tax stated that the State was in dire need of revenue; therefore, "In order to supplement the State's Available School Fund, and to reduce the burdens of ad valorem taxation on the farms and homes and other property of the people, there is hereby levied a tax on all sales in intrastate commerce, in the State, of cigarettes. ..1

Of comparatively recent origin, the tax was adopted by only 11 States during the 1920's, Iowa being first in 1921. Of the first 11 States to adopt a cigarette tax, eight took the action prior to 1926 and the other three followed soon afterward in 1926 and 1927. All acted more or less as the result of the depression of the early 1920's. 

Widespread Adoption of Cigarette Taxes

The depression of the early 1930's produced another rash of cigarette tax enactments. 2 Mississippi passed such a tax in 1930 and Texas and Ohio in 1931 (effective dates August 22 and September 1). By the end of 1935, a total of 19 States had such taxes. The coming of World War II coincided with widespread legislative action adopting cigarette taxes for gaining additional State revenue, and by the end of 1941, 28 States were levying cigarette taxes. 

The most recent flurry of cigarette tax adoptions came in 1947 and 1948, as States began to feel the pressure of increased post-war financial demands. 

By 1950, 40 States and the District of Columbia had tobacco taxes. Of those already having a cigarette tax, 18 States found it necessary to increase their rates during the post-war period. This group included Texas, where a tax increase was approved February 28, 1950, to meet the "compelling and immediate need to provide adequate buildings to house those citizens of Texas who are or may be committed to State Hospitals and Special Schools . . 

Steady Increase of Revenues 

113 Thus, it is apparent that Texas, like most other States, has turned to the cigarette tax during periods of financial need. This tax has been close behind the leading sources of tax revenues and has steadily increased year by year in total collections, rising from about 3.5 million dollars in 1934 to more than 13 million dollars at the end of the war, and then jumping to almost 24 million dollars in 1949. The post-war collections from this source have shown 1 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42d Legislature, R. S., ch. 73. 

2 Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conference, 1936. 

3 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1. 1

a spectacular rise from pre-war levels, with an annual rate of increase for the four years after the war of almost three million dollars against an annual rate of increase for the 1932-45 period of little more than $600,000. 4 This trend is duplicated in all States having a similar tax. 

Other factors besides increasing population have caused the steady increase in cigarette revenues. As is obvious from the steady rise in per capita revenues, their growth has regularly outpaced population growth (see table Cig- 1). The spread of the cigarette habit to new groups of smokers has, perhaps, been the most significant factor. As a consequence, it can reasonably be expected that, without an increase in tax rates, the rate of increase in total cigarette revenues will level off in the years ahead, since quite obviously the extension of the smoking habit to new groups cannot continue indefinitely. This tendency is already apparent in a steadily diminishing rate of increase in each of the four postwar years, although the rate is still well above that of the pre-war period. 

However, with the higher tax rate applied in 1950, stabilization of the rate of increase in total tax collections should not become apparent for at least two years and undoubtedly will be reached at a considerably higher level. 

The Question of a General Taxing Power In the Texas Constitution, four types of taxes are designated as taxes the Legislature may levy: Property taxes, poll taxes, occupation taxes, and income taxes (Art. VIII, Sec. 1). 5 This enumeration has led to what has been described as an "occupation tax complex" of the Legislature; i. e., the labeling of many taxes as occupation taxes, even though they might not be ordinarily so considered. 6 Although a subsequent section of the Constitution (Art. VIII, Sec. 

17) reads, "The specification of the objects and subjects of taxation shall not deprive the Legislature of the power to require other subjects or objects to be taxed. "the courts have avoided a clear-cut decision as to whether or not the four types of taxes are the only kinds the Legislature may impose. Although recent years have brought a legislative swing away from the occupation tax complex and there has been wide acceptance of the opinion that the Legislature has a general taxing power, the issue has not been finally settled, and the seemingly limiting provisions of the Constitution still influence tax législation. 

The questions of what is or is not an occupation tax and of whether sales or excise taxes can be levied without being designated and intended as occupation taxes are complicated by another section of the Constitution which provides that 'one-fourth of the revenue. . . . from State occupation taxes and poll taxes shall be set apart annually for the benefit of the public free schools . 

." (Art. VII, Sec. 3). Thus, the question of occupation 

4Federal-State Tax Co-ordination, Division of Tax Research, United States 

Treasury Department, revision of April 1, 1949. 

5Tex. Const. of 1876. 

6M. M. Mahany, Texas Taxes. 2

taxes is not merely one of constitutional construction but one which vitally affects the power of the Legislature to control the disposition of funds. 

The First Texas Cigarette Tax 

The 1931 legislative action imposing the first Texas cigarette tax bundled together new taxes on the sale on cigarettes and the production of natural gas. 

7 However, apparently still mindful of the seemingly limiting provisions of the Constitution as to types of taxes which can be imposed, the Legislature carefully described the new legislation as follows: "An Act for the purpose of raising revenues by levying an occupation tax upon all producers engaged in the business of producing and selling natural gas. and on dealers engaged in the business of selling cigarettes in intrastate business within the 

State of Texas.

11 Following the pattern set by the Federal Government, the basis for the tax was the weight of the cigarettes, and the rate was $1.50 per thousand cigarettes (or three cents a pack) on cigarettes weighing less than three pounds per thousand, and $3.60 per thousand cigarettes on those weighing more than three pounds per thousand. These rates were exactly one-half of the then current federal rate. The basis has never been changed by subsequent legislation, and the rate remained the same until the 1950 increase. Dealers' licenses were not required by this early act, and the design, manufacture, and sale of tax stamps were made the responsibilities of the State Treasurer. It is interesting to note that Texas followed the common pattern established by most of the other States having cigarette taxes as well as the trend of the Federal Government in that the levy was expressed in terms of a specified number of cigarettes. Only two States have based their rates on the retail price. 

Although penalties for evasion of the tax were included in the first cigarette tax act, responsibilities for enforcement or administration were not set out. The law made it the duty of all persons making "first sales" in intrastate commerce to purchase and affix stamps, but the State Treasurer was not required to keep a record of stamp sales. 

Disposition of collections was prescribed--one-half of the proceeds to be credited to the State Available School Fund and one-half to the General Fund. 

Tightening-up of the Tax Law

The inadequacies of the 1931 act were recognized by the Legislature in May, 1933. Motivated by "the fact that the State of Texas is losing many thousands of dollars annually in cigarette taxes, and the further fact that the present cigarette tax law is vague and indefinite, and the further fact that there are many evasions of the present cigarette law. . .," the 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42d Legislature, R. S., H. B. 547, ch. 73. 

Federal Excise Taxes on Tobacco, United States Treasury Department (February, 1948) revision of February, 1950, Pt. I, p. 2.

legislators passed an act that dealt specifically and solely with the cigarette tax. This new law designated in greater detail the desired administrative and enforcement responsibilities.

It is interesting to note that the 1933 act was still labeled a “Cigarette Occupation Tax," and the title described it as "An Act... providing and imposing an occupation tax on sales in intrastate commerce, in this State, of cigarettes according to their weight . . .'' In spite of this designation, Sec. 8 of the act makes the statement that "It is the intent and purpose of this Act to levy a sales tax on all cigarettes • • A provision was made in the 1933 act for annual licensing of cigarette dealers by the Comptroller, and such dealers were required to keep records of their cigarette and tax stamp purchases and sales (Secs. 6, 7). The Comptroller was given the responsibility for administering the act. (Sec. 9) and the authority to make regulations pertaining to collection of the tax. He was also given certain seizure powers for enforcement purposes. 

To defray the cost of functions, there was earmarked for the use of the Comptroller in administration and enforcement a fund of two per cent of the gross amount collected for the first 12 months after the effective date of the act and, thereafter, a fund of one per cent of gross collections (Sec. 21). 

By means of these and other provisions of the 1933 act, the Legislature took a long step toward achieving a workable tax law, toward stopping evasions, and toward effecting a firm tax adiministration. 

Earmarking of Revenues

However, the habit of earmarking revenues persisted. In addition to the two per cent fund created for the Comptroller, the legislators provided that all revenues from the tax (Sec. 3) were to go to the State Available 

School Fund for two years and, thereafter, one-half to the General Fund and one-half to the Available School Fund; whereas, all revenues from the sale of permits were to be equally divided between the General Fund and the 

Available School Fund (Sec. 4). 

Later in 1933, the First Called Session of the 43d Legislature re-enacted virtually the same legislation.10 However, all earlier acts pertaining to cigarette taxes and permits were repealed, several provisions apparently aimed at preventing further evasion of the tax were added, as were several enforcement control measures, and the administration and enforcement fund of the Comptroller was fixed at two per cent without the time clause of the earlier 1933 measure. This act had no mention of the levy as an occupation tax.

In May, 1933, too, the decalcomania stamp was adopted to replace the gummed type previously used."The effect of this change was salubrious 11 

9Tex. Laws 1933, 43d Legislature, R.S., ch. 153. 

10Tex. Laws 1933, 43d Legislature, 1st. C.S., ch. 90. 

11 Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conference, 1933.

and immediate, resulting in greatly improved enforcement and monthly increases in revenue of as much as $100,000. 

Two provisions intended to prevent evasion were added to the cigarette tax law in 1934. The first, approved February 19, 1934, pro- 12 vided crimimal penalties for use of counterfeit tax stamps. The other approved in March, 1934, provided means for regulating cigarette vending machines. 

Use Tax Clause Is Added 

On May 11, 1935, the Legislature again re-enacted the cigarette tax law, incorporating within it the two acts of 1934 and changing somewhat the 14 provisions of the comprehensive 1933 act.

1909 Most significant, perhaps, of the changes in the cigarette law contained in the 1935 act was the first inclusion of a "use clause." Whereas, the earlier acts had read: "There is hereby levied a tax on sales in intrastate commerce . of cigarettes. the 1935 act read; "A tax is hereby imposed on all cigarettes used or otherwise disposed of in this State for any purpose whatsoever...," and "First sale shall mean and include the first sale or distribution of cigarettes. or the first use or consumption of cigarettes within this State." This clause was upheld by the courts in 1936, when it was ruled that cigarettes are not in interstate commerce after delivery to the customer. A Cigarette Tax Stamp Board was created to take from the State 

Treasurer the burden of designing and manufacturing tax stamps (Sec. 3); the discount for stamp sales was reduced to 2.5 per cent (Sec .3); provisions were added requiring distributors to furnish reports to the Comptroller (Sec. 15d); several provisions were added to facilitate enforcement; and the administration and enforcement fund for the Comptroller was increased to 2.5 per cent of gross collections (Sec. 30). Disposition of taxes collected was changed: all revenues were allocated to the Available School 

Fund until September 1, 1935; thereafter, two-thirds were allotted to the 

School Fund and one-third to the General Fund. 

From 1931 to 1935, the basic provisions of the cigarette tax law remained unchanged except for the addition of the "use clause." However, the vague and indefinite provisions of the first tax act were made more definite; evasion loopholes in the law were closed; and a reasonably effective system of administration and enforcement was established. It was a period of adjustment and "tightening-up,''

Steps Toward Control of Mail-order Sales 

October, 1936, found the Legislature confronted with a need for funds to pay old age benefits Among other sources of revenue, it turned to the 

IZTex. Laws 1933, 43d Legislature, 2d C.S., H.B. 31, ch. 6.
