cigarette tax. Amending the 1935 law, it provided that one-third of the net revenue from the cigarette tax be credited to the Available School Fund and two-thirds to the Texas Old Age Assistance Fund. 16 In addition, the first direct steps were taken toward control of tax evasion by cigarette mail-order schemes.

Loss of revenue resulting from numerous injunctions granted by trial courts holding certain provisions of the law invalid, which provisions were later held valid by the Texas Supreme Court, resulted in another amendment to the cigarette tax law. 17 This amendment, effective August 

31, 1937, was designed to give the State adequate security against losses incurred by reason of such injunctions. At the same time, the discount in stamp sales was changed to four per cent; provision was made to facilitate certain types of sales of stamps; and more detailed provisions for control of mail-order sales were added. 

A few years later (1941), the Legislature authorized certain regulatory powers to the Treasurer to deal with refunds.18 

In 1941, the Legislature passed an omnibus tax law to provide revenues for old age assistance, aid to destitute children, aid to needy blind, and other expenses of the State.19 Following a customary procedure, the Legislature, to provide for the current need, amended the cigarette tax law by providing a different disposition of funds: one-fourth of the net revenues was allocated to the Available School Fund and three fourths to the newly-created Clearance Fund (Art. XX, Sec. 1). 

The same section prescribed that the Clearance Fund would be further allocated to the Blind Assistance Fund, the Children's Assistance 

Fund, the Teacher Retirement System, the Old Age Assistance Fund, and the General Revenue Fund. 

First Change in Tax Rate 

Except for the 1941 change in the allocation of funds collected from the cigarette tax and the addition of certain regulatory powers to the Treasurer in regard to refunds, the Legislature was apparently content with its product. No other changes in the law were made after 1937 for almost 13 years. Then, confronted with a need for new revenues for State Hospitals, the Legislature made the first rate change in the history of the tax.20 State The rate was boosted early in 1950 to two dollars per thousand on cigarettes weighing under three pounds per thousand (four cents per pack), and to $4.02 on cigarettes weighing more than three pounds. However, the temporarily increased rate was to be effective only until August 31, 1957. 

Other changes included, as might have been expected in view of the legislative history of the tax, a new allocation of revenues. The discount 

16 Tex. Laws 1936, 44th Legislature, 3d C.S., ch. 495, art. III. 

17 Tex. Laws 1937, 45th Legislature, R.S., ch. 310. 

18 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R.S., ch. 101. 19 

Ibid., ch. 184 20 

Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 1.

for stamp sales was changed to four per cent of three-quarters of face value, and the administrative fund for the Comptroller was changed to 2.5 per cent of three-quarters of the gross revenue. Each of these changes was limited to the period before August 31, 1957. 

Introduction of Controls on Metering Devices 

A section was added to the cigarette tax reflecting new developments in administration. This provided for the use and control of tax-metering devices.

Outstandingly significant, from the viewpoint of legal basis for the tax, was the statement (Sec. 7) in the 1950 act that read: "The tax herein levied is intended by the Legislature to be an excise or use tax and not an occupation tax..." In the 19-year history of the cigarette tax, the 

Legislature had made a complete about-face. The tax, which in 1931 was entitted and described as an occupation tax, was expressly designated "not an occupation tax' by the Legislature in 1950. Since the 1950 tax was essentially the same as that of 1931, this reversal indicated more of a break with the "occupation tax complex'' by the Legislature than a change in the law. However, the breaking-away from the occupation tax complex is still not complete, for the Legislature added to the 1950 law a qualifying clause, "In the event that any Court of competent jurisdiction shall declare the tax levied herein to be an occupation tax. . .," and then provided an alternate scheme for disposition of tax funds. Thus, it seems apparent that the Legislature was still unsure of having a general taxing power and was still concerned with the possibly limiting tax provisions of the Constitution 

(Art. VIII, Sec. 1), 7

SECTION 

ORGANIZATIONAL FORM 

General

Tobacco taxation, disregarding minor local variations and differences in terminology, usually takes the form of an excise or sales tax upon tobacco products in the channels of distribution. In the normal process of distribution, cigarettes and tobacco products move from the manufacturer to a wholesaler, then to jobbers or retailers, and from retailers to consumers or users. The basic pattern of each State tax is geared to this so-called normal distribution process. Within that process, the tax is generally imposed upon and paid by the wholesaler, or, if the products are received by the jobber or retailer directly from an out-of- State manufacturer, by this first recipient. The Federal Government imposes its tax at the manufacturer's level. 

Once the State tax has been paid upon a package of cigarettes or an item of tobacco, it is not again subject to cigarette or tobacco taxation. 

Texas imposes a tax on cigarettes but not on other tobacco products. Other States are about equally divided between those which tax other tobacco products in addition to cigarettes and those which do not. 

Cigars, smoking tobacco, snuff, chewing tobacco, tubes, and papers are included in the list of other items taxed under various State tobacco tax laws. 

The administration of the tax in Texas affects the operations of, and is primarily concerned with, the distributors, dealers, and solicitors in the cigarette business. In the 12-month period ending in July, 1950, this involved 74,271 retail dealers, 719 wholesale dealers, 414 distributors, and 

237 solicitors operating in the State. 

Administration Divided

The "vague and indefinite" provisions of the original cigarette tax act in 1931 gave very little indication of the organization desired, or necessary, for the administration and enforcement of the tax act. The 

State Treasurer was charged with having tax stamps engraved or printed, of selling them upon demand, and of being custodian of the stamps and proceeds from their sales.21 Subsequent legislation has concerned itself chiefly with enforcement and administration.

Thus, by 1950, considerable legislation had been enacted, most of it directed toward improving the operation of the tax law, which has remained virtually unchanged since its initial enactment.

Under current provisions of the law, primary responsibility for administration and enforcement is placed on the Comptroller.22 However, 21 Tex. Laws 1931, 42d Legislature, R.S., ch. 73. 22 

Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R.S., ch. 101.

the task of handling the bulk of the collections through the sale of tax stamps has remained with the State Treasurer, along with the collection through servicing of meters.

A third State agency charged with responsibilities in the operation of the cigarette tax is the Cigarette Tax Stamp Board, consisting of the members of the Board of Control.24 Its primary functions, according to the law, are designing and manufacturing tax stamps.

In addition to the Comptroller, Treasurer, and Tax Stamp Board, the State Auditor and Attorney General also have duties and responsibilities assigned them by the cigarette tax law. 

The division of activities among these agencies has been along these lines: the Treasurer handles the bulk of collections directly; the 

Comptroller, in a general way, can be considered as handling the assessment of the tax by virtue of his licensing authority and his responsibility for seeing that the tax is levied upon all who are liable for it; the Comptroller and Attorney General share responsibility for enforcement; the 

Tax Stamp Board handles procurement of the tax stamps; and the State 

Auditor, in addition to his general function of examining the activities of all departments periodically, is charged specifically with the duty of prescribing certain forms to be used in administration of the cigarette tax law.25

Viewed in this light, the organizational form of the administration of the cigarette tax law appears rather simple and justifiable, even though five different State departments are involved. Closer scrutiny, however, discloses factors which are not apparent to superficial observation and administrative practices which seem to deviate from the law in spirit, though not in letter.

Sharp delineations of function are always difficult to establish when several agencies or administrative units are involved. Administrative practice frequently prevails where the law is vague or where statutory division of responsibility is not clear. The cigarette tax law is an example, and administrative practice has in actuality predominated. 

Position of the Cigarette Stamp Tax Board 

In providing for the manufacture of tax stamps, the law quite logically gives this function to the Board of Control. Since centralized procurement was an established practice in the State, it followed that procurement personnel might be in the best position to supervise the manufacture of such stamps. 

23Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 1. 24 25 

Tex. Laws 1937, 45th Legislature, R.S., ch. 310. 

Tex. Laws 1937, 45th Legislature, R.S., ch. 310.

However, it is not evident as to why the Legislature felt that this procurement function required organization of the Board of Control into a Cigarette Stamp Tax Board instead of being handled in a more usual and routine fashion. It is also not clear why the Cigarette Stamp Tax Board was specifically given authority to determine denominations and quantities of new stamps to be printed, in view of the fact that the State Treasurer and the Comptroller maintain sales records and authorizations. 

In practice, the stamp procurement function is handled in a routine fashion by the Board of Control, usually by one of its members. The 

Cigarette Stamp Tax Board has functioned as such only nominally. The determination of denominations and quantities of stamps to be printed is made by the Cigarette Tax Division of the Comptroller's office and processed just as any other purchase request.

Changes of Stamp Design 

Although the Comptroller is vested with the responsibility for enforcement, the Legislature has written into the law the following: "The 

Board (Cigarette Stamp Tax Board) is hereby authorized to change the design of the stamps as often as it may deem such change necessary to 26 the best enforcement of the provisions of this Act . . 

In practice, it would seem that the board would have to rely on instructions or directives from the Comptroller to make the determination authorized in a manner and at the times best for enforcement purposes. Control of stamp design. is, of course, highly important to enforcement personnel. Identification of counterfeits cannot be made without it, and action against counterfeiting by means of timely changes in stamp design cannot be taken most effectively unless enforcement agencies have such control.

However, no difficulty has been experienced as a result of this provision of the law because only one change of stamp design occurred from 1935 to 1950 and, in this instance, the new design was initiated by the Comptroller's office. Here, again, is a situation in which a somewhat confusing provision of the law has been simply resolved in actual practice. 

However, in the statutes, the confusion caused by lack of delineation and duplication of authority was continued by another section of the 1935 law and a 1937 amendment to it. 

Supervision of Stamp Manufacture

The 1935 law reads as follows: "The Director of the Cigarette 

Tax Division (Comptroller's office) shall in addition to the duties of supervising and directing the administration and enforcement of the provisions of this Act, personally supervise the printing or manufacturing of all cigarette tax stamps .. also be charged with the responsibility of inspecting the stamps after such stamps have been manufactured or printed and 

26 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 1. 10

• · all stamps that do not meet the specifications required . . . shall be rejected and destroyed by or under the direct personal supervision of said Director; and the Director shall have control of said stamps and be responsible therefor until delivery is made to the Treasurer. 27 

In effect, the 44th Legislature, after evident recognition of the value of utilizing the central purchasing system afforded by the Board of Control, in large part negated the advantage by taking from the Board of Control (acting as Tax Stamp Board) responsibilities for technical supervision of stamp manufacture. At the same time, it gave the Board of 

Control (as Tax Stamp Board) authority to determine when to change stamp design, which, logically, is an integral part of the enforcement procedure previously made the responsibility of the Comptroller. 

The requirement that the director of the Cigarette Tax Division personally accomplish the duties enumerated in the law obviously was impractical, and the Legislature amended the law soon afterward, The new act read: "Whereas, it is impracticable for said Director to give his personal attention at all times to the printing of tax stamps because • • of numerous other duties required of him . . . and, whereas, it is to the best interest of the State . . . if a competent person experienced in printing is present at all times during the actual printing of said stamps and that it will afford better protection to the State if such person is permanently employed to . . . have charge of the stamps which have been printed, which at times reach a value of several hundred thousand dollars and which must be stored at the printing plant on account of lack of space in the Treasurer's vaults; therefore . 28 The act then went on to authorize the director of the Cigarette Tax Division of the State Comptroller's Department to designate a competent person experienced in printing to represent him during the printing of the tax stamps. 00 The Cigarette Tax Stamp Board was instructed to employ this person as soon as designated and was instructed to pay him from the funds appropriated to the State Treasurer by the General Departmental Appropriation Bill. These directions were set forth despite the fact that the employee was to be a personal representative of the director of the Cigarette Tax Division (Comptroller's office) and was to work for and with the Tax Stamp Board (Board of Control). 

As might be expected, a simpler practice has been worked out by the administrative agencies concerned. The personal representative of the 

Comptroller's director of the Cigarette Tax Division supervises the manufacture of the stamps and performs other tasks which might be assigned by the director. The Board of Control pays his salary and the cost of printing the stamps. This money is received from tax revenues before allocations to funds are made. Beyond this, the Board of Control enters the picture only to the extent of negotiating and placing contracts. 

27 Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Legislature, R.S., ch. 241, 28 Tex. Laws 1937, 45th Legislature, 2d C.S., ch. 67. 11

Administrative Practices Differ From Law 

Organizational aspects of the tax administration have been complicated by detailed and sometimes incompatible statutory enactments pertaining to functional allocations. Administrative practice has found it necessary at times to overlook details of the law in order to achieve a workable organization.

Custody of stamps between manufacture and sale could be reasonably expected to be the responsibility of either the purchasing agency (the Board of Control acting as the Tax Stamp Board) or the sales-collection agency (Treasurer). Yet the cigarette tax law makes such custody the responsibility of the personal representative of the director of the Cigarette Tax Division (Comptroller). In actual practice, stamps are packaged at the printer's establishment under the observation of the director's representative and are then delivered by the printer directly to the State Treasurer. 

In the matter of enforcement, the Attorney General actually does not inspect or audit records. This function is carried out entirely by the 

Comptroller's office, and the Attorney General enters the picture only when a suit is brought.

However, in regard to interpretation of the law, the opinions of the Attorney General have been quite frequent and significant. Rulings as to military exemptions, nature of the tax, permit requirements for multicounter establishments, transfers of permits, authority to permit use of metering devices, and authority of the Treasurer to make rules in reference to refunds for stamps already on cigarette packages returned to the manufacturer, all can be considered interpretations of major importance. 29 Several of these, outstandingly those regarding metering machines and refunds, have pointed up the specificity of the law and have resulted in amendments which have broadened the powers of the administrative agencies. 

29Opinions of Atty. Gen: No. 0-4291, March 6, 1942; No. 2264, May 20, 1940; unnumbered, April 3 and March 9, 1937; No. 0-196, December 18, 1939; 

No. V-773, February 15, 1949. 12

SECTION 

ASSESSMENT METHODS AND PROCEDURES 

General

The cigarette tax is levied on the first sale, use, or other disposition of cigarettes within the State. The levy is based on the weight of the cigarettes, with a current (1950) rate of $2.00 for cigarettes weighing less than three pounds per thousand, and a rate of $4.10 for those weighing more than three pounds per thousand. The tax is paid by the affixation, by the taxpayer, of the correct amount of stamps or proper meter impression to each pack of cigarettes.30

Classifications of Objects Taxed 

The two weight classifications are the only categories on which the tax is levied; there has been no change in this basis since the original tax was levied in 1931. These weight classifications were borrowed directly from the federal law, which employed the same basis. The basic tax is actually on the lower-weight group, which includes all the standard brands and amounts to four cents per package of 20 cigarettes. The tax on the higherweight group constitutes more of a regulatory than a revenue-producing tax, apparently aimed at sales of odd-size cigarettes manufactured to avoid the tax and novelty items.31 There have been no collections in five years in the higher-weight category, and, according to the Cigarette Tax Division, a total of less than $500 has been collected in the decade since 1940. 

Thus simply is the cigarette tax levied. However, within the broad classifications of the tax, several types of exemptions are permitted, and a system of tax stamp discounts is authorized. Exemptions

Exemptions apply to cigarettes brought into the State by the user in quantities of 40 (two standard packs) or less, to interstate distributors of cigarettes for that part of their stock used in interstate business, and to military personnel at post exchanges of the armed services. 

The initial cigarette tax law provided for no exemptions. In effect, it left to the courts the determination of exemptions from the law. This has been a common practice of State legislatures, and many State cigarette laws still contain no specific enumeration of exemptions. 

However, in Texas, legislative exemptions from the tax were included in the 1933 act, which permitted distributors to separate interstate stock from their other stock and obtain exemption for that portion involving 

30 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 1. 31 During the depression of the early 1930's, cigarettes several feet in length, though of standard diameter, were sold to avoid taxes based on quantities of cigarettes. These long cigarettes could be cut into standard lenths by the consumer. 13

interstate sales." In 1935, along with the "use clause," the Legislature added to the law the "personal user exemption" of 40 cigarettes.33 It is interesting to note that a similar exemption in New York allows 400 cigarettes (two cartons) to be brought into the State tax-free by the user. 

The coming of World War II brought the third specific legislaive exemption from the provisions of the tax law. 34 This was the exemption of military personnel purchasing cigarettes at post exchanges, motivated by the fact that "the levy of taxes on such exchanges imposes an unjust hardship upon the individual soldier now in the service of his country. 

The exemption was accomplished by the legislative recognition of post exchanges, for the purposes of this tax, as instrumentalities and agencies of the United States Government.35

Exemptions for Federal Instrumentalities 

Preceding this Texas action, Congress in 1940 passed legislation, commonly known as the Buck Act, which authorized the levy and collection of State sales and use taxes in a federal area, but prohibited the levy and collection of such taxes from any instrumentality of the Federal Government. The question remained, however, as to what was and what was not an instrumentality of the Federal Government. In the case of the cigarette tax, the Texas Legislature decreed that military post exchanges were in this category. 36 However, it is interesting to note that, even though post exchanges were exempt by legislative action, a troop train, regardless of its statue as a part of the U.S. Army. . . on military maneuvers'' was not exempt.37 In the same opinion, the American Red Cross was held to be an agency of the Federal Government and exempt from the tax on cigarette sales to soldiers, but the United Services Organization was not afforded this exemption.

Further military exemptions exist by virtue of a section of the Buck Act (Sec. 15b) which exempts sales to military personnel made by "voluntary unincorporated organizations of Army or Navy personnel." 

This has been ruled to include officers' and non-commissioned officers' clubs on military installations.

Confusion surrounding the matter of exemptions on sales to the Federal Government is not unique to Texas. Court rulings have not been entirely consistent in the long series of cases dealing with the matter of State taxation of federal instrumentalities.38 There appears some justification in support of the point of view that the tendency of the courts is toward elimination of the exemption of sales to the Federal Government. 

32 Tex. Laws 1933, 43d Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 90. 

33Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Legislature, R.S., ch. 241. 

34 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R.S., ch. 14. 

35 Tex. Laws 1943, 48th Legislature, R.S., ch. 275. 36 

3954 Stat. 1059 (1940). 37 38 

Opinion of Atty. Gen. to Crim. Dist. Atty. Fort Worth, No. 0-4291, March 6, 1942. 

Alabama v. King & Boozer, 314 U.S. 1, 1941. 14

The State of Illinois, as a matter of fact, has a rule promulgated by its Department of Revenue which provides that, effective January 1, 1948, "a distributor making sales or other disposition of cigarettes to the U. S. Government... incurs tax liability with respect to such sales.39 If upheld in the courts, this rule may help clarify a longconfused situation.

Thus, it can be seen that in spite of specific statutory exemption, actual determination of exemptions is quite largely in the hands of the courts and the Congress. Discounts

Like other States using stamps, Texas offers discounts to distributors in payment for costs incurred in performing the presumably governmental function of affixing stamps to the taxed article, In the fiscal year ending in 1949, discounts amounted to 1.5 million dollars. As pointed out in a recent study in Illinois, the unique aspect of such a provision is that little has been done to reimburse other persons or businesses 40 obliged to affix revenue stamps of a different type. South Carolina was the first State (1923) to adopt a discount for cigarette stamp sales, and all the others followed suit. The present (1950) discount rate in Texas is four per cent of three-quarters of the face value of stamps purchased. The same discount applies when meter impressions are used, Since the theory of a discount presupposes a justification for reimbursement of the distributor for affixing the tax stamps, it might well be supposed that there should be some relationship between the discount and the added costs incurred by dealers performing stamp affixation. However, discounts among the several States range from two to ten per cent. Obviously, the actual costs incurred have not been related to discounts authorized by law. The most popular discount rates among States are five and ten per cent. Certainly, if the object of the discount is to reimburse the dealer for affixing stamps, one or possibly both groups of States must be in error.

Michigan, which uses the inventory method of collection rather than the stamp method, offered no discount prior to 1949. By statutory amendment at that time, however, wholesalers were allowed to deduct a discount of one per cent as compensation for their services. 41 Studies of costs incurred by dealers in affixation of tax stamps have indicated that the expense of affixing stamps will vary but little with the tax rate. Thus, an increase in rate should be accompanied by a reduction of the discount percentage wise, though not absolutely. The Texas Legislature apparently recognized this factor in the 1950 change in tax rate, 39Illinois Department of Revenue, Rules Relating to Cigarette Tax Act, art. 

18. 

40Report of the Revenue Laws Commission, Illinois, 1949. 41 

Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conferences, 1942, 1945.. 15

which reduced the discount from four per cent, prevailing with the previous three-cent-per-package tax, to four per cent of three-quarters with the new four-cent-per-package tax.42 Thus, the actual amount of discount for a case (500 packages) remained at 60 cents. Although available studies of the costs of affixing stamps admit the difficulty of getting reliable data, the Texas rate at present does not appear out of line with the cost experience of most dealers and distributors. The range of costs, as shown by several studies, is from 11 cents to $1.50 per case, but the costs of a majority of distributors evidently fall between 45 cents and 65 cents per case.

Because the large distributor can, presumably, effect greater economies in his stamping operations than the smaller distributor, suggestions have been made occasionally to grant larger discounts to the small distributor. New York at present has such a sliding scale, providing for a four per cent discount for stamp purchases of less than $450,000 and a 3.8 per cent discount for larger purchases. Nebraska for a time provided for a smaller discount when stamping was done with metering devices but changed in 1949 to a straight five per cent discount for both stamps and meters. Because meters are leased by distributors and are usually required in pairs so that one can be in use while the other is sent to the Treasurer, and because the distributor bears the cost of shipping meters to the State Treasurer, it is generally recognized that metering is no less expensive than the stamp method.

Perhaps more basic than the entire matter of amount and detail of discounts is the question of whether any discount is desirable or justified in view of the fact that such procedure has not been adopted for other tax stamps and that discounts amount to a substantial portion of the tax revenue collected. From the viewpoint of the taxpayer, the tax bill is really the total of what is paid to the State plus the cost for making this payment. The cost is usually shifted to someone else by the taxpayer when possible, usually to the ultimate purchaser. In this case, however, the cost is partially shifted to the State through the discount. In effect, the discount simply lowers the tax rate.

From this viewpoint, the discount procedure appears merely a complicated device to lower the tax rate. A number of tax administrators, ... including some in Texas, feel that a complete elimination of discounts would be feasible even with stamp and meter methods of collection, since the cost would simply be passed to the consumer. That such minor adjustments in price (amounting to .12 of one cent per package in Texas), if passed on to the consumer, would cause consumer resistance to cigarette purchases seems improbable in view of the complete lack of such effect from the siz able rate increases throughout the country in recent years. Cigarette consumption has continued to mount year by year in spite of price increases of from one to several cents in virtually all States.43 

42 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1 43 

Annual Report on Tobacco Statistics, 1949, U.S. Department of Agricul ture, CS-39 (December, 1949) p. 50. 16

Loss Allowance and Refunds

To provide relief for the distributor who has suffered damage to stocks of cigarettes already stamped, a method of refunds has been authorized, permitting the Treasurer considerable discretion in the matter of stamp exchanges. According to the 1941 law granting the Treasurer certain powers, this action was necessary because.....the Attorney General has held that the Treasurer does not now have authority under existing law to make rules and regulations to provide for the exchange or replacement of stamps on those cigarettes which have become damaged or unfit ..44 for use, or returned to the manufacturer 4 O Although the law does not prescribe the method for such refunds, regulations by the Treasurer (Rule 1) have adopted a version of the affidavit method. These regulations state that, "Any distributor who desires to return to the manufacturer unsalable cigarettes ., shall remove the stamps from the cigarettes by cutting the cellophane or wrapper around the 

Stamps and retura such stamps to. the Treasurer. accompanied by an affidavit... that such stamps were removed from unsalable cigarettes which have been shipped to the manufacturer... A credit memorandum from the. manufacturer must accompany the stamps. 

Affidavit Method 0 0 

Of the three commonly-used methods for refunding the defacement method, inspection method, and affidavit method -- the latter is least expensive. In addition to Texas, New York and Connecticut also employ this method. It is favored not only because of its relatively cheap administration but also because strict records required of manufacturers by the Federal Government assure reasonable accuracy on credit memoranda. All three methods, with a number of variations, are used by the several States with cigarette tax laws. No uniform practice exists.

The provision requiring that stamps be removed from packages before return to the manufacturer appears to be unique to Texas and is open to some question as to whether it assures the distributor's integrity more positively than would affidavit alone. For that reason and because of the fact that the practice of cutting off the stamps and mailing them to the Treasurer was impractical in application and virtually ineffective, the refund procedure is modified in actual practice. A simple affidavit method is now used, although no new rule has been published. 

The 1950 amendment to the law injects a confusing note into the refund procedure by giving the Comptroller, instead of the Treasurer, authority to determine how refunds on unsalable cigarettes should be made. 

It reads: ''. . . Stamps removed from cigarettes determined by the Comptroller to be unsalable may be redeemed under rules and regulations 

44 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R.S., ch. 101. 45 

Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conference, 1947. 17

promulgated... by the Comptroller."46 Since this amendment is effective only until 1957 and no provision has been made to keep the refund function in the Comptroller's hands after this date, the intent of the Legislature is not clear. In practice, the refund function has been left with the Treasurer. 

46 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 1. 18

SECTION - COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

General

It is always difficult to separate the functions of collection and enforcement. With regard to the cigarette tax, however, it is perhaps possible to consider collection separately. The principal method of collection is by stamps, sold by the Treasurer and affixed to each package 47 of cigarettes by the distributor or dealer making the first sale. Decalcomania stamps have been used since May, 1933, and were the only authorized stamp forms until meter-stamping devices were authorized by the Legislature in 1950:

According to the statutes, the work of collecting the cigarette 48 tax is divided between the State Treasurer and the Comptroller. The 

Comptroller is charged with the duty "to collect, supervise, and enforce the collection" of the tax. However, the Treasurer actually collects the major portion of the revenues by virtue of the provisions (Sec. 2) stating that: "Payment of such tax shall be evidenced by stamps purchased from the Treasurer and.. affixed to each package .. Cigarette stamps shall be sold by the Treasurer in unbroken sheets of one hundred (100) stamps only and shall be purchased from and sold only by said Treasurer. 149 It is evident, of course, that although it is so provided in the law, the Comptroller does not supervise collections made by the Treasurer. In the case of meter-stamping machines, the settings are made by the Treasurer. 50 In the matter of enforcement, the law again divides the function. 

Although primary responsibility is ostensibly given the Comptroller, who is authorized certain police powers, 51 the Treasurer, through his control of stamp sales, also plays a part, and the Attorney General is given certain inspection authority. In practice, however, he does not exercise this power. 47 

Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conference, 1933. 

48 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R. S., ch. 101. 49 

Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Legislature, R. S., ch. 241. 

50 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch:.1. 51 The term "police powers" is used in this discussion in its broad and popular connotation and includes, among others, the right to inspect records and premises and the right to require reports. It is understood, of course, that it is frequently used in a much more restricted sense. 19

Co-ordination of Agencies

The successful administration of a law which so divides responsibility.for discharging the necessary duties would seem to require extremely close co-ordination of the activities of the State Treasury, the Comptroller, and the other agencies involved. To that end, the Legislature has written into the law several provisions designed to insure co-prdination: or to any ,,52 a) The Treasurer "may refuse to sell stamps to any person who has not obtained a permit. distributor whose permit has been revoked. 

Seemingly, it is up to the discretion of the Treasurer whether or not to sell stamps in such circumstances. b) The Treasurer, upon receipt of an affidavit from the Comptroller setting forth a violation of the law, "shall refuse to supply stamps at the discount provided, c) The Treasurer "shall keep a record of all stamps sold by him. of all stamps exchanged by him, and of all refunds made . 1153 d) The Treasurer has the duty to invoice stamps ordered by purchasers, and to send a duplicate of the invoice to the Comptroller.

It is clear that the intent of the Legislature throughout the development of this tax has been to facilitate well co-ordinated administration. It has attempted to achieve this aim by means of detailed legislative prescription of methods and procedures.

However, the difficulties of successful co-ordination inherent in a division of administrative responsibility among several agencies have not been overcome. These difficulties have caused considerable administrative detail to be written into the law. This, in turn, has resulted in occasional inconsistencies and duplication of effort. From this situation have arisen several administrative practices which are in implicit contradiction to details of the law. 52 

Tex. Laws 1937, 45th Legislature, R.S., ch. 310 

53 Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Legislature, R.S.., ch. 241 20

Administrative Detail Prescribed 

Those

Administrative details written into the law are numerous. details are not limited to a few sections of the law but are prevalent throughout. Instead of simply designating the Comptroller as administrator and providing him with broad powers to carry out this responsibility --e.g., the right to require necessary reports and records, the right to promulgate rules, right to conduct audits and inspections, right of seizure in cases of violation, and right to issue and revoke licenses, etc. --the law continued to state in considerable detail how this responsibility is to be carried out--e.g., how many copies were to be made of reports, items of information to be included in records, etc. 

As an example, the preparation of invoices for stamp purchases is covered exhaustively in the law. They are to be issued in triplicate, numbered consecutively, contain seven prescribed items of information, be signed by the Treasurer, and have the original and copies distributed as prescribed in the law. It is not clear what. discretion, if any, was intended by the law to be permitted the administrators. 

Thus the practice of statutory prescription of administrative procedures has evolved. The result has been a paucity of administrative interpretation of the law. To be sure, administrative rules concerning the mechanics of administration have interpreted and implemented some of the provisions of the law, but these rules have usually been narrow in 54 scope. Generally, they have covered such matters as location of the stamp on cigarette package, restrictions on personal checks for stamp payments, etc.

Statutory, Inconsistencies 

One of the apparent inconsistencies mentioned above is the provision which gives the Cigarette Stamp Tax Board authority to change the design of the stamps when it deems such a change necessary for the best enforcement of the law (Sec. 3. 1), although the Comptroller is specifically charged 55 with responsibility for enforcement. As previously discussed, administrative practice: has, in effect, vested this function in the Comptroller. 54 

Rule 1, Comptroller; Rule 1b, Treasurer. 55 

Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1. 21

Another inconsistency appears in the 1950 amendment regarding certain types of refunds. 

Without amending or voiding Sec. 

24c of the cigarette tax law, which was added in 1941, 56 the 1950 amendment temporarily changed Sec. 3 of the law (until 1957). The 1941 act (Sec. 24c) stated: "The Treasurer shall promulgate rules and regulations providing for the exchange . . of new stamps for any stamps affixed to cigarettes, which cigarettes have become unfit for use.. The 

1950 act (amending original Sec. 3) reads: "I 

Stamps removed from cigarettes determined by the Comptroller to be unsalable may be redeemed under rules and regulations promulgated. by the Comptroller."57 

It appears that the doctrine of implied repeal would apply in this case. 

However, the provisions of the 1941 act are still being followed. 

The difficulties of achieving efficient co-ordination among the agencies involved in the administration of the law are considerable because of these apparent inconsistencies. These are further aggravated by the mass of administrative detail prescribed in the law. Such detail makes it difficult at times to determine the desired policy. As a result, the evident statutory policy to facilitate well co-ordinated administration of the tax law has not been entirely successful. 

Duplications of Effort

Duplications of effort are noticeable in the record-keeping functions of the Treasurer and the Comptroller. Similar records,and in some instances, duplicate copies of the same document, are maintained in both offices. Requests for information made to one office are sometimes answered by reference to the records of the other. In short, although laudable efforts have been made, complete co-ordination has been ham pered by the division of the administrative function among several agencies. 

In contrast to the Texas administration, that in a number of other States is much more simple and centralized. Many States have a single agency responsible for all phases of the administration, supervision, collection, and enforcement of the cigarette and tobacco taxes. Each of these central agencies is vested with responsibility and authority to procure and sell stamps, collect tax payments, issue licenses and collect any fees therefor, and to receive and audit reports from cigarette dealers and distributors. 

Each agency has been expressly authorized by statute or has assumed authority to issue interpretative and administrative regulations concerning procedures for enforcing and collecting the tax. 56 57 

Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R. S., ch. 101. 

Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1. 22

Periodic Audits and Inspections Implied 

Although much administrative detail is written into the law governing reports and records, there is almost no statutory directive pertaining to the use of them. An inference that periodic inspections are expected of the Comptroller is contained in the provision (Sec. 7c) that records be maintained for two years "for the inspection at all times of the Comptroller .." Another inference can be taken from the provision (Sec. 8) that "the Comptroller may employ auditors or other persons to ascertain the correct amount due" in cases of delinquency or failure to pay taxes.

Though permitted little or no discretion in the matter of information to be required of distributors and dealers, the Comptroller is left entirely to his own judgment concerning such matters as types and frequencies of audits and inspections, types and content of secondary records, methods for reconciliation of records, etc. These matters appear of equal or greater significance than the items of information to be included in reports and the number of carbon copies to be required. Here again is the previously discussed difficulty, wherein the law prescribes an unusual amount of administrative detail and yet beaves some questions of policy largely unanswered. 

The Comptroller's Field Force

The actual work of audits and inspections is performed by a field force of 49 men from the Cigarette Tax Division of the Comptroller's office. The State is divided into nine districts, each comprising several counties. Each field representative is assigned certain counties, as indicated in Figure Cig-1.

Although definite schedules of audits and inspections are not maintained, each permit holder is visited at least once a year. Field men are responsible for administration of the coin machine license law and other taxes in addition to the cigarette tax and, consequently, cannot devote full time to enforcement of the levy on cigarettes. However, the number of cigarette distributors has been declining regularly since the war, and this has had a tendency to ease the audit situation. 

One problem faced by the tax administrator is dealers' failure to make prompt permit renewals. In spite of a constant informational program aimed toward encouraging permit renewals by mail before their expiration date, most renewals are handled by the field force after the delinquency date, Considerable time is, of course, required for this procedure. It is generally agreed that court action does not contribute materially to a solution of this situation frpm a practical viewpoint, 25

since amounts involved are usually small and itc has been the common observation that juries rarely are inclined to penalize such offenders. 

Some administrators feel that the underlying cause for widespread delinquency in permit renewals (amounting to 33 per cent during the fiscal year 1947-48) is due to the very low cost of most permits and lack of any provision for automatic increase in the cost of permits after the due date. 

Some persons also feel that a provision requiring that renewals be made by mail at the Austin office would permit the field force to devote more time to other matters. 

Police Powers

From the first year of the tax, considerable revenue has flowed into the State Treasury. At the same time, the amount of money involved has provided a substantial financial inducement for evasion, The need for police powers to prevent evasionchas been voiced by tax administrators in 63 many States. However, considerable lack of uniformity exists in this matter among the several States. Some tax administrators, as in Wisconsin, have authority to arrest and retain violators. In Massachusetts, administrative agents may be appointed as special State police officers with power to arrest violators of the cigarette tax law. As many as six have been so appointed at one time.

This relatively broad delegation of police power to the tax administrators is a continuation of the tendency to centralize tax administration within one agency. Instead of having a single agency collect the tax by stamp sales, another regulate by issuance of permits, and still a: third enforce the tax law, several States have combined these functions in one agency.

In Texas, police powers have not been authorized so great an extent as in some other States. In general, police powers of the Texas 

Comptroller are: first, the authority to license; second, the authority to suspend or revoke licenses for violations; third, the authority to confiscate unstamped cigarettes; and fourth, the right to require reports and records and to examine records and places of business. 64 

63 Proceedings, National Tobacco Tax Conference, 1946. 

64 Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Legislature, R. S., ch. 241. 26

Delegation of police powers, however, has not been the only step to eliminate tax evasion and facilitate enforcement. One area of activity which has been widely explored by several States to facilitate enforcement is that of methods of stamping or otherwise accounting for all cigarettes on which tax has been paid.

Stamp Method of Collection 

All but two of the tobacco-taxing States use some type of stamp or impression affixed to individual cigarette packages as evidence of tax payment. Stamps are usually of the gum-label or the decalcomania type or, in recent years, the meter-impression type. As previously mentioned, Texas had employed the decalcomania stamp since 1933, after a relatively unsuccessful trial of the gum-label type in the earlier years of the tax. 

Like many other States, stamps may be purchased in Texas from only one agency. Illinois, Florida, and New York permit sales through branch offices or designated agents. Stamp sales by one individual to another are generally prohibited throughout the country. Advantages of the stamp and meter methods are several: inspection of dealers' stocks and of vending machines is facilitated; cigarettes on which tax has not been paid can easily be detected; and collection of the tax by stamp sales is relatively simple. 

However, there are also a number of disadvantages. Outstanding among these, perhaps, is the need for the expensive and time-consuming operation of opening cases and cartons to stamp individual packages. The cost of affixing stamps is the reason, ostensibly, for the use of discounts, which represent a sizable portion of cigarette tax revenue. Several efforts have been made to eliminate or modify this time-consuming procedure. 65 

Metering Devices

A number of States have in recent years permitted the use of stamp-metering devices. The first of these machines was introduced by the Pitney-Bowes Company in 1933, and its use has been extended to 32 States, Texas passed legislation permitting the use of such devices 66 in 1950. This relatively new method does not eliminate the opening of cases and cartons but does lessen considerably the work involved. 65 

Fortune, August, 1950. 66 

Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1. 27

Meters are leased, not bought, by distributors and are generally used in pairs so that one will be available for metering while the other is sent to the Treasurer for setting. Express costs of sending meters to the Treasurer and having them returned is borne by distributors. 

In this connection, the split responsibility for administration of the tax again appears as a problem. Many distributors have expressed the desire to have meter settings made by field men when audits or inspections are made. However, the field men are on the Comptroller's staff, whereas the law vests the Treasurer with responsibility for meter settings. 

Therefore, all meters must be sent to Austin to be set by the Treasurer's office. It is interesting to note that several States, including Illinois, Florida, and Nebraska, permit branch offices or agencies of the central collection authority to make meter settings.

In some States, more collections are obtained from meter impressions. 

The ratio of stamps to meter impressions in Texas can be seen from the June, 1950, record when 40 million stamps and 33 million meter impressions were sold by the Treasurer. Present indications suggest that the trend toward meters will increase throughout the country. Their popularity results from the advantage they afford the large distributor, who need no longer train and supervise large numbers of employees to affix stamps. However, as has previously been discussed in connection with discounts, the cost of metering is generally considered as great as that for affixing stamps. For small operators, meters are definitely not economical. Therefore, the use of meters will undoubtedly increase, although not particularly rapidly, in coming years. There are also indications that the vending machine companies prefer the decalcomania stamp to the meter for mechanical reasons. Too, administrators indicate that counterfeiting is easier with metering devices than with stamps, although meters have been constantly improved and impressions have been made clear and distinctive. 67 A recent letter from the Louisiana Collector of Revenue reports that that State has found rubber stamps which are used to make counterfeit meter impressions.

Counterfeiting of stamps--and more recently of meter impressions -has long been a serious problem of cigarette tax administrators. In Texas, reuse and counterfeiting of the gummed stamp was prevalent in the early 68 years of the tax.

Adoption of the decalcomania stamp in May, 1933, reduced the counterfeiting problem materially. Accompanying this change was 

67 Proceedings, National Tobacco Conference, 1946. 68 

Ibid., 1933. 28

a provision of the 1933 law which prohibited stamp resale. Together, these changes, though they did not entirely eliminate counterfeiting, reduced it to comparative insignificance. Whether the recent adoption of metering devices will increase counterfeiting cannot be known until some experience with the new device is accumulated. So far, the Comptroller's office in 

Texas had discovered no counterfeiting of meter impressions and, because of the carefully planned and efficiently functioning control system which has been developed, little or na: trouble is anticipated. Layout forms, design variations, and the like permit quick and easy identification of counterfeit impressions.

Inventory Method of Collection 

Most revolutionary of the efforts to find a solution to the expensive stamping method is that made in Massachusetts and Michigan. This innovation is known as the inventory method of collection. Stamps are not used because the tax is assessed on reported inventories of dealers and distributors. Because it is much less expensive to administer and eliminates the discount procedure, this method is staunchly defended by its pro- 70 ponents. Reporting requirements of the State and Federal Governments, they say, preclude appreciable evasion. Inspections and audits reveal discrepancies. Experience of these two States is being observed with considerable interest by tax administrators and legislators throughout the country. 

In 1949, though continuing the inventory method, Michigan authorized a one-per-cent discount. Addition of such discount, even at a substantially lower rate than that prevalent in the stamp States, tends to minimize the advantages claimed by the proponents of the inventory control system. 

Manifestly, the inventory collection method requires extensive inspections and audits to insure compliance. However, Massachusetts, the pioneer of the inventory method, has reported that collection costs were reduced $700,000 below the cost for collection of the same revenue by the stamp method. 71 This estimate considered the cost of stamps, cost of stamp sales and distribution, and discounts to distributors. Savings to distributors, who are relieved under the inventory method of the necessity for opening cases and cartons, affixing stamps, and then repacking cases and cartons, appear significant. 69 

Tex. Laws 1933, 43d Legislature, 1st C.S., ch. 90. 

70 Proceedings, National Tobacco Conference, 1943. 71 Ibid... 29

The appreciable decrease since the early post-war years in number of cigarette distributors in Texas is significant in this connection. 

The trend has quite definitely been toward concentration in the hands of large distributors. 

Interstate Shipments

Although the problem of tax evasion has been discussed in relation to stamp counterfeiting and exemptions and has been implied in discussions of various enforcement provisions of the law, the specific evasion problem brought about by interstate cigarette shipments from non-tax States has been of such magnitude as to require separate consideration. 

All cigarette-tax States have been confronted with the problem resulting from parcel post shipments of cigarettes from non-tax States to pur chasers in States levying a tax. Well organized and efficient, the mail-order business houses engaging in this traffic advertised widely throughout tax States. Loss of revenue to taxing States has been considerable. Estimates of such loss ranged as high as 3 million dollars annually in some States, as indicated in 1948 hearings on the Jenkins Rogers Bill.

Co-operative efforts among the tax States, including exchange of information on interstate shipments, co-operation of tobacco manufacturers and wholesalers, enactment of use taxes, and stringent enactments against possession of unstamped cigarettes have decreased losses from this type of evasion considerably. However, the practice persisted, and the demand of the State tax administrators for federal assistance finally (in 1949) resulted in the passage of the Jenkins Act, requiring that a copy of the invoice of each shipment of cigarettes into a State (other than to licensed distributors) be sent to the tobacco tax administrator of the State to which shipment was made. 72 In July, 1950, the act was being tested in courts in the District of Columbia and Illinois.

If this act withstands attack, as most administrators feel that it will, it is generally expected that the problem of evasion by interstate shipment will be solved. Should such be the case, a number of administrators feel that present objections to the widespread adoption of the inventory collection method will be unfounded and that wider use of the method will result. 72 

Public Law 363, 81st Congress, effective October 19, 1949. 30

In view of the diverse collection and enforcement methods used by the various States and considering the problems confronting tax administrators, Texas appears to be on a substantial middle-ground. Use of the popular, hard-to-counterfeit decalcomania stamps plus protective provisions of the law and a reasonable and effective scale of permit fees have made possible a reasonably tight administration. Approval of the meter-stamping device was given by the Legislature apparently only after careful consideration of the experience of other States. Police powers of a limited nature have been granted tax administrators. Although not so extensive as in a few 

States, these powers have proved adequate in most situations and conform with the practices employed by most other States with a similar tax. 

Earmarking of Funds

Although not strictly a part of the collection procedure, the disposition of revenues immediately follows actual tax collections. Since disposition of funds has been made a part of the cigarette tax law since initial enactment, it should be given careful consideration. 

In recent amendments to the cigarette tax law, earmarking of revenues reaches a new height of complexity. It also carefully takes into account one of the uncertain areas of constitutional law 73 Ostensibly to meet the "compelling and immediate need" of providing adequate buildings to house the inmates of State hospitals and special schools, the 1950 amendment increased the cigarette tax rate. 

Here is how it is spelled out in the law: a) In addition to retaining deductions for the cost of manufacturing tax stamps, a fund of two and one-half per cent of three-fourths of the gross revenues from the tax was authorized to the Comptroller for administration. This allotment varied only percentagewise from previous administrative fund provisions. b) After earmarking this share of revenues from gross collections, the following distribution and restrictions were ordered for the remainder, to be effective until September 1, 1957: 

1) One-fourth of net revenues from cigarettes weighing less than three pounds per thousand, and 74 73 

See discussion on "The. Question of General Taxing Power" in Sec. 1. 

74 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 1. 31

2) Five-forty-firsts (5/41) of net revenues from cigarettes weighing more than three pounds per thousand, to be credited to the newly created 

State Hospitals and Special Schools Building Fund. 

(It is recalled that no revenue has been collected in five years from this category and less than $500 has been collected since 1940.) 

3) One-fourth of the balance remaining to be credited to the Available School Fund, and

Three-fourths of the balance to be credited to the 

Clearance. Fund. Provided, however, that 

5) Not in excess of five million dollars be credited to the State Hospitals and Special Schools Building 

Fund for the biennium ending August 31, 1951, and 

6) Not in excess of five million dollars be credited to this fund each fiscal year thereafter. (By virtue of this limitation and in view of a total cigarette tax collection estimated conservatively at more than 30 million dollars annually under the new tax rate, the five-million-dollar ceiling for the State Hospitals and Special Schools Building 

Fund is the actual limit to the amount which will go to the new fund.) 

7) Any balance in excess of five million dollars in any fiscal year shall be transferred to the State 

Hospital Fund. 

8) Funds credited to the State Hospitals and Special:

Schools Building Fund shall not be spent after Aus: c gust 31, 1951, unless appropriated by an act of the Legislature.

To effect this distribution and avoid the restrictions of the Constitutional provision (Art. VII, Sec. 3) which requires one-fourth of all revenues derived from occupation taxes to be set apart for the benefit of public schools, the Legislature wrote into the amendment the words, "The tax herein levied is intended . . . to be an excise or use tax and not an occupation tax. . ." But in spite of this protective declaration, the law 32

was evidently still felt to be on insecure constitutional ground and was carefully hedged with the following separability expressions: "In the event that any Court of competent jurisdiction shall declare the tax levied herein to be an occupation tax, it is hereby specifically declared to be the intention of the Legislature that such holding shall not affect the validity of the remaining provisions, and, in such event, "One-fourth of the net revenue shall be allocated to the Available 

School Fund. 

"One-fourth of the balance of the net revenue derived from the tax levied... shall be allocated to the State Hospitals and Special Schools 

Building Fund, and "Three-fourths shall be allocated to the Clearance Fund.

Should it be held that this is an occupation tax, the five million dollar ceiling evidently does not apply to money going to the State Hospitals and Special Schools Building Fund. Considering anticipated revenues in coming years, this fund would probably receive more if the tax were held to be an occupation tax than if it were held valid as written--yet it was written ostensibly for the purpose of providing money for the new fund! 

The Legislature can, of course, set up whatever plan of distribution it thinks best, but all the reasons for this complex and awkward pattern of distribution of revenues are not clear. Obviously, there should be a correlation between the needs of the public schools, the State hospitals, and the amount of revenue furnished them. However, since revenue furnished these agencies is determined by a percentage of receipts from the cigarette tax, this earmarking procedure impliedly presupposes some positive correlation between the estimated receipts from the tax and the revenue needs of schools or hospitals. Manifestly, such a correlation would be difficult to substantiate. 

The provision most difficult to understand is that which allocates different amounts of money to State hospitals, depending upon whether a court rules the act to be an occupation tax. In effect, the law states that if the act stands as written, the money requirements of the State hospitals will be approximately one-fourth of the revenues (not to exceed five million dollars), but if the tax is held to be an occupation tax, then the State hospitals should receive a lesser percentage. Actually, in the latter case, as has been mentioned, the amount to go to that particular fund would probably be greater because no ceiling is imposed. 33

Such involved earmarking of revenues from specific taxes tends to tie the hands of the Legislature in the control of expenditures. It bases legislative control upon percentages of unknown quanitites of revenues rather than upon actual money cost of functions. If tax collections drop, as they did for cigarettes in 1945 when returns were more than a million dollars less than for the previous year, needed State functions would suffer, not in a proportion determined by the Legislature, but in proportion to the cigarette purchases made by the smoking population. On the other hand, a large increase of revenue such as occured in 1946 when the cigarette tax yield was almost seven million dollars more than for the preceding year, might well cause allocations of money to certain State functions in excess of needs. From a practical point of view, of course, this earmarking is explainable on the grounds of convenience. 

Although the practice of earmarking cigarette tax revenue is not unique to Texas, and several other States have a similarly involved scheme for revenue disposition, most States with such a tax provide that all revenues be deposited in the General Revenue Fund, from which appropriations are made on the basis of budgetary need. 

The following table shows the earmarking prescriptions for cigarette tax revenues during the entire history of the tax: Act

SECTION - ANALYSIS OF OPERATION Administrative Costs 

In addition to total revenues collected, administration and enforcement costs are probably the items which most interest students of any particular tax. However, unlike figures for total collections, cost data are not easily available. Where they do exist, differences in cost items included, differences in kind of enforcement, and the like make comparisons based upon such figures definitely questionable. 

Collection costs include wages, salaries, supplies, maintenance, rent, and various other charges that would be unnecessary if the tax were not collected. Generally, collection costs for the cigarette tax among the several 

States_range from about one-half of one per cent to two per cent of collec tions.75 Texas showed a collection cost of slightly more than 1.3 per cent in 

1948 and has been close to this percentage for a number of years. However, Texas figures do not include cost to the Treasury of stamp shipments nor salary overhead. What elements of cost are omitted from figures of other 

States is not known. Limitations of time and personnel precluded extensive research into the matter for this survey. ......

It is interesting to note that the no-stamp States--Massachusetts and Michigan--maintain that their collection costs are less than one-fourth of one per cent. This, in large part, is attributable to the cost of stamps, which in 

Texas accounts for more than $100,000 of expense. 

Analysis of Rates 

At present (1950), Texas is in the group with cigarette tax rates above the average for States having such taxes. Rates range from one cent per package in West Virginia and the District of Columbia to eight cents in Louisiana. The most common rate is three cents per package, effective in 

16 States.

Seven States have higher rates than Texas; seven in addition to Texas have the four-cent rate; and 25 have lower rates than Texas. The rate tendency in recent years has been upward in virtually all cigarette tax States. In 1948, only eight States had rates of four cents or higher; now there are 15. In 1936, only three States had rates of four cents or higher, and half of the States with such taxes had rates of one or two cents. 75.

Report of the Revenue Commission, Illinois (1949) p. 503. 36

Analysis of Returns 

A number of factors must be considered in any evaluation of revenues collected from the cigarette tax. The most significant is, of course, the tax rate. Another is the population of the State. Obviously, a populous State can realize a greater yield. To permit State-by-State comparisons which take into account these two factors, the statistical devices of collections per one cent of tax rate and of per capita. collections are generally used. 

Application of these popular evaluation devices to the total revenues collected from the cigarette tax produce interesting comparisons. As shown in Table Cig-1, not only have total annual collections from this tax risen sharply, but there has been a corresponding increase in per capita. collections. 

With no change in the tax rate during the years considered, the collections in per capita increase reflects an increase in the number of cigarette smokers or greater consumption by each smoker, or both.

A consideration of national statistics in this connection is interesting. 

All State tobacco tax revenues increased 127 per cent from 1941 to 1947 and rose from three to four per cent of total State tax revenues in the same period. 

Increased urbanization of a State is said to result in increased consumption of cigarettes. At any rate, the more urbanized areas almost invariably show higher per capital consumption figures than do rural areas. Therefore, it may, perhaps be assumed that increased urbanization in Texas underlies to some extent the stead rise in per capita revenues from the cigarette tax. There has been no conclusive research on this subject, however, and some authorities feel that the correlation between urban population and cigarette consumption has been overrated.76 Nevertheless, they do not deny that a correlation does exist to some extent.

In general, it is perhaps safe to assume that continued urbanization in Texas, coupled with the apparent spread of cigarette smoking to new population groups, will have a tendency to raise per capita collections year by year. As the growth of urban areas slows, however, per capita collections will probably show a tendency to remain more constant, except for the effects of tax rate changes.

With this in mind, a comparison of per capita cigarette tax revenues can give some indication of the relative burden this tax imposes in the several States. As shown in Table Cig-2, Texas derives next to the lowest per 76 

Proceedings, National Tobacco Conference, 1946. 37

capita return of the nine States shown (1949). Except for Nebraska, whose low return might well be expected in view of its predominantly rural population, Texas is well below the others and considerably below the 1949 average for these nine States.

On the basis of a larger group of States and an earlier period, as shown in Table Cig-3, Texas is somewhat above the average but still a considerable distance from the top per capita totals collected by the several States. Hence, it can be concluded that the "effort" expended by Texas in obtaining revenues from this tax is not as great as in numerous other States. Since many authorities feel that the demand for cigarettes is relatively insensitive to fluctuations in income and to changes in price," the results of the 1950 rate increase will be interesting to note for substantiation or refutation of this assumption.77 66 Comparing per capita collections per one cent of tax rate permits an analysis of tax revenues in the various States to show relative efficiency of collection, or as some administrators prefer to call it, the relative com-. pleteness of coverage." Urban-rural ratios and influences of nearby non-tax 

States limit the precision of this device but do not void its value for approximate evaluation. Table Cig-2 shows Texas to be above average in this regard, surpassed only by Illinois, Michigan, and New York, all of which have a considerably higher ratio of urban population than Texas. Thus, it would appear that Texas has handled the problems of tax evasion and exemptions as well, or better than, most other States. In comparison with Louisiana, which has a rate 2.67 times that of Texas, per capita collections in Austin are 53 per cent of those in Baton Rouge, and per capita return per penny of tax in Texas is 1.4 times as great. Comparison with another neighboring State--Oklahoma-shows a similar picture. 

It can perhaps be concluded that, in view of the apparently inelastic demand for cigarettes within the limits of present indications, the increased tax rate applied in 1950 and the apparently continuing trend toward urbanization in Texas will result in increased per capita revenues--and, of course, increased total collections--from this tax for several years. However, there is no present indication that Texas will be drawing as much revenue from this source per person as do many other States with similar taxes. 

In the efficiency of collection, i.e., per capita collections per penny of tax, Texas appears average or above average. With the aid of the recentlyenacted federal law governing parcel post shipments, the State may well show continuing increases by this indicator.
