SECTION 

From the preceding discussion, it is evident that the cigarette tax law is both effective and relatively efficient in its operation. Compared with other States having similar laws, Texas is on substantial middle ground in relation to major aspects of the tax. However, several areas in the operation of the tax law, though relatively satisfactory, could possibly produce even more desirable results. And, of course, the milieu in which the tax functions is never static. Procedures and practices which may have been impractical a few years ago may be worthy of consideration now because of changed conditions.

This section will summarize the above-mentioned areas and indicate some possible methods which could be used in dealing with them. No method considered is intended as a recommendation or proposal; rather, this discussion is included to point up problems and to mention approaches which might be used. Limitations of this study have prevented a comprehensive investigation of all possibilities and even of all aspects of those presented here. 

Three items of major significance are evident in consideration of the cigarette tax law. They are: a) The matter of discounts and stamp costs. b) The matter of split responsibility and duplication of function in the administrative organization. c) The matters of occasional minor inconsistencies, occasional lack of policy enunication, and the plethora of administrative detail in the law.

Discounts and stamp costs are important in regard to net revenue collected by the State. Division of administrative responsibility and duplication of function raises questions of general tax policy. Details of the cigarette tax law are important as guides to efficient tax administration. 

Discounts and Stamp Costs

The first of these considerations--discounts and stamp costs--appears especially significant because of the money involved. Discounts amount to 

1.5 million dollars or more annually. Cost of stamps is more than $100,000 each year. It might well be said that these items of cost are the essence of one of the largest problems of collection and enforcement of the tax--namely, the determination of the most desirable method of collection. 39

Texas at present uses both the decalcomania stamp and the meter impression method. As discussed in Section 3, discounts are given as payment for costs incurred by the distributor in the affixing of stamps or meter impressions to individual cigarette packages. This operation is difficult and expensive, and no well-established standards exist to determine its true costs with any accuracy. Indications are that the cost varies considerably among distributors.

Some tax administrators feel that it might be possible to discontinue the discount without changing in any other respect the currently-used collection method employing stamps and meters. Although cost of stamps would remain unchanged, the result would be considerably more revenue for the State. 

An alternative collection method, rather widely advocated, eliminates both the discount and the use of stamps and meter impressions. This is the so-called inventory method, now used in two States. Proponents maintain that it eliminates the need for administrative organization presently used for stamp sales and meter settings and also greatly simplifies the work of the distributor. However, the contention that the discount encourages better co-operation from distributors than could be otherwise gained cannot be ignored.

Under the inventory method, evasions by means of interstate mail-order sales are generally accepted as less difficult. than with the stamp or meter methods. However, if the recently-enacted federal law regulating interstate sales of cigarettes is upheld in the courts, this alleged weakness may be eliminated.

Administrative Organization 

The second consideration mentioned above -- split responsibility and duplication of function in the administrative organization -- has been discussed in Section 2. This involves the structure of administrative organization and touches the question of the most desirable framework for achieving efficient administration of the tax.

The rather involved provisions of the tax act prescribing organizational form are reflected in only a general way in the actual tax administration organization. Since it evidently functions satisfactorily, it might be desirable to amend present laws to adopt the administrative organization found most practical. 

Specifically, this could involve elimination of the only nominally-functioning Tax Stamp Board. As a central purchasing agency, the Board of Control could, within its normal framework, perform the necessary purchasing-procurement functions required for tax stamps. This change would involve giving 40

statutory sanction to the Comptroller's determination of the regularity of changes in stamp design, the design, denomination, and quantity of stamps desired, and direct supervision of stamp manufacture through his personal representative. It would also involve removing the Attorney General from direct participation in the administration of the law, though leaving him necessary powers to prosecute suits. 

Further unification and simplification of the cigarette tax administrative structure could be achieved by giving the Comptroller the collection function now vested in the Treasurer. This centralization has been effected for other State taxes. In practice, the Comptroller now exercises fairly centralized control over the administration of the cigarette tax except in the collection function.

Such consolidation might well be considered in conjunction with a review of discounts and collection methods. Under the currently-used stamp and meter method, rather elaborate administrative machinery is necessary. 

Processing of requisitions for stamps, recording of stamp sales, supervision of stamp manufacture, servicing of metering machines and control of meter impression patterns all call for considerable administrative organization and activity.

In addition, the present division of administrative responsibility between two major State agencies poses a difficult problem of co-ordination and results in some duplication of effort and records. The statutory provision that the 

Comptroller supervise collection of the tax by the Treasurer is unrealistic. 

Overlapping of responsibility is inevitable under the present law, and centralized administration might simplify the task and tend to eliminate duplication. 

Administration and the Law

The third item enumerated above deals with occasional inconsistencies in the law, its occasional failure to state clearly the desired policy, and its great amount of administrative detail. These are what might be called internal statutory deficiencies. Changes, though not vital to successful operation. of the law, it might be considered to strengthen the law, facilitate administration, and promote efficiency.

One of the most conspicuous instances of apparent inconcistency is the previously-mentioned provision that the Comptroller "collect, super vise, and enforce the collection" of the tax, in contradiction to provisions giving to the Treasurer responsibility and authority for collections. Another is the apparent conflict between the 1941 and 1950 enactments concerning refunds for stamps on damaged cigarettes, which has been discussed in Section 3. One act gives 41

the Treasurer authority to determine the procedure for such refunds; the other gives the same authority to the Comptroller. This conflict has resulted in an administrative practice which, though evidently entirely practical, may not conform with the desired legislative policy. 

In the matter of policy enunications, statutory provisions relating to assessment are in part confusing. Retention of the levy on cigarettes weighing more than three pounds per thousand is not in itself strange if intended as a regulatory feature. However, it is unrealistic when linked with distribution of funds, since no revenues are being collected under this provision. 

It may be that actual significance of the weight categories is not generally known.

Desired policy is somewhat unclear, too, regarding frequency of audits and inspections and in the matter of exemptions. The personal -user exemption, as an example (now at 40 cigarettes), represents an enforcement problem of considerable magnitude. Further and detailed study of exemptions may well be warranted.

The authority given the Tax Stamp Board to design the tax stamps, which seemingly deprives: the Comptroller of an essential part of enforcement authority might also be mentioned. Penalties for delinquent license renewals also involve enforcement. As mentioned in Section 5, more than one-third of such renewals are delinquent. Because the defalcation is generally considered trivial by the numerous offenders and few penalties can be conveniently imposed under the present law, some consideration might be given the possibility of aiding the administrator on this matter. 

Several possibilities could ease this situation. One method suggested by some administrators is an additional provision of the law which would permit automatic application of a delinquency penalty by the administrator. 

Late renewals would simply pay a nominal additional fee. Another suggested method is a requirement that renewals be made by mail to the Comptroller's office in Austin. This procedure, it is contended, would allow the field force to devote more time to other duties in connection with the tax and permit closer control of delinquencies. It is also contended by some persons that an increased license fee for retail dealers, coupled with an automatic, penalty clause, would make them more aware of the license and more interested in prompt renewal.

The problem of the extensive administrative detail with which the tax law is burdened is more difficult to solve by specific change or remedy than the matters discussed above. The motivation for this detail is clear and unquestionably commendable. However, such detailed prescriptions tend to make 42

administrative procedures extremely inflexible and add to the legislative burden the task of keeping procedural details current. As an example, if it is determined, for purposes of efficient management, that a different number (from that prescribed in the law) of carbon copies of stamp purchase invoices is needed, the change could probably not be made without legislative action. 

Similarly, the content of some of the tax forms cannot be altered without an amendment to the law. This places upon the Legislature the responsibility for keeping abreast of administrative procedures on almost a day-by-day basis. An alternative would be to strengthen the position of the administrator somewhat by permitting him more discretion in procedure and method. of detail deemed necessary or significant should not, of course, be omitted, but such provisions could be prescribed as minimums beyond which the administrator could act according to necessity. 

In Conclusion Matters

After almost a score of years, the Texas cigarette tax law can be said to have reached maturity. One of the principal revenue-producers of the State, it has successfully weathered the difficulties of its early years and by a series of revisions and amendments has kept its administration abreast of developments throughout the country.

The problems confronting it today are not those upon which rest its success or failure. Its place in the tax structure of the State is assured. 

Today's problems are typical of a mature entity--problems of procedural refinement, of improved methods, and of increased effectiveness. 

BULLETIN 

The provisions of the Federal Jenkins Act, which requires persons selling cigarettes in interstate commerce to file monthly returns with the tobacco tax administrator of the State into which cigarettes are shipped, have been held valid.

CHAPTER II THE CEMENT TAX SECTION I HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT TORICAL 

Cementing materials have been in use for several thousand years and were first used in large quantities in the United States as a result of the development of water transportation and canal construction. However, this early cement was in large part "natural," and it was only in the latter part of the last century that the presently-known manufactured Portland cement gained extensive usage. This manufactured or produced cement is that which is generally used today.

Portland Cement was so named by its inventor, Joseph Aspdin of Leeds, England, because it resembled in color a building stone found on the Isle of Portland. The process for its manufacture was first developed in 1824, but not until 1845 was Portland cement produced in quantity. First importation into the United States was in 1865.1 

Increased Production After 1900

Production in this country did not increase very rapidly until 1900, when the adoption of standard specifications allowed manufacturers to concentrate on: quantity output. Although cement production increased steadily throughout the early years of the 20th Century, the development of the automotive industry and the discovery that cement could be used for highway construction brought about the greatest jump in amount of cement made. Today cement is used in all types of construction. 

Montana First to Tax Cement

As a natural result of the growth of the automobile industry and the increased building of highways, many States sought new sources of revenue. 

Montana in 1921 was the first State to turn to the cement industry for addit tional money.2 In the same year, it passed its first gasoline tax law designed to help pay for its rapidly expanding highway system. 

The Montana tax was levied on cement dealers, producers, and manufacturers. This tax combined license provisions with a tax based on production, sale, or use of cement, gypsum., and allied products. 1 2 

Edward E. Bauer, Plain Concrete, Mc Graw Hill, (New York, 1936) pp. 1-18. 

Mont. Rev. Codes 1935, vol.5 chs. 213, 214, pp. 1322-1328. 44

Some years later in Texas, because of the "depleted state" of the treasury, an attempt was made to "levy a sales tax on cement and lime" imported into or manufactured and sold in the State.3 However, this 1930 bill was not reported out of committee. The proposed rate was two cents per 100 pounds of cement and one cent per 100 pounds of lime, to be paid by the producer, manufacturer, or, in the case of imported cement, by the seller or user. 

First Texas Cement Tax 

The next year, 1931, an occupation tax was levied on the production of 4 cements A bill similar to the one introduced the preceding year, imposing a tax on cement and lime, was introducted but not reported from House Committee.5 Instead, the cement tax was included as a Senate amendment to a tax and regulatory measure fór itinerant peddlers. 6

Originally introduced as an annual tax of ten cents per 100 pounds of cement produced, it was subsequently amended to amount to a quarterly tax of five cents per 100 pounds, and emerged from conference committee with a tax rate of 1.25 cents per 100 pounds, payable monthly. Despite points of order that the cement tåx violated the Constitutional provision requiring that revenue bills originate in the House and only be amended by the Senate, the measure was passed. It was also argued that the subject mat- ter was not germane to the regulation of peddlers, but, nonetheless, the tax was passed along with taxes on cigarette dealers, sulfur producers, money lenders, carnivals, wax works, brokers, auctioneers, etc. 

Peddlers and Cement

The stated reasons for the tax bill, as a consequence of the process it underwent, were "the fact that itinerant peddlers, many of whom are nonresidents... are establishing themselves in direct competition to local merchants, farmers, and producers ...; whereas, such peddlers pay no taxes and are usually transients who sell an inferior quality of fruits and other products

Unlike the Montana statute, which included gypsum and products of gypsum and cement, the Texas tax was levied only on cement. Where the 

Montana tax provided for issuing licenses to retail cement dealers, the Texas tax contained no licensing provisions. This absence of a licensing requirement has continued to the present. 

Another Occupation * Tax

The generally poor condition of State finances probably prompted the imposition of the cement tax, although the allocation of one-fourth of the tax revenue to the Available School Fund was the only disposition made in the act. Whereas the cement tax proposed the preceding year was frankly labeled "a sales tax on cement and lime," the one enacted in 1931 was termed "an occupation tax." In this tax, as in many others, the apparently restrictive provisions of the State Constitution regarding the taxing power of the Legislature obviously had their influence. 

6 6

The need for additional revenue and the fact that the tax placed on cement is grossly inadequate and should be rectified to provide a proper portion of revenue to the State" provoked an unsuccessful attempt to raise the tax rate from 1.25 cents per 100 pounds of cement to five cents in 1935.13 

The original rate remained in effect until 1941, when it was increased to 2.5 cents per 100 pounds by a Senate amendment to an omnibus tax measure providing needed revenue for State services. A temporary increase of ten per cent of 2.5 cents was imposed by the 51st Legislature in its first called session in 1950 to provide funds for expansion of the State hospital system. 

10 Tex. 11. 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R.S., ch. 212, sec. 4, p. 363. 

Ibid., sec. 1, subdiv. 41a, p. 361. 

12See Section I, Chapter II, General Taxing Power, in this report.

Montana provides for a tax rate of four cents per barrel of 376 pounds of cement, and five cents per ton on cement plaster, gypsum o or gypsum products "manufactured, produced, or imported" into that State. Retail dealers are liable for the tax if it has not previously been paid. 

It is evident both from the rates imposed in Montana and Texas and from the rates proposed but not passed in Texas that there has been no agreement as to a proper tax on cement. Rates in Texas have changed from 1.25 cents in 1931 to 2.5 cents in 1941, with an additional ten per cent of 2.5 cents for 1950 and 

1951. Montana imposes a tax of approximately one cent per 100 pounds of cement. 

Proposed rates in Texas have varied from ten cents to one and one-quarter cents. 

An "inspection fee" of 15 cents per hundredweight on all cement imported into Florida was held invalid by the United States Supreme Court on the ground 15 that it was discriminatory. The Court did not rule as to whether the tax was unreasonable or confiscatory. In this particular case, the taxable imported cement represented 30 per cent of Florida's intrastate consumption. 

Other States Are Larger Cement Producers 

Available records indicate that Montana and Texas are the only States which have levied a tax on cement except for the short-lived Florida tax on cement importers. This is interesting in view of the fact that both Pennsylvania and California considerably outdistance Texas in cement production, and New York almost equals its total. 16 

Iowa, Maine, Nebraska, New York, and Washington list cement as their primary mineral resource. In Indiana, Kansas, Missouri, Pennsylvania, and Tennessee, it is the second most important mineral resource. It ranks third in Alabama, Florida, Maryland, Michigan, and Oregon, and fourth in California and

Illinois. Yet none of these States tax the production or distribution of cement. 

In Texas, cement ranks fifth in importance among minerals. Montana's production is so low as not to be included among its major mineral resources. 

Texas ranked third in 1948in the production of gypsum, surpassed by Michigan and New York and followed by California, Iowa, and Nevada. Production in Montana is evidently much lower, No State considers gypsum among its five primary resources. Montana appears to be alone in its taxation of this commodity. With. recent increases in capacity, Texas probably ranks first among the States producing gypsum products.16a

In 1948, Texas produced 13,786,846 barrels (376 pounds each) of cement valued at $30,352,972, on which a tax of $1,209,000 was collected.

Imports of 862,862 barrels were also reported. This matter of imports raises the principal problem of administering and enforcing the tax law. It is not certain that only 862, 862 barrels were imported in 

1949, as the matter of complete tax coverage seems a serious problem in the case of imported Cement. This will be developed in subsequent sections. 

Thus, this tax, paralleled in only one other State, finds itself after almost a score of years of operation beset by at least one serious problem of enforcement, still relatively low in its revenue yield, and without any real counterpart against which to evaluate its operation.

SOURCE: Minerals Yearbook, 1947, 1948.

No change has been made in the administrative organization of the cement tax since its original enactment in 1931. According to the law, the Comptroller is vested with primary responsibility for the general ad- 20 ministration of the tax, including its collection and enforcement. The 

Comptroller receives monthly tax payments and reports from cement distributors. He may require that additional information be contained in the reports submitted or in records kept by distributors. The statutes also charge the Comptroller with responsibility for the printing of tax receipts with his signature, showing the name of the occupation (in this case, cement distribution), amount of the tax, the year, month, name 21 of the licensee, and the collector (in this case, the Comptroller). As is evident, considerable latitude is given the Comptroller in prescribing the form of reports and records, but specifications concerning tax receipts are enumerated in detail in this statute. He is also responsible for adopt- 22 ing rules and regulations for enforcement of the cement tax. 

However, the Attorney General and the Auditor, under the tax acts, 23 share with the Comptroller the authority to inspect distributors' records. 

The Attorney General, too, may enjoin any distributor who is delinquent in the payment of the tax from "acting as such and may enforce the provisions hereof by suit instituted in Travis County, or other county having venue." "State and county authorities" may inspect places of business to see that tax receipts are conspicuously displayed. 24 This seems to include an indeterminate number of officials in the enforcement phases of the tax. 

Whereas only the Comptroller is responsible for checking reports, three agencies may conduct independent inspections of the records of distributors-the Comptroller, the Attorney General, and the Auditor. Two agencies are specifically charged with enforcement--the Comptroller, generally, and the 20 21 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R. S., ch. 212, sec. 41b and 41c, p. 361.

TEX.REV. CIV. STAT. (1925), art. 7053. 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, op. cit. 22 23 

Ibid., sec. 41d. 24 

Ibid., sec. 42. 49

Attorney General in matters of delinquency; and undesignated State and county authorities may exercise inspection authority to assure display of tax receipts.

Responsibility Divided 

Thus, statutory delineation of responsibility for administration of the tax law is a little confusing. The division of enforcement among at least five separate entities, two of which are undefined, complicates the organizational form. Inclusion of the Auditor as an inspection agent without indicating how the results and conclusions of the inspections are to be used presents the possibility of duplication. 

Despite the apparent confusion of the cement tax law, and as so frequently is true in cases where legal provisions are complex, administrative practice and informal arrangement have effected more direct and simple procedures for administration. This is especially evident in the matter of enforcement.

Although the Comptroller is given authority to require additional information in records of cement distributors, in reality this power has not been exercised. Nor has the Comptroller promulgated any rules and regulations to govern enforcement. The statutory division of inspection authority does not exist in practice. Neither the Attorney General nor the 

Auditor has been known to inspect distributors' records and, in recent years, at least, no injunction suits against delinquent taxpayers have been filed. The indefinite grant of inspection power to "State and county authorities" has produced no reports of violations of the mandatory display of receipts within the recollection of Comptroller's Department personnel. 

In fact, it appears that the display of tax receipts is not, in actuality, enforced even by the Comptroller's Department, since it does not contribute to a solution of the primary enforcement problem, namely, the importation of cement. 50

SECTION 3--ASSESSMENT METHODS AND PROCEDURES 

25 The cement tax is based upon weight units sold or distributed as reported to the Comptroller on or before the 25th day of each month for the preceding month. At present, the tax amounts to 2.5 cents on each 100 pounds of cement26 with a ten per cent temporary increase effective from March 1, 1950, to August 31, 1951. The law, through all its amendments, has imposed the tax on "every person. . . manufacturing or producing in and/or importing cement into this State and who thereafter distributes, sells, or uses "' cement. 27 The original act specified that the tax would accrue on the first intrastate distribution, sale, or use, 28 but this stipulation was omitted from the 1941 amendment. From the law it is not clear whether since 1941 the tax is payable on the amounts of cement produced or only on amounts distributed, sold, or used. In practice, the Comptroller's office collects the tax on total cement production each month, even though some or all of it may be stored.

The cement tax enjoys a distinction in that it contains a "use clause." 

Unlike other occupation taxes where "use" was not made a basis for taxation until some lapse of time after their original levy, the cement tax contained this clause in the original enactment of 1931. It was continued in the 1941 and 1950 amendments.

It is interesting to note that the only other State which taxes cement-- Montana - - levies the tax on persons producing or manufacturing gypsum and 29 allied products, in addition to persons producing or manufacturing cement. 

Persons importing such products into Montana for sale or use, and all retail sellers of these products, are also liable for the tax if it has not previously been paid. In contrast to the Texas rate, this State has a rate of four cents per barrel of 376 pounds of cement (i.e., less than one cent per 100 pounds), and of five cents per 2,000 pounds of cement plaster, gypsum, or gypsum products. 25 26 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R. S., ch. 212, sec. 42h. 

Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Legislature, R. S., ch. 184, art. XII, p. 295. 

27 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, C. S., ch. 2, art. XII, p. 24. 

28 Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, op. cit., sec. 4la, p. 361. 

29 Mont. Rev. Codes, 1935, vol. I, chs. 213, 214, pp. 1322-1328. 51

In Texas, the tax to be paid is determined by the taxpayer, producer, 30 or distributor upon the basis of his records of sales, production, etc. 

Reliance is placed upon the distributors to forward true and accurate reports and payments when due. The Comptroller checks each report to assure that the tax payment is correct for the reported production, distribution, use, or sale.

From the law, the assessment aspects of the tax appear very simple. 

Only one commodity, cement, and one group of taxpayers, distributors, appear to be involved. 

What Is Cement?

No definition of cement has ever been included in the cement tax law. 

Those subject to the tax are unable to determine, from a reading of the law, just what commodity is being taxed. Cement is prepared in several combinations and marketed in many forms. 32 In 1931, the Attorney General, while admitting that the word has a broad and not well defined meaning, stated that it could include any substance of a cementitious nature...31 Later a more comprehensive definition of "cement'' was formulated in connection with the question of "Trinity Mix,'' a product of the Trinity Portland Cement Company. The Attorney General concluded that the word must be interpreted in light of its ordinary use and understanding as being a sort of pasty substance used to join stones and bricks in a building, to cover floors, etc., and which, when dry, becomes hard like stone. 

In 1939, the Trinity Portland Cement Company brought suit against the State of Texas to secure refund of taxes paid under protest, alleging that the act imposing such a tax was unconstitutional in that it was ambiguous, uncertain, vague, and indefinite" and did not define the product with sufficient definiteness to enable the taxpayer to ascertain what was being taxed. The 

Supreme Court of Texas held that, in conformity with the opinion of the Attorney General, cement should include that used in construction work as in building, roads, sidewalks, etc., and exclude such things as dental cement, belting cement, etc,33 The term, according to the court, is to be applied in its generic sense. 

30Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R.S., ch. 212, sec. 42h, p. 361. 

31Opinion of Attorney General, September 11, 1931. 

32Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-545, April 4, 1939. 

33 Trinity Portland Cement Co. v. State, 144 SW 2d 329, (1940). 52

Who is a Distributor ?

Regardless of whether the taxpayer produces, imports, sells, or uses cement, he is classed by the law as a distributor. Yet nowhere on the form prepared by the Comptroller for monthly reports, on the basis of which the cement tax is determined, does the word "distributor" appear. Titled a "Monthly Report of Sales" and subtitled "Tax Computed on Product Sold or

Used," the form actually provides space for "Cement Produced or Imported" and "Less Interstate Shipments." Depending upon the caption selected, the cement tax appears to be a "sales tax," a "sales or use tax," or a "producer's or importer's tax." From a reading of the form, it would seem that distributors are entirely free from payment. It is interesting to note that the form also specifies application of the tax to cement under Act 7047, Section 41a "as therein defined," although the tax law nowhere defines cement, When the form is used by out-of-State distributors or producers to report sales to Texas concerns, the wording appears even more ambiguous in that they report actual exports to Texas rather than "cement produced" or "cement imported. 11 Intrinsically, the form of reports is not vital so long as the required information is contained therein. However, the form now in use reflects the indefiniteness of the present cement tax law. Already handicapped by a lack of enforcement personnel, the Comptroller must answer the numerous questions which arise. For distributors previously acquainted with the cement tax, the form is understandable, but new taxpayers are probably perplexed. 

Not only does the distributor compute the base tax and the temporary 1950-51 increase. He also determines any additional payments due because of delinquency. The assessment depends wholly upon the accuracy and integrity of distributors' reports. The Comptroller checks each report to assure that the computations are arithmetically correct. The classification of persons subject to the tax as "distributors" is purely arbitrary, since they are not ordinarily united within the definition of the term. Exemptions

Though no distributors are specifically exempt from payment of the tax by the statute, provision is made for exemptions in the following words: "No tax shall be imposed upon any interstate sale or transaction, nor upon any sale, 1134 distribution, or use exempt under either the State or Federal Constitutions Thus, a municipality is exempt from payment of the cement tax upon cement purchased from an out-of-State manufacturer under Article VIII, Section I, of the State Constitution. 35 This article excludes municipal corporations from 34 35 

Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R. S., ch. 212, sec. 41f, p. 362.

Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-4847, October 19, 1942. 

53

those persons upon whom an occupation tax may be levied. Nor may the tax be levied on any interstate sale or transaction.36 

Since the State may not tax federal instrumentalities, the problem of payment where an independent contractor was under contract with the United States government for the construction of buildings arose in connection with the wartime defense measures. Humble Oil & Refining Company was held to be liable for the tax on construction work being done for the War Department, although eventual title would be vested in the Federal Government. A similar ruling was made with regard to the Defense Plant Corporation; "the fact that the sale may be made directly to the United States government or to an instrumentality thereof is immaterial" because the vendor is liable for the tax upon the sale of the cement. The act of the initial intrastate use, sale, or distribution has been the deciding factor. 38 Refunds

No refunds, credits, or allowances are permitted under the cement tax law. Even in cases where double payment is made, either through oversight or ignorance of the law, the Comptroller may make no credit against future tax payments, nor may a refund be made. A similar situation exists with regard to spoilage or loss of cement through any means, There is no recourse for the taxpayer. Though the cement may not have been used, it has been produced, sold, or distributed, and the tax is due and payable. In this respect, the 

Comptroller generally follows an inflexible rule because of the statutory omisbion of any provision for credits, refunds, or discounts. 

36 Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-5843, April 25, 1944. 

37Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-3049, February 21, 1941, and Alabama v. King & Boozer, 314 U.S.1 (1941). 

38Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-4731, September 4, 1942. 54

SECTION - COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Responsibility for the collection of the cement tax is not directly assigned to any agency by the tax law. By indirection, the responsibility for collection is vested in the Comptroller by virtue of the provision that tax payments must accompany the monthly reports to him. 39 In reality, the distributors act as agents of the Comptroller in the collection function. 

The tax is due on or before the 25th day of each month for the preceding month. When the Comptroller receives the tax payment, he prepares and sends out a receipt covering the payment. 40 

That simple is the collection function of this tax as spelled out in the law. Based on his records, each distributor computes his own tax, adds any charges for delinquency, and makes payment directly to the Comptroller.

No Licensing Provisions 

However, in the provisions for enforcement, the cement tax law is considerably more involved. Licensing of distributors is not required. 

In this connection, it is interesting to note that Montana has a licensing requirement in its cement law, although only for retail cement dealers. 

The Montana license costs only one dollar annually per distributor and is obviously regulatory in intent. It provides a registration of dealers and a control useful to enforcement personnel. 

Reports and Records as Aids to Enforcement 

Requirements for the maintenance of records and submission of reports are common devices to aid enforcement. In this regard, the cement tax law is quite detailed. To further aid enforcement, provision is made for inspection of records and audit of reports. 

Monthly reports of business done, including all cement used, distributed, or sold, as well as that produced in, imported into, or exported from 41 the State, must be submitted by cement distributors to the Comptroller. 

The Comptroller is charged with responsibiltty for checking these reports. 

39 Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, R. S., ch. 212, sec. 4lb, p. 361. 

40Ibid., sec. 42. 

41Ibid., secs. 41 (b, c). 

42Ibid., sec. 4la. 42 55

The 1950 amendment of the 51st Legislature contains the stipulation that "a report shall be made for the fractional quarter of July and August, 1951. 

This is inconsistent in that monthly rather than quarterly reports are ordinarily required.

In addition to the reports, the law requires that records containing the same information be kept by the distributor to be open for inspection by the Comptroller, Auditor, and the Attorney General. Though the Comptroller "shall adopt rules and regulations for the enforcement" of the cement tax, the Attorney General may enjoin any delinquent distributor from "acting as such and may enforce the provisions hereof by suit instituted in Travis County or other county having venue. "44 As mentioned in Section 2, undesignated State and county authorities may at any time check to assure that tax receipts are 45 conspicuously displayed. Thus, under the cement tax statute, officers may report violations even though they have not been given further enforcement power. It appears from a reading of the law that enforcement as to amounts paid may be exercised by any one of three agencies; or, as to delinquency, by one or more agencies; while enforcement as to receipts is vested in either of two other authorities.

Actual Practice Less Complex 

Despite these intricate and overlapping statutory provisions, a simplified procedure has been evolved through administrative usage. In practice, the enforcement of the cement tax law rests almost entirely upon audits and inspections of the required records. In fact, only one real and regular audit is made, and that by the Comptroller, in verifying the computed tax according to monthly reports. In actuality, the audit of distributors' reports by the Gross Receipts 

Division of the Comptroller's Department is confined to a determination of the accuracy of the taxpayers' arithmetic.

Though the Auditor and Attorney General are legally entitled to inspect cement distributors' records, there is no indication that such inspections have been made by either. Gross Receipts Tax Division field men do make sporadic checks of distributors' books within the limits of the time they are able to devote to enforcement of the cement tax. Only 16 such field men are assigned to this division, however, and they are responsible for the enforcement of 17 taxes. 

Approximately 85 per cent of their time is devoted to enforcement of two, those on natural gas and oil. It is understandable and justifiable that more time is required for enforcement of the oil and natural gas taxes. With limited manpower, the other taxes cannot, of course, get the same detailed attention. 43 

Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Legislature, 1st C. S., ch. 2, art. XII, p. 24. 

44 Tex. Laws 1931, 42nd Legislature, op. cit., sec. 4ld. 

45 Ibid.. sec. 42. 56

Thus, inspections of records depend solely upon time available to field men; they are neither regular, frequent, nor complete as to coverage. And despite the legislative grant of inspection authority to five separate entities, such inspections are in fact made only by the Comptroller and his representatives. 

The Comptroller has not exercised his authority to promulgate rules and regulations for enforcement of the cement tax, nor has the Attorney General instituted any injunction suits against delinquent taxpayers in recent years. 

Delinquency most often results from ignorance of the law or from misunderstanding as to the application of the tax to imported cement. 

State and county authorities have never been known to report violations of the statutory provision that tax receipts be conspicuously displayed. In fact, the Comptroller does not expressly require that they be posted, relying more on examination of records than upon any visible evidence of tax payment. It is possible that the display was intended as a limited licensing process, but this purpose has not been fulfilled in practice.

In reality, then, only the Comptroller acts in enforcement. He audits reports, inspects records when possible, and determines delinquency. It is evident from monthly summaries of cement tax payments that delinquency does occur. Notification of such delinquency by the Comptroller apparently has resulted in payment. 

Police Powers Limited

The inspection provisions of the cement law, as well as requirements for reports and records, constitute the only police powers 46 specifically granted to State agencies. As previously discussed, there is no provision for licensing cement distributors except the required display of receipts, which is not enforced. Most lacking, perhaps, is positive authority to inspect premises and measure or count the volume of the taxable commodity on hand to verify reports. However, the Comptroller's Department reports that its inspectors have never been refused permission to inspect distributors' records. 

This touches upon a question of general policy, i. e., the granting of express police powers to enforcement officials as opposed to the practice of relying upon implied powers possessed by such officials by virtue of their office. In some individual tax laws, police powers are expressly granted. 

In others, as in the cement tax, such specific enumeration of authorities is omitted. 46 The term "police powers," as used in this discussion, is employed in the popular, broad connotation to include virtually all the powers of administrative officials to impose the enforcement of a tax upon the taxpayer. The right to inspect records and premises and require reports are included. It is, of course, understood that the term is frequently used in a much more restrictive sense. 57

The Problem of Imported Cement 

To insure complete coverage, the Comptroller, in practice, relies almost wholly upon reports voluntarily submitted by distributors or upon occasional inspections of his field men. Since imported cement presents the chief enforcement problem, two other means of discovering evasion have been developed.

The Comptroller, under the first of these methods, prepares monthly a list of producers and distributors who have submitted tax payments for the preceding month. This list is forwarded not only to the 16 field representatives of the Gross Receipts Tax Division but also to cement manufacturers or distributors who request it. Thus, the cement producers and distributors are able to, and occasionally do, report evasions.. Thus, they assist in enforcement of the tax.

Secondly, the Comptroller has persuaded some out-of-State producers and distributors to submit records of shipments to Texas purchasers. A number of foreign producers and distributors even forward to the Comptroller tax payments on cement shipped to Texas. Thus, cement tax payments are made from as far away as Birmingham, Ala.; Kansas City, Mo.; Cleveland, Ohio; and Chicago, Ill.

This practice was moderately successful prior to the United States Supreme Court decision in Federal Trade Commission v. Cement Institute, which declared the multiple basing point delivered-price system a violation of the Federal Trade Commission Act, the Clayton Act, and the Robinson- 47 

Patman Act. Since that time, October, 1947, foreign manufacturers have been reluctant to make the tax payments themselves and collect the amounts due from the purchaser. Some.four producers located in Kansas, Oklahoma, Ohio, and Missouri still make payments and reports, along with approximately five distributors in Oklahoma, Kansas, Missouri, and Alabama. Though this procedure appears unorthodox, some of the evasion through importation is thereby eliminated. Much evasion and loss of revenue are evidently due to the ignorance or indifference of small consumers of cement. Individual truck imports from adjoining States and Mexico are virtually impossible to discover under present procedure, and even shipments to domestic distributors from out-of-State producers cannot be traced with any degree of accuracy. 

Some indication of the importance of imported cement is revealed by consideration of production and import statistics. In 1949, Texas produced 

57,895, 273 hundredweights of cement, exported 8, 722, 334, and imported 

47 Federal Trade Commission v. Cement Institute, 333 U. S. 683 (1947). 58

3, 244, 359, according to reports furnished the Comptroller. Reported imports constitute 6.2 per cent of the indicated Texas consumption (based on totals taxed). However, on the basis of available data, there is reason to believe that not all imported cement was reported or discovered. 

Most Payments From, Panhandle

A geographical breakdown of distributors' reports and tax payments on imported cement during the first six months of 1950 reveals an interesting pattern of tax payments. (Reports from the nine Texas producers of cement are not included in this discussion.) In the Panhandle area (Slaton and north to the Oklahoma line, and Vernon and west to the New Mexico line), distributors in 29 cities made 106 reports and payments: In northeast Texas (Fort 

Worth and east to the Arkansas-Louisiana border, and Tyler and north to the 

Oklahoma border), distributors in 11 cities made 59 reports and payments.

In the other three-fourths of Texas, only 34 reports and payments were made by distributors in just six cities. 

Dallas and Fort Worth present a curious comparison. Twenty-three reports covering imported cement were received from Dallas and only three from Fort Worth; yet both Dallas and Tarrant Counties have domestic cement plants. The six cities outside the Panhandle and northeast Texas areas from which reports were received were El Paso, Odessa, Killeen, Waco, Houston, and Eagle Pass--cities widely scattered geographically and. with no apparent similarity as to need for cement importation. Strangely enough, one of the most thinly populated areas of Texas, the Panhandle, accounts for the highest number of reports. In the southeastern and south central sections, much more thickly populated areas, reports and payments were received from only a few widely scattered cities. At the same time, it should be noted that the Panhandle area is much closer to cement producers in Oklahoma and Kansas than to Texas producers. However, the northeast Texas area, where reports were received from 11 cities, is not only relatively closer to domestic producers but is also thickly populated. Houston was the only city in the Gulf 

Coast area from which reports were received. 

The report pattern is not altered significantly when the period of study is extended to cover a full year, as in Figure Cem-1. The majority of reports still originate in the Panhandle area, the second largest number from northeast Texas, and only spotted reports from the remainder of the State. More thickly populated areas show few reports, and the peculiar relationship between Fort. Worth and Dallas is not altered. This pattern of payments, though it does not necessarily indicate lack of enforcement, does 

48 Compiled from Comptroller's monthly reports. 59

raise serious doubts as to coverage. The limitation of time and personnel has precluded a more intensive research into this particular area for the survey. Statistics showing destinations of shipments to the State and construction data, which might more positively disclose the actual amount of cement imported into Texas, were not readily available. 

Penalties 

49 Several penalties for violation of the cement tax act are provided. 

Delinquency is punishable by fines of not less than $25 and not more than $1,000 per day of such violation, plus a two-per-cent forfeiture of all taxes delinquent for 20 days or less. An additional forfeiture of eight per cent for delinquency of more than 20 days is authorized. All delinquent taxes draw interest at the rate of eight per cent from due date. The penalty for 50 not displaying the tax receipt, a misdemeanor, is a fine of $50 or less. 

The State has prior lien on all property used by the delinquent distributor for all claims against him.

However, in actual practice, the penalty for failure to display tax receipts has never been collected. Although the form supplied by the 

Comptroller for reports of cement production or importation provides space for computation of payments due because of delinquency, such penalties are rarely collected. 

Earmarking of Funds 51 One-fourth of the revenue from the cement tax is credited to the Available School Fund and three-fourths is allotted to the Clearance Fund.5 

The additional tax imposed by the first called session of the 51st Legislature, except for the one-fourth to be credited to the Available School Fund, is 52 allotted to the State Hospital Fund, created by that session. 

Though allocation of one-fourth of the revenue from the cement tax revenue was made to the Available School Fund in 1931, there was no allocation of the other three-fourths until 1941, when it was alloted as indicated above. 49

SECTION 5--RESULTS OF OPERATION 

Costs

One of the most significant aspects of any tax, both to the Legislature and to the public, is a measure of the cost of its operation, particularly a measure of such cost in relation to the total revenues produced. Unfortunately, separate cost figures for individual taxes which could give such a measure are seldom maintained. The cement tax is no exception. Hence, there is no easy means whereby the cost of administering the cement tax may be computed. Sixteen other taxes are administered by the Gross Receipts Tax Division of the Comptroller's Department. Since the expenses of this division are not separately appor tioned among the various taxes by any special formula, only general observations can be made.

The fact that some 85 per cent of the time of the division's 16 field inspectors is devoted to enforcement of the natural gas and oil taxes means that the remaining 15 per cent of their time is divided among 15 taxes. A similar division probably obtains in regard to office time and over-all administrative costs. Thus the actual cost of administering the cement tax is apparently small indeed, amounting to no more than one per cent of the total time and expenses of the Gross Receipts Tax Division. 

Such economy of operation would be commendable if the full potential of tax collections were being realized. However, as discussed in Section 4 and indicated by Figure Cem-1, there is good reason to believe that the full potential of cement tax collections is not fully realized. And, although no statistical evidence is available to show the relationship of administrative costs and revenues, it would seem that the commonly accepted principle of tax administration is applicable, namely, that revenues ordinarily increase in proportion to increased enforcement activities. This would logically imply that more extensive coverage and enforcement activity could substantially enlarge revenue. It seems, too, that the revenue from such additional coverage would more than offset the increased administrative cost.

It should be noted in any discussion of cost of administration of the cement tax that litigation costs have been minor factors. The only case involving this tax, in 1940, was primarily concerned with the matter of definition of cement. 

Effectiveness of Operation

Annual returns from the cement tax have followed a somewhat erratic path, buth show an over-all increase from $198,000 in 1932 to $1, 239, 000 in 1949. Payments reached a peak in 1937 which was not surpassed until 1941. 

When the tax was doubled from 1.25 cents per hundred-weight to 2.50 cents, tax payments more than doubled in 1942, the first full year of collections at the higher rate. Another peak occurred in 1943, with returns in 1944 equaling slightly more than half those for the preceding year. Not until 1946 was there another substantial increase, and the 1943 peak was not surpassed until 1948. 61

A comparison with returns from the Montana cement tax reveals a striking similar pattern, even though collections have varied in that State from only $4,000 in 1932 to $37,000 in 1948. As indicated in Figure 

Cem-2, parallel fluctuations in cement tax revenues occurred in Montana since 1941. Since Montana and Texas are the only two States levying cement taxes, this limited comparison seems to justify the conclusion that these fluctuations are attributable to factors within the cement industry rather than to any irregularities in tax collections. 

Texas revenues from the cement tax have never represented more than .71 of one per cent of total tax revenues (in 1943) and were only .4 of one per cent of the total revenue for 1949. Montana cement tax receipts in 1948 were only one-fortieth of the Texas collections for that year, and it is reasonable to assume that the cement tax amounted to even less than .4 of one per cent of that State's total revenue. 62

SECTION 

The primary purpose of this section is to summarize problems which have been developed in previous sections. Possible methods of solution are mentioned but are not intended as recommendations. Neither are they exhaustive, since they are offered only as approaches to the problem.

From the foregoing discussion, it can be seen that certain diffi culties are inherent in the cement tax law. These lie principally in the enforcement and collection provisions. Actually, several distinct and specific areas show up as serious problems, (a) The statutory lack of clear delineation of enforcement authority. (b) The lack of means for assuring complete coverage, (c) Inability of taxpayers to secure redress for duplicate payments and spoilage. (d) The lack of definition of the taxable commodity. (e) The occupation tax label and its attendant restrictions. Enforcement

As the cement tax law is presently written, enforcement authority is delegated to five separate agencies or entities, the Comptroller, the 

Attorney General, the Auditor, and "State and county authorities." Since the

Attorney General and the Auditor have not exercised their power to inspect distributors' records, and the Comptroller, in practice, is the only one who performs this function, simplicity and reality might be realized by a positive delegation of enforcement to a single agency.

In this connection, it must be noted that none of the present enforcement officials has positive statutory authority to enter upon the premises of those subject to the tax to ascertain that payments have been made correctly. 

While the Comptroller reports that field inspectors have not been refused entrance to such premises, specific legal authority to do so would probably facilitate enforcement and possibly prevent suits against the State for ille gal entry.

Because of the provision giving State and county authorities the right to inspect distributors' places of business is vague, it is virtually inapplicable and, in practice, has not contributed to prevention of evasion. Coverage Geographical spread of distributors reporting imported cement, as shown in Figure Cem-1, indicates that a substantial number of persons subject to the tax may not be paying it. This situation, if it exists, indicates that the cement tax in its application violates the commonly accepted principle that a tax be collected from all who are subject to it. Besides the loss of 63

revenue when a tax does not operate in accord with this concept, the taxpayer is not assured that in fairness to him all others within his particular class are taxed on an equal basis. 

Contacting out-of-State producers and distributors does help to prevent total evasion where cement is imported, but all importations: cannot be covered by this means, nor is such a procedure convenient. 

Two possible alternatives present themselves. Either the procedure should permit an easier check of all potential taxpayers, which might be realized through a licensing provision, or enforcement funds might be made available in sufficient amount to assure reasonably complete coverage. It is fair to assume that revenues collected will increase in proportion to enforcement effort expended, since this has generally been true with other taxes.

Licensing of distributors is the keystone of the enforcement function of several other taxes and is often considered necessary for the prevention of evasion. A license requirement for cement distributors, purely regulatory in nature and coupled with a nominal registration fee, would at least insure that they are recorded and facilitate inspections. Should dealers be licensed, there would be no more reason for their reporting cement. Field representatives, too, would be aware of all distributors in their particular districts and could more easily make periodic and regular investigations. Thus, licensing could contribute to more effective enforcement.

The other alternative mentioned above is the provision of funds for expanded enforcement activity. Unlike some of the other occupation taxes administered by the Gross Receipts Division, the cement tax law provides no allocation of funds for enforcement. Both the oil and gas tax statutes provide that one-half of one per cent of gross revenues from these taxes be allocated as enforcement funds, subject to Legislative appropriation.

Enforcement of the cement tax is handled by the same personnel employed for enforcement of the oil and gas taxes and without any additional appropriation.

A combination of these two methods for insuring coverage might be worthy of consideration. This would involve a registration fee for cement distributors which, when levied in conjunction with a licensing provision, might furnish the necessary funds for increased enforcement effort. 

Remedies for Taxpayers

Refunds, discounts, and allowances were designed to correct honest errors or unintentional inequities in tax collections. The absence of any such provisions in the cement tax law has sometimes resulted in the taxpayers' being unable to recover duplicate payments, Recourse can usually be had only through court action, a costly and time-consuming procedure generally not worthwhile because the amounts involved are small. Duplicate tax payments have been made, and the taxpayer is usually informed that there is no means whereby his money can be refunded. 64

A similar situation exists if cement is rendered unusable through spoilage. This product must be prepared and shipped under favorable conditions or it hardens prematurely. Should spoilage occur, the tax is still payable, since no allowance is made for the ruined cement--it has been produced or sold and the tax is not recoverable. This situation may deserve consideration. 

The Taxable Commodity Though largely settled through judicial determination, the question of what is included in the term "cement" has presented some difficulties. 

Potential taxpayers are not always able to determine whether their products are subject to the tax. A more exact legislative definition would preclude the necessity for further elaboration. 

From an examination of the form now being used for reports of cement distributors, -it is evident that it has been in use for some time without revision, except for the addition of a space for computation of the ten-per-cent tax increase for 1950-51. As now printed, the form seems misleading as to the real nature of the cement tax. It might be well to make some provision whereby such forms show clearly the basis of the tax being levied. The Comptroller's Department must naturally devote its time to those taxes which produce the highest revenue, and often neither time nor personnel is available for close attention to lesser inconsistencies. 

Perhaps a more positive legislative direction than that contained in the present cement tax statutes would contribute to clearer understanding. 

An Occupation" Tax

The necessity for calling any tax an "occupation" tax arises from the question of whether the Legislature has a general taxing power in view of the Constitutional enumeration of permitted types of taxes.53 To avoid possible unconstitutionality, many levies which would not otherwise be called "occupation" taxes have been labeled as such. The cement tax is one of these.

Certain restrictions and limitations result from its designation as an occupation" tax. In the first place, considerable attention and space must be devoted to a redefinition of terms to effect conformity with the Constitutional "occupation" label. In the cement tax, the desired result is achieved by uniting "every person in this State manufacturing or producing in and/or importing cement into this State, and who thereafter distributes, sells, or uses the same" under the word "distributor." The tax is then upon the occupation of distributing cement. Such a definition appears arbitrary, but it meets the occupation" requirement, Another resultant restriction is that imposed by the Constitution upon the allocation of revenues from occupation taxes, namely, that one fourth of the revenue derived therefrom be deposited in the Available School Fund. Once a levy is termed a tax on an "occupation there is no escape from this limitation. 

53See discussion of General Taxing Power in Chap. I, Sec. 1, of this report.

CHAPTER III THE MOTOR VEHICLE SALES AND USE TAX SECTION I- HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

The motor vehicle sales and use tax is a recent addition to the Texas structure. It was enacted in 1941 as a part of the Omnibus Tax 

Law. The original act has never been amended, with the exception of a temporary rate increase of one-tenth of the present tax for the period from March 1, 1950, to August 31, 1951.4 2

Though titled a sales tax by the Legislature and possessing most characteristics of that tax, it is unusual in that it is levied on the purchaser rather than the seller. The seller has no responsibility either in the collection procedure or liability for payment. In this sense, it could properly be called a "purchase tax instead of a sales tax.

The statute is brief, and the provisions of the act are clearly and simply stated. Apparently there have been few or no cases of litigation.3 A number of important administrative questions which the act does not specifically answer have arisen, however; so the opinions of the Attorney General now form an important part of the administrative basis of the tax. Several of these opinions are discussed in the text.

The motor vehicle sales tax is one of the State's imposts based on a percentage of value, contrasted to the numerous taxes and licenses levied at a fixed amount. Consequently, revenue will generally tend to follow closely inflationary (or deflationary) movements of the economy, a factor which undoubtedly has accounted for some of the large increase in revenues since the end of World War II. 

Comparatively speaking, the tax has never experienced a “normal" period. When it was enacted in 1941, the nation's preparedness program was already underway, and it was not long before automobile production was curtailed and then suspended entirely because of the war effort. Revenue from this source during the war years was derived almost wholly from sales of used vehicles, and it averaged less than two million dollars.annually. With resumption of commercial motor vehicle production, revenue yields began to increase and reached new highs each succeeding year. For the year ending August 31, 1950, the tax yielded almost 14 million dollars, which Table T-43 shows to be 3.45 per cent of total receipts from taxes and licenses in 1949. The comparable figure for 1950 is probably larger. As a revenue producer, it was exceeded in 1949 by only six other sources crude oil production taxes, property taxes, cigarette taxes, alcoholic beverage taxes, motor fuel taxes, and motor vehicle licenses.

SECTION 

ADMINISTRATION 

The motor vehicle sales tax is collected locally by the county tax assessor-collector and administered at the State level by the 

State Comptroller of Public Accounts. 

The State Comptroller has placed the administrative functions in the Ad Valorem Division, which, as the name implies, is concerned chiefly with the property tax. Here it has been administered with little additional personnel or expense to the State. Auditors who annually check tax rolls and other records in county tax assessorcollectors offices also audit records of the motor vehicle sales tax, usually during the same audit. In the metropolitan counties where greater sums of money are involved, the Comptroller reports that the auditors now attempt to check records at least twice a year. 

Only two full time employees are used exclusively for administering the motor vehicle sales tax records in the State office, In addition, from two to three regular Ad Valorem Division employees are used part-time and for seasonal rushes.

The statute directs the State Comptroller to prescribe the form of the receipt and requires that two duplicate copies be made, one to be sent to the State and the other retained by the county. 

County Administration 

At the local level, the office of the county tax assessor-collector is granted two per cent of the tax as a fee of office to the assessor-collector or as a contribution to the officer's salary fund if the county is operating on a salary basis. Like the State Comptroller's 

Office, the county assessor-collector's office also administers the motor vehicle sales tax along with other levies. Larger counties sometimes have special divisions, but in most cases, work in the office is handled as a unit. For that reason, it would be most difficult to compute cost figures for collecting this tax. It is significant, though, that many persons feel that the two per cent compensation is inadequate, Some county officials have indicated dissatisfaction for several years, and State employees familiar with the problem have said that a larger fee would be in order. A sample from records of a few counties indicates that the average transaction now is probably about $900 or $1,000 per vehicle, which would result in an average compensation of 18 or 20 cents per transaction to the tax assessor-collector's office. The work involved in each transaction is not particularly complicated, but it is more time-consuming than one might suppose. The office usually must answer some questions, especially if the transaction is not handled by an automobile dealer; the affidavit must be received and checked; the receipt must be written in duplicate; files must be maintained; there is occasional correspondence with both the taxpayer and the State Comptroller's Office, and reports must be made and funds transmitted to the State each quarter.

Assuming that some relation exists between costs and the volume of collections (an assumption of declining costs), then counties with a 67

sufficiently large volume of such business, resulting in increased efficiency of operating units, may find the two per cent adequate, or more nearly so.

The county receives no specific compensation for administering affidavits on tax-exempt purchases. To facilitate administration at the county level and to economize on the cost of forms, the State Comptroller's Department and the State Highway Department, in a co-operative endeavor, are utilizing a joint form which covers both application for certificate of title, used by the State Highway Department, and the receipt for the motor vehicle sales tax, used by the State Comptroller. 

In addition to two per cent of the motor vehicle sales tax, the county (or the tax assessor collector) also receives 25 cents of the 50 cents charge for application for title. 

The tax assessor-collector is required to remit funds due the 

State between the 1st and 15th of each April, July, October, and January. Remittances are accompanied by a duplicate copy of each tax receipt issued during that quarter. There is no statutory provision as to the disposition of affidavits, but ordinarily they are filed by the county tax office until the State has audited records for that period. 

A few instances have been reported in which affidavits were destroyed before an audit was made.

In the larger counties, accumulation of revenue for a threemonth period is substantial, Harris County, for example, recently has been collecting about one-half million dollars per quarter. Where large sums are involved, the State Comptroller has been encouraging the counties to make monthly, rather than quarterly, remittances. 

Most of the larger counties are complying, though the monthly payments are frequently from 30 to 60 days behind the month of collection..

The statute makes no provision for deposit of funds in the interim between collection and payment to the State, and no additional bond is required of the tax assessor-collector specifically to protect funds from the motor vehicle sales tax. The State's protection is in the bond required of tax assessor-collectors in the amount of ten per cent of the State's property tax levy, but not exceeding $50,000. In 

Harris County, again chosen as an extreme, this maximum of $50,000 is equivalent to only about ten per cent of à quarter's receipts from the motor vehicle sales tax alone; yet the bond must also protect the 

State from losses of funds from the property tax, motor vehicle licenses, the poll tax, and other sources,

After 1950, bonds will be for considerably smaller sums in most counties because property tax levies will be less after the State abandons that tax for general revenue purposes. The Constitutional maximum rate for State purposes after January 1, 1951, will be 42 cents on the $100 valuation, whereas now it is 72 cents.

Method of Assessment 

The tax due from motor vehicle sales is determined from information submitted by the taxpayer. In a sense, then, the taxpayer assesses himself by executing an affidavit which sets forth the consideration of the 68

trade, or base, on which the tax is computed. It is this phase of the administrative process which apparently is causing substantial losses of revenue to the State.

The mere fact that the taxpayer must furnish information which determines the amount of tax is not unique; indeed it is the basis of many taxes, including the federal income tax. 

But requisite to receiving reasonable compliance with such a method, the taxpayer or persons acting for him should know that the affidavit is subject to verification and that evaders are reasonably liable for detection and penalties. It appears that such is not now the case with the motor vehicle sales tax.

The affidavit is filed with the county tax assessor-collector, and the law provides that he shall refuse to accept any motor vehicle for registration or transfer until the sales tax has been paid. This provision assures that an affidavit is filed on every transfer of ownership. 

The tax rate is one per cent of the total consideration, and an additional one-tenth of one per cent has been levied for the period from March 1, 1950, to August 31, 1951. It is expressly provided that accessories attached to the vehicle at the time of sale shall be included in taxable value. 

Total consideration is specifically defined to include the value of trade-ins and credit as well as cash consideration. When consideration received by a seller includes any federal tax, the amount of such tax may be deducted in determining taxable consideration. A federal excise tax is levied at seven per cent for passenger vehicles and five per cent for trucks and buses, computed on the manufacturer's price.

If a transaction involves the exchange of motor vehicles, the seller is defined as the party transferring the vehicle with greater value. It is specified that no tax be imposed upon the seller in the transfer of a motor vehicle traded in upon the purchase price of another. 

Soon after the tax was enacted, a question arose as to whether radios purchased with automobiles were subject to the motor vehicle sales tax and whether the radio was also subject to the gross receipts tax paid by the radio dealers. (The radio sales tax, also enacted in H.B. 8, is levied at two per cent of the sales price, as contrasted to the motor vehicle sales tax of one per cent.)

An opinion of the Attorney General stated that if the radio is attached to the automobile at the time of the sale, it loses its identity as a radio and becomes an accessory to the automobile.4 As a consequence, the purchase price of the radio so attached is taxable at one per cent and is not subject to the radio sales tax. In an effort to determine the extent to which radios are sold unattached, (subject to the radio sales tax and not the motor vehicle tax), author ized new car dealers in Wichita Falls, Bryan, Waxahachie, Giddings, Linden, Longview, and Houston were selected and their records in the State 

4Attorney General of Texas, Opinion 0-3519, May 25, 1941. 69

Comptroller's Office for 1949 and the first two quarters of 1950 examined. (The radio sales tax is collected quarterly by the State Comptroller on the basis of dealers' reports.) It was found that about two-thirds of the dealers in Houston, 21 of 30, had filed no reports for the period. Of seven dealers in Waxahachie, four in Giddings, two in Linden, and four in Longview, none had filed reports or paid taxes. One of eight dealers in Bryan had filed a report for one quarter; and of six dealers in Wichita Falls, two had filed reports and paid taxes regularly. Dealers who filed reports and paid radio sales taxes had sales averaging a little more than $700 per quarter, apparently about nine or ten units.

The purchaser's affidavit, the basis for computing the motor vehicle sales tax, does not contain a space for the price of a radio. There is, however, a space for recording the price of accessories as a unit. 

Use Tax 

The usual **compensatory** use tax, conventionally utilized as a correlative levy for sales taxes, is applied to motor vehicles purchased at retail outside Texas and brought into this State for use by a resident (or firm or corporation domiciled or doing business in Texas) on the public highways. The use tax is computed on the same basis as the sales tax. Exemptions

Exemption from the tax is granted the purchaser who acquires a vehicle exclusively for resale. Thus, automobile dealers are not taxed on vehicles purchased as stock in trade. Demonstrator vehicles” of automobile dealers, not having been purchased exclusively for resale, are subject to the tax. Farm tractors and road-building machinery are exempt, and trades of vehicles of equal value, with no other consideration involved, are also tax-free.

Freight and all other items added to the cost of the vehicle, except federal taxes, are taxable. Carrying charges representing the cost of credit are not taxed. 

Another important decision of the Attorney General pertains to the taxation of trailers and semi-trailers. Because Texas statutes governing registration of vehicles consider trailers and semi-trailers different vehicles from the trucks and truck-tractors to which they are attached, and because trailers and semi-trailers have no motors, the Attorney General has ruled that the automobile sales tax is not applicable to them.6

States have a general sales tax; so their motor vehicle sales taxes can hardly be considered separate and distinct, as is the case in Texas.
