Of the States, 23 apply the same rate to sales of motor vehicles as to general sales, and Alabama and Washington tax motor vehicle sales at lower rates than general sales. Alabama taxes general retail sales at two per cent, and the tax on automobile sales is only one-half of one per cent. 

In contrast to their tax-free status in Texas, trailers and semi-trailers are taxed in Alabama at a rate of one and one-half per cent, three times that on automobiles. Washington taxes general sales at three and onefourth per cent, whereas motor vehicle sales taxes are levied at three per cent. In New Mexico, sales tax rates vary from one-eighth of one per cent to two per cent, while the rate on motor vehicle sales is one per cent. 

Analysis of the rates used by the 28 States. levying mator vehicle sales taxes may be summarized as follows. The Alabama rate of one-half of one per cent is lowest, just below the one per cent levies in Texas, New Mexico, and Rhode Island. The most prevalent rate, two per cent, is used by 

17 States. Seven States tax motor vehicle sales at three per cent, the highest rate now levied. However, it should be noted that two of these seven States tax only the first sale or use in the State, and there is no tax on subsequent sales.

Four States have recently increased rates. Connecticut changed its rate from one to two per cent, effective July 1, 1949, and Mississippi made the same rate change. South Dakota, one of the three States which tax only the first sale or use, went from a two to a three-per-cent rate. California recently increased the tax from two and one-half to three per cent. 

Texas might also be considered among these States, though the Texas increase of one-tenth of one per cent is a temporary one for the period from March 1, 1950, to August 31, 1951.

North Carolina seems to be the only State with a maximum tax on any transaction, the ceiling being $15 per sale. In 25 States, the motor vehicle tax arises either from direct application of general sales tax laws or by special treatment of such transactions correlative to the general sales tax. Texas and Kentucky are unique among the States levying a motor vehicle sales tax in that neither has a general sales tax. 

Kentucky System

The Kentucky and Texas systems, because of this common character. istic, may have many similar administrative problems. How Kentucky handles its problems is of interest.

Several fundamental differences between these two States, however, tend to detract from the value of the comparison, for each system is premised on a set of basically different conditions. Moreover, it is not known how satisfactorily the Kentucky system operates, and comparison should not be construed as ascribing superiority to it. 

The Kentucky tax is levied only on the first sale or use of a vehicle, as contrasted to the Texas tax, which applies to all sales or uses. The threeper-cent rate in Kentucky, therefore, is not on a comparable basis with the one-per-cent Texas rate. Whether or not the Kentucky tax is higher on a life-of-vehicle basis depends largely on the number of times Texas vehicles change ownership and on their selling prices.

Under the Kentucky system, the incidence of burden would appear at first to rest predominantly on buyers of new vehicles, as contrasted to the Texas tax, which obviously applies to purchasers of both new and used vehicles.

A closer examination of the tax economics, however, gives rise to the probability that such a significant tax on the first transaction, with no levy on subsequent transactions, will tend to be capitalized in the value of the vehicle. Hence, vehicle owners may be effectively bearing a share of the Kentucky tax not altogether out of proportion to that paid by Texas buyers.

In contrast to Texas, Kentucky determines "retail price" not by the actual consideration in each transaction but by standard values. The fact that the Texas system is weak because of its inability to determine consideration on each sale makes such a method of interest. However, the use of standard values when all sales are taxed would cause inequalities by placing uniform values on used vehicles of the same make, model, and style, regardless of widening differentials in real values with age. This disadvantage is probably inconsequential in Kentucky because most vehicles are brought into ownership for the first time as new hence most used-car transactions are not taxable.

The Kentucky and Texas systems are similar in administrative features. Kentucky collects at the county level through the county clerk and administers at the State level through the Department of Revenue, compensating the county by a grant of two per cent, the same rate Texas uses. 

But because the Kentucky tax rate is three times higher and the levy is made only on the first sale or use (which price is almost sure to be higher than the average of all transactions, new and used, in Texas), Kentucky actually pays the county several times as much as Texas on each transaction.

Kentucky has no motor vehicle title law, but payment of the motor vehicle sales tax is a requisite to registration and license. Texas has adopted the uniform motor vehicle title code, essentially, and payment of the sales tax is a requisite to obtaining either title or registration. 

Kentucky exempts sales to charitable, religious, and educational institutions; Texas makes no such exemption. Sales in Kentucky are also ●xempt if the purchaser pays certain other taxes. That State levies a motor passenger carrier (common carrier) tax of $8 per seat up to 16 seats, $15 per seat for the next eight, and $25 for each additional seat, plus 1/36 cent per seat multiplied by the number of miles traveled; and there is a property carrier tax of from $22 to $300 per vehicle.7 If a carrier pays either of these, plus the corporation franchise tax, it is exempt from the motor vehicle sales tax. Violators of the moter vehicle sales tax in Kentucky are subject to a fine of from $25 to $1,000. In Texas, the fine is from $10 to $500 or confinement in county jail from one to 30 days. 

7CCH State Tax Guide, par. 45-371, 45-361. 72

SECTION EVASIONS 

It was apparent early in the study of the motor vehicle sales tax that numerous evasions were causing a significant loss of revenue and that the scope and extent of the problem should be defined to furnish a basis for consideration of remedial measures. 

First, such basic questions as the following should be answered. 

How much is the State losing through evasions? Who are the evaders? 

Is the incident of evasion higher in some counties than others? Why are the evaders not prosecuted?

But such fundamentally important data are by their very nature difficult to compile and present in an objective manner. It is evident that if there were a practical way to ferret out evaders and to establish necessary proof, administrators ordinarily would do so. 

Limitations of time and resources and the difficulty of a comprehensive statistical approach demanded that information about evasions be obtained from observations, small statistical samples, interviews with public officials, and the meager documentary evidence that is available. 

Admittedly, the approach is limited by lack of objective measurements. 

Though available evidence does not furnish the basis for a defensible estimate of annual losses through evasions, it does permit statements that (1) evasions are widespread, and (2) amounts lost may amount to as much as several million dollars a year. 

The Problem 

Apparently officials have been concerned about evasions for several years, but only since about 1946 has the problem begun to have serious implications. Several factors probably account for this. The first year or two after enactment of the tax in 1941 was a proving period when legal interpretations and administrative procedures were being established. 

Weak features of the tax were not so apparent because the dearth of automobile sales during the war period minimized losses. As number of sales and prices of vehicles increased rapidly after the war, losses through evasions naturally would have multiplied even though the proportionate number of evaders was the same. But, according to the State Comptroller of Public 

Accounts, high prices encouraged more evaders because of the larger amount of tax due from new vehicles sold above list price by dealers in socalled "used" new cars and others. When loopholes develop in a given tax, evasions usually increase as the means of escape become more widely known; so it is probable that increased familiarity with techiques has contributed to the number of evasions. BB The report of the State Tax Board for 1947 and 1948 referred to abuses of the law, stating .. the State is losing an enormous amount of revenue on incorrect affidavits filed on the one per cent motor vehicle sales tax." 

The Board made several recommendations to strengthen administration, but none of them have been adopted. The Governor has also shown an interest in correcting abuses of the law. 

Nature of Abuses

Evasions are accomplished by falsifying the affidavit in some way. 

The entire tax is evaded when it is falsely stated on the affidavit that the transaction was either (1) a gift, (2) an even trade, or (3) purchased for

resale only. (Bona fide transactions of these types are legally tax exempt.) 

Apparently, a considerable number of these evasions involve transactions between individuals rather than dealers. 、… Though evasions by claiming exemption appear to be less frequently used than **understating," they result in total loss of revenue and in larger losses per transaction than "understating." 恳​恳 ​The most frequent evader seems to be one who admits a taxable purchase but understates the total consideration. These purchases usually are from dealers, as contrasted to transactions between individuals. Evidence indicates that some dealers are involved in evasions, though their motives are not clear. One possible incentive may be that of profiting from the evasion by collecting the full amount of the tax from the customer and remitting a smaller sum to the tax assessor-collector. In some cases, the dealer seems to have co-operated merely to "save" taxes for the customer. Informed persons do not believe evasions occur through ignorance of the dealers concerned.

How the dealer is involved in the tax may be of some interest. 

Legally, the dealer or seller has no responsibility in the collection process, but many dealers handle all "paper work" for their customers as a courtesy and service and perhaps to expedite the transaction. When a sale is closed, the customer signs all instruments in the dealer's office, including those filed at the court house for title, registration, payment of the motor vehicle sales tax, etc. It is said that often the papers are signed “in blank.** 

The dealer collects the fees and taxes from the customer, completes the papers if this has not been done before signature, files the instruments with the tax assessor-collector, and pays amounts due. The purchaser, in turn, gets a vehicle "ready to go" and is convenienced thereby. 

As would be expected, only a small number of dealers are associated with evasions; some responsible persons believe that most of them are "fly-by-night" used car dealers. However, evidence indicates that sources of falsified affidavits are by no means confined to any class. For instance, one source of large numbers of affidavits that consistently show understated considerations is a major authorized dealer in one of Texas' largest cities.

Often total consideration figures on what appear to be incorrect affidavits filed by the same dealer are consistent on the same model vehicle. When this is the case, it has been found that dealers follow some routine procedure such as showing the factory list price or deducting "freight and handling charges."

Examples of Types of Evasions 

1. One observing and conscientious purchaser noted his records and wrote to the dealer to inquire if the tax was correct. The dealer wrote his customer: Mr. we always take the factory-delivered price on the car less the federal tax to show to the tax office. This is done to save you from paying the one per cent sales tax on any and all accessories that you buy with the car. 74

It was later found that the taxable consideration on this automobile was $2,271; that shown on the affidavit was $1,620. 2. In an attempt to stop evasions originating with a Dallas dealer, the State Comptroller of Public Accounts took photostatic copies of affidavits covering 17 purchases filed during a six-day period. The highest tax shown, $19.47, was for a convertible; the tax on this vehicle is ordinarily about $27. Thirteen affidavits reported a tax from $16 to $17, and three were for $15.86; these models ordinarily sell at prices that would return a tax of about $24 each. 

3. In September, 1950, a county tax assessor-collector wrote to the State Comptroller of Public Accounts asking for instructions about an affidavit filed by a Buick dealer for a customer, stating that he had traded a used vehicle for a new Buick without other consideration. The attitude of the dealer was described in the letter as follows: 

He (the dealer) argued that he does not have to show the regular sales price on any car, that he should have the right to price the cars like he wishes, and show any value that he wants to sell them for, even if it is a loss to him in doing so He says that if he only wants to show a sales price of $1, it should be legal.

A few days later the same tax assessor-collector wrote of an attempt by this dealer to evade the tax on another transaction. 

4. One dealer was found to be deducting $213 on each price shown on the affidavit to cover drayage and handling." 66 

5. Two authorized dealers in the same city have been registering list factory price as the sales price. 

6. An examination of transactions in numerous counties has revealed a surprising number of "gifts" and even trades.^^ Bo

Basic Causes of Evasions 

Evasions are one of the most vexing problems of tax administrators. 

Laws alone can never produce good administration, but good administration is dependent upon adequate laws. The question is whether evasions of the motor vehicle sales tax are an administrative problem and whether existing laws are adequate to achieve stricter enforcement. 

Since evasions are all based on falsifications of affidavits, they violate not only the penalty provision of the tax act for non-payment but make the affiant subject to the penalties of perjury, a felony. 

The penalty provision for non-payment of the tax is directed against any person (not only the purchaser) knowingly operating a vehicle on which the tax has not been paid. The violator is guilty of a misdemeanor and may be fined from $10 to $500 or confined in county jail from one to 30 days. It seems that present penalty provisions are adequate to achieve compliance--if they can be made effective. Why, then, are the penalties not invoked? 75

For one thing, county tax assessor-collectors are elective officials, and it is sometimes difficult for them to prosecute cases involving local citizens. The amount of tax involved in any transaction is not large, and public sympathy may not always be with officials. 

It is difficult to obtain necessary evidence to establish proof. To prosecute successfully for failure to pay the tax, it must be established that the individual knowingly operated a vehicle on which the tax had not been paid. To prosecute: for falsifying the affidavit would require evidence that is difficult to obtain. Inability to convict evaders is also attributed to a concept that purchasers are not morally responsible if dealers aid or abet the evasion.

SECTION 5 

PROPOSED LEGISLATION IN 1949

An attempt to amend certain provisions of the motor vehicle sales tax was made in the regular session of the 51st Legislature by House Bill 313. Two major changes were provided in the original bill. First, compensation to counties for collecting the tax was raised from two to five per cent; and second, provision was made for the State. to share receipts with counties on a fifty-fifty basis. 

An amendment in the House substituted a sliding scale of sharing revenues with counties as follows. (a) If annual revenue in a county does not exceed $100,000, one-half the revenue would go to counties and one-half to State. (b) In counties where revenue is from $100,000 to $500,000, one-half of the first $100,000 would go to the county and, of the remaining revenue, 20 per cent would go to the county and 80 per cent to the State until the county received $100,000. All in excess of $100,000 would go to the State. (c) If total revenues exceed $500,000, one-half of the first $100,000 and 20 per cent thereafter would go to the county until the total received amounted to $150,000, with all additional revenue going to the State. The provision for five per cent as compensation to the tax assessor-collector's office remained in the amended version. 

It is of interest that the House provisions for sharing revenue with counties would have provided a disproportionate break between the last two brackets. For instance, a county with $500,000 gross revenue would have received $100,000 as its share. A county with revenue receipts just above this bracket, say one cent higher, would have received an additional $30,000, or a total of $130,000.

The bill was changed quite drastically in the Senate. The plan for sharing revenue with counties was deleted and replaced by a provision that counties might levy a tax, in addition to the one per cent levied by the State, of one-half of one per cent. The county tax would have been levied entirely on a use basis, supposedly to prevent purchasers' going from the county of residence to another to avoid the tax.

The Senate's version of the bill then went to a conference committee where it again underwent major revisions. The version adopted there omitted the grant of authority to counties to levy a tax. Instead it attempted, ostensibly, to provide for a distribution of present revenue on the basis of 90 per cent to the State and 10 per cent to the counties as compensation for adminis tration. The conference committee report passed both Houses, but the Governor vetoed the bill because of its ambiguity in directing the distribution of funds. 76

The bill was so written that only revenue from "use" (under Section 2), could have been distributed on the 90-10 basis of allocation. 

No provision was made for disposition of revenues from sales, and the bill did not conform to the professed Legislative objective in its caption, which declared an intent to increase revenue to counties. Ten per cent of the use tax obviously would be less than the present two per cent of revenue from sales and use.

SECTION 6 

The motor vehicle sales tax is now a major source of State income. It produced about $14 million in 1949, a sum exceeded only by revenues from six other taxes or licenses. 

Administration of this relatively simple levy is not complex, and there seem to be no ramifications with intense economic, political, or social significance.

The tax of one per cent (plus a temporary one-tenth per cent) is paid by the purchaser, assessed on the basis of his affidavit, to the county tax assessor-collector.

The tax does not apply to trailers and semi-trailers, since these are not technically "motor vehicles. 66 All accessories, including radios if they are attached to the vehicle at the time of sale, are included in the taxable consideration. If so attached, radios are not subject to the radio sales tax of two per cent. In addition to the serious problem of evasion, other aspects of the motor vehicle sales tax present issues worthy of recognition. These may best be discussed within the given problem areas. 

The Evasion Problem 

Though it has not been possible to make a completely reliable estimate, some persons familiar with the tax believe the State's loss of revenue because of evasion may be as much as several million dollars a year. Regardless of amount, there is substantial evidence that evasion is widespread and increasing.

A summary of evasion tactics may facilitate discussion. All evasions are initiated by the purchaser's falsifying the affidavit which forms the basis of assessment. The entire tax is evaded if the purchaser dishonestly swears that a vehicle is either (1) purchased for resale only, (2) acquired by an even trade, or (3) acquired as a gift. A portion of the tax is evaded by simply understating the amount paid for the vehicle. Automobile dealers are sometimes implicated in. evasions, although legally they are not responsible for either collection or payment of the tax. 

Several alternatives offer possibilities that may strengthen enforcement, though none seems ideal. An obvious way to stop evasions by false statements of tax-exempt transactions (gifts, even trades, or bought for 77

resale only) would be to cease granting these exemptions. This, however, would in reality levy another tax, somewhat in the nature of an occupa÷ tional tax, on dealers. In the case of even trades and gifts, it might aid enforcement if the donor, or each party to a trade, were required: to sign the affidavit.

To insure collections if purchasers understate taxable considerations, some technique of obtaining evidence of violations is needed. It appears that most evasions could be prevented if State and local officials had access to records of dealers. There is a question whether or not dealers could be required by statute to allow inspection of records when the occupation is not licensed by the State and when the dealer has no statutory responsibility for the tax. Some States collect the motor vehicle sales tax from dealers and, in turn, license the occupation of dealing in motor vehicles.

There is no record in the State Comptroller's Office of a violator's ever having been prosecuted. The State Comptroller believes better compliance could be achieved if violation cases were transferred to Travis County and the State became a party to the case. 

Compensation to Counties

County tax offices are allowed by statute to retain two per cent of the tax collected as compensation. Apparently, this payment will average about 18 or 20 cents per transaction. The question of how much should be granted involves not only a fair reimbursement but whether larger grants would encourage better enforcement at the local level. If the State wishes to increase compensation, there are three alternatives, each of which has some particular advantages: (1) increase the percentage allowance, or (2) change the method to a fixed-fee basis, or (3) retain the percentage system with a minimum fee for each transaction. 

Advocates of the percentage system say that it provides an incentive for enforcement. Fixed fees have the advantage of being appropriate to the service performed, inasmuch as the amount of work for each transaction is about equal and has no relation to the tax collected. Audit

There is no statutory requirement as to how long affidavits must be retained by county tax offices nor to disposition of these affidavits. In a few instances they have been destroyed before the State's representatives had an opportunity to audit. 

Safeguarding and Transmitting Funds

Though the State has never lost funds from this tax, inadequate protection gives rise to a latent possibility. County tax assessor-collectors are bonded for ten per cent of the State assessment on the property tax roll or a maximum of $50,000. Since motor vehicle sales tax funds are remitted quarterly, this fund alone may be far in excess of the bond--disregarding funds from property taxes, automobile licenses, and other sources. After 

January 1, 1951, the State will no longer levy a property tax for general revenue so in the future bonds will be even smaller in most counties. 78

Another point that may merit recognition in considering the frequency with which moneys should be remitted to the State is that of availability for use. With collections totaling about $3.5 million dollars per quarter, the State has a sizable average daily balance at the local level which could be gainfully employed. 79

Chapter IV THE CHAIN STORE TAX SECTION I HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

Early Development of Chain Stores 

Chain stores as we know them are of comparatively recent origin. 

However, available records indicate that a chain of a great many units existed in the Chinese Empire as early as 200 B. C. Greek and Roman records include references to aggregates of stores, and later examples developed in Germany and Japan. 1

Probably the earliest chain of stores in the Americas was the Hudson Bay Company, chartered in 1670 and still in existence. The second oldest is Companhia Calcado Clarke in Brazil, organized by an emigrant Scotchman over a hundred years ago.

Although a number of small chains came temporarily into existence during the first half of the 19th century, the Great Atlantic and Pacific Tea Company is generally credited with being the first chain merchandising system in the United States. Beginning in 1858 with a single store in 

Manhattan and a cargo of tea, by 1938 it was operating approximately 15,000 stores. The F. W. Woolworth Company began as a single store in Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and by 1919 consisted of nearly 1,000 stares in the United States, Canada, and Great Britain. A similar variety chain, the S. S. Kresge Company, was organized in 1885. Other early chains include the Kroger Grocery and Baking Company, the United Cigar Stores

Company, and the J. C. Penney Company. The year 1915 saw the beginnings of McCrory, a variety chain, Walgreen in the drug field and Safeway, the second largest grocery chain.

By 1900 there were about 700 chain systems operating approximately 4,500 stores; this number had increased to 20, 000 systems and 200,000 stores by 1928. The volume of business handled by chain stores increased from approximately four per cent of the total retail trade in 1919 to between 

25 to 35 per cent of retail store sales in 1938. 1 

Theodore E. Beckman and Herman C. Nolen, The Chain Store Problem, McGraw-Hill, New York, 1938, p. 14. 80

Anti-Chain Store Movement 1,2 Taxation of chain stores seems to fit the anti-trust pattern of legislation in the late 19th and early 20th Centuries. "For generations rugged individualism and free-for-all competion characterized American business. 

In this competitive spirit, enterprising businessmen learned that operations on a large scale could greatly enhance their chances of success, and mergers and combinations resulted in the meteoric rise of corporate undertakings. 

Sma 11 independent businessmen began to feel that their giant competitors were waging unfair warfare and turned to the government for legislative protection. 

Thus was born the era of anti-trust legislation and government regulation. 

Large-scale business continued to develop, however, despite the Sherman and Clayton Anti-Trust Acts, the Federal Trade Commission, and other such legislative measures.

Chain stores were not the first retail merchandising establishments against which resentment of independent retailers was directed. Attempts were made to regulate department stores in the late 1800's, mail order houses in the early 1900's, and after the first World War, house-to-house salesmen. The first efforts to curb chain store expansion took the form of pressure on manufacturers to persuade them to discontinue sales to chain systems. When this method realized little success, attempts were made to discourage consumers from patronizing such stores. Finally, the chain store antagonists sought and received political aid.

Another circumstance, however, contributed to the anti-chain store movement. The pressing need for additional State revenues in most states in the late 1920's effected the enactment of many new taxes, including some on chain stores. 

Early Chain Store Taxation 

Legislative action against chain stores did not first express itself through taxation. A law in Pennsylvania requiring that all drug stores in that State be owned by registered pharmacists was declared unconstitutional by the United States Supreme Court in 1929. Maryland, in 1927, attempted to prohibit the growth of chain stores. Such direct attempts to regulate chain stores were unsuccessful chiefly because of their discriminatory character. 2 

Theodore E. Beckman and Herman C. Nolen, The Chain Store Problem, McGraw-Hill, New York, 1938, p. 273. 

3 Ibid., pp. 246-248. 

4 Liggett Co. v. Baldridge, 278 U. S. 105, (1929). 81

Taxation was the next resort. Chain store taxation has not been restricted to the United States. Bavaria in 1910 imposed a sales tax on chain stores; and France in 1912 levied a graduated tax on multiple stores.

As can be seen from Table Chain I, four States enacted chain store taxes in 1927. These States were Wisconsin, North Carolina, Georgia, and Maryland, All four such taxes were subsequently invalidated through court action. The first two taxes to be upheld by the courts were those enacted by Georgia and Indiana in 1929. The Indiana case was epochal in that it was the first decision by the United States Supreme Court involving a graduated chain store license tax and historic in that the tax was held constitutional. Following this case, decided in 1931, the number of bills introduced in State legislatures proposing chain store taxes jumped to 175 (the preceding year only 13 such bills had been introduced) and reached a peak of 225 in 1933. In this latter year 13 States enacted such taxes. 

Types of Chain Store Taxes 

Chain store taxes have been of two general types, graduated license taxes and taxes graduated according to gross receipts. The first type is generally levied at a progressive upward rate depending upon the number of stores operated in each State. The stores are bracketed by groups and : either a certain fee charged for each additional store or a flat rate charged for each store, depending upon the number bracket into which the chain falls. Louisiana in 1932 introduced a new basis for determining the license tax. Chain stores were charged a graduated rate for each store in Louisiana with the rate determined by the total number of stores operated by the chain in the whole United States.

The graduated gross receipts tax first appeared in Kentucky in 1930. 

It was levied at a progressive upward rate of gross receipts. Several other 

States enacted this type of tax in the early 1930's.

Both graduated license and graduated gross receipts chain store taxes have been challenged in the courts. The court decisions have been significant in determining the general pattern of such taxation throughout the country. 

Beckman & Nolen, op. cit., p. 249.

Graduated License Taxes

The validity of a chain store tax was first challenged in the United States Supreme Court in 1931.7 The tax in question was enacted by North 

Carolina and levied a flat rate on each store in excess of one. Though the 

Court held the tax to be constitutional, the question of graduated license taxes was not an issue.

However, the same year, the matter was clarified by a United States Supreme Court decision involving an Indiana graduated license tax enacted in 1929. Prior to that time, considerable confusion had existed as to the constitutionality of graduated chain store license taxes because State courts had decided both for and against taxes of this type. ... The principles enumerated by Mr. Justice.Roberts in the Indiana case are important because they have guided subsequent decisions relating to chain store taxation. 8 Several distinctions enjoyed by chain stores were first recorded: quantity buying, cash buying and cash discounts, : skill in buying, warehousing, abundant supply of capital, "slightly lower prices, "greater turnover, "unified and therefore cheaper and better advertising," standard forms of display, superior management and methods, concentration of management in special lines of goods, special accounting methods, and standardization of management, sales policies, and goods.

Adding that discrimination alone was not sufficient grounds for declaring a tax invalid if founded upon "reasonable distinctions," the Court held the distinctions with regard to chain stores sufficient as a basis for classification. In conclusion the Court considered itself incompetent "to consider the propriety or justness of the tax, to seek for the motives, or to critize the public policy, which prompted the adoption of the legislation." This latter dictum has been repeatedly quoted by chain stare proponents as suggesting the impropriety and unjustness of chain store taxes.

With the decision in the Indiana case opening the way, a virtual rash of chain store taxes appeared on legislative rosters throughout the United States. In the face of this development, the United States Supreme Court 

The Great Atlantic & Pacific Tea Co. v. Maxwell, 284 U. S. 576, (1931). 

8 State Board of Tax Commissioners v. Jackson, 283 U. S. 527 (1931). 

83

in ruled a Florida statute levying a higher tax on chain stores operating in two or more counties uncounstitutional, arguing that an increase in the levy consequent upon the mere physical fact that the new one (store) lies a few feet over the county line finds no foundation in reason or in any fact of business experience.

However, this triumph of the chain stores in their court battles was more than offset by several later decisions. 

In 1935, a case reached the Supreme Court in which Standard Oil. 

Company of New Jersey challenged a West Virginia statute classifying filling stations as stores. Standard Oil claimed the tax was burdensome and unconstitutional under the Fourteenth Amendment because the company had paid 42 per cent of the taxes collected under the Act on a total of 1,003 filling stations. Further proof was offered purporting to show that five oil companies doing only four per cent of the chain store business paid 83.5

ΤΟ per cent of the total tax received. 

The Court upheld the classification of filling stations as stores and denied the charge that the tax was oppressive: 

When the power to tax exists, the extent of the burden is a matter for the discretion of the lawmakers. . .(they) may make the tax so heavy as to discourage multiplication of the units to an extent deemed inordinate, and by the incidence of the burden develop other forms of industry. .. 

Even if the tax should destroy a business, it would not be made invalid or require compensation on that ground alone. 11

The following year, 1936, the Court ruled that the leasing of filling stations in West Virginia by distributions to the operators did not exempt the leased stations.

Up to 1937, litigation in both federal and state courts had been confined to a determination of the constitutionality of graduated license taxes with rates based upon the number of stores within an individual State. In 

1937 the Louisiana tax, which was graduated according to the number of stores in the United States although the tax was payable only on the stores in 

Louisianan, came before the Supreme Court. 

Liggett & Co. et. al. v. Amos, 288 U.S. 516. 

10D. Bloomfield, Chain Stores and Legislation, pp. 247-248. 

11Fox v. Standard Oil of New Jersey, 294 U.S. 87 ('35). 

12 Gulf Refining Co. v. Fox, 297 U.S. 381 ('36). 84

In holding the method of classification valid, the Court reasoned: the opportunities and powers of a chain operator become greater with the growth of the number of units maintained.

All the stores of a retail chain contribute to the central purchasing power of the chain irrespective of state lines and location of stores, and increase the per unit multiple advantage enjoyed by the operator of the system · 0 0 

If the competitive advantages of a chain increase with the number of its component links, it is hard to see how these advantages cease at the state boundary. 13 While admitting that chain systems enjoy advantages over independent operators, some writers have criticized the Court's assumption that the longer the chain the greater its advantage, i.e., a longer chain is in a more advantageous position than a shorter chain. 14 It would seem that there may be a point of diminishing returns beyond which the marginal return decreases with each unit.

Graduated license taxes were also held constitutional in Alabama, Idaho, Michigan, Mississippi, and South Carolina.

Thus, it is evident that the courts have established certain fairly definite patterns of chain store taxation: chains may be classified on the basis of the number of stores, either within a single State, or within the United States, but not within a county; graduated license types of taxes are evidently valid; and rates are not generally to be grounds for invalidity of such taxes.

The pattern is not without exception, however. Against the apparent trend of judicial determinations, the Pennsylvania Supreme Court in 1939 held that that State's graduated license chain store and theater tax was incompatible with the State Constitutional provision that all taxes be uniform because it was a "graded tax. 15 In the same year the Kentucky Court of Appeals went even further against the tide of judicial opinion in a case involving a graduated license tax which had been enacted to replace the graduated gross receipts tax previously held invalid. This chain store license tax did not depart markedly from the type of taxes then being enacted in other states, and largely upheld in the courts. While admitting that the State Legislature might regulate, 76 

13 Great Atlantic & Pacific Tea Co. v. Grosjean, 81 U.S. 735 ('37). 

14 Burkman & Nolen, The Chain Store Problem, pp.257-258. 

15American Stores Co. v. Boardman Stanley Co. of America v. Boardman, 

6 Atl. 2d 826. ('39). 

16Great Atlantic & Pacific Tea Co. y. Ky. Tax Commission, 128 S.W. 2d 

581 (39). 85

under the police power, occupations affecting the public interest, the Court explained that such regulation must not be "unduly oppressive." 

The chain store tax, however, was deemed a revenue-raiser and thus subject to the requirement that the tax be uniform on all subjects within each class. The court then held the classification of stores into groups "based on number of stores operated, without respect to volume of business and having no relation to the advantages alleged to attach to multiple store operation" "unreasonable and arbitrary." In contradiction to the holdings of other State and Federal Courts that the classification according to numbers was just and based upon real differences, the Court said that such alleged differences existed among independent operators as well. 

Graduated Gross Receipts Taxes 

In contrast to the graduated license taxes, those which have been graduated according to gross receipts have not enjoyed as much judicial blessing. In 1930, Kentucky enacted such a graduated gross receipts tax by which the gross sales of a chain were determined by the total sales of all its units. The tax was contested by both chain and independent store operators and reached the Supreme Court in 1935.17 The Court called the tax "unjustifiably unequal, whimsical, and arbitrary" in that “it exacts from two persons different amounts for the privilege of doing exactly similar acts because the one has performed the act oftener than the other" and disregards "the absence of any reasonable relation between the chosen criterion of classification and the privilege the enjoyment of which is said to be the subject taxed.''

An Iowa tax of the same type was also declared unconstitutional by the United States Supreme Court. Graduated gross receipts taxes were invalidated by State Supreme Courts in Wisconsin, Vermont, and New Mexico in 1935. A Minnesota tax was also considered unconstitutional because of the adverse decision in the Iowa.case.

A combination graduated gross receipts and graduated license tax was enacted by Florida in 1935, containing a stipulation that the license rates would be doubled automatically if the gross receipts tax was declared valid. Though the portion of the law relating to gross receipts was held void, the provision doubling the license tax was upheld.18 

Early Efforts in Texas

The widespread activity in regard to chain store taxes throughout the country was not limited to other States. As early as 1929 a chain store tax bill was introduced in the Texas Legislature. Earlier attempts to enact such taxes as amendments to other bills may have been made, but are not separately indexed in records of legislative proceedings and, thus, cannot easily be found. 

17Stewart Dry Goods, et al., v. Lewis, 294 U.S. 550. 18.

Lane Drug Stores, Inc. v. Lee, Florida C. T. Par 7978 W. 

86

The 1929 bill was denominated an "occupation tax" on the operation of three or more stores in a chain. It was not reported from committe.19 

In 1930 four chain store tax bills were introduced in the Texas Legislature. 

Two of these were license tax proposals on one or more stores, and two were designated as occupation taxes to be levied on operators of more than five stores. One of the latter was passed by the House but was not reported from Senate committee. firs

Two more efforts to pass chain store taxes were made in 1931 with no success," but in 1933 a bill which introduced for the first time in Texas a graduated license tax passed both the House and Senate. However, there was never any agreement to the Senate amendments, and the bill did not become law. In the same year, an attempt was also made to enact a gross sales tax on chain stores. Thus, in one session, both major types of chain store taxation were proposed. However, these efforts to pass such tax measures met defeat.

In 1933 an attempt was made to make it unlawful for any retail distributor with a sales volume of $60,000 or more in any three-month period "to engage in manufacturing, processing or packaging" of foods, beverages or groceries.23 The avowed intent of this measure was "to regulate a type of competition that, unless it be regulated, may ultimately destroy the independent grocer, and Texas industries depending for distribution upon grocery stores 

This Senate bill died in committee. Proponents of chain store taxation and advocates of chain store regulation, however, were undaunted by the continued lack of success, and further efforts to pass such legislation were made in 1935 and 1936. 

Through 1936, 18 separate bills were introduced attempting to levy taxes on chain stores. One of these proposed a tax on gross receipts, another on net income, and the remainder were license or occupation taxes (both graduated and ungraduated).

The First Texas Chain Store Tax 

Finally, in 1936, a chain store tax of the graduated license type was enacted. This legislation, with its amendments, is the present law. The rates set forth ranged from one dollar for a single store to $750 for each store in excess of 50.

One of the principal problems of this form of taxation--exemptions arose immediately. Added to the original bill by the Senate were exemptions

Collection of the chain store tax was enjoined immediately after its enactment and the act was challenged as unconstitutional. The litigation lasted two years and ended when the tax was upheld by the State Supreme 25

Later Legislative Action 

Repeal of the chain store tax was proposed in 1937 and 1941 but received little attention.26 Litigation costs in conjunction with the challenged constitutionality of the chain store tax were recognized by an attempted appropriation of $10,000 to meet this expense in 1937. 

Exemptions, too, continued to be elaborated. Strict interpretation of the word exclusively in the filling station and lumber business exemptions by the Attorney General had operated so as to prevent practically all these stores from claiming exemption.28 Following dissolution of the injunction against collection of the chain store tax in 1938, several recovery actions were instituted by the Comptroller which resulted in the collection of considerable additional revenue. At the same time and possibly partially as the result of these court actions, pressure was increased for an extension or redefinition of exemptions. 29 In 1941, religious book stores were added to exemptions. In the same act, the provision exempting filling stations was altered to exclude from payment of the tax all filling stations deriving 75 per cent or more of their gross receipts from the sale, storage or distribution of petroleum products. Thus, the word exclusively was eliminated and the "75 per cent rule" initiated. In 1943 this rule was extended to cover all lumber and building material stores and to include utility stores in towns of 3,000 or less population.30 An attempt was made in 1945 to apply the rule to businesses selling ice, but, as finally passed, the amendment permitted exemption to stores where "nothing is sold except ice."31 

Thus, the Texas chain store tax, after nearly 15 years of operation and modification has not yet established a static pattern. This is in
