contrast with some other State taxes, which have reached a stage of relative stability. Such continuing evolution is not unique to Texas, however, for chain store taxes in other States are still undergoing a process of basic change. This tax will, perhaps, receive, as it deserves, both attention and thought in the years ahead. The original philosophy upon which the tax is based has been materially altered in Texas and throughout the nation. Many and various methods for dealing with its particular problems have been tried, but as yet there is no generally recognized pattern of application or administration of this tax. 

Eighteen states now levy chain store taxes. Louisiana, South

Dakota, Mississippi and Florida levy graduated license taxes according to the number of stores located anywhere in the United States. 

Texas, Alabama, Georgia, South Carolina, West Virginia, Indiana,

Michigan, Iowa, Montana and Colorado levy graduated license taxes on the basis of the number of stores within the State. North Carolina levies a tax of $65 per year for each store in excess of one, with a tax on operators of filling stations graduated according to the number of pumps. 

Tennessee levies a tax of $3 per 100 square feet of floor space in all stores. Delaware levies a tax of $10 on persons or corporations having principal places of business outside the State but maintaining wholesale or retail branch stores or distributing depots in Delaware, plus ten cents per $100 of cost value of goods received in excess of $5,000. Maryland levies both a graduated license tax and a graduated tax on stock in trade. Early chain store taxes were enacted and repealed in Montana, Maine and Arizona without court action. The California electorate rejected a chain store tax in a 1936 referendum, as did the people of Utah in 1942. 

A Wisconsin tax was repealed in 1939 and not replaced. Idaho repealed its chain store tax in 1949. 89

SECTION - ORGANIZATIONAL FORM 

General

The Chain Store Tax is actually a misnomer, for the tax is levied on all stores in the State, with only certain exceptions. Even stores operating as single units are liable for the tax. By court ruling, it has been construed to be an occupation tax" and a revenue measure although these descriptions nowhere appear in the statute. 32 The administration of the tax is based upon the issuance of annual licenses by the Comptroller, and payment is made at a graduated rate according to the number of stores located in Texas. All authority for administration of the tax is vested in the Comptroller by the law. Recent legislation has provided for a temporary increase in the basic scale of ::: rates. 33 

Statutory Provisions 

A reading of the Chain Store Tax Law leaves a definite impression of simplicity of administrative organization. The organizational form prescribed is compact and integrated; the administration is apparently centralized. Responsibility for collection, supervision, and enforcement of application and license fees is given to the Comptroller.34 He is authorized to prescribe the form of license applications, require additional information to that specified in the Act, and promulgate rules and regulations governing tax collection methods. Determination as to the sufficiency of application is left to his discretion. Initial and subsequent licenses are issued by the Comptroller on forms prescribed by him, and he is further "vested with all of the power and authority conferred by this Act. 35 He is required to prepare such blanks, forms, reports, receipts, and any and all other things" necessary for collection of the tax. The expenses incurred in administration of the chain store store tax may not exceed the amount collected from application fees of 50 cents per store. These are the statutory provisions governing the organizational form of the chain store tax administration.

Practice More Complex 

A study of the actual administration, however, reveals a some what different picture-one more complex and far less integrated. Until 

1939, the chain store tax was administered by the Gross Receipts Tax Division of the Comptroller's Department. In that year a Store Tax Division was created which now handles both this tax and those on cosmetics and radios. The Director of this Division performs the duties of the 

Comptroller outlined by the tax law. 

32 Hurt v. Cooper, 1937, 110 SW 2d 896. 

33 Tex. Laws, 1950, 51st Leg. 1st Called Session, Ch. 2, Art. XVIII. 

34Tex. Laws, 44th Leg., 1st C.S., 1935, ch. 400, Sec. 2. 

35Ibid., Sec. 4. 90

It would appear that centralized administration of the tax had been achieved since the law specifies that the Comptroller exercise "all authority conferred by this Act." In practice, however, responsibility is divided between several divisions of the Comptroller's Department. Having available only the allotted filing fees, the Store Tax Division is unable to employ a field force. To achieve some sort of regular inspection of places of business to assure display of store licenses and payment of fees, an administrative arrangement has been made with both the Cigarette Tax Division and the Motor Fuel Tax Division whereby some of their field representatives devote fairly regular time to enforcement of the Chain Store Tax. 

This arrangement, though prompted by necessity, some what complicates the work of the Store Tax Division in that its Director must rely, in large part, upon enforcement by field personnel who are not subject to his jurisdiction.

The authority to require additional information is sometimes exercised to determine sufficiency of tax payments. Field representatives of the Motor Fuel Tax Division and the Cigarette Tax Division.are sometimes utilized to obtain additional information or to assist taxpayers in understanding amounts due. Some local municipal and county officials distribute application forms and furnish information relative to the tax to store owners or operators.

During the early years of the Chain Store Tax, policy determinations as to tax liability were made largely by the courts and the Attorney General. 

At present, by reference to these early interpretations, the Director of the Store Tax Division is usually able to settle such questions as continue to arise.

Thus, although administration of the Chain Store Tax is centralized by the law in a single State agency, the enforcement function is handled by two unrelated divisions of that agency. Lack of enforcement personnel in the division assigned the administration of this tax brings about this situation. 91

SECTION 

ASSESSMENT 

As has been previously mentioned, the name of the chain store tax is misleading, for the tax is levied on all stores in the State, with only certain exceptions. According to the law, the tax must be paid by "every person, agent, receiver, trustee, firm,corporation, association,:or copartnership opening, establishing, operating or maintaining one or more mercantile establishment within the State..." 1036

Assessment is made by the taxpayer himself, who is required at the time of starting business and annually thereafter to submit, along with payment of the annual tax, an application for a license. The amount of tax is determined by the number of store units under one mangement or ownership, with rates ranging according to the following graduated scale: first store $1, second $6, third through fifth $25 each, sixth through tenth $50 each, eleventh through twentieth $150,each, twenty-first through thirtyfifth $250 each, thirty-sixth through fiftieth $500 each, and fifty-first and above $750 each.

In its first called session, the 51st Legislature levied an additional tax of three-fourths of ten per cent of the computed tax for 1950, to be paid in that year, and of two-thirds of ten per cent of the computed tax for 1951, to be paid that year.

In addition to the graduated tax, a fee of 50 cents per store is charged to defray the cost of administering the tax. 

Considered only on its essentials, the assessment function of this tax appears quite clear and simple; the tax is levied against store owners, who compute their own taxes according to a graduated rate scale based on the number of stores in the State under control of one owner, and who submit the tax payments at the time they submit their applications for the annual store license. However, within this framework is contained a complex system of exemptions and exceptions initiated in the original act and developed and elaborated over a period of years by legislative amendments, court interpretations and rulings of the Attorney General. 

Court Interpretations Frequent

From the earliest years of the tax, the broad classifications uppn which the tax is levied according to the law gave rise to many questions regarding liability of individual businesses. What is a chain? Are subsidiary units of a chain or the central agency alone liable for the tax? Is the seller or owner or merchandise liable for the tax where these are not the same? To further confuse the picture, the law was challenged as unconstitutional shortly after its passage and an injunction against its enforcement was granted. 

36 Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Leg., 1st C.S. ch. 400, sec. 5. 92

One by one the questions were answered in the courts. The Texas 

Supreme Court in 1937 held the tax law valid, stating that the language of the constitution "implies power in the Legislature to classify the subjects of occupation taxes and only requires that the tax should be equal and uniform upon the same class."37 Classification upon the basis of numbers of stores is permissible, the court said, and the determination as to where to draw the line is a legislative power not subject to judicial determination. The argument that the exemptions contained in the original tax law 38 also impose an unequal burden and are discriminatory was overruled on the grounds that such exemptions are a mere extension of the classification which is permissible under the Texas constitution. 

In this decision, the Texas Supreme Court followed the predominant trend of judicial determination both in other States and in the United States 

Supreme Court.

Following this decision in Hurt v. Cooper, the injunction against the enforcement of the tax was dissolved early in 1938. Other rulings followed in rapid succession. The ultimate control over, or ownership of, an enterprise was established as the determining factor for the existence of a chain.39 Once this determination is made, responsibility for payment ôf the tax may be allocated to subsidiary units. 

Also established was the rule that the act of selling merchandise to the consumer is the basis for determination of the necessity for payment of the tax; ownership of the merchandise being sold is not a determining factor.41 Also "where you have an established place of business for the sale of... merchandise of any kind, whether the same is in a build- ..42 ing or not, the same would be subject to the Texas chain store tax.

These and numerous other interpretations during the seven or eight years following passage of the tax bill served to clarify the several vague areas in the original statute.

They also served to strengthen the position of the administrator who, as the years went by, found himself armed with judicial rulings concerning most of the problems which arose. As a consequence, the administrator of this tax has occupied the relatively unique position in recent years of being able to answer most questions of policy coming before him without having to request further interpretation. Due to this fact, no rules and regulations have been formulated by the Comptroller to govern administration of the tax. Instead, reference usually is made to easily available authoritative tax service publications which are designed specifically for the use of taxpayers. 

37Hurt v. Cooper, 1937, 110 S. W. 2d 896. 

38 Tex. Laws, 1935, 44th Leg., 1st C.S., ch. 400, sec. 5. 

39Opinion of Attorney General, Aug. &, 1938. 

40Safeway Stores, Inc. v. Sheppard, 1941, 158 S. W. 2d 319. 

41Opinion of Attorney General, May 4, 1938. 

42 Opinion of Attorney General, No. 0-3373, May 14, 1941. 93

Exemptions

Most significant of the assessment problems of the chain store tax throughout its history has been that posed by the exemptions written into the law. These have been of great variety, and during the process of their development have tended to alter the basic philosophy underlying this tax. As has been discussed in Section 1, the early efforts to effect passage of chain store taxes throughout the country were aimed at merchandising establishments which, by virtue of their size and numbers, had at least an alleged competitive advantage over the small independent merchant operating a single store. However, due to the effect of the exemptions which have been written into the law, the present tax in many instances lies heavier on the small independent one-store merchant, who pays a chain store tax in Texas as in most States having such tax, than upon many of the large, multi-unit organizations. 

Specifically, exemptions from the chain store tax in Texas include: a) Businesses processing dairy products for retail sale; b Oil and gas well supplies and equipment dealers; c) Filling stations and service stations deriving at least 75 per cent of gross income from the sale, storage or distribution of petroleum products; d) Wholesale and retail businesses. deriving 75 per cent of their gross income from sale of lumber or building materials (“) the practical effect is the same as if the chain store tax law was repealed insofar as the lumber concerns throughout the 

State . are concerned.)43 44. e) Gas and electric utilities operating in incorporated" towns of less than 3,000 inhabitants and deriving at least 75 per cent of their gross income from the sale of gas and electric service the practical effect is the same as if the chain store tax law was repealed insofar as . . . the utilities in said towns 43 are concerned."); 

6. 6. f) Businesses where nothing is sold except ice; g) Businesses distributing, but not selling, products of their own manufacture; h) Businesses paying an occupation tax on gross receipts; i) Religious book stores.

Effect of Exemptions 

Some idea of the operation of exemptions in specific cases can be gathered from the following comparisons. A hardware store owner who deals in electrical and gas appliances, general hardware, tools, etc., with a yearly gross of $50,000 is subject to the chain store tax. On the other hand, a multi-unit lumber company in the same city, with an annual gross of more than $100,000 on its store sales of electrical and gas appliances, is not liable to the tax. This is because the lumber company's gross from lumber sales 

43Opinion of the Attorney General No. 0-5235, April 23, 1943. 94

presumably accounts for more than 75 per cent of its total sales. In towns of less than 3,000 similar situations sometimes exist with regard to stores operated by multiple-unit utility concerns. which are exempt under the 

75 per cent rule and local appliance stores which are subject to the tax. Again, a one-man automobile accessory store in a larger city is required to pay the tax, although numerous filling stations throughout the city may each gross considerably more from accessory sales. Similar comparisons could be cited. for most of the other exemptions. It is true that the tax payments involved may be small but the peculiar results are apparent.

The somewhat incongruous situations discussed above, in which the tax generally recognized as a chain store tax is in actuality applied not to the chains but only to independents in some lines of business, are not the only problems produced by exemptions. They also cause administrative difficulty in the matter of effecting complete coverage. Under the 75 per cent rule; there is little chance of ascertaining whether or not stores are actually exempt from the tax without an audit of sales records. It must be remembered that a store owner or operator makes virtually the sole determination as to his exemptability. Lacking a field force, the Store Division of the 

Comptroller's office is unable to conduct such audits except on very rare occasions when the director of the division takes time from his other duties to do it himself.

Exemptions in Other States 

Texas is by no means alone, however, among the States levying such taxes, in having exemptions. Only seven of the 18 States having such taxes restrict the tax to multiple-unit stores, and only one-third of the States have no exemptions in their chain store statutes.

Of those with exemptions, all exempt filling stations. Three exempt ice businesses, two exempt: lumber businesses, while three others exempt all building materials concerns. Only two exempt dairies and three exempt public utility stores. Instead of the 75 per cent rule, however, the determination of exemptability in these States varies. In Iowa a 95 per cent rule applies; in South Dakota 90 per cent of gross must be in the exempted product before the exemption applies. Several of the States have criteria of average daily volume in order to determine exemptability. 

Thus, it can be seen that no generally accepted pattern for assessment and exemption has been developed among the States having this tax, nor even a workable method of determining exemptions. 95

SECTION COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT Collection Procedures 

Licensing is the basis of the collection function, which consists simply of the taxpayer's submission of the annual tax and 50 cent fee along with an application for license to the Comptroller, as discussed in Section 3. The statute requires that the application for license be notarized and submitted to the Comptroller before December 31 of each year for the succeeding year. 

In some detail the law requires that each application show the name of the owner, manager, trustee, lessee, receiver, or other person desiring such license; the name of such store or mercantile establishment; and the location, including the street number of such store or mercantile establishment. The application may contain "such other information as the applicant may wish to include, or as the Comptroller may require.' More than one store may be listed on each application, but a fee of 50 cents must be paid for each. 

The Comptroller, if he determines that any application is not in proper form or does not contain the requisite information, may return it to the applicant for correction. Administrative expenses of the chain store tax may not exceed the amount received from application fees. 45 99 In actual practice, collection procedure follows the statute rather closely. Obvious or easily detected discrepancies are usually discovered through an examination of the application when received in the Store Tax Division, and the tax computations are checked for accuracy. When additional information is desired from domestic businesses, a request is mailed from the Austin office or field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division are asked to contact applicants directly. When additional information is desired from foreign concerns, the threat of delay in the issuance of licenses is usually sufficient to obtain it.

Businesses which are in the exempt categories are not required to submit applications for licenses or exemption nor to furnish any information relative to their exemptability.

Though the statute requires that applications be notarized, this procedure is not always followed. When applications are received by field representatives, notarizing is not considered necessary, and, in fact, this reason is sometimes advanced to account for delinquency stores are thereby saved the bothersome task of seeing a notary. There have been few prosecutions for false affidavits, and the added expense and time necessary to return applications have not been considered worthwhile. 

Rates, though not ordinarily pertinent to collection methods, are significant in the administration of the chain store tax. The majority of licenses, as many as 82 to 85 per cent of all applications, as issued for single stores rather than chains and involve a license of only one dollar plus a 50cent fee. The temporary increase for 1950, amounting to only eight cents for an independent store, has considerably multiplied the correspondence and telephone requests for information to the Store Tax Division, since many taxpayers have found it difficult to compute their 1950 taxes. 

44Tex. Laws 1935, 44th Leg., 1st C.S., ch. 400, sec. 2. 

45 Ibid., sec. 9. 96

Delinquencies 

As mentioned above, the statute requires that the application and payment for the annual license be submitted to the Comptroller before December 31 of each year for the succeeding year. The law further provides a penalty ranging from $25 to $100 for each day a tax receipt is not displayed. Thus, a storekeeper who failed to submit application and fee for his 1951 license by December 31, 1950, became technically liable for the penalty immediately after January 1, 1951.

Actually, payments are made throughout the year, and penalties are virtually never assessed. The only penalty (for $25) in recent years was applied against a storekeeper who refused to pay his license fee after being contacted. The penalty is considered so high that its practical value in preventing delinquency is negligible.

In practice, the Store Tax Division, in May or June following the due date for renewal of licenses (December 31), furnishes the field men of the Cigarette Tax Division a list of. store-owners who were licensed in the previous year but who have not yet submitted the current year's application and fees. 

This is usually a sizeable list which in a recent year showed 2,200 names in the Dallas area alone. Technically, all stores on this list are delinquent by some six months. However, collections are made by the field men on the basis that these are current payments, and no penalties are invoked. To check the list of those who have not submitted their tax payments requires approximately two months of almost full-time work by the Cigarette Tax Division's field men. 

In the lack of a specific penalty for delinquency, the chain store tax. law is unique unless the penalty for failure to display a tax receipt may be considered as aimed at delinquency. This lack is especially noteworthy since there are no provisions for lien or attachment to assure tax payment. Reports from field personnel show frequent delinquent payments throughout the year, not only for the current year, which are shown as current payments, but also delinquencies for previous years. Even against the se, penalties are rarely assessed.

During the 1949-1950 fiscal year, 13.62 per cent of the total collections were paid to field men. Since a large majority of these were for singleunit stores, this percentage of collections involved a considerably greater percentage of the total number of licenses - possibly 50 per cent or more of the total. A number were delinquent for several years. Coverage 

As can be seen from the preceding discussion, the problem of handling delinquencies constitutes a major difficulty of enforcement. In addition, however, there is the even greater problem of coverage. In considering these administrative problems, it must be remembered that the enforcement workload is divided between two divisions of the Comptroller's Department. This arrangement, as has been discussed in Section 2, requires the assistance of the field force of the Cigarette Tax Division (and occasionally the Motor Fuel Division) in administering the chain store tax. The Store Tax Division employs 18 in its Austin office and is responsible for the administration of the tax on radios and cosmetics in addition to the Chain Store Tax. The problems and inadequacies of such methods are apparent, and certainly both divisions deserve recognition for their efforts to overcome obvious difficulties. 97

-- However, the problem of assuring complete coverage of the tax is complicated somewhat by the duality of enforcement responsibility. According to the statute, licenses must be conspicuously displayed, presumably for someone's inspection. 46 In the fiscal year 1949-50, 98,074 store licenses were issued. It appears reasonable to assume that not all these are checked even once each year. For a single inspector to spend five minutes in each of these stores not allowing for time between stores-- would require 204 40hour weeks, or approximately four years. Exempted stores are, of course, excluded from this computation. Thus the physical problem of field contacts of only the licensed stores, aggravated as it is by the numerous delinquencies, is undoubtedly difficult. Particularly is this 'true when it is remembered that these field contacts are made by personnel of a division which alrady has a large workload from the other taxes assigned to it. It must be remembered, too, that the cost of field enforcement for the chain store tax is borne by the administrative fund received from the cigarette tax. 

But to this point the problem of coverage has not been discussed. It must be added as still another task presumably borne primarily by the field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division. Exemptions, of course, produce a great many of the problems. Determination of corporate ownership or control of chain systems also presents questions of coverage, and the establishment of new stores requires consideration of methods for assuring complete tax collections. All these require considerable work by the field force. Yet, as can be seen, the director of the Store Tax Division, working through the field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division, already has a large workload for the field personnel without considering complete coverage. 

As discussed above, the statutory provisions permit each store owner or operator to determine his own exemptability. If he decides that he qualifies under the exemption provisions of the law, he does not have to file an application or any other information. Yet the 75 per cent rule seems to assume some more positive determination. At least occasional audits of sales records to determine whether the required percentage of sales was in the exempted commodities could reasonably be expected to assure complete tax coverage. Manifestly, such audits could not be expected to be made by the field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division if for no other reason than lack of time. Infrequently, the director of the Store Division takes time to make a few such spot audits, but in general this aspect of coverage is virtually untouched.

It is interesting to note that in 1943, when such audits were made to a considerable extent by the Store Tax Division due to a lesser workload and some additional personnel, revenues from this tax were almost double the figure for each of the succeeding years. 

Determination of ownership or control of stores to an extent which would assure complete coverage requires audits even more time-consuming than the sales audits discussed above. This problem is especially difficult when a chain system is owned by a foreign corporation.47 The rapid expansion of holding companies in recent years complicates determination of control. To add such audits to the present work of the field force of the Cigarette Tax Division would be unrealistic, since the field personnel would have to eliminate some of their other current and necessary work. Yet, in the opinion of some tax administrators, a great deal of additional tax revenue might be forthcoming if such audits were made. 

46Ibid., sec. 8 

47Op. Atty. Gen. 0-307, July 14, 1939. 98

A few years ago, a storekeeper who controlled a chain of several stores failed to pay his tax. He claimed that all but the parent store were owned in partnership with others and that he had one-half or less of the ownership of each. The State nevertheless established that he controlled the chain and was liable for the tax. This case can hardly be accepted as the only one of its kind. Yet if this particular situation had not been brought to the attention of the administrators almost by chance, revenues from this source would probably have gone uncollected. Another example is a recovery suit which was successfully brought by the State against one of the utility companies several years ago on the basis of its ownership of two subsidiary companies. Ownership in this instance was vested in a Delaware licensed corporation. Such determinations and court actions are, of course, lengthy and involved. Investigations to determine methods of control require considerably more time, effort, and expenditure than is now available. In this connection, however, it is important to remember, as is generally conceded, that tax collections increase proportionately to enforce ment effort expended, at least to a certain point. This point quite obviously has not been reached in the enforcement of the chain store tax. In the matter of assuring coverage of new stores and establishments, a somewhat better situation exists. The Store Tax Division, through the field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division, does attempt a street-by-street canvass of all stores in the State approximately every two years. The cursory nature of this canvass is indicated by consideration of the time it would take to contact only the licensed stores, as discussed above. Complicatin g such canvasses, according to the tax administrators, are the frequent changes in number of retail outlets of the large chains, requiring correspondence and contact with regional and national offices for information, and the frequent changes in number and type of the separately-owned departments in large department stores.

For these reasons, it is quite apparent that complete coverage of the chain store tax is not assured and that under existing conditions, its extension can hardly be expected in more than a small way. 

Police Powers 

As used in the following discussion, "police powers" include only those useful to tax administrators to assure tax payment.48 Even if it were decided that such matters as ownership or control of chain systems should be investigated, there is some doubt that the chain store tax law contains sufficient authority to permit thorough examination of store ownership structures. No reports or records other than applications for license are required, and the information included thereon is certainly insufficient for such determination. True, the Comptroller is given "all of the power and authority conferred by this Act' and the power to do ". any and all other things" necessary to its administration. But whether these grants permit him or his agents access to store records and entry upon the taxpayer's premises is questionable, and any attempt to make a positive determination might possibly result in suit against the State.

Incidents have occurred wherein taxpayers and exemptees successfully evaded audits of their books. In one such evasion, records were removed while a representative of the Comptroller's Department was making a telephone call to report their discovery. Too, the representative of the 

48See footnote 46, p. 57, supra, for those powers included. 99

Comptroller must usually be accompanied by a member of the Attorney General's staff; store owners or operators are quite reluctant to permit examiners to make audits under such conditions. 100

SECTION - ANALYSIS OF OPERATION Administrative Costs 

Collection costs vary considerably from tax to tax. High-revenue producing taxes generally show a much lower cost per dollar collected than do the low revenue producing taxes. Comparability, tax by tax, is difficult if not impossible, since cost data are frequently not available and vary considerably in composition. Lacking cost data for the administration of the chain store tax, this survey has not attempted extensive research to determine any precise unit costs for the tax. It will be remembered that administrative expenses are restricted by law to the amounts collected through assessment of the 50 cent fee per store.

However, certain observations in regard to cost of this tax seem pertinent. Outstanding is that the greatest part of the workload consists of processing license applications of single-unit stores. These not only represent the preponderance of taxpayers, approximately 82 to 85 per cent of the total, but consume most of the time and attention of the Store Tax Division's personnel. 

Remembering that these applicants pay a tax of only one dollar plus a 50 cent application fee, it is apparent that for a great many of the licensees the cost of administration is as great as or greater than the return to the State. It would be necessary to average less than one man/machine hour per single-unit licensee per year (disregarding material costs, office space, etc.) to approximate a balance betwen revenue and expense. Obviously, such an average would be difficult to maintain. The time required for review of applications plus filing time (of applications, copies of licenses, and addressograph plates) necessitates almost that amount of time without consideration of machine and mailing time or time needed for telephone calls or correspondence. And, significantly, this time consideration makes no allowance for field work. 

In this connection, it will be remembered that this tax has been construed primarily as a revenue measure rather than an exercise of the State's police powers. If the 50 cent filing fee was intended to reflect the administrative cost accurately, it is undoubtedly unrealistic, since it was provided in 1936, and administrative expense has increased considerably since that time. 

As previously mentioned, an additional tax of three-fourths of ten per cent of the computed tax was levied for 1950, and two-thirds of ten per cent for 1951. The additional tax for 1950 amounts to eight cents for single-unit stores. 

Inasmuch as the odd amount of additional tax has apparently been confusing to numerous taxpayers, the Store. Tax Division has been swamped with phone calls and letters requesting information. This, of course, tends to increase the cost of administration greatly, although increasing the revenues collected from single-unit stores only slightly. All indications point to a similar situation in 1951, when the rate for the additional levy changes to two-thirds of ten per cent of the computed tax, or seven cents for each single-unit store. 

49Hurt v. Cooper, 1937, 110 S. W. 2d 896. 101

Effectiveness of Operation

A rather incongruous situation results from the factors discussed above. 

Ninety per cent or more of the administrative time devoted to this tax brings in only slightly more than 10 per cent of revenues collected. Since the field work is handled by a division other than that primarily responsible for administration of the tax and no records are maintained apportioning time among the several taxes, it is extremely difficult to arrive at precise ratios. However, the marked unbalance of administrative load is apparent even upon cursory examination.

With this large workload of low-return taxpayers, little if any time can be devoted by the Store Tax Division to the matter of coverage, as has been mentioned in Section 4. As a consequence, thousands of store owners, including many multi-unit operators, are permitted to consider themselves exempt under the 75 per cent rule without any fear of having their records checked to determine the validity of such exemption. 

Analysis of Rates 

State-by-State comparison of rates, as in Table Chain-2, demonstrates clearly that evolution of this tax is not yet complete. The variety of rates is as great as the number of such taxes. No clear patterns are evident. 

Single stores are taxed in 11 States but are not assessed in seven States. Rates for single stores range as high as ten dollars. In several States, the tax to be paid on the second store is as low as five dollars, whereas other States levy as much as $50 or $65 on the second store. 

The graduations beyond this point vary extremely. Florida levies a tax of $100 per store on chains of six to ten units, whereas West Virginia requires but ten dollars per store for similar chains. For chains of more than 25 stores, rates among the several States vary from $25 to $300 per store. 

In general, it can be observed that Texas has rates at or near the bottom of the scale for single-unit stores and for chains of five stores or fewer. Fram that point, however, the Texas rates climb steeply, and on chains of more than 

50 stores, Texas has the highest rate of any State $750 per store. 

Analysis of Returns

Table Chain-3 shows collections received from the chain store tax and collections as a percentage of total State revenue. 

An almost imperceptible long-term trend upward can be observed in annual collections since inauguration of this tax. Except for the peak collections of 1943, occasioned by successful suits against alleged exemptees, annual revenues have tended to remain near the one million mark. To a certain extent, this tendency may be considered the result of an apparent stabilization of the number of licenses issued, which since 1947 have varied only slightly from the 100,000 level. 102
