In the face of a period of expanding economic activity and an apparently growing population, the almost static number of licensed stores in Texas may be hard to understand. An explanation may lie in administrative difficulties which prevent the obtaining of any greater coverage than that based on stores licensed in the previous year, as discussed in Section 4. 

In collections, Texas totals have been exceeded in recent years only by Florida. Using the devices of collections per $1,000 of retail sales and of collections per retail establishment, Texas appears to collect somewhat more than the average in comparison with other States having similar taxes. However, these evaluation devices are apt to be misleading, since data on which they are based vary appreciably from one source to another. For this reason, no detail on the matter of statistical evaluation of rates and returns has been included in this survey.

Precise data showing the total number of sales establishments (wholesale and retail) in Texas are not easily available. Available information presents wide variations and is of limited value for comparative purposes. For example, preliminary figures for 1948 published by the Census of Business, U.S. Census Bureau, indicate 90,090 retail establishments in Texas, but 

101,490 licenses were issued by the Store Tax Division in that fiscal year. 

The disparity is greater when it is remembered that the number of licenses issued excludes stores selling dairy products at retail, filling stations, lumber and building materials dealers, religious book stores, businesses selling ice only, outlets selling products of their own manufacture, businesses paying gross receipts taxes, and utility stores in incorporated towns of 3,000 population or less deriving at least 75 per cent of their gross income from the sale of gas or electric service. According to the classification system used by the 

Census of Business, too, nearly half the stores included in the 90,090 total are exempt from the chain store tax. This variance can be explained partially by the several and different classification systems employed by the reporting agencies and the varying definitions of "store." Limitations of this study prevented the making of a separate tabulation.

Despite lack of precise data, however, some general observations are possible. These generalizations have some statistical value but cannot be readily substantiated. For this discussion, they reveal a striking picture. 

Stores subject to the chain store tax probably represent roughly threefifths of retail business establishments in the State. In the fiscal year 1950, for example, when 98,074 licenses were issued, probably some 60,000 businesses were exempt. Of this number, the largest percentage were probably filling stations and lumber and building materials dealers. Upon the basis of sales volume, exempt stores had total retail sales equal to or exceeding the sales of stores subject to the tax. This may be explained by the fact that some 85 per cent of licenses issued represent single-unit or independent stores, many of which probably have small sales volumes, while exemptions include stores with relatively higher total sales. 103

SECTION 

Several difficulties which have arisen in connection with the chain store tax have been indicated in the preceding discussion of its operation. The ma► jority of these problems are concerned with tax administration, but basic issues of tax policy are involved in some instances. Areas which have proved most troublesome may be delineated as follows: ) Lack of statutory provision for refunds. (2) Absence of realistic provisions for handling tax delinquency. (3) Insufficient filing fees to provide administrative funds. 

4) Lack of methods for assuring complete coverage. (5) Determinations of exemptability and the whole question of exemptions. (6) A gradual alteration of underlying tax philosophy. 

The first four problems are limited to tax administration. The latter two involve policy matters. Refunds

Though refunds of chain store tax overpayments are not provided in the tax law, allowances are made to taxpayers in practice. This procedure has been followed to rectify unintentional errors. Statutory provision for refunds or credits might contribute more satisfactorily to a solution of this problem, which almost invariably arises in the administration of any tax. 

Delinquency Penalties 

As has been discussed in preceding sections, delinquency in tax payments is one of the most costly problems faced by chain store tax administrators. Possibly as many as one-half of all tax payments are made after the due date and then only after contact by the Comptroller's field representatives. In view of the relatively low tax rate applying to most delinquency cases (one dollar annually for single stores plus a 50 cents fee), the need for prompting through correspondence or contact by a field representative brings the administrative cost/revenue ratio to an uneconomical point for most of the tax payments. Taxpayers are not always prompt in making payment, especially if no harmful effects result from their tardiness. In the chain store tax this tendency is accentuated by the fact that a taxpayer can avoid the expense and bother of having his license application notarized by simply delaying his payment and waiting for a field representative to call.

Penalties for delinquency are usually considered essential to efficient tax enforcement. However, there are no practical provisions for delinquency penalties in the chain store tax law. Existing penalties range from $25 to $100 for each day a tax receipt is not displayed, but they are seldom or never applied and are of such size as to discourage their application for delinquency. 

It should be pointed out that delinquencies in payment of this tax can scarcely be classed as tax evasions, although a technical distinction might be difficult. In practically all delinquencies encountered under this tax law, no 104

-- 
-- evidence of intent to evade is apparent. It is simply easier and far more convenient and in some cases less expensive to be delinquent than to pay the tax at the required date. Lack of realistic penalty provisions in the law and the collection methods which have been developed have fostered, to a great extent, this widespread delinquency practice.

Effective solutions to this problem are not, of course, easy to find. However, several possibilities exist. Other tax laws provide penalties equal to a percentage of amounts due, automatically effective on the day following due date.

Addition of a percentage penalty provision might facilitate the task of those responsible for the administration of the chain store tax by discouraging. delinquencies.

Some tax administrators have also suggested that the problem of delinquencies might be eased if a service charge would be assessed in cases where collections are made by field personnel after the due date. Such service charge --perhaps eight or ten dollars -- would help defray the additional cost of field colle ctions. In this way, the law could accept, as present practice already does, the delinquency habit without requiring the State to pay the unusually high enforcement cost prevailing under the present system. 

Administrative Funds

The 50 cent fee per store allotted for administrative costs was provided by a 1936 statute and has not been changed since. If computed upon the basis of then-prevailing administrative costs, it seems reasonable to assume that it is no longer realistic. In view of actual administration, enforcement appears to have suffered especially because of lack of funds. 

As discussed above, most of the enforcement of this tax law is carried out by field personnel of the Cigarette Tax Division. This use of an already existing field force is undeniably in the interest of efficiency and economy. However, no provision is made in enforcement funds for the addition of this workload to the Cigarette Tax Division. Individual enforcement or administrative funds are not provided for all taxes levied by the State of Texas, and there has developed a practice of adding the administration duties of tax laws not having an administrative fund to divisions responsible for taxes which have provisions for such funds. In this case, the administrative fund of the cigarette tax pays for much of the enforcement of the chain store tax. The 50 cent fee of the chain store tax cannot be considered realistically as covering the administrative cost of this tax. Actually, it provides only for employees of the Store Tax Division, who not only process applications of some 98,000 store owners or operator but are also responsible for collecting cosmetics and radio taxes. 

It seems reasonable to assume that in this tax, as in many others, increased enforcement effort would be reflected in increased revenue to the State. 

To this end, it has been suggested that an administrative fund greater than that available from the 50 cent fee be allowed the Comptroller. This might be accomplished in one of several ways by an increase of the fee, by adding a service charge for delinquent collections, or by providing that a certain percentage of collections: be reserved for enforcement costs. 

105

In this way, it might be possible for the Store Tax Division to employ a few (perhaps one or two might be sufficient) field auditors to handle the problem of coverage, which at present is too great to be handled completely by Cigarette Tax Division field personnel. Such an administrative fund could also help defray the field costs incurred by the Cigarette Tax Division in the enforcement of the chain store tax.

To a great extent, of course, administrative costs are determined by the number of delinquencies, as discussed earlier. If these could be materially reduced, enforcement costs would be greatly lessened. One revision of the statute which would probably reduce aministrative and overhead costs considerably would be that of setting a minimum figure ( of perhaps one dollar) for the additional tax to be applied in 1951 i.e. change the additional tax provision to read: two-thirds of ten per cent but not less than one dollar.

Coverage 

Another costly problem of the chain store tax is that of coverage. Under the present law and administrative procedure, it is impossible to determine whether all who are liable for the tax are actually paying it. There are several indications that coverage is not complete, and although it would be extremely difficult to substantiate these indications, they probably warrant thoughtful consideration on the part of persons interested in the administration of this tax. 

In spite of an era of expanding economic activity and the growing population, there has been practically no increase in the number of Texas stores licensed in the post-war years. Although periodic canvasses are attempted, their scope is seriously hampered by lack of time and field personnel. 

Some other conclusions may be drawn from the limited data available for a determination of the chain store tax coverage. Available records indicate that 98,074 store licenses were issued during the 1949 fiscal year, of which from 82 to 85 per cent were for independent stores. Thus only 15 to 18 per cent, or approximately 15,000 licenses, were issued to stores which are part of chain systems. As previously discussed, inspection of each licensed store to assure display of license would require approximately four men working a 40hour week for a year. To inspect every store in the State would require considerably more time.

However, this computation is based on the assumption that each of the four inspectors would spend only five minutes in each store. For stores exempt under the 75 per cent rule, this time limit would be insufficient for a true determination of exemptability. It is true that the exemption applying to the lumber business and to utility stores in incorporated towns of 3,000 or less population has been interpreted as exempting all such stores in the State, but few determinations have been made by the Store Tax Division as to whether those considering themselves exempt under the 75 per cent rule do, in fact, qualify. It is not unreasonable to assume that some stores enjoying exemption are not entitled to it; at least such assumption has as much validity as its reverse, since no determination has been made. Thus, it would appear that complete coverage may not yet have been attained. 106

Several methods for assuring complete coverage of tax laws have been tried in various States, although nnne is entirely satisfactory and all, of course, have deficiencies. Most widely used procedure to achieve full coverage is licensing, which accomplishes a registration or listing of all potential taxpayers from which list individual determination of tax liability can be made. 

Licensing is the basis of the chain store tax, too. However, due to the numer ous exemptions, the licensing provision of this tax permits no control of coverage.

Stores in Texas which fall into the exempted categories are not required to register nor even to file an application for license. Since the determination of exemptability is primarily made by the store operator himself, the director of the Store Tax Division has no way of knowing whether exemptions are valid. 

As a consequence, he has no accurate method by which to determine or even estimate the number of retail stores in the State. Approximately 15,000 chain stores (in addition to the single-unit stores) made application for licenses in the fiscal year 1949. It is probable that the number of exemptions considerably exceeded this figure. 

A registration requirement for all stores, coupled with positive application for exemption, has been suggested as a possible solution for the problem of coverage. Such a procedure would necessitate application by all stores in the State, either for a license or for exemption. In this way, the Store Tax 

Division would have a complete listing of all stores in the State, which would greatly facilitate the field work required in the periodic canvasses. If the applications for exemption were designed so as to give information pertinent to the determination of exemptability, much of the burden of proving exemptability would be shifted from the tax administrator. Of course, such a procedure would considerably increase the processing workload of the Store Tax Division. 

However, payment of a fee by all applicants, whether for license or exemption, would probably permit additional personnel, particularly if the application fees were adequate to cover current costs of administrative activity. 

If exemption applications were submitted showing the basis upon which exemption is claimed, the tax administrator would at least have the basic information upon which to determine exemptability. Except in cases where deliberate misinformation is submitted -- which could probably be kept at a minimum by use of periodic spot audits of sales records to compare such records with the reports each case of exemption would be positively determined. 

Other means used for assuring complete coverage include the frequent application of penalty provisions; the granting of extensive police powers to administrators; and the use of frequent audits and inspections of records and books. However, all these rely on a large field force, and as has been discussed the field force available for the chain store tax is only that of another tax division already well-burdened. 

In the Texas chain store tax law, no provision is made for periodic audits or inspections. Though there is reference to the display of tax receipts by store owners or operators, enforcement of this provision has not been easily possible under the conditions of prevailing enforcement. Nor is there realistic provision for delinquency penalties, as has been discussed above. No specific police 107

powers are granted the tax administrator, although Comptroller personnel have inspected records occasionally and entered on the store premises in spite of the lack of statutory permission to do so. This lack of specific authority in the statute to make effective examinations has been a factor tending to deter investigations into the validity of exemption. 

Thus, for effecting complete coverage, a system of licensing of all stores; a provision requiring application for exemption, along with submission of data upon which determination of exemptability could be made; a statutory authorization to the Comptroller permitting his tax administrators to enter the business premises of stores to inspect books, records, and merchandise; a statutory authorization to the Comptroller to make it possible for his personnel to inspect and audit books and records, are all methods which warrant consideration. Individually, or in combination, these methods, if adopted, might accomplish an assurance of complete coverage, the existence of which is questionable at present.

The Question of Exemptions 

Ordinarily, exemptions are of secondary importance in the consideration of a tax statute. However, the Texas chain store tax law presents an unusual picture where in the number of exempt chain stores probably exceeds the number of chain stores subject to the tax. Too, some 85 per cent of the stores paying the tax are independents. This situation is in large part the result of the exemptions listed in the law and raises what is perhaps a most serious question of policy and underlying tax philosophy. There is considerable justification for the point of view which has occasionally been expressed that the philosophy of chain store taxation has been materially altered through application. 

As was discussed in Section 1, the chain store tax was generally in its early years aimed at multiple-unit stores as a means of reducing somewhat their competitive advantage over the single-unit retail establishments. Whether this theory is still applicable in view of the present law is difficult to demonstrate, since almost six times as many single-unit stores as chain stores are liable for the tax and as many or more chain units are not liable for the tax as are required to pay it. It is understandable that single-unit stores be included to provide coverage control, although the intent of the law was primarily to tax chain units. However, the exemptions in the law largely void the value of this control method if it was intended as such. 

Further complicating the underlying tax philosophy is the 75 per cent rule. It will be recalled that the law provides that a store which does 75 per cent of its business in an exempt line is not liable for any tax, even if the 25 per cent of business which is not in one of the exempt categories constitutes a sizable total.

Although construed to be an "occupation" tax, the chain store tax, by virtue of the 75 per cent rule, poses a difficult question as to what the taxed occupation may be. If it is a tax on the occupation of selling any commodity except those enumerated in the exemption provisions, then the question arises as to the 25 per cent of sales in non-exempt commodities by stores primarily engaged in an exempt line. The law fails to take into account the absolute value 108

of the business covered by the 25 per cent allowance. It is probable that the intent of the 75 per cent rule was to afford a reasonable flexibility which did not exist under the former provision granting exemption only to organizations engaged "exclusively" in a specified business. However, the percentage provision, unsupported by an absolute minimum figure, sales has led to situations such as those discussed earlier wherein a small store pays the tax on a volume of business considerably less than that of a larger store selling the same com:modity because the larger store is exempt under the 75 per cent rule. If an absolute figure were specified as a minimum and applied in conjunction with the 75 per cent rule, these situations in large part would be eliminated. Such a procedure would simply mean that all businesses in certain categories would be exempt if no more than 25 per cent of their gross sales (but not more than $1,000 or some similar figure) was received from non-exempt lines. 

A further question which has been posed from the inception of this tax is that of whether the tax base the store unit is the best which might be used. Many attempts have been made, as has been discussed in Section 1, to enact chain store taxes graduated according to gross receipts. Court decisions have generally invalidated this basis. As alternatives, some States have used as bases the floor area of stores, the number of gasoline pumps operated by a filling station, and similar devices. However, as yet no generally accepted basis except that of the store unit has been found. Some expressions have been voiced in recent years that a basis of gross receipts might today find acceptance by the courts if all the other objectionable features which voided the early laws were omitted. 

In Conclusion

From the preceding discussion it can be seen that the Texas chain store tax is difficult to enforce under current statutes. It is costly in that its administrative expenses in relation to the amount of revenue produced are considerably higher than for many other types of taxes. For almost 85 per cent of the total taxpayers, the cost of collection to the State is as great or greater than the revenues received. This costliness is accentuated by a delinquency problem which plagues the tax administrators and which is fostered at least in part by existing procedures.

Underlying this situation is the lack of a definite pattern or philosophy of taxation, characterized by numerous exemptions and complicated by a lack of administrative funds and enforcement powers for its administration. 

At this point in time it is impossible to foretell the future of the chain store tax. It has not yet developed to a stage of procedural or administrative stability. Although beset by numerous problems, it exhibits latent possibilities as a substantial element in the State's total tax structure. Lacking direction and vitality today, it is a "nuisance" tax for the majority of taxpayers and for the State, a relatively minor revenue-raiser. 109

CARBON BLACK PRODUCTION TAX SECTION I .. HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

Carbon black is generally regarded as a manufactured rather than a natural mineral resource. However, it is frequently considered among those products upon which so-called severance taxes are levied. In Texas the carbon black tax is usually included among those taxes levied on the removal or severance of mineral resources from the earth. Since carbon black is produced, for the most part, from natural gas and crude oil, such a classification has some validity.

Known to many as "lamp black" or "gas black", this product is defined in the dictionary as “a fine bulky carbon obtained as soot. . .'' Carbon black is used as a black pigment in the manufacture of numerous commodities, including rubber tires, inks, telephone receivers, paints and floor coverings. It is manufactured principally from natural gas and crude oil although other sources are sometimes used.

Four methods are generally employed for the manufacture of carbon black from natural gas:

Channel Method. A box-like metal affair moving backwards and forwards over gas burners.

Plate Method. A revolving metal sheet, circular in shape, moving over gas burners.

Disk Method. Operates similarly to the plate method, but uses a smaller surface. 

Cylinder Method. A revolving metal cylinder rotating above and in front of gas burners.

The precipitated carbon is collected and packaged similarly in each method. Besides these four so-called "contact" processes, there is another process known as the "furnace" method which uses either natural gas or crude oil.

Developed in recent years and now being widely adopted, the furnace process accounted for over 50 per cent of total United States carbon black production in 1947, as contrasted with only 17 per cent in 1941.2 This extremely rapid expansion of the use of the furnace method has been due to the more efficient recovery of carbon by this process. Furnace plants producing carbon black from gas achieved a recovery of 23 per cent of carbon content in 1948 in 

1 Peerless Carbon Black Co. v. Sheppard (1938) 113 SW 2d 996. (Tex. Civ. App. 

1938, writ refused.) 2 

The Petroleum Data Book, 2d Ed. Petroleum Engineer Pub. Co., Dallas, Texas, 1948, p. G-45. 110

contrast to a recovery of approximately seven per cent by contact plants, according to national statistics."

Recovery From Oil 

Even more significant, however, than the role the furnace method is playing in production of carbon black from gas, is the fact that this process has made possible the conversion of heavy fractions of crude oil to carbon black much more efficiently than by the use of natural gas. This recovery is as high as 50 per cent of carbon content, according to trade information." 

Because of this the increasing importance of oil in the manufacture of carbon black is manifest. In fact the constantly increasing demand for natural gas as high grade fuel and as a raw material for motor fuel and synthetic chemicals is a forerunner of the day when heavy oil fractions will probably replace gas as the raw feed to carbon black plants." 

All early commercial production of carbon black was from gas. However, the development of the furnace method has, in a few years, made drastic alterations.. United States production from oil in 1948 doubled such production in the preceding year. Although currently only about one-tenth of total United States production is represented by the carbon black manufactured from oil, the extraordinary growth of the use of this source material in recent years gives substantial indication that the ratio may soon be considerably changed. The significance of the consideration of processes of manufacture of carbon black lies largely in the fact that the Texas carbon black production tax is levied only against the production from gas. In this light the comparative statistics take on added importance. As has been previously mentioned the average recovery of carbon black from gas in contact plants is about seven per cent of total carbon content, in furnace plants using gas it is about 23 per cent, whereas recovery from oil runs as high as 50 per cent of total content. The high recovery rate from oil cannot be overlooked in any consideration of the future of the carbon black industry. 

Natural gas contains about 35 pounds of carbon per thousand cubic feet. The percentages recited above are based upon the national average recovery of 2.41 pounds of carbon black by contact plants and 8.07 pounds by furnace method using gas. In contrast to the national averages, Texas producers showed an average of only 1.5 pounds per thousand cubic feet or four per cent of total carbon content. Available records do not show how many 

Texas producers employ crude oil in the manufacture of carbon black. It seems reasonable to assume, however, that at least ten per cent of Texas production utilizes oil, in view of the national ratio, and possibly as much as 25 per cent may utilize this source material. 3 Minerals Yearbook 1948, U.S. Bureau of Mines Government Printing Office, 1950, p. 203. 4Ibid. 

5Huntington, R.L., Natural Gas and Gasoline, McGraw-Hill Book Co., New 

York, 1950, p. 10. 111

Texas Dominates Production 

Knowledge of another factor is essential to an understanding of the environment in which the carbon black tax functions. That is the dominant position of Texas in the production of this commodity. Texas plants manufacture two-thirds of the total United States production of carbon black. 

According to the United States Bureau of Mines, in 1948 Texas produced over 903 million pounds of a total national production of 1,297 million. This production was achieved by 33 contact plants and nine furnances, owned by some 15 producers. Louisiana in 1948 followed with production totals of 165 million pounds, Other producing States are California, Kansas, New Mexico, and Oklahoma.

To a great extent the carbon black manufacturing industry is dependent upon the rubber industry which uses about 90 per cent of the carbon black consumed in the United States. Therefore, manufacture of carbon black has generally fluctuated according to tire production. The early war years saw a sharp decline in carbon black production with the near-cessation of tire manufacture. However, the demands of the rapidly-expanding synthetic rubber industry began to be felt in 1944 and continued to grow throughout the war, with a resultant increase in carbon black production. 

Probably as a result of the fact that carbon black production has been so dominated by Texas and totals produced in other States have been low, only two other States have levied a tax specifically on the production of carbon black. For a brief period (1929 to 1933) Montana levied such a tax but repealed it during the nineteen-thirties in the face of almost negligible revenues. 

Louisiana also imposed a tax on carbon black production for a four-year period 1928-32. It was discontinued for the same reason. Thus, in this tax, unlike most others, Texas has not had the benefit of the experience of other States by which to help guide its actions. 

In view of this situation it is interesting to note that the large carbon black production industry in Texas, in spite of its national importance, is actually only an incidental result of concern about the regulation of the gas production industry. Not only the commodity carbon black but the entire carbon black industry in Texas can be described as by-products of natural gas production. 

Early Carbon Black Manufacture 

Although produced in various ways for hundreds of years, large scale commercial production of this product did not come abut until the development and utilization of extensive natural gas deposits. Not until the decade of 1915-1925 was such production realized in the Monroe Field of northern Louisiana. However, when the completion of a pipeline from that field to Atlanta, Georgia opened a vast new market, the price of Louisiana gas rose 

6Minerals Yearbook, 1948, op. cit., p. 202. 

7 Texas Almanac, 1949-50, p. 281. 

8T.N. Farris, "Severance Taxation in Louisiana," Part 2, Louisiana Business 

Bulletin, Louisiana Univ. Press, 1938, p. 18. 112

sharply and became prohibitive for carbon black manufacture. The development of the Texas Panhandle fields in the early 1920's offered an answer to the carbon black producers. Large quantities of cheap gas brought the manufacturers from Louisiana to Texas.9

This transfer of the industry was made in spite of the fact that the production of carbon black from natural gas was prohibited by law in Texas from 1899 to 1931 under the first gas conservation statute. 10 This prohibition was not strictly enforced, and the U.S. Bureau of Mines records carbon black production in Texas beginning in 1927, although, as discussed above, there are indications that some production actually took place several years earlier. 

The first statutory permission for the manufacture of carbon black in Texas came in 1931.12 This allowed the use of "sour" gas (that containing 

1.5 grams of hydrogen sulphide per 100 cubic feet, making it unsuitable for light and fuel) for the manufacture of carbon black. Factors within the gas production industry after the full development of the Panhandle field, in large part, effected this relaxation of the conservation laws. 

Pipeline companies at that time owned approximately 80 per cent of the leases in the field. The production from their leases was sufficient to serve their needs. Since these pipelines were the only outlet for the field, the remaining producers had no market for their gas. It was the result of an attempt to assure these other producers some sort of market that the 1931 law was passed permitting the use of certain kinds of gas for the manufacture of carbon black. 13 

The conservation statutes have since that time been modified on several occasions. Over the intervening years these modifications have in general tended to permit wider use of gas for the manufacture of carbon black. However each extension has been surrounded by a set of conditions relating to the specific conservation demands which produced the extension of use to the manufacture of carbon black. As a result, although gas may now be rather widely used for the manufacture of carbon black, the conditions under which it may be used are extremely lengthy and complex. 

First Tax in 1936

This is the setting in which carbon black is manufactured in Texas, and into which, in 1936, was introduced the carbon black production tax. Enacted as an occupation tax, it was levied upon carbon black producers. Following the pattern of the natural gas and oil taxes the rate was made, in some cases, dependent upon "market value." One-twelfth of one cent per pound was levied on all carbon black sold for four cents or less a pound and three per cent of the value of all black sold for more than four cents. A formula was included for determining market value.14 

9Huntington, op. cit., p. 9-10. 

10 Tex. Laws, 26th Leg., 1899, R.S., Ch. 49. 

11 Texas Almanac, 1945-1946 

12 Tex. Laws, 42d Leg., 1931, 1st C.S., Ch. 26. 

13Cheek, Maurice, Legal History of Conservation of Gas in Texas, pp 270-271. 

14 Tex. Laws, 1936, 44th Leg., 3d C.S., Ch. 495, Art. IV. 113

A dispute as to coverage of production methods soon arose. In 1937 the Peerless Carbon Black Company brought suit to recover taxes paid under protest on the grounds that its cylinder method of producing carbon black was not covered by the tax statute. The Court of Civil Appeals held the production of carbon black by the plaintiff taxable, declaring that: ... When the entire act is read it shows that the tax was not imposed upon a method or manner used in manufacturing or producing carbon black, but upon the business or occupation of manufacturing . . carbon black in its entirety, and that the methods of production referred to were illustrative of the carbon black industry and in aid 15 of the officials charged with the duty of collecting the tax. 

Basis of Tax Charged

This decision would appear to settle the controversy, possibly even as to coverage of processes employing oil. However, in 1941, the rate of the tax was changed and for the first time the basis of the tax included a consideration 16 of the amount of gas consumed in the manufacture of carbon black. Actually four separate rates were established: 

Class "A"

Class "B" · 

that manufactured by use of less than 200 cubic feet of gas per pound of carbon black was taxed at 122/1200 of one cent per pound if valued at less than four cents per pound and at 4.1 per cent of value if priced at more than four cents per pound. that manufactured by use of more than 200 cubic feet of gas per pound of carbon black was taxed at 31/240 of one cent per pound if valued at four cents or less per pound, and at 5.2 per cent of value if priced at more than four cents per pound.

A formula was provided for determining market value. Thus producers were required to segregate their production according to class and price and pay the requisite tax. The tax was due and payable on all production whether sold or stored. The 1941 basis and rate remains today altered only by the addi- 17 tion of a ten per cent temporary tax levied in 1950 for one year. 

The increasing use of oil in the manufacture of carbon black apparently prompted another determination as to whether this production was subject to the tax. In 1947 the Attorney General informed the Comptroller that the tax on carbon black was due and payable whether the raw material was (a) part gas and part crude oil, (b) all crude oil or (c) refineries residue. "The tax is levied . irrespective of . . . the raw materials used in manufacture.'18 

15Peerless Carbon Black Co. v. Sheppard, 113 S. W. 2d 996, 998 (Tex Civ. App., 1938, writ refused) 

16 Tex. Laws, 1941, 44th.Leg., Ch. 184, Art XI. 

17 Tex. Laws, 1950, 51st Leg., 1st C.S., Ch. 2, Art XI 18 Opinion of Attorney General, No. V-459, Dec. 18, 1947. 

114

On April 13, 1949, however, the Court of Civil Appeals ruled contrary to this opinion of the Attorney General in the case of Philtex Chemical Co. v. 

Sheppard. 19 The Court said: 

We believe that Art. 7047 (47), RCS of Texas (Carbon black tax statute), levies a tax upon the occupation of manufacturing carbon black only when the same is made from gas in whole or in part. . . 

Thus production from crude oil was held exempt. The Court evidently also saw some regulatory intent in the enaction of the tax statute, for after reviewing the history of conservation laws affecting natural gas, the Court continued: . . . Further the levy of a tax upon an occupation producing carbon clack by the use of gas is in aid of regulation . . . The tax was greater on inefficient use of gas, thus clearly disclosing the legislative intent to discourage the use of gas for carbon black manufacture and where used, to encourage the efficient use thereof. Shortly after this decision the tax statute was amended the same year to assure its extension to carbon black produced by any gas process. Carbon black was redefined to include "all black pigment produced in whole or in part from natural gas, casinghead gas or residue gas by the impinging of a flame upon a channel, disk, or plate, or by any other method . . 

20 It should be noted, however, that the source material was not altered and that the rate basis was left unchanged the amount of natural gas consumed per pound of black, in addition to market value. In reality, the tax is on carbon black manufactured from gas and so no tax or tax rate exists for carbon black produced from oil. 

The Tax Law and the Conservation Statute s 

The Railroad Commission is given authority to enforce restriction upon the uses of natural gas in carbon black manufacture and to require reports from gas producers. This supervisory authority, for purposes of resources conservation, results in a situation somewhat analogous to that prevailing in the oil and gas taxes. One State agency regulates production of a raw material used in the manufacture of a commodity upon which a tax is levied and thereby indirectly affects the revenue from that tax. great

Additionally, of course, are the conservation statutes which spell out in bla detail the restrictions upon the use of gas for the manufacture of carbon 21 These statutory limitations upon the use of natural gas in carbon black production require that from one to five pounds of carbon black be recovered from each thousand cubic feet of gas, depending upon the composition of the gas and several other factors. The tax rate classifications generally reflect these 

19Philtex Chemical Co. v. Sheppard, 219 S. W. 2d 1010, 1949 (Tex. Civ. App., refused N.R.E. 

20 Tex. Laws, 1949, 51st Leg., R.S., Ch. 624. 

21 These restrictions are found in Tex. Civ. Statutes, (Vernon's, 1950), Art. 4008, Secs. 3 and 7, and Art. 6008a, Sec. 2. 

115

limitations, with higher rates for least efficient recovery methods. It is understandable then why the courts interpreted the tax law as being “in aid of regulation."

This tax, unique to Texas because of the State's predominance in the industry, has today arrived at a crucial crossroad in its history. Based as it is now upon the production of carbon black from natural gas, and having been interpreted as being essentially regulatory, it faces a continuing existence of possibly decreasing revenues and of regulation supplementing the numerous gas conservation statutes. Or, if its purpose is recognized as primarily that of revenue raising, and it is decided that the function of conservation will be left to the conservation statutes, then it may again become, as it was in the original law, an occupation tax on the production of carbon black, from whatever source derived.

SECTION 2 ORGANIZATIONAL FORM 

General

The tax on carbon black is an "occupation" tax levied on all producers or manufacturers on the basis of (1) the amount produced, (2) the amount of gas consumed per pound produced and (3) the market value per pound. For the purposes of taxation, carbon black is divided into four classifications. 

Each producer is required to compute his own tax and make payments to the Comptroller, along with accompanying production reports, the tax being due regardless of the method used in its production or the amount actually sold. Administration The Comptroller is statutorily responsible for the administration of the carbon black tax.22 He receives monthly tax payments and production reports and has authority to validate reports, to require additional information, and to adopt rules governing tax collections and enforcement. In addition to the grant of enforcement authority to the Comptroller, the tax statute also permits the Attorney General and the Auditor to inspect records kept by the producers. 

The inclusion of the Auditor and the Attorney General, however, reflects the general tendency to make specific mention of these two agencies in tax statutes even though the power given them may duplicate authority already inherent in their offices. In actual practice, only the Comptroller, through the Gross 

Receipts Division, performs the administrative functions of the carbon black tax. Neither the Auditor nor the Attorney General has been known to exercise the inspection authority granted them.

Despite the evident simplicity of carbon black tax administration an unusual relationship exists between the Comptroller and the Railroad Commission. 

This situation arises from their individual duties relative to carbon black production. Under the conservation statutes, the Railroad Commission has indirect effect upon the quantity of carbon black produced, through the establishment of 

22 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Leg., R.S., Ch. 184, Art XI, Sec. 1. 

116

production allowables for natural gas. In addition, the Commission is responsible for enforcing the restrictions upon the use of gas in carbon black manufacture. The effect of Commission action upon tax revenue would probably be difficult to evaluate. Nevertheless, revenue from the carbon black tax, because it is based upon production, is related to Commission activity. 

On the other hand, the Comptroller is charged with responsibility for administration of the tax statute. In practice, there is no co-ordination between these two agencies in performing their respective functions. Both require production reports; the Commission, however, is given express and extensive investigative authority to verify its reports through field inspections, while the Comptroller has no such power. Neither depends upon production reports to the other, nor are the two totals compared for differences. In 1948 and 1949, for example, carbon black production as reported to the Railroad Commission 23 exceeded that reported to the Comptroller by a considerable amount. When related to total State manufactures, this disparity is relatively insignificant though revenue-wise the amount might be of interest. 

SECTION 3 ASSESSMENT 

Like several other Texas taxes, the carbon black tax is self-assessed by the producer upon the basis of production records. The applicable rate depends upon the volume of gas consumed per pound of black and the market value per pound, both of which are computed by the producer. Designated by law an "occupation tax," it is levied on "every person, agent, receiver, trustee, firm, association or co-partnership manufacturing carbon black."24 The statute to this point provides no classification of taxpayers; it appears that all producers are liable. Nor are there any, statutory exemptions. Also from a reading of this portion of the law, tax liability does not appear to be qualified by raw materials and processes used but seems to apply to all carbon black. · • However, carbon black in a following section of the statute is defined to "include all black pigment produced in whole or in part from natural gas, casinghead gas ar residue gas by impinging of a flame upon a channel disk or plate or by any other method, and the tax. shall reach all products produced in such manners."25 Thus, in the definition of the taxable commodity, an implied exemption is found. Only carbon black produced from gas is taxable. 

However, it is interesting to note that if any gas is used, regardless of how small the quantity, the carbon black so produced is taxable. 

The addition of the phrase "or by any other method" in 1949 may have been intended to assure coverage of all methods of production from gas and to supply statutory sanction for previous court rulings and opinions of the Attorney General. Rates and classifications are based upon the number of cubic feet of gas consumed per pound of carbon black.26 

23See discussion below, Section 4. 

24 Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Leg., op. cit. 

25 Tex. Laws, 1949, 51st Leg., R.S., Ch. 624, Sec. 1. 

26 Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Leg., R.S., op. cit. 117

Class "A" 

produced by use of less than 200 cubic feet gas per lb. of carbon black and valued at four cents or less per lb.
