Volume of gas consumed per pound of carbon black is computed by the producer, a cubic foot of gas being defined by the Legislature. 

Should either of these classifications be declared unconstitutional, discriminatory or invalid the rate would be: 

Carbon black valued at four cents or less per lb.--122/1200 of, one cent. 

Carbon black valued at more than four cents per lb. -- 4.1 per cent of market value. 

An increase amounting to ten per cent of the tax due from March 1, 1950 to August 31, 1951 was enacted by the 51st Legislature in its first called session. 

The prescription of the several rates has the practical effect of creating a classification not otherwise achieved in the tax statute. Although in earlier sections the apparent intent of the law was to levy the tax against all producers, the rate sections provide for assessment only of those using gas as the raw material. The gas used in the manufacture of carbon black is taxed, as is any other gas, under the gas production tax of the State. 

These rates and classifications may have been designed to encourage the efficient utilization of natural gas in carbon black manufacture, providing higher rates for less efficient recovery. Where the tax statute makes a division between classes A and B at an average recovery of five pounds per thousand feet of gas, the conservation laws enforced by the Railroad Commission require an average recovery ranging from one to five pounds depending upon the type of gas used and its content of substances other than gas. 

In spite of the evident desire to prevent waste, reports made to the Comp troller reveal that the major portion of production is in the less efficient cate gories. This production ratio seems to indicate that the restrictions as imposed 

27Tex. Laws, 1950, 51st Leg., 1st C.S., Ch. 2, Art XI. 

27 118

by conservation laws are sufficiently elastic to permit minimum recoveries to satisfy the larger part of market demand. Despite the court holding that this tax was passed "in aid of regulation," payment of higher rate does not seem to have acted as a deterrent to less efficient recovery in view of the 

Texas average of 1.5 pounds per 1000 cubic feet of gas. 

Market Value

Besides the somewhat awkward rate scale apparently provided in conjunction with conservation policies, another factor enters into tax computations for carbon black producers. This variable is "market value," a factor also used in the natural gas and oil production taxes. Market value of carbon black is determined by averaging the sales price of all sales, by types, made during the month and subtracting the cost of packing, freight, and cartage.. Although there are many grades of carbon black, the price is fairly constant. Should carbon black have been produced in a given month but not sold, its actual market value is the average sales price of such grade during the last preceding month when a sale was made. 29 Market value determinations, in practice, are made by the taxpayer and these determinations are accepted by the Comptroller without question, except in rare instances. When considering the computa tion of taxes based upon market value, it is important to remember that most carbon black falls within the categories requiring payment of a percentage of market value rather than in those specifying a flat rate, either 122/1200 or 31/240 of one cent per pound. These peculiarly awkward fractions have had limited application, and have not produced the problems that would probably have developed with wider usage. On the form which producers compute tax payments, the Comptroller specifies the rates as $0.0010667 and $0.00129167. There is no easily apparent reason for their adoption nor any indication of the process by which they were selected. -L Several rulings have been required of the Attorney General to determine true market value. Segregation of production according to sales price is required; a producer may not consolidate sales of carbon black below four cents per pound with those selling for more than four cents and pay tax on the average. 30 If there is no market for carbon black during a month the market value is equal to its value at a place where there is a market, minus the 31 cost of transportation to that place. This determination appears complicated but it has in fact had very little application. There was one instance in recent years involving an export firm which computed its tax upon this basis. Discounts

Permitting producers to subtract the cost of packing, freight, and cartage in reality constituties a discount. This is the only such provision in the carbon black tax statute. Forming carbon black into pellets is employed in the industry. 29Ibid. 

30Opinion of the Atty Gen., No. 0-1557, October 23, 1939. 

31 Opinion of the Atty Gen., No. 0-2428, June 21, 1940. 119

to facilitate transportation; the cost of pelletizing may be deducted as a packing expense because it permits more economic shipment. 32 In reality, the deductions permitted for transportation consist largely of packing expenses since most manufacturers ship F.O.B. The majority of producers have submitted to the Comptroller a "reasonable" figure which they employ for all production during the year. This figure is generally constant for all shipments of a particular company in one year and is usually revised annually. A procedure of this sort tends to minimize the difficulties of computing market value. As revealed in reports to the Comptroller, the deductions range from .5 to 1.85 cents per pound depending upon the company and the type of black.

Refunds are not specifically provided for, but are made in practice to adjust unintentional errors.

SECTION 4 COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Collection

Collection of the carbon black tax is based upon production reports which are submitted to the Comptroller on or before the 25th of each month along with the tax payment. As in other State production taxes, the producer acts as collector. The form of the reports is prescribed by the Comptroller, and he is authorized by the Statute to require any additional information he deems necessary.

A separate report is required for each carbon black manufacturing plant, regardless of the number of plants operated by a producer. It is required that total production be separated on the report to indicate the amounts manufactured within each of the two classes of carbon black, and further segregated to show the price or market value of each class. Space is provided on the reports for deductions of packing, freight, and cartage expenses in those cases where market value must be computed.

The collection machinery actually consists of an office audit of the reports which are submitted. In the audit of monthly reports and payments, the 

Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department generally accepts the price or market value as indicated by the producer. It will be recalled that the carbon black valued under four cents is taxed on a flat rate basis. Practically no attention is devoted to a verification of the sales price or value, partly because prices of carbon black are fairly constant and partly because such determination is administratively difficult. In addition, taxable value for carbon black valued at more than four cents (taxed on a percentage of value basis) is accepted, as is also the producers' determination of the deduction or discount, unless discrepancies are obvious. 

32 Opinion of the Atty. Gen., No. 0-7145, October 16, 1946. 

120

Enforcement Enforcement of the carbon black tax is in large part dependent upon inspection of records in addition to the audit of reports mentioned above. 

Besides these monthly reports, records maintained at the offices of the producers containing the same information as is in the reports, are required. 

These records are open, under the tax law, to inspection by the Comptroller, the Attorney General, and the Auditor; in reality, only the Comptroller exercises this function. Although this inspection appears simple, it is complicated by the nature of the carbon black industry. 

Of the 12 or 15 producers operating in Texas, only three or four have their headquarters here. Prior to 1949, representatives of the Comptroller's 

Department traveled out-of-State to the respective headquarters of foreign producers. This practice was prohibited in that year, and audits of this nature have been discontinued. There is at present no place in Texas where such producers are required to maintain records for the purposes of inspection. Thus, a check of production records extends only to some 25 per cent of producers. Verification of sales prices presents the same difficulty, since sales records are the only means for such verification. 

Administrative Requirements 

Only a minimum amount of time is required for administration of the carbon black tax. No separate personnel are maintained for its administration; it is one of the seventeen taxes handled by the Gross Receipts Division. 

No special administration fund is provided to defray administrative costs, the expense being borne by the oil and gas funds. It is estimated that ten man-hours per month are required in its collection and enforcement. In addition, a few days each year are devoted to an audit of records of the three or four domestic producers. It is reasonable to assume that inspection of out-of-State producers would add to this time requirement. Interpretations

Interpretations of the carbon black tax statute were chiefly concerned with production methods during the early years of its application. Considerable attention has also been required for determination of permissible deductions in computing market value. The Comptroller is now able, however, to decide most questions arising under the law because of extensive court interpretations, previous rulings of the Attorney General, and the clarifying amendments to the statute. Too, the tax is relatively inflexible, and the re are no statutory exemptions. The taxability of oil production methods has been recently resolved, and its attendant questions are not current. 

No Licensing Provision 

Licensing of taxpayers is generally employed in tax administration to assure coverage. There is no requirement that carbon black producers be licensed in Texas. In spite of the fact that there are only a few of them, the matter of identification has, on occasion, been a problem. One corporation produced carbon black for two years before it was discovered by the Comptroller. 121

In connection with coverage and the absence of licensing, a com-. parison of production totals as reported to the Comptroller with those to the Railroad Commission is interesting: 

Production in pounds 1948 

As reported to Railroad Commission 773,208,382

As shown on Comptroller records 32a 575,727,935 1949 707,402,220 586,783,962

A comparison covering a longer period of time is not possible because production totals reported to the Comptroller are unavailable for previous years. Records maintained by the Comptroller are employed for the purpose of monthly audits of individual producer's reports and cannot be taken as statistically absolute for totals. A tabulation was made for the purposes of this study from office files and is presented as the basis for general observations only.

Both production totals (Railroad Commission and Comptroller) represent the amount on which taxes were collected. Part of the difference may be accounted for by differing reporting and measuring procedures, but it is not likely that this variation alone would produce so large a disparity. 

It might be inferred that coverage is not complete. 

Penalties 

Violation of any provisions of the tax statute are punishable, upon conviction, by a fine ranging from $25 to $1,000 for each day of violation. 

These penalties are evidently intended for flagrant violations. They have not been invoked often, and there is no evidence of deliberate evasion. Delinquency also incurs penalties. Taxes become due on or before the 25th of each month for the preceding month and are delinquent the following day. No exceptions are provided. For the first 20 days of delinquency, the penalty is two per cent of taxes due. Delinquency beyond this period incurs an additional penalty of eight per cent. Unlike the usual provision specifying percentage delinquency penalties, the carbon black tax statute does not provide that both taxes and penalties draw interest from due date. Only delinquent taxes draw interest at the rate of eight per cent. Though the Attorney General is given statutory authority to bring suit to enjoin delinquent producers from producing carbon black until taxes due are paid, there is no evidence this authority has been exercised. 

Police Powers

Very few police powers, as generally employed by administrators in tax enforcement, are granted the Comptroller in the carbon black statute. 

Inspection of producers' records is the chief method of enforcement under the law. As discussed above, this audit is limited in practice to only three or four producers, those with headquarters in the State. Thus, this police power does not extend to the majority of producers.

Actual inspection of premises has not, under the present arrangement, been attempted. Whether or not statutory authority exists for such inspections 

32agee Lage 120 for text

122

by the Comptroller is not easily evident. In view of the difficulty encountered with audits of records, however, this lack is especially noteworthy. 

There have evidently been no means expressly provided in the law whereby the plants or records of foreign producers may be checked to assure complete coverage. Earmarking

Revenue from the carbon black tax is allocated 1/4 to the Available School Fund and 3/4 to the Clearance Fund.33 The ten per cent increase provided by the First Called Session of the 51st Legislature is allocated 1/4 to the Available School Fund and 3/4 to a State Hospital Fund created by the same act.34

SECTION 5 ANALYSIS OF OPERATION 

Administration Costs 

As with other taxes considered in this study, the cost of administering the carbon black tax is not easily established, largely because it is not separately recorded. This tax is one of the seventeen taxes handled by the 

Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department. An estimated ten man-hours per month for the maintenance of office records and routine examination of reports, and a few days each year in an audit of three or four companies are devoted to this tax. Thus the administrative cost is relatively low. Should it be possible to audit the records of all producers, both within the State and outside, more time would be required. Verification of reported sales prices and production would also require additional time and effort. It is probable, however, that increased enforcement effort, made possible by more extensive and express inspection authority than now exists might result in increased revenues to the State. The present relatively low cost /revenue ratio would thus probably be unaffected. Rates The Texas carbon black tax is the only one of its kind in the United States, and comparisons with other States cannot be made. As previously indicated, however, Texas accounts for at least two-thirds of national production and the industry therefore looms larger on the Texas scene than in any other State. In addition this tax has been interpreted as having been enacted "in aid of regulation" for the conservation of natural gas, long a primary concern of the State. Considered alone, the carbon black tax rates seem to appear complicated and unwieldly. The provision of four separate rates depending upon volume of gas consumed and the market value are comprehensible only when 

33 Tex. Laws 1941, 47th Leg., R.S., Ch. 184, Art XX. 

34Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Leg., 1st C.S., Ch. 2, Art XIX. 

123

viewed in the light of the conservation statutes. Even a study of the se statutes, permitting an average recovery of one to five pounds per thousand fee of gas, does not account for the division of classes at five pounds per thousand. A study of the historical development of these conservation acts, however, reveals that five pounds was the basic required recovery and that modifications and exceptions have been added which permit recovery as low as one pound. The tax statute does not reflect these subsequent modifications and exceptions. In fact the alleged correlation between the two sets of enactments has not extended beyond the 1941 tax rate change. 

Regulatory Effect of Rates 

Any regulatory effect of the carbon black tax, as may have been intended by the statutory rate provisions, is not revealed by an examination of production totals, within the various categories. As tabulated from Comptroller's production reports, largest quantities are producted with less efficient recovery processes. More than three-fourths of carbon black produced in 1948 and 1949 was in Class B, that with an average recovery of less than five pounds per thousand cubic feet of gas. Statistics giving the average recovery within this class are not available but the average recovery throughout the State as reported by the United States Bureau of Mines for all carbon black was only 1.5 pounds per thousand cubic feet of gas in 1948. As indicated in the 1949 annual report of the Railroad Commission, average recovery was 2.18 pounds. Thus, the tax rates appear to have had little regulatory effect.

Average Recovery, Market Value, and Reported Production 

The total tax paid by a producer is determined by three factors --average recovery, market value, and reported production. As previously mentioned each individual producer computes all three. Under present procedures the Comptroller cannot effectively determine the volume of gas actually consumed per pound of carbon black. An examination of sales and shipping records is the only method whereby the Comptroller could be assured that reported market value is correct, and he is unable to do this in all cases, for at least two-thirds of producers are located out of state. Nor may he thoroughly check actual production to assure that all carbon black produced is reported.

As reported to the Railroad Commission, the production of carbon black considerably exceeds that upon which taxes are paid to the Comptroller. 

The Commission exercises extensive investigative authority, under conservation laws, to assure that gas reported as so used is in reality employed in such production. The Comptroller has no comparable authority and relies largely upon monthly reports and the discovery of obvious discrepancies. 

Production from Liquid Hydrocarbons 

As previously discussed, when production of carbon black from oil was first employed in Texas, the Comptroller attempted to collect taxes on such black. In the face of court rulings holding the tax inapplicable, this at temp was unsuccessful. No accurate estimate of carbon black produced from raw materials other than natural gas can be made. Employing the same reported production totals of the Railroad Commission and the Comptroller used 124

and adding the production total as recorded by the Bureau of Mines the following comparison is possible: 1948 

Gas and Liquid

Hydrocarbons 

Bureau of Mines 

903,324,000 lbs. 

Gas Only 

Railroad Comm. 

773,208,382 lbs. Comptroller 

575,727,935 lbs. Probably most, if not all, of the difference between the figures as recorded by the Bureau of Mines and those from local sources represents production from oil. This would indicate that possibly as much as one-fourth or more of total Texas carbon black is produced from liquid hydrocarbons, including oil.

More important than the exact proportion of carbon black produced from liquid hydrocarbons is the increasing use of these raw materials and the resulting decline in proportionate use of gas. Returns Revenues from the carbon black tax have shown a general upward trend since 1937. There was a decline in 1941, and fluctuations during the war years probably resulted from the variations in rubber manufacture. 

Since 1945, the upward trend has been uninterrupted. In spite of this, in 

1947 receipts from this tax represented .84 per cent of total tax revenue; by 

1949 they accounted for only .69 per cent, due, of course, to large increases in revenues from other taxes. 

Whether indicated revenues represent the maximum due for taxable carbon black production is debatable. As has been discussed above, there is some indication that coverage may not be complete. 

Regulation and Taxation

The relationship of the Comptroller to the Railroad Commission in exercising their respective functions affecting carbon black production and taxation has been previously discussed in several of the above sections. An analysis of the effect of Commission action upon carbon black tax revenue has not been attempted, nor is it within the scope of this study. With the Commission establishing allowables which govern natural gas production, however, it is not unreasonable to assume that its actions indirectly affect carbon black production and thus the tax revenue. 

SECTION 6  

It is apparent from the preceding discussion that numerous unanswered questions of policy and practice underlie the administration and application of the carbon black production tax. The problem areas created by these questions include: 125

1) The lack of coverage of all methods of production and the attendant implications in the matter of revenues; and, lack of methods for insuring complete coverage. 

2) Lack of statutory provisions for refunds. 

3) The determination of discounts (deductions). 

4) Methods of assessment. 

5) The lack of realistic and practical enforcement methods. 

6) Lack of administrative funds.

Some of these problems do not cause actual difficulties of administration, but involve fundamental matters of policy on which the future of the tax rests.

Coverage Foremost of the questions of policy in regard to the carbon black tax is the matter of coverage. As discussed in earlier sections the tax is applicable only to producers using gas as their raw material. Though this limitation is not expressly stated in the statute, the lack of any basis for taxation of any production other than that from gas, in reality provides an exemption of those using other source materials. The production of carbon black from oil, now untaxed, is of course the most significant. 

Three approaches are possible to this matter. One, if this tax is to be considered primarily a revenue-raising measure, and its regulatory features are considered to be secondary, the tax would be most productive if applied to all carbon black produced from whatever raw material it is manufactured. The rapid expansion of production from oil, and the efficiency of recovery from oil as compared to that from gas, give substantial indication that production from oil will seriously challenge or even supplant production from gas as the principal means of manufacture of carbon black in the near future.

Two, if this tax is to be considered primarily a regulatory or conservation measure, as it has been interpreted by the courts to be now, serious thought might be warranted on the matter of making more effective its regulatory aspects. As has been discussed above, the rate differences now in the tax law, bearing more heavily on inefficient recovery, seemingly have had no apparent deterrent effect upon the use of the relatively inefficient recovery methods. Three times as much black is manufactured with the use of the less efficient recovery methods than is produced by means of methods having a higher recovery ratio in spite of the slightly higher tax rate. Texas has a recovery ratio considerably lower than that for the rest of the country. 

There is some feeling among tax administrators that the approach to conservation of gas would be perhaps more effective if made through the conservation statutes than through the tax statutes. In view of the fact that present conservation laws permit, under certain conditions, a recovery of as low as one or one and one-half pounds per 1000 cubic feet of gas, whereas the rate differential in the carbon black tax law is effective at five pounds per 1000 cubic feet, this point of view may have some justification. The question of what is efficient recovery appears to be different in the tax laws from what is set up in the conservation statutes. 126

Third, a combination of both of the above approaches may warrant consideration. Such a combination would entail simply the retention of the present basis of taxation in regard to production of carbon black from gas, and the addition (perhaps as a flat rate) of a basis for taxing that produced from oil or other sources. Too, under such plan, a new definition of the taxable commodity might be advisable in the law. 

Another feature of coverage which has not been generally considered in regard to the carbon black tax due to the limited number of producers is the use of some procedure to assure complete coverage or application of the tax to all those who are liable for it. The significance of such procedure is indicated by the example of the producer whose case resulted in the court determination that carbon black produced from oil was not taxable. Although the State eventually failed to collect the tax, the interesting point is that this producer had been manufacturing carbon black for over two years without it being known to State tax officials. A licensing or registration procedure required of all carbon black manufacturers would prevent to a large extent such a situation from occurring. Licensing or registration would give to the administrator a list of all producers. It would require that the producer make himself known, rather than placing the burden of locating him on the administrator. Refunds

In this tax, as in most others, unintentional errors of payment periodically occur. However, the present statute makes no provision for refunding or crediting overpayments. The addition of such provision to the tax statute would facilitate the administration of the tax, and would probably permit more equitable corrections of unintentional errors than is now possible. Assessment

Although a complicated assessment procedure is spelled out in the statute, its practical effect is almost negligible. As previously discussed, assessment is made on four separate categories of carbon black. The categories are determined in three distinct steps or tabulations. First, Class A carbon black is differentiated from Class B by a determination of the amount of gas used in production. Second, within each class the carbon black valued at more than four cents is tallied in a separate category from that valued at four cents or less per pound. Third, computation of the tax is made on a flat rate basis for the cheaper categories, and on the basis of a percentage of market value for the more expensive categories. 

Thus, to levy the tax accurately, it is necessary to determine the quantities of gas used per unit manufactured, to determine the sales price of all carbon black, and to use this value to compute the tax on two of the categories. In practice, all these determinations are made by the producer himself and the determinations are accepted by the administrators because there is no practical way that the accuracy of the self-assessment can be determined. 127

Application of a flat-rate tax for each of the categories would greatly simplify the assessment procedures. If a higer rate for certain categories is desired, such would still be possible with a graduated rateper-pound procedure. A considerable simplification of audit procedures could be effected in this manner, and it would also permit extension of coverage to production from oil, if this is desired. 

On the other hand, a flat rate may be considered undesirable from a policy standpoint in that a rate based on per cent of market value causes the tax burden to remain relatively the same no matter what happens to the price while in a flat rate the relative tax increases as the price decreases, and decreases as the price increases. Thus the relative tax burden will tend to be lower in periods of expanding economic activity and higher when business conditions worsen.

If, however, the rate of the tax is continued on a basis of percentage of market value it might be well to consider some means of making possible the ascertaining of this value by the administrators. Specifically, a provision making it possible for the Comptroller to actually check the sales records would be helpful. 

Discounts or Deductions 

The rather unusual discount provisions in the carbon black tax law permit a deduction of the cost of packing, crating, and pelletizing. As has been discussed above, this provision deserves attention for several reasons. 

One, this cost varies considerably among the several producers and is impossible for the Comptroller to determine. Two, such a discount provision has practically no counterpart in any other tax. Three, the discounts amount to almost 15 per cent of the total taxable value, and as such are certainly not a negligible total. .

Serious consideration seems warranted in regard to the basis for this discount procedure. If a discount method is desired and continued it would greatly simplify all administrative and audit procedures if an arbitrarily determined percentage or flat rate discount were established. Since there is no way for the Comptroller to determine actual costs of packing, etc. there appears to be some question of the merit of the present procedure, as far as protection of the State's interest is concerned. Too, the present procedure permits the less efficient plant to gain a larger deduction -- essentially a rate reduction -- while the more efficient plant pays a larger tax for the same amount of taxable commodity. Some administrators feel that the discount procedure is unwarranted, and the same result could be obtained, if desired, by a reduction in rates with far fewer complications. The tax policy behind the discount may be an effort to tax the net market value of the production but the rationale and tax policy are not clear. 

Enforcement Methods and Funds 

It has been mentioned that enforcement of this tax consists almost entirely of an office review of reports submitted. Due to the nature of the industry most producers maintain records of sales and production at headquarters out of Texas. Since field men of the Gross Receipts Division find it 128

no longer feasible to leave the State on field inspections, audits of the records of the out-of-State companies are no longer made. In view of the complex computations by which the tax is determined, the possibilities of honest error are manifold. Deliberate evasion is not indicated in the experience of the administrators, but its existence couldn't be determined under present procedures if it were prevalent. That there may be some justification for question is indicated by the apparent discrepancies between reports of carbon black produced and reports of tax payments. 

A statutory provision for periodic audits at the places where sales and production records are maintained--even though these may be out of the State--would give some assurance of the accuracy of the tax returns submitted. Such audits would not need to be more frequent than once a year, perhaps, or even less often. Or, a requirement that producers maintain records within the State might be possible. 

To pay for the expense of such field audits and to contribute to the maintenance of the field and office force of the Gross Receipts Division, a provision for an administrative fund from the revenues collected from the carbon black tax might be a matter worthy of consideration; or an audit fee might be considered. At present the administrative funds of the gas and oil production taxes are used to pay for the expenses of administering the carbon black and all the other taxes administered by the Gross Receipts Division. 

In Conclusion

Technological developments in the years since the first adoption of this tax have considerably altered the environment in which it functions. 

Due to this, serious consideration of the basis upon which the carbon black tax is levied is warranted now. The future of the tax may well be that of regulation--or it may be that of revenue-raising. There is little indication. that the policy decision involved has before now been made as such by the Legislature. It has not been necessary nor possible until very recently. 

Now it is important for both the future of this tax and the State's revenue structure. 32a (Footnote for p. 122) Subsequent to a release of preliminary copies of this text, the Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department investigated the discrepancy indicated between the records of the Railroad Commission and the Comptroller. The search revealed that the discrepancy indicated for 1949 was in large part the result of posting omissions and the like. 

However, though corrections of these omissions substantially reduced the amount of discrepancy shown for 1949, reconciliation was not effected. 129

SECTION 

Chapter VI THE SULPHUR TAX HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

Sulphur has been known since antiquity. It was used by the Egyptians as early as 2000 B.C. in the bleaching of linen and somewhat later as a component of paint. The Greeks employed it in skin ointments and as a disinfectant. It is mentioned in the Bible and was given the name "sulfur" by the Romans. In the time of the Caesars, sulphur provided an incendiary material for use in warfare and in the casting of triumphal bronze statues. Medieval chemists considered it the principle of combustion and it was long considered a peculiar kind of earth. The name "brimstone" designates certain compounds of sulphur existing as a mineral ore.

Sicilian deposits of brimstone have been mined for hundreds of years and are the principal source of European sulphur. When a French firm secured a monopoly in 1838 from the King of Naples for Italian production and tripled the price, efforts were made to utilize other compounds in the manufacture of sulphuric acid, and Sicily, though it never regained the sulphuric acid market, continued to supply 95 per cent of the world's sulphur requirements until 1903.

European sulphur deposits are easily mined by conventional mining methods generally employed for the production of ores. Negligible quantities of sulphur are similarly mined in Texas, Wyoming, Colorado, and

California and employed chiefly in the treatment of fertilizers. The preponderance of United States production, in Texas and Louisiana, however, is realized from sulphur deposits beneath cap-rock overlying certain socalled salt domes at depths ranging from 500 to 1,500 feet. Although about two hundred of these domes are known in the United States, only eight have produced commercial sulphur. The domes are similar to those from which oil is produced in the Spindletop field, Early efforts to sink shafts and recover the sulphur by ordinary methods failed chiefly because of the nature of the ground and the presence of hydrogen sulphide above the sulphur stratum." 

Deposits of sulphur were discovered in Louisiana in the middle 1800's. A process whereby they could be exploited was developed by Dr. 

Herman Frasch at Sulphur, Louisiana and was first patented in 1891. This process did not realize widespread commercial usage until 1903. Production of sulphur in the United States was only 5,000 tons in 1902, soared to 35,000 tons in 1903 and to 190,000 tons in 1904.2a "Sulphur," Texas Gulf Sulphur Co., N.Y., 1940, p.l. 

2Ibid., p. 7. 2a. 

2ª Ibid., p. 1. 130

The Frasch process utilizes three concentric pipes of varying length and diameter lowered into a sulphur well drilled with rotary rigs like those employed in the petroleum industry. Superheated steam is forced under pressure through the outer pipe to melt the sulphur; air is forced through the center pipe; and liquefied sulphur flows up the space between the inner and second pipes. The liquid sulphur solidifies in vats and is approximately 

99 per cent pure.

Texas sulphur was discovered in 1901 by Captain Anthony F. Lucas, the discoverer of Spindle top oil field, but its utilization awaited perfection of the Frasch process.3 In fact quantity production was not realized until the decade 1915-1925, and two of the five producing areas were not developed until 1928-1929.3a

1912 1915 1916 1928* 1927 1929 Sulphur is at present produced in Brazoria, Liberty, Wharton, and Fort Bend 

Counties by four companies. By 1948, Texas was producing 79 per cent of total United States production and 73 per cent of the world supply. 

Besides sulphur production in Texas and Louisiana, minor quantities are produced in California, Colorado, Nevada, and Wyoming. World deposits besides those in the United States are located in Italy, Chile, Greece, Turkey and Mexico."

Sulphur is used largely as sulphuric acid in the production of industrial chemicals. It is utilized in the manufacture of explosives, pulp and paper, fertilizers, insecticides, dyes, rubber, medicine, paint and varnish, etc. There is hardly an aspect of modern life into which sulphur or its chemical products do not enter.

Taxation of Sulphur Production 

Taxation of sulphur was first attempted in Louisiana. That State in 

1910 enacted a general severance license tax and amended its Constitution to permit the levying of such taxes, which had been prohibited under a previous Constitution." Sulphur was included among the taxed articles at the rate of two cents per ton. However, because the approval of the tax statute antedated the adoption of the constitutional amendment by some four 

3 Texas Almanac, 1943-1944, p. 178. 

3aRobert H. Ridgway, "Sulphur," U.S. Bureau of Mines Information Circular 

No. 6329, August 1930, pp. 9-15. 

4Mineral Yearbook, U.S. Bureau of Mines, 1948, pp. 1182-1192. 

5 Acts of the State of Louisiana, Act 196 of 1910. 

131

months, the tax was held null and void. The Legislature of 1912 then re-enacted the 1910 statute virtually without change, except to apply a rate based on value.7 The value rate basis remained in effect until 1928 when the rate was changed to 15 cents per long ton of 2,240 pounds. 1 In the meantime, however, a constitutional convention was held in Louisiana. This convention is noteworthy because of the extensive State wide controversy which developed over the principle of severance taxation. 

In 1920 the General Assembly of Louisiana created a Commission to study the State's tax system and make recommendations to the Constitutional Convention called to meet the following year. Among the Commission findings, a passage was devoted to the severance tax: owing to the public nature of the severance or production tax, and in view of the fact that this tax can be collected only but once from the same commodity, and considering that the production from which the tax is derived necessarily depletes the natural wealths and capital assets of the State, we think it would be well for the Legislature to adopt the policy of reinvesting the revenue, thus derived, in something of a substantial and permanent character; thereby giving back to the public for the enjoyment of future generations, as well as the present one, in so far, as is possible compensation for that which has been destroyed and consumed, and which in no otherwise can be replaced.9

The generally accepted principles of severance taxation as matured in this controversy have been summarized as follows: 

Advocates favoring the taxation of the severance of the natural resources in Louisiana argued that these resources were God-given and belonged to all the people; that the present generation was a guardian of the future social interests and could not be permitted to rob the future of a natural heritage; that if extraction was permitted, taxes should be so contrived as to procure a part of the severed value to be dedicated to future welfare. . . It can be said with certainty that fiscal expediency was instrumental in the adoption of the severance principle and this same fiscal expediency will be expected to be sufficient cause for the continued retention of the severance principle. ro Admitting that the controversy over severance taxation was late in developing, it was observed that "the Legislature during the earlier history 

6Etchinson Drilling Co. v. Flournoy (1912), 131 La. 442. 

7Acts of the State of Louisiana, Act 209 of 1912. 

8Ibid., Act No. 5 of 1928. 

9Report of the Assessment and Taxation Commission to the Constitutional 

Convention, 1921, Ramires-Jones Printing Company, Baton Rouge, La.

State Press, pp. 1,2. 132

of the severance tax was almost unanimous in its deliberations and votes on severance legislation. . . . no serious difference of opinion outside the courts came up for settlement prior to 1921. 1 A study of the sulphur tax in Louisiana from 1920 to 1937 reveals several interesting developments, useful for comparative purposes. First, the rate was altered in 1928 from a gross value basis to a flat rate per ton, and then successively decreased and increased until it reached $2.00 per ton in 1937. Secondly, no revenue was forthcoming in the years 1926 to 1933 because of the exhaustion of then known sulphur deposits. Production began again in 1933 from new mines and revenues increased progressively, reaching slightly more than half a million dollars in 1937. 

Thirdly, the influence of the severance tax principle-- that revenues be reinvested in "something of a substantial and permanent character''-- did not receive express recognition until 1936 and that, according to some, was slight. "Some of the funds were used for purposes that cannot be expected to conserve the patrimony of the State in perpetuity."12 

Arkansas Tax

Arkansas in 1923 imposed a general severance tax upon removal of certain objects from the soil or water. Sulphur was included at a percentage of value. This value basis has prevailed until the present day, the rate now being four per cent of value. Available records do now show any sulphur production for Arkansas, however, during the past few years. 

Texas Sulphur Tax 

The first Texas tax on sulphur producers was levied in 1923 at a rate of two per cent of market value.13 In the same year another act dealing with this tax was passed in special session, devoted largely to clarifying administrative procedure and making provision for penalties. 14 The reason given for this new tax was the need for additional revenue. In these statutes the pattern of assessing this tax upon a value basis was not broken. 

In 1930, however, the tax rate was changed from a percentage of value to a flat rate, 55 cents per ton. 15 Penalties for failure to keep records were considerably increased. Allocation of revenues was also provided for the first time, 1/4 to the Available School Fund, 3/4 to the General Fund. This division was to be effective until July 1, 1931, after which date the entire revenue was to be credited to the Available School Fund. The State Tax 

Board was instructed to assess an ad valorem tax equal to the per capita need for all children of scholastic age minus "such a percentage and rate as would be necessary to produce a sum of money equal to the amount paid 

IlIbid., p. 10. 

12T.N. Farris, Severance Taxation in Louisiana, State Press, p. 26. 

13 Tex. Laws 1923, 38th Leg., R.S., ch. 158. 

14 Tex. Laws 

15 Tex. Laws

. into the Treasury under the provisions of this Act during the first half of the current calendar year and the latter half of the preceding calendar year

The earmarking provision tied together two almost wholly unrelated taxes, but seems to reflect the general theory advocated for the allocation of severance tax revenue. This act was passed, because the tax then in operation was "totally insufficient and inadequate," according to the statement in the emergency clause.

The following year, 1931, the rate was increased from 55 to 75 cents per ton:.16 The complicated formula provided for determination of the ad valorem tax rate was replaced before it could be applied. The new allocation gave 55 cents of the revenue to the Available School Fund and the remaining 20 cents to the General Fund. Other provisions of the statute remained virtually unchanged and the new act was said to be necessary because the occupation tax laws "contain errors, . . . are vague and indefinite (and need to be) properly defined and clarified . . . and . . . reformed." The need for funds for old age assistance produced another rate. increase in 1936 to $1.03 per ton. Allocation of 55 cents to the Available

School Fund was continued, but the portion allotted to the General Fund was increased to 48 cents.17

In the same act an occupation tax was levied on all producers of ores in quantities of more than 1,000 pounds (five cents per ton), marble (ten cents per ton), and cinnabar ore, a compound of sulphur (HgS), (ten cents per ton).18 This tax was patterned after the sulphur tax in its administrative provisions, and was allotted one-fourth to the Available School Fund and three-fourths to the General Revenue Fund.

A further increase in the sulphur production tax was imposed in 1941, raising the rate from $1.03 to $1.272 per ton. 19 Allocation of revenues was altered only in that three-fourths was to go to the Clearance Fund established at that time.

The tax on ores, marble, and cinnabar ore was repealed by the 48th Legislature in 1943 because it had yielded only a small amount of revenue, was not graduated in proportion to the value of ores produced, retarded the development of the natural resources of Texas, "which abounds in mineral wealth, mainly in low grade ores," and burdened the war effort.20 

The development of the sulphur production tax in Texas has not been spectacular, as compared with other taxes. It has at no stage experienced the administrative and policy difficulties which so often beset the adminis tration of many taxes. As a revenue producer, the tax has grown steadily. 

From a depression low of $677,000 in 1933, collections have increased until in 1949, a total of $4,871,000 was brought into the State Treasury by the sulphur tax.

SECTION 

ORGANIZATION FORM 

General 99 Originally considered and presently levied as an "occupation tax," the Texas tax on sulphur production is now generally referred to as a "severance tax. It is now administered by the Comptroller and collected upon the basis of a flat rate of $1.272 per long ton of 2,240 pounds. An additional tax of ten per cent of $1.272 was provided for the years 1950-51 by the 51st Legislature in 1950. Revenue from the sulphur production tax, except for the temporary 1950-51 increase (three-fourths of which is allotted to the State Hospital Fund) is allocated three-fourths to the Clearance Fund and one-fourth to the Available School Fund.

Simple Administration 

The Comptroller performs practically all functions relating to the administration of this tax. Quarterly tax payments and production reports are submitted upon forms prescribed by him. The only other agency given any statutory responsibility for administration of the tax is the Attorney General, for whose examination and inspection producers' records must be open. This authority has been rarely exercised however. Actual administration is handled by the Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department.

The administration of the sulphur tax is perhaps one of the simplest encountered in this survey. No more than one agency is responsible and there appear to be no serious administrative problems connected with its collection or. enforcement. Perhaps the fact that there are only four producers of sulphur in the State has contributed to its smooth operation. A long formative history has also probably produced an effective and concise tax statute.

SECTION 3 - ASSESSMENT 

Like other Texas production taxes, the sulphur tax is self-assessed by the producer, who must submit quarterly tax payments and production reports on the first day of January, April, July, and October. Producers are defined as any "person, firm, association, or corporation who owns, controls, manages, leases, or operates any sulphur mine, or mines, wells, or shafts, or who produces sulphur by any method, system or manner." Since the nature of Texas sulphur deposits practically precludes the use of any method other than the Frasch process, the problem of production methods has not presented difficulties. Negligible quantities of sulphur are produced by conventional mining but the words of the Act clearly make such production taxable. "Person" is further defined to include "persons, firms, partnerships, companies, corporations, associations, common law trusts, or other concern by whatever name or howsoever organized, formed or created.'' Thus there are no classifications of producers; all are liable. Nor are there any exemptions. As previously mentioned, there are at present only four producers in Texas. 135

The prescribed rate is a flat charge per long ton of sulphur produced, $1,272 plus 10 per cent of the amount due from March 1, 1950 to September 1, 1951 or an additional charge of $0.1272 per long ton. The producer records production quantities and computes the quarterly tax due by a simple multiplication of rate times production. 

Apparently no interpretative difficulties have arisen in connection with the sulphur production tax, for available records contain no mention of such problems. The absence of any exemptions and the provision of a flat rate are probably other factors contributing to the smooth operation of this tax. 

No Refunds

There is no statutory provision for credits or for refunds of overpayments of sulphur taxes.

SECTION 4 COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Collection of the sulphur production tax is the responsibility of the Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department. Quarterly payments are due on the the first days of January, April, July and October accompanied by production reports covering the preceding quarter. An office audit is made by the Gross Receipts Division to assure that the payment is the actual amount due.

Delinquency Penalties 

Taxes not paid within thirty days after due date are considered delinquent and delinquency incurs an additional fee equal to ten per cent of the amount due with both tax and penalty drawing interest at the rate of six per cent per year. These explicit penalty provisions were perfected early in the operation of the sulphur production tax and probably reflect an initial enforcement difficulty. They have been rarely applied in recent years. One recent application occurred when an audit revealed that the production of sulphur had been incorrectly estimated upon the basis of the formula used for making allowances for impurities. The Attorney General or any district or county Attorney at the direction of the Attorney General may bring suit for recovery of delinquent taxes, penalties, and interest.

This provision permitting decentralization of recovery suits may also reflect an early concern over delinquency. 

Records and Enforcement In addition to quarterly production reports, sulphur producers are required to maintain records containing the same information. Such records "shall be open at all times to official inspection and examination by the Comptroller or the Attorney General" or their authorized representa tives. In reality only the Comptroller makes audits. These inspections are made approximately every three or four years by the field force of the Gross Receipts Tax Division, also charged with enforcement of the oil, gas, 136

and fourteen other taxes. Because sulphur producers are concentrated in six counties, this audit load is borne by relatively few personnel at widely spaced intervals.

Failure to maintain production records is punishable by a fine of not less than $500 nor more than $5,000 for each ten days of such failure. 

As with delinquency, the high penalties imposed for lack of production records probably reflect early enforcement problems. The chief value of this provision evidently lies in its deterrent effect upon producers where records are concerned, for it has never been invoked. Production records may be destroyed three years after the date of their last entry. This provision has potential significance in view of the infrequent inspections. However, there is no available indication that records have ever been destroyed before an audit. Coverage

Coverage of the sulphur production tax is apparently complete. The nature of the industry and its limited geographical distribution in six counties of the Texas Coastal Plain contributes to ease of collection and enforcement. The extremely low number of producers, only four, also facilitates the work of tax administrators. Production statistics from 1923 to the present as reported by the United States Bureau of Mines are almost indentical with those maintained by the Comptroller's Department. Hence, there is little reason to believe that any sulphur escapes tax payment.

No Administrative Fund 

No separate fund is provided for the administration of the sulphur tax. 

Instead, its administration is handled by the Gross Receipts Division and actually financed from funds supplied for administration of the oil and gas taxes. This lack is not unique to this tax, but is noteworthy in view of the fact that some taxes provide such funds, whereas others do not. Thus, in reality, the expense of administering the taxes lacking such funds is borne by those which have such funds. Earmarking

Three-fourths of the revenue from permanent levy of $1.272 per long ton of sulphur is allocated to the Clearance Fund created in 1941. Threefourths of the temporary rate of ten per cent of $1.272 imposed by the 51st 

Legislature is allotted to the State Hospital Fund. The other one-fourth of the sulphur tax revenue is credited to the Available School Fund. 

Although previous allocations, which in former years gave a large part of the revenue from this tax to the schools, may have been prompted by a consideration of the severance tax principle discussed in Section 1, it is difficult to identify such motivation underlying the current allocations. The 

Constitutional direction on the matter of allocation of collections from occupation taxes may well have been the sole reason for the present distribution of funds to the schools. 137

SECTION ANALYSIS OF OPERATION 

Rates 

Available records show that only three States levy taxes upon sulphur production, Texas, Arkansas, and Louisiana. Two of these States, Texas and Louisiana, impose the tax at a flat rate while Arkansas bases its tax upon a percentage of value. Since there are no indications that Arkansas produces significant quantities of sulphur, however, its rate from per cent of value is omitted from this comparison. A historical resume of rate development in Louisiana and Texas shows considerable similarity. Louisiana 1912

Both States' original levies provided for a rate based on value and the same percentage was applied. Where Louisiana changed to a flat rate in 

1928, Texas did not make this alteration until two years later. Both rates were increased during the 1930's, but that in Louisiana was lowered in 1938 to the prevailing rate in Texas. The Texas rate was increased in 1941 and 

1950 but the Louisiana rate has remained constant at $1.03 since 1938. Perhaps the geographical proximity of these two States has tended to stabilize sulphur tax rates between them, At the same time, because of the quantitative production advantage enjoyed by Texas, the Louisiana rate has had less effect on this State than the reverse. The absence of receipts in Louisiana from 1927 to 1933 is attributed to the exhaustion of then known deposits of sulphur in that State. Utilization of newly discovered mines was begun in 1933 and tax revenue began again in that year. The variations in Texas revenues prior to 1930 probably resulted from computation of the tax upon a value basis and the sulphur discoveries in 1928-1929. Fluctuations in the early 1930's reflect the business depression of those years. Rate increases produced marked increases in revenue for 

1937 and 1942. Since 1932 revenues have generally increased, with a slight reversal in 1944 possibly due to wartime conditions. From 1923 to the end of the fiscal year 1950 sulphur producers had paid a total of more than $56 million in taxes on a total production of some 60 million tons of sulphur produced or approximately one dollar per ton. 

Administration Costs

Although a separate cost computation cannot be made, the sulphur tax is probably the least expensive to administer among the seventeen taxes handled by the Gross Receipts Division. Only four production reports dealing with sulphur must be processed each quarter. In addition an audit of producers' records is made roughly every three or four years. Producers are easily identifiable and there has been no litigation cost. Interpretative problems have been practically negligible. Routine office expenses and the cost of triennial 139

or quadrennial audits are the major expenses of administration and they have consistently been low.

SECTION 6  

The sulphur production tax is characterized by an almost total lack of administrative problems. As compared with other taxes, its operation is simple and effective. There are only two matters of relatively minor importance which might warrant consideration: 

1. The lack of provision for credits or refunds. 

2. The lack of provision for an administrative fund.

A statutory refund or credit provision might permit the correction of unintentional errors which almost always present difficulties to tax administrators.

The absence of an administrative fund in the sulphur tax statute is not so great a deficiency as that lack in other tax laws because the administrative cost is low. However, if administrative funds are employed for some taxes but not provided for others, and unequal burden is usually borne by those funds provided. There is little merit, of course, in creating numerous enforcement funds when a centralized fund can handle the costs of several taxes. However, the lack of such separate funds frequently has the effect of subordinating the enforcement activities of the non-contributing: taxes, which often fail to get effective enforcement due to lack of administrative funds to no more than cover the requirements of the major taxes. 140

Chapter VII OIL PRODUCTION TAX SECTION 1 HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

The oil production tax is today (1950) a primary income -producer in the Texas tax structure. Accounting for revenues of more than 87 million dollars in 1949, the returns from this one tax represented approximately 28, per cent of the total State tax income for that year, excluding payroll taxes. 

In view of the significant role the oil production tax plays in State finances, any consideration of it must of necessity take note of the factors which underlie and govern the amounts of revenue forthcoming to the State from this tax. These factors lie within the oil industry itself and to a great extent are beyond the borders of Texas and the scope of the State's oil production tax statute." 1 The implications which arise from this set of circumstances are, of course, tremendous and ramified. Full consideration of them, it can be understood, has been beyond the scope of this survey. Nevertheless, in their broad outlines, these implications cannot be ignored in any appraisal of the tax law. They involve the method of administration of the tax, the method of regulation of the industry's production, and the factors which influence the regulation of production,

Because of this, it is essential that the oil production tax be looked at in relation to the development of the oil industry -- in Texas and throughout the world-- and in relation to the factors which have from time to time come into play to influence the industry.

Early Texas Oil 

The oil industry of Texas was born near the city of Nacogdoches. 

There the State's first drilled well was completed and pumping operations begun in 1866. Plans for the drilling had actually been made in 1859, the same year the Titusville discovery well in Pennsylvania was completed, but the Civil War caused a seven-year delay in the pioneer Texas oilmen's activities.

No rapid expansion of the industry, however, followed the drilling of the first well. Although other wells were completed in the following years, the oil production industry remained small and unimportant. It was almost 

30 years later that the first major oil field in the State was discovered. 

1 These percentages apply to totals of tax income; consideration of total 

State income, of course, provides considerably different ratios. 2 For a more extensive examination of many of the features of the Texas 66 oil industry and its impact upon State finances see Staff Research Report 

No. 51-5, The Texas Petroleum Industry and State Finances" (1950) and 

The Report of the Council to the Legislature" (January, 1951), ch. IV, Texas Legislative Council. 141

In 1894, in the city of Corsicana, water well drilling activities brought in oil. Since the uses of oil at that time were limited, a new source of water was considered more important, and attempts were made to case off the nuisance" oil. However, oil seepage persistently hampered drilling for water and, finally, some time after the initial discovery, a company was formed to begin production of the oil. By the end of 1897, the output of the 

Corsicana field had grown to an annual production of 66,000 barrels of oil.³ 

A new factor had come onto the scene at about this time the automobile. Paved streets and roads were beginning to appear as the Twentieth 

Century ushered in the "motor age." As the new mode of transportation became more generally accepted, the demand for oil increased rapidly. No longer was it a "nuisance" with limited usage. Concurrent with the new demand came oil discoveries in other Texas areas. 

Production boomed from the meager 66,000 barrels in 1897 to more than four million barrels in 1901. With the spectacular Spindletop field adding its impetus, the following year brought a production total of more than 

18 million barrels.4 4

The First Texas Oil Tax 

But in spite of the phenomenal growth of the industry during this period there is little evidence that anyone foresaw the tremendous future significance of the Texas oil industry. There is virtually no indication that early proposals for taxes on oil production even remotely contemplated such taxes becoming the State's leading revenue producer. Taxation of oil production came about simply because in 1905 the State was faced with a deficit in the treasury and was seeking new sources of tax revenues. 

The oil industry, only 11 years old in terms of major production, was looked to as one such new source of tax revenue. The first proposal for an * occupation tax on oil production was introduced as one section of a gross receipts tax levy on several types of industries. At that time, the prospect of a new type of tax on oil did not seem to be a matter of major concern. The editorialists of the press and the oil producers themselves had a few comments to make, and the discussion in the halls of the capitol seemed to make little impression on the people of the State.5 

This first oil tax law was passed during the final days of the 1905 session, and levied on "every individual, joint stock company, co-partnership or corporation, . . which owns, controls, manages or leases any oil well within this State .. 16 a tax of one per cent on the gross products of such producer at the average market value thereof. 

3Bruce Roach,,Increasing the Severance Tax on Natural Resources, The University of Texas, 1948, p. 184. 

4 Texas Almanac and State Industrial Guide, 1945-1946, The Dallas Morning 

News, 1945, p. 241. 

5L.D. Belzung, The History of Special Taxation of Natural Resources in 

Texas, unpublished thesis, The University of Texas, 1950, p.32. 

6 Tex. Laws, 29th Leg., 1905, ch. 148. 142

Several things seem to substantiate the observation that the initial tax on oil production was largely passed only to provide immediate tax revenue for the State, and not with any thought of the long range future of such a tax. First, although production and total value of oil produced had increased annually, the approximate value of oil produced in 1904, the last year for which figures would have been available, was only eight million dollars.7 On the basis of similar production and value, the tax of one per cent would thus bring to the State in the neighborhood of 80 thousand dollars annually, or only about two per cent of the State's annual total cash receipts at that time. Second, the "emergency clause" written into the initial tax law stated specifically the existence of a deficit in the treasury which this tax and the other taxes included in the act would correct. Third, the press, oil producers, and the people did not indicate concern with any future implications which such a tax might have, as is indicated by the lack of controversy in connection with the passage of the law. Thus, it is perhaps safe to say that little thought was given in 1905 to the possibility that the occupation tax on oil would become the colossus in 1948 of all individual taxes. 

Legislation of this sort was novel in. character in 1905 and the constitutionality of the gross receipts tax on oil was soon tested in the courts. In the first test case, Producers' Oil Co. v. Stephens, it was contended that the tax law violated the State Constitution as to rate equality, uniformity, assessment of property in the county where the same was situated, and as to equalization of values. However, the Court upheld the constitutionality of the oil tax law stating that it was not in the nature of an ad valorem tax but was, rather, a tax levied on the occupation of producing oil. Furthermore, the court denied the contention of inequality and non-uniformity, since the tax was levied equally and uniformly on all members of the same class of subjects. In more recent tests, the courts have been consistent in upholding the constitutionality of this law and its revisions. 

Rate Soon Reduced 1. 99 The tax on oil production had been in effect only two years when in 1907 the tax rate was cut in half and a tax of "one-half of one per cent of the total amount of all oil produced at the average value thereof. 10 A deficit still existed in the State Treasury, and the reduction in tax rate can thus not be attributed to a brighter picture in terms of State income. Two factors may have contributed to the reduction. First, at the end of 1906, oil production had declined sharply to less than half of the 1905 peak. It might have been felt that a lower tax rate would stimulate production. Second, objections to all types of indirect taxes on the people, if not specifically on the oil tax as such, were being expressed more firmly in the press.. 

For the next 12 years, the tax on oil production remained unchanged.

Annual tax revenue from this source ranged from a low of 19 thousand dollars in 1909 to 69 thousand dollars in 1915, fluctuating with production and with the 

71945-1946 Texas Almanac and State Industrial Guide, op. cit. 

99 S.W. 157 (Tex. Civ. App. 1906). 

1 CCH State Tax Rep. (Tex.) para. 45-004. 

10 Tex. Laws, 30th Leg., 1st C.S., 1907, ch. 18. 143

price of oil. World War I brought with it a boom in production, a rise in price, and a consequent increase in oil tax revenue so that during the years 1916, 1917, and 1918 annual oil tax receipts first topped 100 thousand dollars and continued to climb until the 200 thousand dollar mark was reached.11 

Factors other than the war added to an ever-growing national interest in oil. First, Henry Ford, with his Model T, had established mass production of motor vehicles as a definite part of the pattern of American economic life during the years immediately preceding World War I. Automobile regis trations had soared rapidly to almost one and one-half millions, and gasoline to turn their motors replaced kerosene for lighting purposes as the most important product of petroleum. Second, the oil-burning naval vessel çame into use at this time and at the outbreak of World War I both Great Britain and United States navies included many oil-burning ships. Plans for future ship-building of both naval and merchant-marine vessels called exclusively for oil-burning types, and source of oil fuel became of immediate and international importance.

Finally, in 1917, the world situation was such that the United States was looked to as the primary source of oil for the Allied war machine.. 

Polish and Rumanian oil fields were in the hands of the Central Powers. 

Even if Russia had had a surplus of oil beyond her own military needs, her exports were blocked by Turkish forts. Not until 1918 did Iranian oil contribute significantly to the world supply. Mexican fields were producing but lacked shipping facilities, and South American fields were yet to be developed. 

Serious consideration was given to the dangers of a world-wide oil shortage during the later years of World War I, and the sky-high prices which might result from such a shortage. 12 All of these events focused the attention of the people on oil.

In 1917 the United States produced 67 per cent of the world's oil. 

Texas, with the highly productive Ranger field then early in its development, was the fourth largest producing State in the nation. Oklahoma, California, and Kansas were first, second, and third respectively.13 In comparison with 

Texas' tax of one-half of one per cent of the value of gross production in 1917, Oklahoma had a tax of three per cent and California and Kansas had no taxes on oil at that time. 86 

Entry of Railroad Commission 

With its new-found international recognition, the oil production industry had arrived" as a important factor in the total economy of the nation, and particularly of the State of Texas. Its arrival was reflected in the actions of the Legislature and in the columns of the leading newspapers of the State. In 

1917 an entirely separate and additional tax of "one-twentieth of one per cent of the market value of crude petroleum produced within the State 14 levied as a part of an act which originated in the Senate, regulating the operation of pipe lines as "common carriers" of petroleum. This tax was to was 

11Petroleum Data Book, Petroleum Engineer Publishing Co., 1948, p. K-2. 

12Leonard M. Fanning, Our Oil Resources, McGraw Hill Book Co., Inc., New 

York, 1950, ch. X. 

13 World Oil, vol. 130, no, 3, Feb. 15, 1950, pp. 123-124 and 202. 

14 Tex. Laws, 35th Leg., 1917, ch. 30. 144

be collected in the same manner as the gross production tax then in effect; however, revenue received from this tax was specifically designated for use in paying salaries and expenses of agents of the Railroad Commission enforcing the provisions of the pipe line regulatory act. 

This act, designating petroleum pipe lines as "common carriers," and as such under the jurisdiction of the Railroad Commission, marks the Commission's entrance into the Texas petroleum industry as a regulatory agency. In order to give the Commission power to effect its regulation of pipe lines there was included in the act a section which authorized the Commission to "make and enforce rules and regulations either general in nature or applicable to particular oil fields for the prevention of actual waste of oil or operations in the field dangerous to life or property."15 This act became effective in May, 1917, and was the first act to designate the Railroad Commission as the agency to administer the conservation laws relating to oil and From this statement, apparently, the Commission's important regulatory powers in enforcing the conservation laws of Texas as they relate to oil and gas have grown. gas.

Concurrent with the passage of the above mentioned act, a Senate Joint Resolution was passed which proposed a constitutional amendment which declared that "the conservation and development of all of the natural resources of this State . . . are each and all hereby declared public rights and duties; and the Legislature shall pass all such laws as may be appropriate thereto."17 

The proposed amendment also included provision for dividing the State into conservation districts as necessary to the accomplishment of the purposes of the amendment. This amendment was adopted in an election in August of 1917 and was proclaimed by the Governor in October of that year. 

This amendment gave the State power to regulate oil and gas production for purposes of conservation.

Oil production, and more important, the value of oil produced in 1918 doubled the corresponding figures for 1917,18 and every indication pointed toward further production and value increases in the future. In 1919 with World 

War I at an end, the oil shortage was short lived as development in the Ranger field continued, discovery at Burkburnett brought new production, and pipe lines were completed into North Texas to move the oil to its markets. With a booming economic scene in prospect, the oil production tax was raised in 1919 from the one-half of one per cent rate of 1907 to a rate "one and one-half (1.5) per cent of the total amount of oil produced in this State . . . at the average market value thereof."19 A different and significant reason was given for the passage of this tax law. Rather than the reason simply that a deficit in the State Treasury existed, given previously, the emergency clause declared the reason to be 15Ibid.

Chicago, 1939, p. 217.
