181945-1946 Texas Almanac and State Industrial Guide, op. cit. 

19Tex. Laws, 36th Leg., 1919, ch. 77. 145

"the fact that the oil producers of the State have the benefit of the protection of the law and are paying an inadequate gross receipts tax." ,,20

A divided opinion concerning the increase in tax was expressed in the columns of two of the leading newspapers, but both were concerned with the current tax issue in relation to the future of the oil industry. One commented: the supply of oil is so small relatively to the demand that it could be taxed to some extent without interfering with the efforts of producers to sell it in competition with oil produced in other States ... Besides, nearly all the oil produced is so taxed, so Texas-produced oil would be under no handicap because of the tax.21

On the other hand another carried this comment: 

The representatives of the oil interests feel that the imposition of such a tax can not but be harmful, con- 22 sidering that there are other taxes to be paid. 

Principles of Severance Taxation

Despite the fact that Texas and other States had taxes on natural resources in effect earlier, it appears that the principle of severance taxation as a means of returning a part of the natural resources heritage to all the people was first brought clearly into the open during the period from 1918 to 1923. Until that time natural resources taxes were thought of primarily as another source of general revenue..

Louisiana was the pioneer State in taking the stand that natural resources in the land and waters of a particular region were a heritage which belonged to all the people, and that to allow a limited number of people to derive all the benefits accruing from severance of such resources from the earth was to deprive the remainder of the populace from their natural rights. 

Severance taxes were, therefore, a means of obtaining funds which when used in certain kinds of public expenditures, returned to the people a meas ure of the benefits of their natural heritage. One writer, in describing the attitude expressed in Louisiana in the early 1920's had this to say: 20Ibid.

Advocates favoring the taxation of the severance of the natural resources in Louisiana argued that the se resources were God-given and belonged to all the people; that the present generation was a guardian of the future social interests and could not be permitted to rob the future of a natural heritage, that if extraction was permitted, taxes should be so 

21 The Dallas Morning News, March 15, 1919, p. 15, col. 5. 

22 The Houston Post, March 1, 1919, p. 6, col.5; Belzung Thesis, p. 43. 146

contrived as to procure a part of the severed value to be dedicated to future welfare.23 

Other States followed Louisiana's lead, and the principle of severance taxation has become generally established as time has passed. 

In 1923, the inadequacy of the tax burden borne by the oil industry was again cited in proposing further increases in oil production taxes. 

National production had more than doubled since 1918, and Texas production had more than tripled in a like period. The price of oil dropped from around two dollars per barrel early in 1923 to slightly more than one dollar per barrel late in the same year. It seems likely that the problems of too much production and further drops in price were also in the minds of the legisla tors.

The 1923 oil tax legislation experienced a difficult time in both the House and the Senate as increased tax rates ranging from two per cent to seven per cent on gross production were proposed. The new tax law, with a rate of two per cent, was passed only after the then Governor had convened a second called session. The oil interests in the State now stood united against any increase in taxation on oil production, according to the newspapers. It was argued that increases in existing tax levies would satisfy the financial needs of the State, and attack from the standpoint of non-uniformity of taxation was also made.

The total picture of the changes made in the oil production tax law at this time indicates that there were two major ends in view. First, to raise the tax rate itself and thus bring increased amounts of revenue to the State; and second, to restrict evasion of tax payment by extending the scope of the law and wording its parts more precisely. 

The former was accomplished by the increase in tax rate mentioned in the preceding paragraph. To effect the latter, several additions were made. First, those subject to the tax were now described as: "Each and every person owning, controlling, managing, operating or leasing in this State any oil well, or any person who produces in any other manner any oil by taking it from the earth in this State, "25 The underlined clause was probably inserted in order to include oil produced from the surface of the earth as taxable oil production. When a "gusher" was brought in, thousands of barrels of oil would escape before the well could be capped or otherwise brought under control. There have been instances in which such oil flowed into a ravine or valley and collected there to form a "lake" of oil. Also, such oil has been trapped by ditching it to a collecting point, or by skimming the oil from the surface of a stream into a collecting point. Production from such surface pools was not production from an oil well, and hence was not subject to taxation under the earlier statements of the law. Further, the 

23J.N. Farris, Severance Taxation in Louisiana, Louisiana Business Bulletin 

Vol. 2, No. 1, Louisiana State University Press, 1938, p. 6. 

24 Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 2d C.S., 1923, ch. 45. 

25 Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 2d C.S., 1923, ch. 45. (Emphasis added) 147

tapping of existing oil wells by means of directional drilling, without drilling a well as such, was practical in some cases, and may have been a circumvention of the law.

Second, the word "person" was defified in an extended sense to include all types of oil producing concerns. Third, the world “oil” was defined for the first time, to include petroleum oil, mineral oil or other oil taken from earth. Fourth, the market value of oil was stated to be inclusive of any bonus or premium paid for oil in addition to the actual sale price. 

Fifth, a general tightening up of penalty procedure and amounts was accomplished, and the State was granted a lien on the oil rights or interests of any person owing any deliquent tax for the amount of such delinquency. Finally, producers were now required to report not only total production but also a detailed description of the disposition of such oil indicating when and to whom sold or, if not sold, where stored. This appears to be the forerunner of later changes in the oil tax law which, as will be discussed later, make the purchaser of oil a prominent person in the administration, control, and collection of the tax moneys.

During the years 1923 to 1933 no changes were made in the oil production tax laws except a change in rate from one-twentieth of one per cent to one-tenth of one per cent on gross production in the regulation tax originally passed in 1917. Since this tax was designed specifically to provide only necessary administrative funds, no great increase in State revenue was effected by this change.

Effect of Depression 

Production of oil increased steadily from year to year during the decade of the 1920's and the price of oil remained relatively stable and in excess of one dollar per barrel until the depression years of 1930, 1931, 1932, and 1933. Since the tax on oil was assessed as a percentage of its market value, revenues dropped significantly during these four years. In 1933, the combined effects of the nationwide depression and production of oil over and above market demand sent the value of Texas oil plummeting to all-time lows and the average price per barrel in Texas during that year was only slightly more than 50 cents.

Price stabilization to prevent waste had been tried in 1930, but had never been fully enforced and had been ruled unconstitutional.26 Prorationing of production on the basis of the early conservation statutes had also been unsuccessfully tried in 1931.27 Meanwhile, the ravages of the nation-wide depression continued. Then, to make a bad situation worse, the gigantic East 

Texas field was opened in 1931. The situation in the oil industry bordered on the chaotic. The price of oil for a time in some localities went as low as ten cents a barrel. 

26 Texoma Natural Gas Company v. Railroad Commission, 59F 2d 750.(W.D. 

Tex. 1932) 

27 MacMillan v. Railroad Commission, 51 F. 2d (W.D. Tex. 1931) 

148

From this situation grew the Market Demand Act of 1932 permitting a method of oil proration which exists in much the same form today, and which has tended to prevent the previous wide fluctuations in price in the Texas oil industry.28 On the basis of this the Railroad Commission moved into its present role of regulation of oil production. 

As a result of the effects of the depression and in addition to its actions in regard to conservation and price stabilization, the Legislature in 1933 made basic changes in the oil production tax laws. Future declines in 

State tax revenue, due to declines in the price of oil were averted by placing in effect a minimum amount of tax per barrel of oil produced. When finally passed the tax rate clause of the Act read; There is hereby levied an occupation tax on oil produced within this State of two (2) cents per barrel of forty-two (42) standard gallons

Provided, however, that the occupation tax herein levied on oil shall be two per cent (2%) of the market value of said oil whenever the market value thereof is in excess of One Dollar ($1.00) per barrel of forty-two (42) standard gallons."

No serious objection was raised in connection with this change by those interested and immediately affected. Revenues in 1934 were more than $6 million as against slightly more than $4 million in 1933. The downward trend of revenues had been stopped, at least in part from the effect of the new legislation.

In addition to strengthening the tax rate clause, the fundamental pattern of the administration of the law was changed. Monthly, rather than quarterly reports of total oil production were now required of the producer, but although the primary responsibility for tax payment remained with the producer, the purchaser of oil made actual payment to the State in most instances. 

In 1923, the producer was first required to report to whom oil produced was sold, or where it was stored, and such a report was continued in effect in 1933. Under the 1933 Act, however, the purchaser of oil acted as a "tax collector' for the State by deducting the amount of tax from the price paid to the producer for oil purchased. Monthly reports to the Comptroller were also required of the purchaser showing source and amount of purchases and amount of tax deducted, and the payment of such deductions accompanied these reports. 

The amount of oil produced but not sold during a given month was now reported to the Comptroller by the producer, and only on such oil did the producer actually make a direct tax-payment. In the event that a purchaser deducted the amount of tax due on a purchase but failed to remit such deduction to the Comptroller, the State collected the required amount of tax from the producer and 28 Tex. Laws 1932, 4th C.S., ch. 2. The constitutionality of this statute was recognized by the Supreme Court in Railroad Commission v. Rowan & Nichols 

Oil Co., 310 U.S. 573 (1940). 

29Tex. Laws, 43rd Leg., 1933, ch. 162. 149

provided a legal course of action for the producer to gain redress from the purchaser who failed to make remission.

The placing of the purchaser of oil in the role of tax-collector seems at first to be a cumbersome procedure. It appears to be more reasonable to have the producer keep records of total production, and remit payment of his production tax directly to the Comptroller. However, this indirect and seemingly circuitous payment method has a practical aspect in the light of conditions now existing. While there are almost 4,000 producers of oil in this State, the bulk of oil produced is purchased by slightly less than 200 purchasers. Reports to the Comptroller are not reduced in number but the auditing of them is greatly simplified by having the purchasers report and pay taxes on the bulk of oil produced, and the producers report and pay taxes on a relatively small remainder..

Another factor which probably influenced the adoption of this collection method was the fact that since 1925 Oklahoma, a leading oil producing State, had an apparently successful tax law in effect which employed the purchaser collection and payment technique, and which apparently influenced other phases of the law, too.

Interpretation of the amount or value of "total oil produced" was written into the law to include all royalty or other interest, and the holders of such royalty interests were made responsible for bearing a portion of the tax burden. These interpretations were challenged in the courts as being in contravention of due process of law, but the courts have upheld the law by stating that it was within the province of the Legislature to provide that royalty owners are engaged in the production of oil and are therefore subject to the occupation tax.30 Penalty provisions for delinquent taxes were made slightly more stringent, and the State's lien for recovery of delinquent taxes was broadened.

Finally a significant statement concerning allocation of taxes derived from the oil production tax appeared for the first time in the tax statute. "One-half of said tax when and as received by the Comptroller shall be paid to the State Treasurer to be placed to the credit of the General Fund of the State.31 The allocation of funds to the public schools is another apparent acceptance of the severance tax principle, for in almost every State where that principle had been openly accepted, major allocations to the public schools had been made. Although as an occupation tax the one-fourth allocation to the schools is required by the Constitution, this 1933 provision giving 50 per cent of the revenues to the schools was the first statutory appearance of this allocation.

The first called sessions of the Legislature in 1933 put the final touches on the oil production tax law of that time by amending the due date for production reports and tax returns from the 15th to the 25th of each month. This 

30 Barwise v. Sheppard, 299 U.S. 33 (1936). 

31 Tex. Laws, 43rd Leg., 1933, op. cit. 150

amendment was made to allow producers and purchasers sufficient time to 32 compile their reports.

Tax Not Applicable to Casinghead Gas 

During the years 1930 to 1935 there appears to have been substantial demand for the clarification of certain parts of the oil tax laws. In 1930 the 

Attorney General wrote an opinion for the State Auditor 33 stating that the oil production tax did not apply to casinghead gas. The Attorney General also stated in an opinion to the Comptroller in 1934 that the oil tax attaches when oil is separated from other substances and is first identified as oil. 

Administrative details were clarified through amendments to the law.35 

Most prominent among them were the inclusion for the first time of a provivision for crediting producers' taxes for a current period with over payments from a previous period, and a provision for six additional supervisors or auditors in the Comptroller's Department.

In 1935, the separate regulation tax on oil was also increased 36 and the tax rate of 3/16 cents per barrel which was levied at that time prevails today. Prior to this time revenue from this regulation tax had been pooled with other funds, but had been reserved for payment of salaries and expenses involved in enforcing the conservation laws relating to oil and gas. Now a special Oil and Gas Enforcement Fund was created to which all revenue received from the regulation tax on oil was credited; and from which, in ace cordance with a detailed statement in the law, allocations were made to the Attorney General's Department, the Railroad Commission, and the Labor, Statistics Department. Such allocations were to cover salaries of persons employed in these departments to enforce conservation laws and labor laws as related to oil and gas. The Oil and Gas Division of the Railroad Commission was greatly increased in size to make possible fuller enforcement of the oil and gas conservation laws. All of the above changes indicate that the principal purpose at the time was to eliminate tax evasions. This is further indicated by the emergency clause under which the amendments to the oil production tax law were passed, which states:

The fact that under existing Statutes the State is losing large sums of money by enormous tax evasions due to the ambiguity and inadequacies of said Act, the fact that by the passage of this bill the Comptroller will be able to ferret out such evasions and enforce the payment of taxes due the 

State, and said tax will be more uniform, create an 37 emergency.

Atty. Gen. to State Auditor, December 22, 1930. 

34 Atty. Gen, to Comptroller of Public Accounts, December 6, 1934. 35 36 

Tex. Laws, 44th Leg., 1935, ch. 353. 

Tex. Laws, 44th Leg., 1935, ch. 245. 

37 Tex. Laws, 44th Leg., 1935, ch. 353. 151

It seems apparent that the occupation tax on oil had by this time been accorded its full stature as a major source of tax income for the State. 

In 1936 the tax rate was raised from two cents per barrel or two per cent of the market value when the price of oil per barrel exceeded one dollar to 2 3/4 cents per barrel or 2 3/4 per cent of the market value when the price of oil exceeded one dollar per barrel.38 Allocation of the tax revenue remained the same, and while the value of oil produced in 1937 was only about 35 per cent greater than the corresponding 1936 figure,39 tax revenues from oil in 1937 increased over those of 1936 by about 60 per cent. 

Rate Change in 1941 

Except for minor administrative changes in 1939, no further revision of the oil tax laws was accomplished until 1941 when once more the tax rate was increased. For several years the price of oil had remained consistently in excess of one dollar per barrel, and the State since 1936 had been collecting a tax of 2 3/4 per cent of the market value of oil. The 1941 revision (included as part of the omnibus tax bill of that year) proposed an increase to 4 1/4 per cent or more, and was attacked bitterly by the oil interests. One newspaper carried the comment: "I doubt if the oil industry is able generally to pay increased taxes and maintain its expansion and development program..' 

Another attack analyzed the entire omnibus tax bill, separating out the gasoline tax, gas processing tax, oil well servicing tax and others related to the oil industry and showed their total to be 47.4 per cent of all taxes to be raised 41 by the bill."

In spite of tremendous pressure the omnibus tax bill, which included the increased tax on oil, was passed and the tax rate was levied as: 

Four and one hundred twenty-five thousandths (4.125) cents per barrel of forty-two (42) standard gallons provided, however, that the occupation tax herein levied on oil shall be four and one hundred twenty-five thousandths (4.125) per cent of the market value of said oil whenever the market value thereof is in excess of One Dollar ($1) per barrel of forty-two (42) standard gallons.42

Tax moneys derived were allocated one-fourth to the Available School Fund and three-fourths to the Omnibus Clearance Fund in the Treasury to be reallocated by the State Treasurer in accordance with detailed provisions of the Act.43 Noteworthy was the return to the one-fourth allocation to the schools. 40

39 

Texas Almanac, 1945-1946, p. 241.

41 The Houston Post, April 21, 1941. 42 

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, ch. 184, Art. 1.

152

The most recent change in the occupation tax on oil occurred in 1950 when an additional tax levy of ten per cent of the then current tax was passed for a restricted period, effective on all oil produced from March 1, 1950 until September 1, 1951.44

In Conclusion 

Throughout the years of its existence, it can be seen that the oil production tax has developed in response to the problems, the changes and particularly the growth of the oil production industry. Most of the administrative changes in the law have reflected the needs of application to the rapidly expanding industry. 

However, throughout the development of this tax law express recognition of a traditionally basic principle of severance taxation -- that some measure of return be made to future generations for the present severance of natural resources has rarely been manifested. It is true that from 

25 to 50 per cent of the revenues have been allocated over the years to the support of the public schools, and that this may be viewed as a recognition of the principle and an implementation of it. But whether this allocation has been made primarily in response to this concept of severance taxation or primarily in response to the Constitutional provision is not clear. In view of the allocations of revenues which have been prescribed in the tax law it seems that this tax has been viewed primarily as a revenue producer. Too, during the entire history of the tax, and particularly since 1932, consideration of the impact of the operation of the conservation and regulation laws upon the revenues from this tax is not apparent in the tax laws. It may be argued with considerable force that the State's interest in conservation and regulation of the petroleum resources and its interest in revenue from crude production are two distinct areas. Hence, any effort to co-ordinate the State's efforts in these two areas would be improper and unworkable, and that any effort to have the administration of the conservation and regulation laws take into account the State's need for tax revenues would be injecting a factor into the conservation effort that is not germane and may be antithetical to it.

On the other hand, it could be urged that the public interest is involved in both areas -- conservation and public finance, that the two areas are related and therefore the State's activities in them should be co-ordinated, at least in some measure. A large part of State income is today dependent upon the amount of oil produced. The amount of production is controlled by regulation performed in the interests of conservation. Such regulation is based on several factors such as national stock levels and others over which the State has very little or no control - but not on the factor of State revenues. This is the serious though perhaps not fatal Achilles heel of the oil production tax from the point of view of State finances. 

44 Tex, Laws, 51st Leg., 1st C.S., 1950, ch. 2, Art. 1. 153

The question of how and for what purposes the efforts to conserve and regulate production may be co-ordinated with the taxation of oil production is a vast, complex and relatively unexplored area. It is particularly significant at this time in view of the extremely rapid expansion of oil production during the past decade. It cannot be ignored in any evaluation of this tax which has grown to be one of the two largest revenue-producers in the 'State's tax structure. 154

SECTION ORGANIZATIONAL FORM 

The current organizational pattern of Texas' tax on oil production was established in 1933, at which time basic changes were made in the then existing tax law. The first oil occupation tax law levied in 1905 gave no single State agency administrative jurisdiction. It simply required that reports be made to the Comptroller of Public Accounts. The 1933 enactment for the first time specifically required that "It shall be the duty of the Comp troller to promulgate rules and regulations for the detail administration of the terms and requirements of this Act not specifically mentioned herein;. . 

This provision gave the Comptroller broad administrative powers that remain in the current oil tax statute.

The Comptroller receives tax payments conqurrently with the reports now required of producers and purchasers. This procedure of production reports and tax payments being made to the same agency appears to be the general practice in the 17 States now levying oil severance taxes. In some States these functions are handled by a tax commission, an oil and gas board, or by a department of revenue, but in almost every case the designated agency receives both reports and payments. 

Administrative Authority Divided 

..45

From the authority given the Comptroller by the statutes, his enforcement function appears to be limited to inspection of records and reports submitted to him. Despite the fact that he is responsible for administration of the law, the Comptroller is not granted express authority to make physical inspections of oil producing leases. Such physical checks are to be made, according to the statutes, by a second State agency, the Railroad Commission. 

The tax law gives any authorized agent of the Railroad Commission the right of free access at any time to oil leases, wells, and transportation media for inspection purposes. Penalties may be imposed on any person who deters the Commission in determining the volume and time of oil production.46 

The basic powers of the Railroad Commission, however, do not stem from the oil tax law, but are stated in an entirely separate portion of the Texas statutes.47 The Commission, specifically the Oil and Gas Division, is granted extensive authority to regulate the production of oil and gas. This grant of inspection powers in connection with the oil taxes might be considered redundant, for almost identical. authority to inspect property and records are included in the Commission's basic powers as stated in the oil and gas conservation laws.48 By virtue of these powers, agents of the Commission are in a position to determine whether actual and reported production, purchase, and shipment are valid. This is only a part of the Commission's larger function, enforcement of all the oil and gas conservation laws of the State of Texas. It is possible that this section of the oil tax law was included as a specific reminder to oil producers that the Commission exercises broad powers of 

45Tex Laws, 43rd Leg., 1933, ch. 162, sec. 9. 

46 Tex. Pen. Code (Vernon, 1948) Art. 11la, sec. 9a. 

47.

Tex. Civ. Stat. (Vernon, 1948) Title 102. 

48 Tex. Civ. Stat. (Vernon, 1948) Art. 6049c, sec. 5. 155

regulation and inspection.. The Commission is not mentioned in any other section of the tax law.

Significantly, however, there is no requirement for co-ordination between the Comptroller's Department and the Commission, nor is there a statement in the oil tax law which implies that co-operation between, and common effort exerted by, these two agencies is essential to the effective enforcement of the oil taxes. Moreover, there is no indication in actual practice that liaison exists between the office of the Comptroller and the office of the Commission. Rather the indication is that the Comptroller enforces tax payment and the Commission enforces conservation measures, each operating virtually independent of the other. Statutory statements assign each agency its duties, but do not clearly enunciate all elements of administrative policy. This results in a hiatus between functions of the 

Comptroller and those of the Commission for which solution must be found by administrative determination if the laws are to be enforced with maximum efficiency.

Though charged with total responsibility for administering the oil tax laws, the limitation of the Comptroller to an inspection of producers' and purchasers' records and a check of their reports necessitates an assumption by him that those reports are, in fact, in accordance with actual production of oil. The gap in organization immediately raises a question: Who ascertains that actual production and disposition agrees with that reported?

At first glance it would seem that the Commission and its agents fill that gap by making physical inspection to determine agreement between reported and actual production. However, the Commission requires that separate production reports be made to it and does not use as the basis for its field inspection the reports made to the Comptroller. Thus it is possible that variance between production reported to the Comptroller and that reported to the Commission would go unnoticed since, as has already been mentioned, no day-to-day liaison between these two agencies exists in practice.

Other Administrative Agencies 

The Attorney General is a third agent in the organizational structure of the oil tax laws. He is specifically granted authority to exercise his nor mal function of bringing suit against delinquent taxpayers and against persons who refuse to comply with provisions of the law.49 In addition the Attorney 

General is authorized to inspect at any time all records and reports which the oil tax laws require producers, purchasers, royalty interests, and carriers of oil to maintain.50 The implication seems to be that the Attorney General plays a part in the administration of the tax law in addition to his usual function. There is no indication that he has ever actually made any inspections of the records.

Finally, sheriffs, rangers, and other peace officeres are given a part to play in the enforcement of the oil tax laws. Written into the tax laws 

49 Tex. Civ. Stat. (Vernon, 1948), Art. 7057a, sec. 7 and sec. 7a. 

50Ibid., sec. 3. 156

during the "hot oil" days, this provision still remains in the statute although there is no record nor recollection by administrators that it has ever been invoked in recent years. In the event that these officers discover any oil on which taxes have not been properly paid, they have the authority not only to levy on such oil for taxes due, but may, after due notice, hold a public auction to sell the oil in order to recover taxes due.51 Although the powers of these officers are completely described there is no statutory. indication as to how they may determine whether or not taxes have been paid on a given volume of oil. 

51Ibid, sec. 6. 157

SECTION - ASSESSMENT 

The present occupation taxes on oil are levied on the total number of barrels of oil produced or salvaged from the earth or waters of this State.52 

All producers of oil are subject to the tax and holders of royalty interests are included as producers.

Three distinct statutory provisions levy the current taxes on oil production. There is no actual assessment of tax amounts made by the State,, and no tax bills are presented to the persons subject to the taxes. In short, the taxes are self-assessed and self-computed by purchasers and producers of oil. In purchasing oil, a purchaser deducts from the price paid to the producer the proper tax amount which he has computed at the time of purchase. 

This is done for all three oil taxes at the same time. At the end of each month he reports and remits such deductions to the Comptroller. The producers determine the actual market value of and compute the taxes due on oil produced but not sold during a given reporting period. In this connection. the law provides that " the market value of oil shall be the actual market value thereof plus any bonus or premiums or other things of value paid therefor or which such oil will reasonably bring if produced in accordance with the laws, rules, and regulations of the State of Texas. '53These taxes are then remitted to the Comptroller with the producer's reports. Adjustments for prorating taxes to royalty interests and for exempting from taxation royalty payments to the State are provided for in reporting procedures, and will be discussed in connection with tax collection later in this report. Rates 

The basic tax on oil is levied at the rate of 4.125 cents per barrel of 42 standard gallons when the price of oil is one dollar per barrel or less, and 4.125 per cent of the market value of oil if in excess of one dollar per barrel. This is the tax which originated in 1905 and has grown through the years from one per cent of the gross value of oil produced, through two per cent, and then 2.75 per cent, to the present rate. The second tax is in actuality an extension of the basic tax and is levied as an additional 10 per cent of it to apply to all oil produced during the period March 1, 1950 to 

September 1, 1951.54 The third is the regulation tax which originated in 

1917 and is employed to provide funds for the Railroad Commission's use in enforcement of the oil and gas conservation laws. It constitutes an additional levy on the producer of oil of 3/16 cents per barrel of oil produced.5 55 Though this regulation tax is not designed to provide revenue for use in the same manner as the oil occupation taxes, it is collected with those taxes and hence is included in this discussion. 

52 TEX. CIV. STAT (Vernon, 1948), Art. 7057a, sec. 2. 

53Ibid., sec. 1. 

54 Tex. Laws 1950, 51st Leg., 1st C.S., ch. a, Art. 1. 

55 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon, 1948) Art. 6032. 

158

Assessment Procedures 

The Comptroller requires producers and purchasers to submit monthly reports which cover total oil production and its disposition through sale or storage during the preceding month. In accordance with the law, a large part of the taxes paid each month are remitted to the Comptroller by the purchasers of oil, who deduct taxes from the purchase price paid the producer, hold such deductions until the end of a reporting period, and then remit taxes thus collected to the State with their respective purchase reports. Approximately 95 per cent of all oil taxes are so paid. Direct payment is made to the Comptroller by the producer only on the remaining five per cent oil which is produced and stored but not sold during a given reporting period. In accordance with the measurement procedures described below, oil is not considered produced, for taxation purposes, until it leaves the lease tanks..

From the manner in which the tax is paid, it appears to be levied against the purchaser of oil. However, the purchaser acts merely as a taxcollector and the law states that "the tax hereby levied shall be a liability of the producer of oil"56 and that "in no event shall the producer be relieved of responsibility for the tax until same shall have been paid."57 

Since the producer is so emphatically held responsible for tax payments, why is the payment procedure made indirect by placing the purchaser in the role of tax collector? The apparent reason bears repetition. Almost 4,000 producers of oil are currently operating in this State, but the bulk of oil produced is purchased by slightly less than 200 purchasers. Thus, by requiring purchasers to deduct taxes from the purchase price, report such purchases, and remit deductions to the Comptroller monthly, the analysis and audit of reports and returns is greatly simplified.

Measurement of Production 

The production of oil is measured in barrels, and the volume thus measured, minus the amount of oil used to pay exempt royalty interests, constitutes the amount of taxable oil. The actual point of measurement is not stipulated in the law which states only that a producer of oil shall not allow any oil produced to pass from his control to the control of another person without an accurate measurement.58 A brief description of the oil production process seems appropriate at this point, therefore, in order to outline the procedure used in the measurement of production volume for tax purposes. 

On a particular producing lease several wells may be operating, and production from these wells is piped to a common "lease tank." It is here at the lease tank, rather than at the individual well, that the measurement of total production is made. Purchases of oil are made from these lease tanks. 

The purchaser measures the volume of oil removed from the lease tank and after measurement, runs that oil to a "terminal tank" in which oil from several lease tanks is commingled. At the time of its removal from the 

56Ibid., sec. 2 (2). 

57Ibid., sec. 2 (4). 

58TEX. PEN. CODE, (Vernon, 1948) Art. 1111a, sec. 9b. 

159

lease tank the allowable deduction for "basic sediment and water, "59 which remains suspended in the oil, and a mathematical correction which adjusts for volume changes resulting from the difference between the actual temperature at the time of removal and the standard temperature of 60 degrees Fahrenheit are made.60

A large part of the basic sediment and water which was suspended in the oil when produced settles out at the bottom of the lease tank prior to the sale of oil. Periodically these settlings are removed from the tank by tank cleaning agents and are re-processed for their crude oil content. Oil extracted in this manner is known as "tank bottom oil." It is of particular interest at this time because of a court decision.61 which has ruled that "tank bottom oil” is not subject to tax, being a part of the basic sediment and water which is legally deducted from production totals. However, sizeable volumes of oil are obtained as "tank bottom oil." Though not taxed those volumes constitute merchantable oil and are reported to the Railroad Commission as part of the allowable production of the producer from whose tanks the settlings were obtained. 

Volume of oil purchased is again measured at the terminal tank and in the event that such volume is greater than the aggregate volume of runs, the Attorney General has ruled that such oil is taxable and the operator of the gathering system is liable for payment.62 On the other hand, if terminal tank volume is less than the volume of purchase runs, no deduction for tax purposes is allowed. The law specifically states that no deductions will be allowed for average or loss in handling oil during its processing after initial measurement. .

Should the producer of oil elect to store a portion of the oil produced on a given lease, such oil is measured when removed from the lease tank and is run to a "storage tank." Procedures and adjustments in measurement correspond to the procedures outlined in the two preceding paragraphs. 

In the event of an excess in the storage tank over the aggregate of storage runs, the producer is held liable for tax on the larger volume: Since all oil produced is run from lease tanks either to terminal tanks or to storage tanks, the sum of the adjusted volumes of purchase runs and storage runs becomes the total production figure on which the tax is based. 

Royalty Interests as Producers

The law expressly provides that the oil taxes levied shall be borne ratably by all interested parties, including royalty.interests.64 Taxation of royalty interests was attacked in the courts on the grounds that application of the tax to such interests was in contravention of due process of law as guaranteed by the Fourteenth Amendment.65 The validity of the law was upheld, the ruling being that, as interested parties, royalty interests could be considered as producers of oil and as such subject to the tax. 

59Ibid., sec. 1 (8) 60Ibid. 

61State v. J.A. Stack, 199 S.W. 2d 701 (1947). 621bid. 

63 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon, 1948), Art. 7057a, sec. 1 (8). 

64Ibid., sec. 2 (6). 

65 Barwise v. Sheppard, 299 U.S. 33, 57 S Ct. 70 (1936). 160

Only one exception is made to the rule of holding royalty interests responsible for a share of the tax burden. The law directs that if the State of Texas or any political subdivision thereof (such as cities, school districts, eleemosynary institutions) or the University of Texas hold a royalty interest, such interest is not subject to the tax.66 Moreover, producers are allowed to deduct from total production the amount of oil used to pay such royalties prior to computation of taxes due. This in effect is an exemption of those amounts of oil from taxation. The policy of allowing producers to deduct amounts of oil used for payments of State held royalties prior to tax computation stems from Sheppard v. Stanolind Oil and Gas Co. 67 The substance of the court's decision was that the producer could not be required to pay taxes on oil used in payment of royalties to inerests which are exempted from tax responsibility. In certain other occupation tax levies, exemptions are made for certain classes of the products taxed or for certain circumstances which attend the processing of the product taxed. This is not the case with oil, the single exemption being that described. However, as has been previously mentioned, because the statute omits mention of "tank bottom' oil, it has been ruled as not taxable, simply a part of the basic sediment and water which is an allowable deduction. Thus, the oil extracted from the "B.S.&W."' is not taxed, although in 1939 the Attorney General opined that such oil was 68 to be taxed. 

66 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon, 1948), Art. 7057a, sec. 1 (12). 

67 125 S.W. 2d. 643 (Tex. Civ. App., 1939, writ refused). 68 

Opinion of the Attorney General to Criminal District Attorney, Longview, No. 0-1320, October 14, 1939 and State v. J.A. Stack, 199 S.W. 2d 701 

(1947). 161

SECTION COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Collection

Collection of the taxes on oil production is handled for the State by the Comptroller's Department. Responsibility for determining that taxes are being properly paid rests with him. On the 25th day of each month the 

Comptroller receives purchasers' and producers' reports and their tax remittances covering total oil production for the preceding month. The Comptroller has taken advantage of the common basis for computation of all three of the current taxes on oil, namely, the total number of barrels of oil produced. As a result, only one monthly report is required from each producer and each purchaser which encompasses the necessary information for the collection and payment of all taxes due. Thus the two revenue producing taxes and the separate regulation tax are collected at the same time. Reports

The purchaser's monthly report to the Comptroller shows the total number of barrels of oil purchased during the preceding month on which taxes had not already been paid, the specific source of each purchase, and the price paid therefor.69 From these items the 4.125 per cent tax, the temporary ten per cent tax and the regulation tax are computed. A single remittance for all three taxes, the total of all amounts deducted from pur chase prices by the purchaser, accompanies the report. 

In addition to acting as a tax-collector for the State, the purchaser in most instances assumes one of the producer's responsibilities that of paying to royalty interests their proportionate shares of the purchase price. 

Having agreed on a purchase price with the producer, the purchaser deducts the full amount of taxes due, and then divides the resulting net value into the producer's share and proportionate shares for each royalty interest concerned. Direct payment is normally made to each royalty interest by the purchaser, rather than by the producer operating the lease. 

Oil which is produced but not sold during the month of production is classified as oil stored, and on such oil the producer pays the taxes directly to the Comptroller. The producer's monthly report to the Comptroller is based on the total number of barrels of oil produced during the preceding month. The total number of barrels of oil produced and sold is accounted for in a detailed breakdown showing each purchaser of oil, the number of barrels purchased, and the value of each purchase.70 The remainder of total production is, then, the total number of barrels of oil stored, and its value is stated in the report. On the basis of this value the producer computes each of the taxes and remits the total amount due the Comptroller at the time the report is submitted. 

69TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon, 1948) Art. 7057a, sec. 3 (3). 

70Ibid, sec. 3 (2). 162

In the event that a portion of the value of oil produced is paid as a tax exempt royalty,71 an additional report is required. This report is a statement of royalty interest on which no tax is paid. It is submitted with the purchaser's report which records the sale of such oil, or with the producer's report which records the storage of such oil, The inclusion of this report permits an accounting of 100 per cent production, 100 per cent disposition, and a justification of the amount of taxes paid thereon by producers and purchasers.

Enforcement The Comptroller's department has in these three reports sufficient information to make a complete audit of all tax returns. However, it should be remembered that this audit does not in any way extend to a comparison of reports with actual production on the leases. 

A comparison of total annual production reports for the fiscal years 1946 through 1950 reveals that different totals are reported by the Comptroller, who is the tax collecting officer, and by the Railroad Commission which is the conservation agency and which actually makes physical checks on production. The Comptroller's totals have been consistently greater (during these years) than the corresponding totals reported by the Commission, but the difference has always been less than one per cent of the total reported by the Commission. This is a relatively small variance and apparantly no determination of the factors which cause it has been made. However, should the variance swing in the other direction, with the 

Comptroller receiving the smaller reported production figures, even this small percentage of unreported, and hence untaxed, oil might result in the State's loss of several hundred thousand dollars in annual tax revenue. 

Statutory provision is made for an audit of production and tax reports, and such audit is carried on continuously as the monthly reports are submitted. Each report is checked for accuracy of computation and adequacy of the tax remitted. At approximately bi-monthly intervals, the reports of producers and purchasers are compared, and any discrepancies which are revealed between reported production and reported purchases are investigated first by letter to the delinquent taxpayer and later, if necessary, by application of the Comptroller's more stringent "police powers." 

This cross-check is the Comptroller's primary means of verifying 100 per cent production of oil and the adequacy of tax returns. Discrepancies between the reports reveal failures to report purchases and also variance in reported prices. In the event that a letter to the producer in error does not result in submission of delinquent reports or the correction of errors in reported purchase price, the Comptroller may prohibit further removal of oil from the lease involved.

Records In addition to monthly reports, producers must maintain records of production including the location of leases from which produced, the number 

71See State Tax Reporter, Texas, Commerce Clearing House, vol. 1, p. 4521 para. 45-021 163

of barrels produced, the amount sold and to whom, the price received therefor, and the amount stored, and its location. There is no time limit after which these records may be discarded, but they must be open to ins spection by the Attorney General, the Comptroller, or their authorized agents.

Original and subsequent purchasers of oil are also required to keep records. These records must show the origin of all oil purchased as well as the disposition of all oil sold or used. The Attorney General and Comptroller may also inspect these records. A combination of these records with those of producers shows a detailed breakdown of all oil from production until it enters the refining process. 

Although they do not make reports to the Comptroller, royalty' owners are required by law to keep records indicating all moneys received from each lease in which they hold interest, the number of barrels of oil from which royalty was received, and the amount of tax deducted from royalty payment.72 Carriers of oil are also required to keep records of all oil handled each month, showing the date received, the number of barrels,from whom recieved, point of delivery, to whom delivered, and the manner of transportation.73 Royalty owners' records are open to inspection by the 

Comptroller, the Attorney General, or their authorized agents. Carriers' records are subject to the same inspection, and reports as needed may be required of carriers by the Comptroller. Through these records additional checks on production, disposition, and tax payment would be possible, but according to the tax administrators no inspections of them are made. 

Police Powers

As mentioned above, the Comptroller has authority to forbid further removal of oil from leases until such time as reports are in order.74 This restriction is tantamount to prohibiting production and is the usual means by which the Comptroller enforces the reporting of oil production. Producer and purchaser are notified that no further purchases can be made from the lease concerned until the delinquency in reports has been corrected. 

In almost every case such notification brings immediate results. 

Extensive powers for examination of producers', purchasers', royalty owners', and carriers' records are also granted to the Comptroller. 

Such examinations are carried out by field auditors who periodically audit the records of purchasers, and, to some extent, of producers at their home offices. Primary attention is devoted to purchasers and producers whose offices are in this State. Out-of-State purchasers receive, attention as time permits. The Comptroller has no specific authority to make physical checks, at the place of production, of oil produced and sold during a given period, and no inspections of this sort are made by his agents. Such checks are made, as previously discussed, by the Railroad Commission but are independent of the work of the Comptroller. 

72 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon, 1948) Art. 7057a, sec. 3 (6) 

731bid., sec. 3 (7). 

741bid., sec. 8a. 164

In analyzing the "police powers" by which enforcement of the oil production tax is accomplished, perhaps the broadest of such provisions is that which grants the State a prior and preferred lien on virtually all of the products, equipment, and property of oil producers and persons made liable for the tax, for recovery of taxes due.75 This provision specifically states that such property is not exempt from forced sale to provide funds with which to pay taxes. However, experience has shown that only in rare instances has this lien been enforced by the Comptroller and a tax sale held. Requests to the Attorney General to bring suit for the recovery of taxes are also rare.

Penalties 

Penalty provisions for delinquent tax payments have also been written into the oil tax laws which provide the Comptroller with another means of enforcing the proper payment of taxes in the amounts and within the time limits prescribed.76 These penalties are imposed on purchasers and the producers in the event that their reports are submitted to the Comptroller after the due date.

In most cases producers and purchasers assess these penalties themselves, and the forms used for their regular monthly reports include a section in which penalties may be computed. In the event that the Comp troller's Department determines that reports and taxes have not been submitted, have been improperly computed, or have not included the proper penalty payments, he may require the delinquent purchaser or producer to make adjustments. Should the delinquent taxpayer fail to comply with the

Comptroller's request for adjustment, the Comptroller may then bring the Attorney General into the enforcement phase by requesting him to bring suit against the delinquent taxpayer for recovery of taxes past due.

Administrative Fund 

To pay the expenses of administrative duties in connection with the oil production tax, the Comptroller is authorized a fund of one-half of one per cent of the gross tax revenues received. Salaries of personnel and expenses for necessary office supplies and equipment are paid from this fund. As has been previously mentioned, personnel paid from this fund are also utilized to assure collection and enforcement of several other taxes handled by the Gross Receipts Division. Earmarking

While disbursement of tax revenues is not an integral part of the collection and enforcement phase of the tax administration, the pattern of disbursement is included in the statutes and is of considerable significance. 

A discussion of the oil tax laws would not be complete without consideration of the ultimate disposition of revenues received from this source. 

75Ibid., sec. 8. 

761bid., sec. 5. 165

Because of the importance of the oil severance tax to State tax revenue and the general tendency to identify oil taxes with tax support of the School, Old Age Assistance, and General Revenue Funds, (and because of the current problem concerning the lack of sufficient revenue in the General Revenue Fund), it seems appropriate to discuss briefly the distribution of oil taxes to and from the various disbursement funds. It must be remembered, however, that except for the primary distribution of collections to three funds, which is the distribution stated in the tax statute, it is virtually impossible to fully trace the ultimate disbursement or expenditure of these tax moneys..

Revenue received from the basic oil severance tax (the 4.125 per cent levy together with the additional 10 per cent temporary levy) is alloe cated in the following manner: One-half of one per cent of the gross amount received is allocated to the office of the Comptroller to cover the expense of administering the tax. Detailed expenditures from this enforcement fund are fixed by the Legislature in the General Appropriation Bill.77 The remainder of the gross amount is distributed to two funds, one-fourth to the Available School Fund and three-fourths to the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund.78 This is the distribution set up in the tax law. 

Money is disbursed from the Available School Fund under the geno eral supervision of the State Board of Education and the State Commissioner of Education according to several statutes.79 Of course, oil occupation taxes are not the only source of revenue made available for the public free school program of the State, for, as required by the Constitution, the program receives one-fourth of all occupation taxes.80 As shall be pointed out, however, the oil production tax does furnish a substantial share of such revenue. During the last few years this tax has also furnished about 50 per cent of all receipts deposited in the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund from which revenue is distributed to a number of funds according to complex and changing statutory priorities.81

When the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund was originally created in 1941, it was provided that distribution would be made from the fund in the follow ing order: (1) the Blind Assistance, (2) Children's Assistance, (3) Teacher's 

Retirement System, and (4) Old Age Assistance Funds. Any remaining moneys were to be distributed to the General Revenue Fund.82 In 1941 and 

1942 the Old Age Assistance Fund and the General Revenue Fund received approximately 80 per cent of the total deposited in the Clearance Fund, with the Old Age Assistance Fund receiving slightly more than the General Revenue Fund. In 1949 these two funds received about 85 per cent of the total 

77Ibid., sec. 9. 

781bid., sec. 2. 

79Ibid., 1950 Supp↓ Art. 2823, 2827, and 2922-16. 

80Constitution of the State of Texas, Art. VII, sec. 3. 

81 TEX. CIV. STAT, (Vernon), Art. 7083a, as amended. 

82 Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, ch. 184, Art. XX. 

166

with the General Revenue Fund getting over 78 million dollars or more than twice the amount distributed to the Old Age Assistance Fund.83 

For the fiscal year 1950, however, there was a substantial change in the pattern of distribution of revenue from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund. In addition to the above-mentioned priority allocations to four funds the Legislature provided for a distribution of 15 million dollars per annum to the Farm-to-Market Road Fund, about 10 million dollars to the State Hospital Fund, and allocation in "such an amount as is determined by the 

Foundation School Fund Budget Committee" to the Foundation School Pro gram Fund.84 These changes in the distribution pattern and in priorities, and the addition of two funds to receive large amounts of revenue from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund resulted in the distribution of only 19.4 million dollars to the General Revenue Fund during the fiscal year 1950. And even this amount was a bookkeeping distribution, for some 26 million dollars were in fact transferred from the General Revenue Fund during the year to finance the free school program which had been unable to meet expenses from the normal allocation of one-fourth of all occupation taxes, the additional amounts provided from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund, and other sources.

Review of the above information and examination of the Comptroller's Annual Reports for 1949 and 1950 indicate that the oil severance tax, in 1949, accounted for about 24 million dollars or approximately 27 per cent of the revenue deposited in the Available School Fund and about 39 million dollars or approximately 48 per cent of that deposited in the General Revenue Fund. 

In 1950, however, it appears that the tax accounted for about 38.5 million dollars deposited in the Available School Fund and only 8.9 million in the 

General Revenue Fund. The allocation to the General Revenue Fund is mis leading and does not represent a net or exact amount, for, it must be recalled, some 26 million dollars were transferred from the fund into the school fund during the year. It is this and other complex interchanges of collections which make it impossible to identify the ultimate recipient of oil tax moneys. 

In view of the changing pattern of disbursements from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund the following observations seem appropriate. Neither the past surplus nor the approaching dearth of revenue in the General Revenue Fund is explained by reference to the oil severance tax. Neither the administration of the tax nor flucuating receipts from this source of revenue explain the existing condition of the General Revenue Fund.

ANALYSIS OF OPERATION Administrative Costs 

Some understanding of the operation of the practice of providing the funds for the administration of the tax through allocation of a portion of tax collections for that purose may be obtained through an examination of the table set out below. The oil production tax laws allocate to the administrative fund one-half of one per cent of total collections; the proceeds of this go into an administrative fund. Because the fund is subject to legislative appropriation, the tax collector is limited in use of the fund to that amount appropriated for tax administration purposes.

Only in the period 1940-43 did appropriations approach the percentage written into the tax law. However, the amounts appropriated were not provided for administration and enforcement of the oil production tax alone but, in accordance with appropriation acts, were for the entire Gross Receipts 

Tax Division. 

This division is responsible for some seventeen taxes, of which the two largest revenue producers are the oil and gas taxes. Since some 85 per cent of the time and effort of this division is devoted to these two taxes the major portion of the amounts appropriated were expended for administration and enforcement of these two taxes. In terms of revenue/cost ratios, the cost of administering the oil production tax is small. There is, however, no means under present procedures, whereby this cost may be isolated from other expenses of the Gross Receipts Division. 1 168

The total staff of the Gross Receipts Division numbers 40 people. 

Twenty-six of this number constitute the office force of the division, and the remaining 14 compose the field force. It should be recalled that about 85 per cent of the time spent by the entire division is devoted to the administration of the oil and gas taxes. Only seven members of the office, the clerks who post the oil tax reports, devote all of their time to the oil tax. 

The remainder have duties related to the oil tax and also to the other gross receipts taxes. The 14-man field force operates in the seven enforcement districts over the State. These men are auditors and are primarily concerned with the gas and oil taxes, but also have duties connected with all of the gross receipts taxes. One auditor is assigned to each district except the 

Houston district. Because of the concentration of the oil industry in that area, a staff of eight, one senior auditor and seven junior auditors, operates there. Rates

Seventeen States at present levy taxes on the production of oil. Thirteen of these collect taxes designed to produce "revenue," while four collect taxes designed only to finance conservation activities. Of the thirteen 

States which have revenue measures in effect, eight also levy taxes for conservation expenses.

Only two States specify fixed or flat rates "per barrel, "' while seven determine tax due upon the basis of some aspect of "value," two employ both measurements, and two provide for the use of either one or the other. 

Texas is one of the latter two States providing a rate of 4.125 cents per 42 gallon barrel or 4.125 per cent of market value when it is in excess of $1.00 per barrel.

The tax rates based upon value range from one-half of one per cent in Kentucky to six per cent in Alabama and Mississippi. The flat rates per barrel range from 1/8 cents in Michigan and Oklahoma (both States also collect a per cent of value tax) to 26 cents in Louisiana.

The Texas rate is higher than that in five States and lower than that in the remaining seven; thus it appears in about the middle of the rate scale. 

The Louisiana rate is highest. The present average market value of Texas oil is in the neighborhood of $2.57 per barrel; using this value as a basis, the average revenue per barrel in Texas is about 16 cents. This amount is slightly less than the minimum tax in Louisiana and almost 40 per cent less than the maximum.

Levies designed to defray the expense of administering conservation statutes vary considerably from State to State as to basis and amount. Some are levied at a flat rate per barrel, some upon value, others at a flat rate per well, and still others at combined rates. The position of Texas' rate in this scale is difficult to evaluate, but of the seven rates based upon barrels produced, Texas is third highest, surpassed by Montana and Oregon. 169
