Returns

The tremendous margin enjoyed by Texas production over other oil-producing States is reflected in a comparison of gross oil tax revenue received. In 1947, the latest year for which comparative figures are available, Texas' oil tax revenues were over three times as great as those of Louisiana, the State receiving the second largest revenue. However, during the same year, Texas oil production was five times greater than that of Louisiana.

During the forty-five years of its existence, the Texas occupation tax on oil production has become one of the State's leading revenue-producing measures. In 1906, the first full year in which the tax was collected, total oil production was 12,567,897 barrels valued at $655,578.85 With a tax rate of two per cent of average market value, Texas received $101,403 in tax reve- 86 nue. By way of contrast, total Texas oil production during fiscal 1949 was 

796,123,905 barrels valued at $2,030,155,958.87 With a tax of 4.125 per cent of value, revenue in that year was $87,435,984.

Dependence of Revenues on Out-of-State Factors 

Perhaps as significant as any other matter related to the oil production tax and the revenues derived from it is the consideration of the actual and potential effect of the actions of the Railroad Commission in setting production allowables. On the basis of surveillance of numerous factors which pertain to the petroleum industry, the Texas Railroad Commission establishes and limits the amount of oil which may be produced in this State. These factors include national stock levels, available storage, market demand as indicated by Bureau of Mines reports and by purchaser "nominations," and the maximum volumes which can be produced efficiently in particular fields 88 Only the last mentioned factor is determined upon the basis of circumstances lying wholly within Texas boundaries. Despite the fact that Texas production leads the nation, production activities in other States materially affect the national stock level. Texas has no control on oil production in other States materially affect the national stock level. Texas has no control on oil production in other States. Regulation of Texas production does, however, bring about control of national stock levels; and on occasions Texas has borne a disproportionate share of production restrictions to reduce national stocks to satisfactory levels.

In connection with market demand and purchaser "nominations," the reducing effect upon such nominations of increased production in other States, and for that matter, in other parts of the world, are readily discernible. 

Questions concerning the possibility of continued competition of Texas oil 85 

Texas Almanac, 1945-46, p. 241 

86 Petroleum Data Book, Petroleum Engineee Publishing Co., Dallas, Texas, 1948, p. K-2. 

87 The Texas Petroleum Industry and State Finances, Texas Legislative Coun cil, Austin, Texas, 1950, Table XXVIII, opposite p. 14. 

88Ibid., chaps. IV-VI. 

170

wells producing hundreds of barrels daily or less with Iranian wells producing thousands of barrels daily has already been raised by certain segments of the oil industry, and perhaps should not be totally ignored as barren of future implications. Imports of oil from foreign countries might increase to such a degree that market demand for Texas oil would be se... verely curtailed. In this event, would that market demand remain a valid criteria for production control?

It is true that Texas can "cross these bridges" as the need arises, but in any event it should be recognized that such bridges exist. 171

SECTION. 6  

Having surveyed the subject in the preceding sections, it seems now in order to point up several significant factors concerning the Texas oil production tax laws, and mention, where possible, future implications. The following areas appear worthy of consideration: 

1. Coordination of the activities of the Comptroller and the 

Railroad Commission. 

2. The influence of out-of-State factors on revenues from the oil tax. 

3. Relation of oil tax revenues and funds in the General 

Revenue Fund. 

4. Exemption from tax of State royalty interests. 

5. "Tank bottom" oil 

6. A few apparently obsolete statutory provisions. 

The first three of these areas are broad in their implications and extend far beyond the scope of the tax laws, and thus can only be dealt with partially through the tax statutes. The other three are relatively minor matters of policy determination, which affect to a certain extent the ease of tax administration and the amount of revenues received. 

Coordination of Agencies

A reading of the statutes reveals that the law does not clearly define the extent of the Comptroller's authority, nor does it spell out a complete picture of the function of the Railroad Commission in relation to administration of the tax law. The coordination of the activities of these two agencies is apparently not considered by the tax laws, for the statutes in no way intimate the necessity of cooperation between them. Moreover, in practice there is little or no dependence of one agency on the other, and but little information is exchanged between them. The Comptroller collects the tax and makes his audits; the Commission ascertains that the actual production is as reported; but each gathers and uses its own information and only limited crosschecks are made.

The Comptroller in determining adequacy of tax returns, and in determining that all production is being reported is dependent on the ducers' and purchasers' reports submitted to him. A comparison of these pro- 172

reports is his means of determining who is producing and who purchasing oil and whether all oil is reported for tax purposes. This does not prevent the possibility, however infrequent, of unreported production and purchase of oil. At the same time, the Commission maintains records of all wells initiated, all oil producers, and all oil purchasers; information which could be of value to the Comptroller.

The present organization and method, of course, is not to be considered ineffective. Its functioning has been relatively free of difficulty for the administrators, and there is little indication that there has been any appreciable loss of revenue at any time to the State. However, in order to clarify the functions of the two major agencies enumerated in the tax stauttes, any one of several courses could be taken. 

First, the mention of the Railroad Commission might be deleted from the tax statutes. As has been seen no practical purpose is now served by the enunciation of powers of the Commission in the tax law. Precedent for such deletion can be found in the gas production tax law, which in earlier years contained similar provisions with regard to the Commission, but from which in recent years these provisions were removed. The Comptroller, if such action were taken, would then remain as the sole administrator according to the tax law.

Secondly, in conjunction with the above or even without such action, there might be added to the tax laws a requirement of complete interchange of the reports of the two agencies, with a reconciliation of the records of the two agencies being the ultimate goal. As has been pointed out in earlier sections, such reconciliation is not required nor made at present. From a management and procedural point of view, there appears to be considerable merit in the suggestion that a careful scrutiny of the present reporting methods used by both agencies be made. A study of the reports required and reporting procedures used, made with the thought in mind of adapting the reports and procedures to automatic punch-card operations might well reveal a means of evolving one set of records which would adequately serve the needs of both the Comptroller and the Railroad Commission. 

Thirdly, the field force of the Railroad Commission might be given the function of checking, in the field, the production records and reports received by the Comptroller, as needed, thus serving both agencies, and filling an inspection gap now existing in the enforcement powers of the Comptroller. This procedure was prescribed in the natural gas tax laws. until 1941, when it was discontinued. 1 173

Vulnerability of Revenues 

It is already an accepted fact that the Railroad Commission in setting the monthly production allowable takes into consideration the national oil picture in terms of market demand, and may be influenced by the world scene in this regard. This, in short, means that an important factor in the control of production lies beyond the control of the State of Texas. If pro- duction in Texas reaches a point of stability, this outside factor will come to have an increased effect upon Texas' production relative to production in other parts of the nation and the world, upon the demand for Texas' oil relative to the demand for oil from other oil producing areas, and hence upon the price which can be obtained for oil produced in Texas. The possibility of a general decline in oil production and in sale price of oil because of the factors outlined above should be borne in mind. Such a decline would result in a decline in the revenue which the State gains from this tax revenue source. In other words, this important part of the State tax structure is at least in an extreme circumstance subject to considerable deflation or even virtual elimination which present local measures would be unable to prevent. 

Should oil development in other parts of the nation increase their importance, or should oil developments in Iran, South America, or Russia take on greater world prominence the present predominance of Texas' oil would be reduced, and this could occur to such an extent that the tax revenues of the State might be seriously damaged. If this occurs, Texas would be in need of other tax revenue sources to replace the revenue which would no longer be gained from the oil tax.

What might be done to bolster the State's defenses against such a reduction? First, since one of the functions of the, Railroad Commission is to evaluate the foreseeable future of the oil industry locally, nationally, and on a world-wide basis, it seems likely that the Commissions estimates of the future in terms of production and price would be of value to the Comptroller in estimating State income and to the Board of Control or the Legisla tive Budget Board in making future budget recommendations, and to the Legislature. Such a procedure would not prevent the occurence of changes in the economic future of Texas oil, but it would serve the purpose of increasing the awareness of impending changes. 

Second, as a more direct preventive measure a "floor" could be placed in Texas oil production below which the Commission could not reduce allowables without consulting other State agencies which would be affected. This raises issues far beyond the scope of this survey in other fields of State administration than taxation, and no attempt has been made to determine the methods or procedures which might be utilized to accomplish such a control nor to examine its implications. 174

Finally, as an immediately applicable and at the same time lasting measure, the "severance tax" principle might be emphasized by utilizing present oil tax revenues less for current expenses and more for projects of a permanent nature which would be of greater benefit to Texans of future generations. A maximum might be placed on the amount of oil tax revenue permissible for current use. Any revenue over and above such a maximum could be used to effect long range plans, or could be placed in a reservoir to be used when future revenues dwindle significantly. If current collections are maintained for use as is desired now, but all future collections above the present level are set aside for permanent projects rather than for current needs, a start can be made toward the application of the severance tax principle. This would mean simply that the possibility of sizable decreases in oil revenues in the future is recognized, and that precautions are taken to prevent such an eventuality from crippling regular operations of the State. 

General Revenue Fund 

Contrary to the belief that is held by some individuals, an increase in oil tax revenues will not increase to a proportionate degree the balance in the General Revenue Fund. The existing dearth of monies in the General 

Revenue Fund with which to meet general expenses and expected civil defense appropriations is not the direct result of faulty tax collecting statutes, oil or otherwise, nor of ineffective collecting procedures, nor of decreased oil severance tax collections, but rather is the result of tax disbursing statutes establishing disbursement practice wherein the earmarking of monies, together with Constitutional priority provisions, have decimated the balance and captured the current receipts of the fund. 

Causative factors involved are: (1) all the revenue requirements of the several funds are now accorded higher priority than the General Revenue Fund within the distribution of monies from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund, and (2) the Constitution provides that deficits in public free school funds, which are the result of occupation taxes in general not furnishing sufficient revenue, "may be met by appropriation from the general funds of the State." The State has created so many priority funds and "must" projects which are financed from revenue sources formerly accruing to the General Revenue Fund that this fund, standing at the end of the line, so to speak, now receives very little of the monies which formerly were debited to it, normal oil tax collections and effective tax collection procedures notwithstanding. 175

Tax Exempt Royalties 

A more specific problem is the exempting from taxation of all oil royalty payments made to the State, its political subdivisions, and The University of Texas. It could be said that taxing such royalties would merely be taxing money in one pocket to put money in the other pocket, but when consideration is given to the fact that oil royalties are allocated almost entirely to Permanent School and University funds whereas tax revenues are allocated to available funds the picture changes. In 1949 oil tax revenues which would have accrued had not the State oil royalties been exempted would have amounted to approximately $300,000. Had this money been collected as oil tax rather than left as oil royalties that amount of money would have been available to meet 1949 needs. This is a policy question which rests upon the determination of the primary purpose of this tax, and touches the principle of severance taxation. 

Tank Bottom Oil

The present inability of the Comptroller to collect the oil production tax on "tank bottom" oil has already been discussed. Just how much marketable oil escapes taxation because of the method now employed for cleaning oil storage tanks is difficult to determine. However, it is noteworthy that some produced oil is not liable to tax. This oil is included in the Railroad Commis sion's consideration of allowables. Whether or not this oil is to be taxed is a policy matter, of course, but the intent of the Legislature expressed in the statute might help clarify the situation. 

Carry-Overs from "Hot Oil" days

There appear in the law, provisions which are no longer necessary. 

Two of these are the sections which authorize sheriffs, rangers, and other peace officers to levy for taxes on oil on which taxes have not been properly paid; and the section which provides monetary fines from $100 to $1,000 to be required of producers who fail or refuse to report production. According to administrators of the law neither of these sections has ever been utilized in enforcing tax payment within the memory of the present administrators. 

The power to effect' stoppage of production, in the opinion of the administrators, is a more effective enforcement power, and is administratively more practical. In addition to these are the sections which require royalty interests to keep detailed records of royalty payments received, and carriers of oil to maintain complete records of all oil transported. These required records are apparently not used by the Comptroller in the enforcement procedure. 

Since the Railroad Commission requires almost identical records for other purposes it would seem that these sections could be deleted from the tax laws without seriously altering the enforcement picture. 176

Closing

In general, the oil production tax, in the statute and in actual administration, reflects the great amount of thought and attention devoted to it during the near half-century of its existence. It functions efficiently as compared to other taxes; it collects very sizeable revenues. Yet it presents at least one fundamental defect as a continuing substantial money-making segment of the State's tax structure--the dependence of present production control measures upon factors over which the State has no control. 

Internal administration, though well-developed, has not kept pace with technological and administrative developments throughout the land. As a consequence, procedures of enforcement of this tax and techniques of accounting for its now multi-million dollar revenues are still based on methods of 20 years ago. Although large-scale inaccuracies are not apparent, preciseness of operation and accounting are not evident. In a sense, the administration of this tax lacks modernization. 

And, in spite of the fact that the oil production tax has long been the center of the continuing and loud controversy over the matter of severance of natural resources, no clear policy appears in the tax law or its administration as to the status of this tax applied to a severance industry. Future events throughout the world may bring about settlement of some of these questions. Yet, reliance on such laissez-faire determinations may well occasion unnecessary difficulty to the State. 177

Chapter VIII NATURAL GAS PRODUCTION TAX SECTION 1--HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

1 Record of the first use of natural gas is lost to history, but it seems that first useful production in the United States was accomplished at Fredonia, New York in 1821. According to reports, a gunsmith, who had drilled a well 

27 feet deep in an effort to get a larger flow of gas from a seep during the preceding year, piped gas through small lead pipes into nearby homes and stores in the village. Apparently, the gas was used to light about 30 burners. 

By 1841, gas was being used to manufacture salt from brine near Charleston. 

It was many years later that the first useful production of natural gas in Texas was accomplished near Greenvine in Washington County in 1879. A portion of the gas was piped to a near-by house where it was used as fuel, but no commercial use was made of the remainder. 2 This first use of natural gas in 

Texas followed by nine years the attempt which was made in 1870 to pipe it 3 into Rochester, N. Y., for use in that city. The wooden pipelines used proved unsatisfactory because of the gas pressure involved, and the project was soon abandoned. 4

The first successful attempt to secure gas in commercial quantities in Texas followed in 1888 as a development of the Washington County discovery. The first successful production for consumption in a Texas city was accomplished at Corsicana 5 in 1901, eighty years after it had first been used thus in the State of New York. These early attempts to use natural gas in Texas, however, could scarcely be considered harbingers of coming events, for there was little indication at the turn of the century of the future which was ahead for the natural gas industry in the State. 

It was another decade before widespread development in the State began. 1 

Encyclopedia Americana, 1946 edition, articles "Gas, Natural," Vol. 12, pp. 302 ff., and "Pipelines, "Vol. 22, pp. 109 ff. 

2 C. A. Warner, Texas Oil and Gas Since 1543, pp. 10-11. Other authorities 3 agree with Warner as to the first year of useful production in the State but report the location of the incident and the details somewhat differently. For example, according to the 1945-1946 edition of the Texas Almanac and State 

Industrial Guide, (p. 238), published by the Dallas Morning News, Dallas, Texas, first useful production was in 1879 but at Greenville in Washington County.

P. 244. 178

First Long Pipelines 

In 1872, the first long iron pipeline in the United States was constructed near Titusville, Pennsylvania. Oil and gas development in that area continued and soon apread into other regions. In 1891, two long-distance high-pressure pipelines were constructed from the natural gas fields of northern Indiana to Chicago, Illinois. Thirty-eight years after the first long line was completed in Pennsylvania and almost twenty years after the completion of the highpressure pipeline to Chicago, the first long pipeline for gas transmission was completed in Texas. It was constructed in 1910 and carried gas produced in the 

Petrolia field in Clay County to Fort Worth, and later, to Dallas.

It is reported that nearly 15,000 consumers within the State were using gas as a fuel in 1910. 8 During the fifteen years which followed additional gas pipelines were laid, and virtually all regions of the State were given the advantages of natural gas for use as a fuel.

In the late 1920's the Texas natural gas industry first became engaged in inter-state commerce as high-pressure pipelines were completed which carried Texas gas from the Panhandle area to Middle Western and Northern States. It is interesting to note that Louisiana entered the inter-state commerce field as a gas producing state at about the same time. 

The successful solution of the gas transportation problem became vital to the gas industry during the first quarter of the century as uses for natural gas beyond its use for fuel were discovered and developed. The necessary transportation facilities were achieved through the more general use of first threaded and later welded iron pipe. 

New Uses for Gas

During the second decade of the 20th Century the process of extracting gasoline from "casinghead gas," which is produced from oil wells along with oil, and which has a high liquid content suspended in it, was made practical. 

Casinghead gasoline became an important petroleum product. Since this "natural gasoline" as it is sometimes called, is highly volatile, it was used primarily by refineries in producing blended gasolines of higher than normal octane ratings. The stumbling block of excess butane and propane, by-products of the production of gasoline by extraction from gas, was also surmounted during the period of the first World War. These gases were found suitable for specialized uses as fuels, in synthetic rubber production, and in dewaxing lubricating oils. The manufacture of carbon black from natural gas also reached significant proportions during and after World War I. 

The more recent discovery of "gas-cracking" processes which break down the gas 

7 Texas Almanac, op. cit., p. 244. 

Warner, op. cit., p. 52. 

9 Texas Almanac, op. cit., p. 244. 179

molecules and reform them to obtain various commercially important chemicals have added to the uses for natural gas. 10 All of these events indicated the ever-growing importance of gas as a by-product of oil production and as a commodity deserving of production in its own right. 

Texas Becomes Leading Producer 

The natural gas picture in Texas changed drastically during the years from 1924 to 1929. In 1924 Texas ranked fifth in the nation in terms of marketed production volume of natural gas, but by 1929 it led all other States with a marketed production of almost 465 billion cubic feet, over 100 billion cubic feet greater than Oklahoma, the second state in market production. 11 Texas has consistently led the nation in marketed production since 1929, and in 1948 producers in the State marketed almost two trillion cubic feet of natural gas, or 45 per cent of the nation's total marketed production. 12 It should be emphasized that these figures refer to marketed production of natural gas in the State. They do not include approximately 750 billion cubic feet which were produced and wasted.

In recycling operations the gas which remains after extraction of the liquid content is returned to a gas formation in the earth to be reproduced later when it can be profitably used. In the use of gas for lifting oil, produced gas is returned to an oil formation in the earth, increasing the pressure in that formation and thus adding to the producing life of the formation. 

Prior to the initiation of the relatively recently developed recycling and oil lifting operations, gas for which no fuel use was immediately available was vented into the air, or flared as produced. A desire to prevent this enormous waste was undoubtedly a factor which influenced the development of these conservation processes. From the point of view of the gas tax laws alone, however, these technological developments have been questionable blessings, for, as shall be seen, they have greatly complicated the administration of the tax laws. 

Significance of Low Gas Price Another factor which has significantly affected the administration of the gas tax law has been sales price of gas shipped to other States via pipe lines. 10 

1949-1950 The Texas Almanac and State Industrial Guide, The Dallas 

Morning News, Dallas, 1950, p. 281 

11 Petroleum Data Book, Petroleum Engineering Publishing Co., Dallas, 1948, p. E-111. 

12 Ibid. 180

On the sales price is dependent to a great extent the revenue from this tax. 

Along with the growth and expansion of the industry has been a constantly increasing volume of gas produced and piped to other States. In recent years the low price of gas including the piped gas has been an item of considerable concern. 13 In 1946, natural gas from Texas was moved by pipelines into 27 states and the Republic of Mexico. The total volume moved by interstate pipelines was 451 billion cubic feet, or about 25 per cent of Texas' total marketed production for the year. In 1948 there were 17 major pipelines in operation or planned which carried Texas' gas to points ranging 14 from the East Coast to the West Coast, and to Monterrey, Mexico. For this gas, much of which was destined for use in the Eastern states, Texas producers received an average price of three cents per 1000 cubic feet.

This figure takes on more meaning when it is viewed in relation to the following analysis:

An astounding fact revealed by analysis of marketed gas production in relationship to the prices received by producers at wells is that 71 per cent of the total supply is sold by producers at prices ranging from 1.4 to 3.8 cents per thousand cubic feet. The bulk of this supply comes from the Southwestern States of Texas, Oklahoma, Louisiana, Arkansas and

New Mexico. These are the States that supply the major share of the gas transported by long lines to the consuming centers in the Eastern half of the United States. Three States, New 

York, Pennsylvania and Ohio, supplying only four per cent of the nation's marketed gas production, report prices at wells of producers ranging from 18.3 cents to 26.6 cents per thousand cubic feet. All other States, with 25 per cent of the gas supply, reported prices at wells of producers ranging from 

4.6 cents to 15 cents per thousand cubic feet: 15 In terms of price at the mouth of the well, the price on which, as we shall see, the natural gas occupation tax is based, Texas' producers are among those who receive the lowest price for their product. There are 13 

Petroleum Data Book, op. cit., p. E-109. 

14 Bruce Roach, Increasing the Severance Tax on Natural Resources, University of Texas Press, Austin, 1948, p. 78. 15 

Petroleum Data Book, op. cit., p. E-109 181

yout

The first tax levied on the production of natural gas in Texas was called an "occupation" tax. It was levied against the producer and accrued at the time of the first sale of gas in intrastate commerce. As with the oil tax laws of that time, quarterly reports of sales, accompanied by tax remittances, were made by producers to the Comptroller of Public Accounts. 

The method of payment and the tax period were changed in 1936, and because of the need for additional revenue to pay for Old Age Assistance, the tax was increased from two per cent to three per cent of the market value of gas produced. 21 

Assessment and Collection Procedures Changed 

The assessment and collection procedural changes which were effected in 1936 were apparently drawn to parallel the changes in oil tax legislation which had been enacted three years earlier in 1933. 22 Monthly, instead of quarterly, reports and payments were now required of both the producer and the purchaser, who first entered the picture at this time as a "tax-collector" for the State. These procedures are basically the same as those currently used. Changes in tax rate and clarification of definitions, exemptions, and enforcement powers have been made but the purchaser and producer play the same relative roles in tax assessment and collection today as they did then. Thus from 1936 to the present time the oil tax law appears to be the "big brother" of natural gas tax legislation.

The first specific allocation of revenue derived from the natural gas tax was also included in the law passed in 1936. No mention of the use of funds was made in the original law of 1931, but in 1936 one-fourth of the total revenue was allocated to the Available School Fund, and three-fourths to the General Revenue Fund. Despite the fact that the tax increase was made, according to the law, to provide additional funds for old age assistance, no allocation was made to that fund. Analysis of the Comptroller's report for the fiscal year 1937 reveals, however, that the three-fourths of the revenue derived from this tax which was allocated by law to the General Revenue Fund was in actuality apportioned in large measure directly to the Old Age Assistance Fund without ever being credited to the General Revenue Fund. 

Gas Tax Law Revised in 1941

The years from 1936 to 1941 were all but barren of any natural gas tax legislation. The few administrative and enforcement changes made in 21 22 

Tex. Laws, 1936, 44th Leg., 3rd C. S., Ch. 495, Art. IV, Sec. 8. 

Tex. Laws, 1933, -43rd Leg., Chapters 12 and 162. 183

were incorporated in the 1941 revision of the entire natural gas tax law. 24 The 1941 revision remains basically unchanged. The present tax rate of 5. 2 per cent of the market value of gas as and when produced was set in 1941, the rate being increased from the three per cent rate then in effect. A minimum tax rate was also placed in effect to counter any future drop in natural gas values. Much effort was devoted to clarification of the "market value of gas" an ever present thorn in the flesh of the administration of the natural gas tax laws. Production of gas which was tax exempt was re-defined and the measurement of volume was standardized at a fixed temperature and pressure base. The provision that royalty interests should hare the tax burden was placed in effect, and not least in importance, the Comptroller was authorized a portion of the tax receipts to defray administrative expenses.

Several original provisions of the natural gas tax laws were similar to those found in the oil occupation ta x laws. As in the case of the latter, co-operation between the Comptroller and the Railroad Commission in enforcing the gas tax law was outlined in the initial enactment. The Comptroller was designated as the person responsible for administration and enforcement. 

The Commission was placed in the role of enforcement assistant. The Comptroller was to allow the Commission to inspect all records and reports or to make copies of them available to the Commission. These co-ordination provisions were more clearly stated than similar provisions of the oil occupation tax laws. They remained in effect until the complete revision of the gas tax law, in 1941, at which time the Commission was relieved of its enforcement function and the Comptroller remained as the administrator and the sole enforcing authority. Unlike the present oil tax laws, no mention of assistance by the Commission is made in current natural gas tax legislation. 

It appears that preciseness of phraseology, clearness of definition, distinctness of applicability, and ease of enforcement were primary consider ations in the forming of the 1941 natural gas law. Undoubtedly these objectives were accomplished to some degree. But the fact that they were not wholly successful is attested by the veritable rash of Attorney General's opinions which appeared in the late months of 1941.

Since the original enactment of the natural gas tax law in 1931, both the Comptroller of Public Accounts, who was charged with ministration and enforcement of the law, and the gas producers, who were subject to the tax, 23 

Tex. Laws, 1939, 46th Leg., Ch. 3 and 4. 24 

Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Leg., Ch. 184, Art. II. 

184

experienced difficulties in interpreting the provisions of the law, and revisions of it through the years have by no means eliminated their difficulties. Their difficulties stem, by and large, from two factors. First, assessment of the tax is based on the "average market value" of gas as and when produced, which in some cases proves to be different from the sale price. Second, several exemptions and exceptions have been included in the law from the beginning which have proven to be a source of trouble. 

The first of these stated that tax payment was not required in those cases where it would constitute a burden on interstate commerce. In addition the initial law allowed a two per cent deduction from gross production as a loss and record keeping expense allowance. Both of these provisions remained in effect until 1941 when the entire tax law was rewritten. The only exemptions cited in the new law were for gas returned to the earth for storage, recycling, repressuring, and lifting oil, unless such gas was sold for these purposes; and for gas lawfully vented or flared. 25 

Use Clause Enacted

Instead of the original exemptions which were concerned with burdening interstate, commerce, the Legislators substituted in the law of 1941 a "first use" clause concerned with the tax on imported gas which declared that "the market value of gas produced in another State and imported into this State shall be the average value at the mouth of the well of like gas sold within this 1126 

State . 

The "first use" clause also stated: "Should the provision herein with reference to the basis to be used in calculating the tax on gas imported into this State be declared for any reason to be discriminatory or unconstitutional or for any reason invalid, then there is hereby levied on all gas imported into this State a tax of five (5) per cent of the market value of said gas based upon the first sale of said gas within this State. "127 

The new law became effective in May 1941, and in September of that year the Attorney General wrote an opinion28 which declared in essence that the portions of the law which levied the tax on gas imported into this State were first, in violation of the United States Constitution's provision limiting a State's power of taxation to within its own borders and also those provisions forbidding a burden on interstate commerce; and second, were unenforceable because of faulty internal construction. Apparently, the law was both unenforceable and in violation of the Constitution because the tax was levied on producers, 25 26 

27 Ibid.

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, Ch. 184, Art. II. 

Ibid. 28 

Atty. Gen. Opinion, 0-3909, Sept. 15, 1941. 185

not purchasers within this State.

Where the initial enactment of 1931 included the novel definition that the purchasers of gas imported into this State were "producers' and engaged in the business of producing natural gas within this State, 1129 the 1941 revision gave no such definition but stated that "'producer' shall mean any person owning, controlling, managing, or leasing any gas well and/or any person who produces in any manner any gas by taking it from the earth or waters in this State, 130 It had been possible to interpret the 1931 law in such a way that while imported gas was taxed, the tax was actually levied on "producers" within the borders of this State. But under the revised definition this interpretation was not in any way possible for even though the tax was still levied on producers within this State as it had been, purchasers of imported gas were no longer defined as producers. 

Although present assessment procedures and problems will be discussed more fully in a following section, it should be pointed out that many of the above mentioned "rash of opinions" concerning the natural gas tax rendered by the Attorney General after 1941 were rendered in connection with assessment problems, especially with proper determination of "market value." No less than six opinions concerning the tax were written by the Attorney General to the Comptroller during the last four months of 1941. The first of these has already been discussed in connection with taxing imported gas. The second31 was a policy forming opinion which outlined for the Comptroller seven different rules by which the taxable value of natural gas could be determined. The Attorney 

General described his office's opinion of the clarification of the term "market value" as follows:

"To our way of thinking this... Article 7047b (natural gas tax law) has certainly muddied the waters of the ocean of law with its ramifications and complications on market value. 

The plain term "market value" has been the subject of controversy and discussion by the courts for many years, but we are not confronted with a plain form of "market value" here because the definition of the term in this case is adorned with an intricate set of provisos and qualifications that excel all previous 132 legislative ingenuity.

The last four opinions in this group were related to calrifying the determination of the taxable value of gas under certain sets of conditions surrounding production and sale. 29 

Tex. Laws, 42nd Leg., 1931, Ch. 73. 30 

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, Ch. 184, Art. II. 

31 

Atty. Gen. Opinion, 0-3516, Oct. 16, 1941. 

32 Ibid. 186

As a consequence of the confusion caused by the 1941 revision of the natural gas tax law, which is evidenced by the flurry of opinions described above, the Legislature in 194533 amended the law by repealing all portions of the law related to the taxation of gas imported into this State. Circumstances surrounding this repeal have already been discussed. The amendment also attempted to make more precise and more simple the statements concerning exemptions and the determination of market value and taxable value of gas produced. Administrative changes were also made at this time. Except for a re-definition of standards for measurement of one cubic foot of gas which was effected in 1949, 34 the form of the natural gas tax laws enacted in 1945 is the same today. The tax rate of 5.2 per cent of the market value of gas produced and saved within this State was fixed in 1941 but the tax rate in this instance is a secondary consideration as compared with the determination of "market value." Because of the variety of ways in which gas is marketed and in which it is used or processed after production, the market value determination remains, as we shall see, a complex problem in the administration of the natural gas tax laws. 

In Conclusion

The most recent change in Texas' natural gas tax law was enacted in 195035 when the need for funds for the State hospitals and special schools became acute. A temporary extension of the natural gas tax was levied at a rate of ten per cent of the then current tax. This extension applies only to gas produced during the period March 1, 1950 to September 1, 1951, and is administered in the same manner as the basic tax. 

The current picture of natural gas taxation in Texas, as is revealed by the preceding discussion, is not portrayed by the simple statement that a tax is levied on all gas produced and saved within this State at a rate of 

5.2 per cent of the market value thereof. Exemptions from taxation and provisions for market value determination stated in the law itself, together with a body of court decisions and Attorney General's opinions which have grown up with the law must be considered as a vital part of any discussion of this tax.

The natural gas producing, refining, and processing industries have grown tremendously since the close of the first World War. During the last 

33 Tex. Laws, 49th Leg., 1945, Ch. 269. 34 

Tex. Laws, 51st Leg., 1949, Ch. 519, sec. 4. 35 

Tex. Laws, 51st Leg., 1st C. S., 1950, ch. 2, art. II. 187

five years further progress has been made with the conversion to gas transmission of oil pipelines built during World War II. 

The completion of new gas pipelines and the discovery of new uses for natural gas in a variety of industries indicates that future demand for gas will be consistent with, or greater than, the demand at the present time. The possibility of another world conflict, no doubt, will add even greater impetus to the continued development of gas production. 

During the fiscal year 1950 a gross production of almost four trillion cubic feet of gas, both taxable and non-taxable, was reported to the Railroad Commission. 36 Tax revenues which the State gained from this production were in excess of nine and a half million dollars. 37 Although the natural gas tax does not approach its "big brother, " the oil occupation tax, as a revenue producer, it does stand among the ten largest revenue producing State taxes, and as such holds an important place in the total tax economy of the State. 36 

The Texas Petroleum Industry and State Finances, Texas Legislative 

Council, Austin, Texas, Nov.

1950, table XXIX, immediately preceding 15. p. 37 

Ibid., table XXV, immediately preceding p. 9. 188

There are distinct similarities in the organizational structure used to administer the natural gas and oil occupation tax laws. Basic administrative procedures in most instances are almost identical, the greatest difference occurring in the manner in which the taxes are assessed. Some administrative and assessment procedural differences are to be expected because of the differences which occur in gas production and oil production and in the processing of these two commodities for further use. The tax on the business of producing natural gas is styled, as is its more lucrative revenue-producing correlate, the oil production tax, as an "occupation tas." In this instance also, compliance with Texas' apparent constitutional restrictions is the most likely reason for the levying of an occupation tax rather than a "severance" tax. However, it should be pointed out that the severance tax principle was indicated in the emergency clause of the original act passed in 1931 which stressed the fact that "all of the natural gas resources of Texas are now being rapidly dissipated and depleted, and may soon be exhausted, . . ."'1

Comptroller Responsible for Administration. 

The Comptroller of Public Accounts is designated as the State agency responsible for administration and enforcement of the natural gas occupation tax. He has express authority to promulgate administrative procedures beyond those stated in the law itself, and also to enforce the payment of taxes by exacting penalties stated in the law. Until the revision of 1941, the Railroad Commission was required to assist the Comptroller in enforcement. But when the statute was re-written that year, the Commission was not mentioned and the Comptroller was given complete responsibility. The Attorney General, of course, is granted the authority, common to most tax laws, to bring suits in the name of the State against delinquent taxpayers. 

Current assessment and payment procedures are similar to those used in connection with the occupation tax on oil. The purchaser is placed in the role of "tax-collector" for the State, and deducts taxes due on gas purchased from the price paid to the producer. He remits these deductions to the State at the time he submits his monthly report of purchases to the Comptroller. The producer also reports monthly on all gas produced but not sold during the reporting period and remits taxes on such gas directly to the Comptroller at the time the report is submitted. The monthly reports submitted by purchasers 

38Tex. Laws, 1931, 42nd Leg., Ch. 73. 189

and producers account for the production of all natural gas subject to taxation. 

Some production. which is exempted from taxation is not reported. However, it has been indicated that future reports will show total production in the State as well as that being taxed.

The purchaser's responsibility for making payment does not, however, relieve the producer of liability for payment of the tax. The law specifically states that the producer shall be held liable for the tax until it is actually paid and that royalty holders shall bear their proportional share of the tax burden. 

In the event that a purchaser fails to remit deductions the tax may be collected from the producer; the producer is substantially a surety. In order to protect the producer from a double deduction, he is granted the right to bring suit against the delinquent purchaser for recovery of taxes due plus court costs and expenses involved in the court proceedings. This procedure, in effect, gives the gas producer an enforcement function; the threat of suit by himanoo against the purchaser encourages the latter to make payment as required by inq law. on

Second, in the event that the Comptroller is not satisfied with any reports submitted to him he may employ auditors to investigate the affairs and records of the persons who submitted the reports in question to ascertain them correct amount of taxes due.
