Comptroller Responsible for Administration 

The Comptroller of Public Accounts is designated as the State agency responsible for administration and enforcement of the natural gas occupation tax. He has express authority to promulgate administrative procedures beyond those stated in the law itself, and also to enforce the payment of taxes by exacting penalties stated in the law. Until the revision of 1941, the Railroad Commission was required to assist the Comptroller in enforcement. But when the statute was re-written that year, the Commission was not mentioned and the Comptroller was given complete responsibility. The Attorney General, of course, is granted the authority, common to most tax laws, to bring suits in the name of the State against delinquent taxpayers. 

Current assessment and payment procedures are similar to those used in connection with the occupation tax on oil. The purchaser is placed in the role of "tax-collector" for the State, and deducts taxes due on gas purchased from the price paid to the producer. He remits these deductions to the State at the time he submits his monthly report of purchases to the Comptroller. The producer also reports monthly on all gas produced but not sold during the reporting period and remits taxes on such gas directly to the Comptroller at the tim the report is submitted. The monthly reports submitted by purchasers 38 47 73.

and producers account for the production of all natural gas subject to taxation. 

Some production which is exempted from taxation is not reported. However, it has been indicated that future reports will show total production in the State as well as that being taxed.

The purchaser's responsibility for making payment does not, however, relieve the producer of liability for payment of the tax. The law specifically states that the producer shall be held liable for the tax until it is actually paid and that royalty holders shall bear their proportional share of the tax burden. 

In the event that a purchaser fails to remit deductions the tax may be collected from the producer; the producer is substantially a surety. In order to protect the producer from a double deduction, he is granted the right to bring suit against the delinquent purchaser for recovery of taxes due plus court costs and expenses involved in the court proceedings. This procedure, in effect, gives the gas producer an enforcement function; the threat of suit by him against the purchaser encourages the latter to make payment as required by law. 

Comptroller's Administrative Powers

The Comptroller is given specific powers in addition to the general powers of making administrative regulations, collecting the taxes, and exacting penalties. First, the Comptroller verifies the reports submitted to him and is authorized one-half of one per cent of the gross receipts from this tax which, when appropriated by the Legislature, is used to pay auditors' salaries and to meet expenses for office supplies and equipment needed for such verification. Second, in the event that the Comptroller is not satisfied with any reports submitted to him he may employ auditors to investigate the affairs and records of the persons who submitted the reports in question to ascertain the correct amount of taxes due. The expenses of such an audit are required to be paid by the persons being investigated and are placed in a permanent revolving fund used only for such purposes. Finally, should the Comptroller be dissatisfied with the market value of gas reported in a particular case he is empowered to hold a public hearing, after ten days notice, and to determine as a result of such hearing the correct market value of gas in the case involved. 

This administrative adjudication of market value by the Comptroller is final, except for recourse by the taxpayer to payment under protest and subsequent legal action to get judicial review of the Comptroller's determination. The organizational structure created to administer the natural gas tax is simple. Likewise, the duties and powers of the designated agencies are quite explicit and comprehensive. In this respect, the law is almost a model of simplicity. The Comptroller of Public Accounts is designated as the agency for levying and collecting the tax. It appears that he has ample powers, both 190

general and specific, to accomplish the task. He is not required to coordinate his efforts with any other State agency, except the Attorney General in bringing suit for collection of delinquent taxes. Furthermore, the Comptroller's collection efforts are largely accomplished by the purchasers and producers of gas, primarily by the purchasers.

But simplicity of organizational structure and clear delineation of levy, collection, and enforcement functions ought not obscure the fact that the natural gas tax is one of the most, if not the most, complex and most difficult of all State tax laws to administer. Simple levy procedures and ample collection and enforcement powers do not solve the ever-changing technological arrangements and concepts concerning what gas production is to be taxed and what the market value thereof is for tax collecting purposes. 191

SECTION 

ASSESSMENT. 

The present tax on natural gas produced in the State of Texas is an occupation tax levied on the business or occupation of producing gas within the State. 39 All Texas producers of natural gas are liable for the tax and the law defines the word "producer" to include all persons or firms producing gas from gas wells or in any manner by removing it from the earth or waters in this State. Royalty owners are specifically included as producers of natural gas, and the law requires that they bear a proportionate share of the 40 tax burden. 

Basis and Rate

As in the oil tax, the basis for the natural gas tax is the value of the volume of gas produced, a percentage of that value constituting the tax levied. 

The basic levying statement is in the following form: "A tax shall be paid by each producer on the amount of gas produced and saved within this State equivalent to five and two-tenths (5. 2) per cent of the market value thereof as and when produced, The statute goes on to say "The market value . . shall be the value . . . at the mouth of the well . . . ." There are, however, two qualifications to this rate. ,,41 42

The first is a floor or minimum amount of tax--11/150 of one cent per 1000 cubic feet of sweet and sour natural gas. These types of gas are defined in the law in terms of hydrogen-sulfide content, and both are considered as distinct from casinghead gas for which no minimum tax is stipulated. The derivation of this seemingly cumbersome fraction, which when converted to its monetary value amounts to .0733 of one cent, is not discernible in the law or in the literature available. Computation reveals that it takes effect when the market value of such gas is equal to or less than 1.41 cents per 1000 cubic feet. It is possible that when the minimum was created in 1941 this price level closely approximated the prevailing 43 average market value at that time. The second qualification changes the tax base in those cases where gas is processed to remove its liquid hydrocarbon content and the residue gas is returned to the earth by cycling methods. For such operation, the basic rate is applied to three-fifths (3/5) of the value of the products removed. 44 39 40 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's) 1948, art. 7047b, sec. 1. 

Ibid., sec. 2 and sec. 1(8). 

41 Ibid. 42 43 Ibid. sec. 1. 

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg,, 1941, ch. 184, art. II. 

44 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's) 1948, art 7047b, sec. 1(2). 192

Exemptions

Another qualification, though not affecting the rate, reduces the amount of gas production which is taxable by exempting "(a) gas injected into the earth in this State, unless sold for such purpose; (b) gas produced from oil wells with oil and lawfully vented or flared; and, (c) gas used for lifting oil unless sold for such purpose. 

45 There is, in addition to the above stated exceptions, an indirectly stated exemption from taxation of that gas produced on lands owned by the State which is used in payment of State royalties. The statement of the exemption reads as follows: "This Section shall not affect any pending lawsuit in the State of Texas or any lease agreement or contract now or that hereafter may be in effect between the State of Texas or any political subdivision thereof and/or The University of Texas and any gas producer. "46 This exemption of taxes from royalty gas is worded exactly as is the exemption from taxation of oil used for State royalty payments, and a more complete discussion of it will be found in that collection. Actual assessment of the amount of gas taxes due is accomplished in a manner basically the same as the procedure used in the case of the oil occupa tion tax. No tax bill is rendered by the Comptroller to producers. Instead, the purchaser is placed in the role of tax assessor and collector, for in order to "deduct the tax. . . from the payment due the producer" he must determine the proper amount of taxes due on all the gas he purchases. 47 Each month the purchaser reports all purchases to the Comptroller and remits the taxes he has deducted along with the report. In this manner the greatest portion of the gas tax revenue is assessed and collected. The purchaser also assures that the tax is borne proportionately by all royalty holders since he pays their shares directly to them. Any gas produced and stored during a given month is covered by the provision that the producer must report all gas "produced and saved" to the Comptroller and that "such report . . . be accompanied by legal tender or cashier's check payable. . for the proper amount of taxes..

The above described sections inserted into the natural gas tax law in 1936 correspond very closely to sections of the oil tax law written in 1933. 

Perhaps, as was the case with oil, an important factor in instituting this system was the reduction effected in the number of audits of reports received by the Comptroller. There are currently operating in Texas approximately 

1800 producers of gas. Purchasers of gas in the State number about 275.

Thus, by checking and auditing relatively few purchasers' reports, the Comptroller can account for most gas production and thereby most tax revenue due the State. 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's) 1948, art. 7047b, sec. 1(2). 45 46 Ibid. 47 Ibid. 

48 Ibid. 1148 193

At first glance, no outstandingly difficult feature is apparent in the assessment provisions of the tax law. However, even casual inquiry reveals that practically all the difficulties of application and administration of this tax stem from the sections of the law prescribing the assessment methods. 

Specifically, these difficulties arise from the use of market value as abasis for this tax, and from the exemptions which are contained in the law. 

Administrative Difficulties Caused by Exemptions 

As mentioned earlier, gas injected into the earth and gas used for lifting oil, unless sold for such purpose,are exempt from the tax. The difficulty presented by these exemptions results from the impossibility of physically separating and recognizing such gas when reproduced, from that being produced for the first time.

Late in 1941 the Attorney General wrote an opinion49 concerning the taxability of gas which had been sold and the tax paid thereon, but which was then used for lifting oil, and in which use, consequently, was re-produced. 

It was the Attorney General's opinion that the tax accrued on a given volume of gas only once and that if "there is any method whereby you can determine what proportion of the volume of gas emerging. in casinghead gas produced from that well as distinguished from injected gas being re-produced "50 and on which the tax had once been paid, such re-produced gas should be excluded from consideration for tax purposes. A similar interpretation was made in 1947 in another opinion51 which declared in essence that re-produced gas on which a tax had once been paid was exempt from a second taxation, but re-produced gas on which a tax had not been paid at the time of first production was subject to tax in accordance with the provisions of the law covering the use made of it when re-produced. 

The motives which prompted these exemptions are clear, but the facts necessary to make application of the exemption rules are difficult to determine. Although the question brought up by the exemptions could possibly be determined by judicial proceeding through the use of a court or jury as a fact-finding body, the question of fact is such that it cannot be easily determined by a tax collector. In connection with exemptions, an opinion of the Attorney General in 194752 should be considered. This opinion denied the exemption from taxa tion of gas produced and used on an oil lease as fuel to operate a pumping engine which lifts oil. Only gas injected into the ground for pressure purposes to lift oil was held to be included in this exemption. 49 

Atty. Gen. Opinion, 0-4100, Dec. 3, 1941. 

50 Ibid. 51 

Atty. Gen. Opinion, V-335, Aug. 13, 1947. 

52 . Atty. Gen. Opinion, U-467, Dec. 30, 1947. 194

The Problem of Determination of Market Value 

The second troublesome assessment feature of the natural gas tax law is the necessity for determining the market value of gas produced and saved. 

It will be recalled that this market value of the gas "shall be the value at the mouth of the well" as and when the gas is produced. 

The statute goes on to spell out how the market value concept is to be applied under various circumstances: (1) In case gas is sold for cash only, the tax is computed on the producer's gross cash receipts; (2) when the consideration for the sale of gas is a portion of the products extracted from the gas or a portion of the residue gas, or both, the tax is computed on the gross value of all things of value received by the producer; (3) when gas is processed for its liquid hydrocarbon content and the residue gas is returned by cycling methods to a gas producing formation, the taxable value is considered as three-fifths of the gross value of all liquids extracted and prior to absorption, refining or processing of such hydrocarbons. 

By means of these provisions, the statute attempts to provide the considerations necessary to arrive at market value. This, of course, is an imputed or calculated value in most cases, but the technique is not an uncommon one. It is frequently employed in the law of damages to get a standard for determination of the amount of compensation due a party to a suit resulting from a tort or breach of contract by another party. 53 By means of this market value concept, it is possible to determine a taxable value where no sales price or publicly recorded sales value exists. It will be recalled that posted prices or crude oil are used in the oil tax administration to obatin a taxable value in those circumstances where no actual sale has taken place. 

To state the situation in another way: The gas production tax is based on the value of the commodity (gas), not simply on the volume, as are some others of the taxes on production (e.g., the sulphur and cement taxes). As a tax on production, the point of application of the tax is at the mouth of the well, or what might be called the initial step in the production process. 

However, as a result of the trade practices within the industry, only a relatively small volume of production is sold for cash considerations at the point of production, i. e. the mouth of the well. Therefore, cash sales price is not adequate for a determination of the taxable value. As a consequence the statute uses the concept of market value, which simply means the taxable value of the gas against which the tax can be applied. This taxable value is arrived at by means of the several formulas provided for in the law as enumerated above. 53 

McCormick, Damages (Hornbook, 1935) s. 44. 195

Although some writers in the field of resources taxation quarrel with the validity of such concept for use in determining taxable value, the theory is generally accepted. However, the practical application of the theory or concept to the administration of the gas production tax finds few supporters. 

As is the case with the practical application of the exemptions, the working application of the value concept raises such questions of fact as cannot be easily determined, and possibly cannot be determined at all by the tax administrator under any reasonable set of circumstances. 

This situation results in large part from the changing techniques and marketing arrangements within the industry. From a technological point of view, the industry has been most dynamic, whereas the tax statute by comparison has been relatively static in its basic form. 

A look at some of the assessment problems in detail seems appropriate for clearer understanding. The types and combinations of technological, marketing and contractual arrangements in the industry are myriad. Therefore, the examples offered here must understandably represent only a small number of the actual situations confronting the administrator in the application of this tax law.

Different Assessment Situations 

(1) For the first example a simple arrangement may be considered.

The producer sells gas to the purchaser at the well for cash only. The tax is computed as 5.2 per cent of the producer's gross cash receipts, that is, the sales price. The purchaser has only to calculate the amount of the tax, withhold it from his payment to the producer and royalty owners, and remit it to the State.

But even this simple marketing arrangement can be complicated under some circumstances. Often the different parties who have an interest in the gas being so sold, may be receiving different compensations for their shares of the gas. For example, the royalty owners, or some of them, might be getting for their share four cents per M. C. F. and the producer five cents. 

Thus, although the tax administrator receives payment from the purchaser, and the report accompanying the payment can be compared with the reports of the producer for each lease involved, the administrator needs to know the terms of the contracts involved before he can ascertain whether the correct amount of taxes has been paid. These contracts include not only those between the purchaser and the lease operators, but also those with the royalty holders. 

As has been pointed out above, the provisions of these contracts may differ, even for the same lease. Although it is the practice in the industry for the producer to sell gas under long-term contracts, these contracts are renegotiated from time to time, especially where the producer or royalty owner is placed in an advantageous bargaining position because of his other gas holdings. Thus, the price set by the original contract may not continue to be the 196

price for the duration of the contract. To conduct the audits necessary to determine accuracy of taxes paid is a task of considerable proportions. (2) A second example illustrating some of the problems in determining "market value" presents a situation in which the producer sells the gas to the purchaser at the well, but the consideration or price in the sale is not a sum certain but instead is a portion of the products of the gas. 

No cycling operations are involved. Normally, the producer and his royalty owners have no facilities to market the products but make a marketing arrangement with either the processor or a third party. The law is quite clear in this instance. Taxes are to be paid on the "gross value of all things of value received by the producer." This, then, becomes the market value. 

But beyond the bare statement of the law is an almost limitless array of possible contractual arrangements. The producer may receive a given share of all the products or receive a different share of each product. He may use some of the residue gas for fuel purposes and receive varying shares of the reamining products. Or, he may receive a share, or varying shares, of the sales values of the products processed in the plant and sold to a third person.

Thus, although the market value concept is understandable in such instances, its practical application presents considerable difficulty. Again the difficulty is the determination of an actual dollar value upon which to levy the tax. If all transactions were ultimately reduced to cash values, it would be possible to determine the value of the portions of the various products the producer receives. If, however, other things of value, such as shares of stock, leases, royalty interests, etc., are involved, the task of determining the value of the gas requires that a valuation be placed on the intangibles received. The difficulty in such a procedure is manifest. If the Comptroller accepts the value declared by the producer, of course there is no problem. But since the producer is tax assessor, tax collector and taxpayer, a more positive determination by the Comptroller would appear to be assumed. Such determination, it will be remembered, has to take into consideration also the varying royalty interests and possibility of contract changes. (3) For a third example, it may be well to consider the situation wherein the producer transports the gas some distance, and then (a) markets it for cash, or (b) markets it to a processor for a share of the products of the gas, or (c) processes it himself and markets the products. Here there is no sale at the mouth of the well; hence, it is necessary to find, impute or assign a market value to the gas produced. That is, it is necessary to determine what the gas "when and as produced" would have sold for had it been offered for sale at that time. Where the producer sells the gas for cash or for a share of the products, the market value of the gas produced is the cash value of what he received less the cost of transporting the gas to the marketing 197

point. However, where he produces, transports and processes the gas and then makes the first sale, the determination of the market value of the gas at the well is considerably more difficult. 

Effect of Recycling on Assessment 

(4) For another example, a situation involving recycling operations. should be considered. Here the gas is produced, transported to the recycling plant where the liquid hydrocarbons are extracted and the remainder of the gas is put back into some gas formation. Market value is here defined as "taxable value," established by the statute as three-fifths (3/5) of the gross value of all liquids made and saved from gas.

Under circumstances requiring the application of this section of the law, the administrator is faced with the question: At what point is the gross value of these liquid products to be calculated--at the well, the recycling plant, the refinery, or the point of first sale of the products? This question was raised in W. R. Davis, Inc. v. State. 54 The Davis company, which was the purchaser and processor, contracted with producers to recycle their gas for one-half of the products (condensate) from the gas. The purchaser then bought the producer's share of condensate for $1.36 per barrel for a time, and later for $1.44 per barrel. All products(producer's share and processor's share) were than transported to a marketing point and sold at prices ranging from $1.90 to $2.25 per barrel. Taxes were paid on three-fifths of the value received for all products--both the producer's and company's share--but computed upon the basis of the price paid the producers for their share. Contending that the price received when all of the products were first sold should be used instead, the State brought an action to recover taxes. The lower court held that the tax should be computed on the basis of the higher sales price; but the Supreme Court reversed and found for the taxpayer. The court declared that ". . . the occupation of refining liquids from gas is not . . taxed." The three-fifths clause concerning recycling operations "merely provides the method of ascertaining the value of the original gas, and the producer thereof only is taxed, not the refiner. " Subsequent to the decision in this case, the Legislature in 1945 enacted an amendment stating that the value of the liquid products made from the gas is "to be determined upon separation and extraction and prior to absorption, refining or processing of such liquid hydrocarbons and the quantity of the products shall be measured by the total yield of the processing plant from such gas. 

1155 This clarified somewhat the intent of the Legislature as to the 54 

180 S. W. 2d. 429 (1944). 

55 Tex. Laws 1945, ch. 269 (49th Leg.) 198

point of production at which the value determination is to be made, and emphasized the intent to have the tax levied against all of the products, not simply those returned to the producer. 

However, since a number of recycling plants now operate as integral parts of larger refinery operations producing many hydrocarbon products, the administrator is faced with the difficulty of getting a measurement of value after separation and extraction but before the subsequent "absorption, refining or processing." Thus, though the designation in the statute of the point of measurement of value is understandable and the objectives clear, the fact determinations required of the administrator, in situations involving recycling, are difficult if not impossible to achieve in practical operation of the tax law. 

Comparable Crude Oil Prices Used 

This situation is in part the result of the changing conditions and technology of the industry. So also is the circumstance which now exists in the assessment of the tax in some of the older gas fields of Texas. For a time it was found that most liquid products derived from recycling operations were somewhat comparable to crude oil of 400 A. P. I. gravity. Hence, in order to devise practical and workable methods to determine the gross value (three-fifths of which is considered the taxable value) of gas products, the practice developed to assess the products at the posted prices of 40 degrees, or comparable crude in the area. When no posted price for comparable crude is available in the area, the price at the nearest marketing point adjusted by deduction of the cost of transporting the products to such point, is used. However, prolonged operations in a field, after repeated cycling operations, tend to cause the gas to become lean and less productive of rich hydrocarbon products. Not only must a greater quantity of gas be processed to obtain a given volume of products but the quality of the products also deteriorates. Thus, after a time, the easy yardstick of the posted price of comparable crude for determination of taxable value of liquid products is no longer valid.

This situation has already caused administrative difficulties and, according to administrators, bids fair to become a major problem in the future when additional fields begin to yield leaner gas (less rich in liquid hydrocarbons). There is no crude oil counterpart from which to borrow a value for taxing purposes. The gross sales value of such products at the processing plant is less than the most comparable crude. Obviously, another device will have to be developed for continued application of the tax under the present law in such circumstances, or an adjustment of the law may be desirable. 199

To complicate further the administration of this tax, the statute states that "all hydrocarbons that are recovered from gas by means of a separator or by other non-mechanical methods, incidental to the production of said gas. shall be taxed at the same rate as oil. Technological developments have made very difficult the determination of where recovery is by separator or non-separator equipment, when the method is mechanical or non-mechanical, and whether or not such recovery is incidental to the production of gas.

From these examples of specific areas of assessment difficulty, which are not by any means exhaustive, some understanding can perhaps be had of the tremendous administrative problems brought up in applica tion of the law, which in theory and motive appears quite understandable. 56 

Tex. Laws (Vernon) 1950 art. 7047b, sec. 1(3). 200

SECTION 4--COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

The collection procedures for the natural gas production tax as set up in the law are simple and clear. Purchasers and producers compute their own tax and remit payments monthly to the Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's office. Accompanying these tax remittances are reports giving the date on which the tax computation is based. The reports and remittances are due on the last day of the calendar month. Enforcement of the tax law, however, though clearly enough stated in the statute and supported by substantial powers granted to the administrator, is complicated by the assessment difficulties discussed in the preceding Section. 

Most of the revenue received by the State under the natural gas tax law is collected for the State by the first purchaser 57 who buys gas from the producer. The parallel with the oil tax laws in this respect is again noticeable. The law requires each purchaser of gas to keep accurate records of all gas purchased and to deduct from the price paid for gas any taxes due, such deductions to be remitted monthly to the Comptroller. 

Nowhere in the statute 58 is the purchaser required to submit a report of purchases and deductions to substantiate his his tax remittance, but such a report is required by the Comptroller in order to administer the tax. 

Producers Reports Required. 

Producers, however, are required by the statute to make detailed monthly reports to the Comptroller showing the gross amount of gas produced, including a statement of the amount, source, and purchaser of each sale of gas. Producers are further required to accompany their reports with payment of "the proper amount of taxes herein levied. "59 Presumably, this makes the producer responsible not only for payment of taxes which purchasers do not remit, but also for payment of taxes directly to the Comptroller on gas which is produced and stored but not sold during a given monthly reporting period. Actually, little gas is stored by the producer, as contrasted with oil. 

For the future, however, there are strong indications that consider able gas may be produced and tranported to areas where it can be injected into depleted formations or caves and stored for future use; these underground storage "tanks" will be located near areas where there is a large 57.

According to the law, secondarypurchasers are also liable for payment of taxes not paid by first purchasers as, of course, are the producers. See 

Vernon's Texas Civil Statutes, art. 7047b, sec. 2a(1), (3), and (4). 

58 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon 1948) art. 7047b. 

59 Ibid., sec. 1(6). 201

consumption of gas.

This practice is nowemployed extensively in other parts of the country, frequently using Texas gas. It will be recalled that a tax is levied on "gas produced and saved within this State, " except "gas injected into the earth in this State, unless sold for such purpose." It is clear, then, that gas produced and sold and subsequently stored underground is subject to the tax; and probably equally clear that Texas gas would be subject to the tax if it were stored outside of Texas whether sold by the producer or not. But there seems to be a question since the statute does not expressly address itself to the matter, whether gas produced, transported and then stored underground within the State without any sale being involved would be subject to the tax. This could result from a producer's desire to have a substantial supply available immediately to a large gas market area in Texas or from a producer's desire to produce gas now in order to take advantage of the present tax on the basis of present market price of gas instead of at some future time when the price is expected to be higher, or the rate altered. 

In this regard, the statement of the law is vague in that no means of determining the market value of stored gas is included. Apparently, the 

Comptroller could determine its price as he now does for situations in which "market value" information is not available. According to an Attorney

General's opinion written to the Comptroller, "you (the Comptroller) are advised that if the gas has no market value at the well, you may determine the market value. by taking the actual market value where there is a market and deducting the cost of taking the gas to that market. opinion outlines the general formula used. 

Penalty Provisions Included 

160 This

Enforcment of timely submission of reports and tax remittances on due dates is implemented by the penalty provision of the law covering delinquency in tax payments. This penalty requires the payment of a fee in the amount of ten per cent of the tax due, and in addition, interest at the rate of six per cent on the tax and the penalty accrues from due date until date paid. 61 A fine of from $100 to $1,000 also can be required of the producer for each day of violation of the provisions of the law and the State holds a first lien on the producer's property and equipment for any delinquent taxes and penalties due. 62 Although use of the lien provisions have been threatened several times, there is no record of actual application. Only a few cases in recent years have involved application of the percentage penalty clause. No fines have been levied within the recollection of the present administration. 

60 Atty. Gen. Opinion, 0-633, April 26, 1939. 61 62 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon) 1948, art. 7047b, sec. 1(7). 

Ibid., sec. 5. 202

The producers' reports cover total taxable gas produced in the State during a given reporting period and list the purchasers and amounts for which gas was sold. 63 Purchasers' reports also state from whom and for what price gas was purchased and the tax deduction made in connection with each purchase. 

Thus, as with the oil tax reports, the Comptroller has available data which cạn be used for cross-checking to determing accuracy of reports of production and purchases. Following this comparison of reports, auditors in the Comptroller's office check the reports to determine that the correct tax was deducted from the purchase price. Later field auditors analyze purchasers' home office records to determine whether or not the taxes shown on the various reports were computed on the basis of the proper rule or method for determining market value as stated in the law. If this analysis shows purchasers' or producers' tax returns to contain discrepancies or omissions, the Comptroller proceeds with the task of enforcing adjustment either by correction of production reports or the collection of additional taxes due.

Production Can Be Prohibited 

Although other enforcement devices are set forth in the law, the primary means used by the Comptroller to enforce adjustment of producers' reports and to collect delinquent taxes is to prohibit further production on the lease involved in the delinquency. The Comptroller may prohibit production by procedures established in three separate statutory provisions. He may request the Attorney General to file suit to enjoin production until reports are corrected and delinquent taxes are paid. 64 He may, in the case of casinghead gas, order production of oil to cease in accordance with the provision making unlawful the removal of oil from a lease for which proper reports have not been filed. 65 Or he may prohibit production or removal of "any natural and/or casinghead gas" for which proper reports have not been filed. 

In practice, the Comptroller uses the method of prohibiting the purchaser to remove gas from such delinquent lease. He issues about four or five such orders per month, and delinquent producers are, in almost every instance, prompt in submitting the required reports and in remitting due taxes. Preceding such orders, several warning letters are sent to the offender. The Comptroller seldom uses the power to enjoin production through 63 66 Although current reports do not require data on some production exempted from taxation, future reports now being evaluated for adoption will include both taxable and exempted production. Royalty interests owned by the State or its subdivisions are currently shown on reports as exemptions. 

64 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon) 1950, art. 7047b, sec. 4. 65 

Ibid., art. 7057a, sec. 8a. 66 

Ibid., art. 7047b, sec. 6(c). 203

action by the Attorney General. Apparently, he requests such action about two or three times per year. In this connection, it is interesting to note that even though the purchaser of gas may be the actual delinquent in remitting taxes, it is the producer who is penalized until the flelinquent taxes are collected. The 67 producer must pay the tax and then, in accordance with the law, bring suit against the delinquent purchaser to recover the tax, interest, penalties, legal fees, and costs.

Other Enforcement Powers 

Other enforcement powers given to the Comptroller are as follows:

First, as an aid in conducting his audit of returns he is given authority to enter the premises of any person liable for the tax, and any other premises, in order to ascertain correct tax liability. 68 Second, he has the power to call a public hearing to fix the market value of gas for tax purposes in cases in 69 which he is not satisfied with the producer's declared market value. Because of the general reluctance of persons possessing the required knowledge for properly fixing a fair market value to declare a value different from that claimed by the producer, this power is ineffective and has been used only twice. Third, in the event a producer fails to remit proper tax amounts, the 

Comptroller may subject all of the producer's reocrds to an additional audit to ascertain the correct amount of taxes due. Such audit, when required, is conducted at the producer's expense. 70 Finally, the Attorney General is specifically authorized to perform his usual function of bringing suits in the name of the State against delinquent taxpayers at the request of the Comptroller. 71 Coverage Enforcement of a tax law deals largely with the problem of coverage. 

As has been discussed in relation to some of the other taxes in this survey, coverage has several aspects. From the point of view of enforcement, however, there are but two significant parts to the coverage problem. One is to see that all who are liable for the tax are actually paying it, and the other is to be certain that complete tax liability is being met by the taxpayers. 

Evasions of tax payment can, of course, be of either type. 67 

TEX CIV STAT. (Vernon) 1950, art. 7047b, sec. 1(6). 

68 Ibid., sec. 3. 

69Ibid., sec. 3a. 

70Ibid., sec. 5. 

71 Ibid. 204

In the gas production,tax assurance that taxes are collected for all reported taxable gas production in the State is dependent upon the administrator's cross-check of producers' and purchasers' reports. Such comparison reveals the failure of a producer to report production and also the failure of a purchaser to report purchases and remit taxes due. Producers' reports, in addition to other information, are required to show current ownership of leases. This is accomplished by having the operator who sells or transfers a lease indicate on his last producers' report to whom the sale or transfer was made. The new owner is also required to show, in his first report, from whom the lease was obtained. 72 However, no established procedure of reconciliation of Comptroller's reports with the records of the Railroad Commission has been established, although parallel reports are received by both agencies. Thus, although the 

Comptroller has considerable authority to verify and enforce tax payments on reported production, under present procedures he does not have verified assurance that reported and actual production are the same. A comparison of production figures published by the Comptroller and those published by the 

Railroad Commission (See Table Gas-I and discussion Section 5) reveals a significant discrepancy in totals shown. Although for every year considered in this survey the Comptroller's figures exceeded those of the Railroad Commission, the very existence of such discrepancies (amounting to approximately ten per cent of the total reported in some years) raises a question as to completeness of coverage.

Most significantly, however, coverage becomes a problem in the matter of determination of the correctness of the amounts of taxes paid. As was discussed in Section 3, the practical determination of an accurate market value is a near-impossibility for the tax administrator under many of the situations arising under the tax law. The determination, too, of accurate measurement of taxable volume is most difficult in view of the technological processes used in the industry, particularly in those cases where cycling methods are used to return residue gas to the earth. Added to these difficulties is the complication of frequent changes in contract terms, sales agreements and the like, which can and often do materially affect the gas value upon which the tax is computed. 

As the result of these difficulties which confront the administrator, he is substantially dependent upon not only the taxpayer's integrity, but also his accuracy of computation. Since the taxpayer is making his tax computation is faced by the same difficulties in a practical determination of market value 72 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon) 1950 art. 7047b, sec. 6(d). 205

Hence, and volume, there can logically be little reason to accept without question the accuracy of his computations or to deny the possibility of honest error. the possibility of inadequate coverage of the tax can certainly not be ignored. 

Disbursement of Tax Revenues 

The revenues received from the natural gas tax are used in much the same manner as are those received from the oil occupation tax. Allocation of gas tax revenues is prescribed in the same section of the current omnibus tax law as is the allocation of oil tax receipts. 73 After one-half of one per cent of gross receipts from the natural gas tax has been deducted and allocated to the Comptroller for use in defraying expenses incurred in administering the law, 74 the remainder is allocated one-fourth to the Available School Fund and three-fourths to the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund, The Comptroller's annual report for the fiscal year 1949 reveals that the 2.2 million dollars which the natural gas tax receipts added to the Available School Fund were slightly more than two per cent of that fund's total and the 6.7 million dollars in natural gas tax revenues which were allocated to the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund were almost six per cent of the total allocations to that fund. 75 A large part of the one-half per cent earmarked to defray administrative costs of the gas tax revefts to the General Revenue Fund because appropriations seldom approach or equal this amount. 76 Money credited to the Available School Fund is apportioned to the counties of the State for use in maintaining the free public schools. Such apportionment is authorized in the laws pertaining to education which also outline the purposes for which such money may be spent. The money which is credited to the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund is re-allocated largely to the 

Blind Assistance Fund, Children Assistance Fund, Teacher Retirement System, Old Age Assistance Fund, and Farm-to-Market Road Fund. 

There is also 77 an allocation from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund to the Foundation School Fund which supplements the above-mentioned primary allocation to the Available School Fund, and any surplus remaining after all of the above allocations have been made reverts to the General Revenue Fund. 

Details concerning priorities and amounts distributed from the Omnibus Tax Clearance Fund are prescribed in the omnibus tax law. It is sufficient 1 78

SECTION 5--ANALYSIS OF OPERATIONS 

Returns

The Texas natural gas production tax has been in operation for almost 20 years. During this time it has never accounted for more than 1.59 per cent 81 of total State income, nor more than 2.83 per cent of total State taxes; yet it is numbered among the top tax revenue sources available to the State. During the last decade it began furnishing more than one million dollars, and by 1949 was bringing in almost nine million dollars annually. In the fiscal year 1950 it produced $9,659,024, 82 and this total is expected to be in excess of ten million dollars for the fiscal year ending August 31, 1951. 

As indicated in Table Gas-I, the increase in revenue from this tax since 1932 has been, proportionately, much greater than the increase in the volume of marketed gas. When the tax was first levied, the State collected an average of $294 per billion cubic feet of marketed production. In 1937, after the rate was increased (1936) from two per cent to three per cent of market value, collections averaged about $720 per billion cubic feet. 

Even though rates were increased only from three to 5.2 per cent, that is almost doubled, in the revision of the law in 1941, comparison of returns received in 1940 and 1942 indicates that collections more than tripled. 

Since both average price per thousand cubic feet (M. C. F.) and total marketed production in the State increased by only 22 per cent during this time it would appear that a substantial portion of the increase in collections resulted from the change in exemptions and collection and enforcement procedures effected at the time. 83

Review of Table Gas-I indicates that total collections have increased another 3.6 times since 1942, while marketed production as reported by the Bureau of Mines, has increased 2.6 times. During the same period, however, collections in terms of a billion cubic feet have increased by only 68 per cent, from $2040 in 1942 to $3423 in 1949. According to the marketed production information in Table Gas-II, which is based upon Comptrollers records, values, and averages, and the tax information in Table Gas-I, average collections per billion cubic feet of marketed production have increased since 1945, when imported gas was exempted from taxation, by about 74 per cent, from $1800 to $3132 per billion cubic feet.

Data are not conclusive, but indications are that average market value during the period from 1942 to 1949 has almost doubled. As revealed in Table 

Gas-II, it increased from 3.2 cents to 5.3 cents per M. C. F. from 1945 to 1948. 

According to another source of information, previously cited, but which is not completely comparable to the data quoted in Table II, the average value in 1942 was 2.2 cents per M. C. F. 84 If value has doubled and production has more than doubled since 1942, it might be expected that average collections in terms of billion cubic feet would have more than quadrupled in view of the fact that there has been no change in rate during the period. According to the Comptroller's reports, average collections per billion cubic feet have increased since 1945 by only 74 per cent, while average value increased by 61 per cent and total marketed vaolume by 37 per cent. Again, the indications are that average collections might have been expected to increase, by about one hundred per cent for the period instead of by only 74 per cent.

Obviously, there is an unexplained disparity between the increase in marketed production and the increase in average collections per billion cubic feet for both periods, 1942 as compared with 1949 and 1945 as compared with 1949.

Lest hasty conclusions be drawn from this information, however, it must be emphasized that in addition to the non-comparability of the information concerning average market value mentioned above with regard to the period 1942-1949, there are other qualifications upon the use of the data. 

First, tax collections are for fiscal years while marketed production and average market value are for calendar years. For trend purposes, however, this difference does not introduce a serious error into the analysis.
