209

Second, average market values or prices for natural gas production in Texas, regardless of the source of information, when used for tax analysis purposes, are of dubious merit. So long as any two value figures taken from the same source are used to indicate the trend in changes for a decade or a similar period they have some validity, but the use of such data for detailed analysis of tax returns is subject to considerable question. One has only to review the section on assessment herein where the problems of determining market value are discussed to be reminded of the analytical hazards involved. 

Third, even the production data used to calculate an average collection per billion cubic feet are questionable. For example, in the calculations to determine the average collection per billion cubic feet during 1949, the marketed production figure (2, 588, 921, 000, 000 cu. ft.) entered in Table Gas-I was derived from Bureau of Mines data. The amount does not agree with the 2,829,459,000,000 cubic feet of gas marketed in the State according to the Comptroller of Public Accounts; nor does it agree with the 2, 559, 029, 000, 000 cubic feet reported as marketed production during 1949 by the Texas Railroad Commission. 

In short, because of (1) inadequate data (or data that does not lend itself to comparison) and (2) the extremely difficult problem of determining market value (average or otherwise) for gas production in the State, it is hazardous to do much more than cite total tax collections. Further statistical analysis of tax collections has not been attempted in this study in view of the above-mentioned shortcomings of available data.

However, the lack of preciseness of available data, and the apparent discrepancies in the published totals of the reporting agencies might warrant the observation that at least the possibility exists of incomplete tax coverage. 

At least a definite lack of reporting certainty is evident. 

Administrative Costs 

Administrative costs for collection and enforcement of the natural gas tax are bound together with costs of administering the oil tax and 15 other taxes. 

Information concerning administrative costs common to the oil and gas taxes may be found in connection with the oil tax in Chapter VII of this report. Onehalf of one per cent of all natural gas tax collections are set aside to provide for its administration. 85 In the fiscal year 1949 this amounted to $43, 979. 

Since this sum is commingled with the $448, 118 collected and set aside for administering the oil tax, it is not possible to trace further its disposition. 

Table Oil-I reveals that only $180, 536 were acutally appropriated from the sum of the two amounts ($492, 097) for the operation of the entire Gross Receipts Division of the Comptroller's Department. The remainder reverted to the General Revenue Fund. 

85 

TEX. REV. CIV. STAT. ANN. (Vernon) 1950, art. 7047b, sec. 3. 

210

Apparently some 85 per cent of the time and effort of the Gross Receipts Division is devoted to administration of the oil and gas taxes. Five of the twenty-six personnel in the Austin office devote their time and efforts to administering the gas tax. Since the fourteen field personnel (auditors) a are not assigned exclusively to natural gas tax matters but work on both oil and other tax problems, no revenue/cost ratios or similar indices for administrative costs can be established. Supervisory personnel of the Austin office, however, estimate that field audits of oil tax records and reports require far less of the field auditors' time and efforts than do audits of gas tax records and reports.

It is evident that a greater share of the administrative cost of the Gross Receipts Division is represented by the demands of the gas tax administration than is represented by the enforcement activities for the oil tax. Thus, it is indicated that proceeds from the oil production tax, already utilized to pay for the enforcement of the other taxes administered by the Gross Receipts Division, also in some part pay for the administration of the gas production tax.

Comparison with Other States 

In a previous paragraph, it was indicated that analysis of the effect and impact of the natural gas tax within Texas and estimates of the revenue potential of the tax were filled with hazards because of inadequate and noncomparable data. Likewise, its comparison with natural gas taxes levied in other states is most difficult for similar reasons. 

Review of the latest edition of a generally recognized national compilation of tax data reveals that twelve states (Alabama, Arkansas, Florida, Georgia, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Michigan, Mississippi, New Mexico,

Oklahoma, and Tennessee) levy some kind of a severance or production tax 86 on natural gas for revenue purposes, such as that levied by Texas. But further inspection of the data indicates that few, if any, of the taxes levied by other states compare with Texas' tax.

Apparently,, only Mississippi levies at a rate greater than that of Texas. According to the above-mentioned publication the rate in Mississippi is six per cent of "value at point of production" or three mills per 1,000 cu. ft. (MCF), whichever is greater, as compared with the minimum of 11/150 cents per MCF and maximum of 5.2 per cent of market value levied in Texas.

Oklahoma levies a five per cent tax on "gross value." Whether or not such gross value or "value at point of production" compare with Texas' market value would require detailed analysis of the Oklahoma and Mississippi statutes. 86 Tax Systems, Commerce Clearing House, Inc., 12th Edition, 1950, pp. 

221 ff. 211

and of actual assessment, collection and enforcement procedures within these States; which has not been attempted in this survey. 

Louisiana levies a gas tax based on a flat rate of 3/10 cents per MCF. But it differentiates between, and taxes differently, natural and casinghead gasoline (two cents per barrel of 42 gallons) and distillates, condensates, and similar products (26 cents per barrel of 42 gallons). Comparison of the taxes levied in Louisiana on these products with the taxes levied on them by Texas would require considerable detailed analysis. And because of the complex market value computations involved, it is doubtful that the Texas tax could be converted, even for analytical purposes, to a comparable centsper-barrel value.

Arkansas, too, levies at a flat rate, 3/20 of one cent per MCF. 

There is another tax of not more than 1/2 mill per MCF which apparently varies according to conservation needs since it is paid to the Oil and Gas 

Commission of the State.

Two other States normally included in a discussion of oil and gas, namely, Kansas and California, do not levy a revenue tax, per se, or gas. 

They do, however, levy conservation taxes on this resource. California's tax varies according to conservation costs; Kansas tax is a flat rate of 

1/2 mill per MCF.

But as previously indicated, valid comparison between the Texas natural gas tax and its rates with those levied in other States would require a detailed examination of the appropriate statutes and collection procedures somewhat comparable to the analysis made herein of the tax levied in Texas. 

Full significance of such comparison could not be realized without consideration of other taxes bearing on the industry, such as ad valorem taxes, income taxes and the like. This, of course, was beyond the scope of this survey. 

For these reasons it must be observed that rate comparison between States should be used with considerable caution.

Gas Storage 

As has been mentioned in earlier sections, the practice of storing gas underground has developed extensively in recent years. Such storage is widely used in Michigan, throughout the Appalachian region and is being considered in Illinois. The reasons for such storage are threefold: (1) to have gas quickly available where and when needed, (2) as a means of improving the seasonal load factor both on the production and transmission plants, and (3) to improve the load factor at which gas cen be purchased, thereby obtaining a lower price.87 The practice was begun as early as 1919, but substantial expansion of underground storage facilities was not evident until the 1930's. 

Since that time, there has been constant and considerable expansion of the practice. 

87 Wagner, H. J., Underground Storage of Natural Gas in the Appalachian Area, World Oil, December 1950. 212

In West Virginia alone, which State was in 1949 the sixth largest producer of natural gas in the country, more than half of the State's gas supply in that year came from the Southwest. Much of this imported gas was stored. To accommodate the gas being piped in, underground storage capacity in West Virginia has been developed at a rapid rate, increasing at an average annual rate, during the past 15 years, of almost 22 billion cubic feet. In 1949 storage capacity in that State totaled 334 billion cubic feet. 88

Several types of storage facilities are used, but the most common is that utilizing depleted or partially depleted oil and gas sands. Most of these storage fields use Texas and Southwest-produced gas piped to them. 

The significance of this development to the natural gas tax in Texas is considerable, particularly during a period of rising gas prices. The full impact of this practice can scarcely be evaluated at this time, but it is clear that continued extension of the underground storage practice will have some effect upon the future revenues from this tax. 88 

Wagner, H. J., Underground Storage of Natural Gas in the Appalachian 

Area, World Oil, December 1950. 213

SECTION 6-- 

Review of the foregoing discussion of the natural gas tax in Texas indicates that although administrative organization is relatively simple, and the theory of the tax reasonably clear, the administrators are beset by numerous problems of enforcement. These arise from practical application of the assessment features of the tax and the lack of established policy in certain significant matters. They come about in part, too, as a result of the constantly changing practices and methods of the industry in which the tax functions. 

This section will summarize some of the problem areas and discuss some possible methods which could be used to deal with them. No method is intended as a recommendation or proposal, but each is presented simply as an approach which might be considered. Limitations of this survey have prevented comprehensive investigation of all possibilities and even of all aspects of those presented here. 

Some of the more significant problem areas of this tax are: (1) Determination of market value and its implications as to coverage and enforcement. (2) Application of exemptions. (3) Storage of gas for future use. (4) Determination of taxability of extracted liquid hydrocarbons. (5) Modernization of administration to keep pace with technology of industry, and its implications in relation to coverage. 

Determination of Market Value 

As has been discussed in Section 3, the practical determination of market value has caused considerable difficulty of administration. This has arisen because the law requires that the tax be levied on the value of the gas at the mouth of the well. Since measurable cash value in most production situations does not result until after several production steps are taken subsequent to removal from the earth, market (taxable) value at the well mouth must, of necessity, be an imputed or calculated figure. 

To make possible the determination of value at the well mouth, the statute provides several formulae to be applied. These, in substance, permit taking the cash value of the gas at a point in production where it is ascertainable, and "working back" to the value at the mouth of the well. 

The difficulties, for both the taxpayer and administrator, of deter mining the facts necessary to accurately compute the taxable value of gas under such procedures is manifest and has been discussed in some detail 214

earlier. However, consideration of the difficulties as such is not of extreme importance from the point of view of State revenues, although the problems undoubtedly greatly increase the administrative costs of this tax. What is most significant is that in spite of the complicated and troublesome procedure of value determination there is little assurance that the tax finally computed is accurate. To a very great extent the administrator is dependent upon both the integrity and accuracy of the taxpayer. The lack of preciseness in the revenue and production data available from the several sources publishing such figures at least indicates that there is some question as to whether coverage of this tax is complete.

What this means is simply that the tax law as it is now written requires the use of a procedure of tax determination so complicated and difficult that assurance of complete and accurate tax coverage is not feasible. Hence, it follows that the possibility of lost revenue to the State is ever existent, in spite of hard and sincere effort on the part of the tax administrator and the taxpayer.

Several possibilities exist which might better the present situation. 

None solves all the problems. 1 (A) Perhaps themost simple and direct, and a method suggested by many, is the replacement of the present tax based on a percentage of value, with a flat rate tax based simply on volume of gas produced. Such a flat rate tax, of course, has the same limitations as any other tax using this technique of assessment. It requires a regular review by the Legislature to keep the rate in accord with the current economic situation-revision upward when commodity prices increase, revision downward when commodity prices plunge. Such revisions are not always politically feasible for the Legislature to make, and thus there is strong argument for the per cent of value levy which presumably compensates for changing economic situations. This technique, however, applied to the gas production tax, is what has caused the difficulties of administration. (B) In view of this, there have been suggestions aimed at minimizing the limitations of the flat rate levy. One such suggestion is to have several separate and distinct levies to replace the single levy at present used. It is argued that the root of the difficulty at present is the attempt to assess all production of gas on the same basis, namely per cent of value at well mouth, whereas in reality a large portion of gas produced is not practically susceptible to such methods. 215

The basic levy, according to the proponents of this plan, could be a flat rate tax based on volume of production at the mouth of the sell. This. then, would be in the nature of a true severance tax, levied for the privilege of extracting natural resources from the earth of the State. The revenue from this levy might be used in whole or part, if severance tax principles are desired to be followed, for projects of permanent character. 

These might include schools, hospitals, parks, dams, conservation projects, research projects particularly designed to study problems of natural resources conservation, and the like. Such projects, it is contended by the proponents of the severance tax principles, would repay future generations for depletion of natural resources by the present generation. The rate for this levy might be periodically adjusted on the basis of such factors as rate of depletion of known reserves, changing importance of this resource in the State's economy, and significance of gas sales contracts on revenues and gas reserves. 

Coupled with this basic levy it has been suggested that there be additional levies based on a per cent of value. These additional levies would be applied, not at the mouth of the well, but at the different stages of production where such determination of value can actually be made. This would not necessarily be at the same stage of processing for all gas, but would vary depending upon what the gas was being processed for. Such an assessment procedure is actually not greatly different from the practical application of the present tax (as distinguished from the theory). Nor is it without existing counterparts, for the Texas carbon black tax is just such a tax on a secondary step in the processing of natural gas, and the gas gathering tax, utilized in Louisiana, is another example of the levy being applied on the production and processing of gas somewhere other than at the mouth of the well.

In brief, this plan would combine a basic flat rate severance or occupational-privilege tax with a per cent of value tax on the gross production of gas processors. (C) A third proposal advocated by some is to retain the present tax method with its market value concept, but to remove the determination-ofvalue problem from the individual taxpayer and the tax administrator. This can be done, it is suggested, by having a State-wide, or district-wide value set by the Railroad Commission periodically, using formulae similar to those now in the gas tax law. Thus, the computation of the tax would not have to be based on factors unique to each lease or producer, but would be simply the application of the tax rate to the set value and reported volume. 

Such a procedure would greatly relieve both the taxpayer and the tax administrator, and would free the latter for more extensive enforcement work. Having a State-wide or regional value established would, of course, cause some individual inequities, but it is contended that these would be no more than exist in almost every other tax law. 216

Exemptions 

As stated earlier the exemptions in the present law which relieve from taxation such gas as is "injected into the earth in this, State, unless sold for such purpose" and "gas used for lifting oil, unless sold for such purpose" underlie one of the principal practical difficulties in the administration of this tax. Since it is not possible to physically separate or recognize such gas when it is reproduced, from gas being produced for the first time, the tax administrator is faced with a fact question which is not easy to answer. Gas which is sold by the producer and subsequently injected into the earth loses its identity. Upon reproduction it is commingled with casinghead gas produced by the oil well, or with gas remaining in the original deposit into which injection was made. There are now producers who maintain that all of the current production is reproduced gas, i.e. that there is no gas in the deposit on which tax has not been paid. 

To find a practical solution to this problem it has been suggested that all gas injected into the earth be exempted from the tax whether sold or not. Another suggestion which has been made is to tax all production, whether reproduced gas or original gas, with the basic flat-rate levy discussed above ("B" under discussion of market value), but to exempt gas injected into the ground from the per cent of value secondary levy. 

A third alternative would be the requirement that complete records be maintained showing the amount of gas injected into the earth and amounts produced. Such a record would essentially require the carrying-forward of a cumulative balance. If these balances were audited and reconciled periodically by the tax administrator, there would be no reason for maintenance of such records beyond the last audit period. 

Storage of Gas 

Arising in part from the problem of application of present exemptions is the problem involving the underground storage of gas. This has been discussed in Section 5. Such storage is rapidly becoming a common practice in many parts of the country. The volume of such stored gas is greatly increasing year by year, and already represents a sizeable portion of production totals. A large proportion, if not most, of gas being so stored is Texas gas. 

Although as yet the practice does not appear to be widely prevalent in Texas, tax administrators indicate that the possibilities of such storage in the State are not remote. One reason for adoption of this practice is to equalize seasonally the lpad factor on transmission systems. Another, to take advantage of present prices in view of future price increases. 217

From the point of view of tax administration the practict of storing gas raises two significant questions of policy. One is the question of taxability of such gas--at time of production or at time of removal from storage. 

The other is the significance of this practice in the light of the theory of severance taxation.

Under the present law, the tax is to be paid on gas "produced and saved. " However, one of the exemptions is for gas injected into the ground. 

There is little question so long as the gas is sold for storage. But, if producers move their own gas and return it to a depleted formation without a sale being involved, the question arises whether the present exemptions apply and no tax liability exists or whether liability accrues at the time of production. In the case of the former, such gas would then be taxable when it is reproduced and sold. 89

In the event that a producer wishes to transport his gas to a new area for storage and desires to pay the tax at the time of production, the present law is not clear if no sale is involved. Normally, though not always, there is some storage loss to the depleted formation. Therefore, it would appear that if the tax is paid upon reproduction rather than original production, there would be a virtual exemption of storage and transmission losses incidental to the storage procedure.

A clear statutory statement of policy in this regard would be of help to the administrator. If under the present procedure it is determined that the tax shall apply at the time of original production, there would be the problem of determining value where no sale is made. This problem would be avoided if the law expressly prohibited the payment of taxes at time of first production but required them when the gas was reproduced. 

Under the suggestion discussed in conjunction with exemptions above, some thought has been that the application of the basic flat-rate tax at time of production, whether original or reproduced, as an occupational levy would avoid any possibility of loss to the State from transmission or storage. In such event, the secondary levy ("B" above in discussion of market value) would not apply until the gas was removed from storage, at which time the prevailing value would be the basis of assessment. 

But in addition to the assessment problem, the rapidly-growing practice of storage of gas raises another question: What is the long-range social and economic effect of the practice? There are some who contend that the continued use of Texas gas to fill reservoirs in other parts of the 89 

Opinion of the Attorney General, No. V-335, August 13, 1947. 

218

country is an unjustified depletion of Texas resources unless the return to the State is sufficient to pay present and future generations for the depletion of irreplaceable resources. Under the present tax method the contractual agreements of the industry have a large impact upon the return to the State.

Many suggestions have been made to adjust or change the present situation. Some, such as price-fixing, have brought about tremendous political and legal repercussions. However, there are some students in the field of resources taxation who maintain that such measures are not necessary. Instead, much of the desired effect can be achieved without the difficulties of price-fixing by relatively simple changes in the tax. 

One of the changes that has been suggested is the adjustment of the floor or minimum on the gas tax. This, it is contended, would protect the 

State against loss of adequate recompense for its resources through lowprice sales contracts. Another is the use of the primary flat rate levy along with secondary levies as discussed above. Such a plan, it is said, would permit the Legislature to adjust the tax rate on each portion of the industry separately without of necessity affecting the entire industry. 

Coupled with this problem again is the allocation of revenues. 

Should severance tax principles be observed and a portion of revenues from this tax be put into projects of permanent character and lasting benefit?

Extracted Liquid Hydrocarbons 

The law at present reads that "all hydrocarbons . . . recovered from gas by means of a separator or by other non-mechanical methods, incidental to the production of said gas. " shall be taxed at the same rate as oil. Technological developments have made very difficult the determination of where recovery is by separator equipment, when the method is mechanical, and whether or not such recovery is incidental to the production of gas.

It has been suggested that for the circumstances to which this provision applies that the qualifying statements in the law be removed so that a practical determination can be made. There have also been suggestions made by some who have worked with such taxes, that a tax on gas by volume anda separate levy on extracted or recovered products by value at the same rate as oil would go far toward easing the problems now found in the application of the gas tax. 219

Modernization of Administration 

The industry upon which this tax is applied has grown complex through the years. Scientific progress has made great advances, and the resulting technological processes are changing year by year. Administratively the tax law has not kept pace with the developments in the industry. The tools given to the administrator in many instances fit the industry of 20 and 30 years ago, but not today. It would, however, be difficult after a brief survey to identify every administrative area which can or should be changed or modernized. However, manual posting and personal consideration of the multidudinous factors which are changing daily, and all of which have a bearing on the tax revenues received, are not fast enough nor accurate enough to keep abreast of the industry's activities.

Lack of integration, too, is evidenced in the differences of totals which appear in the reports of the Railroad Commission and the Comptroller Use of parallel data by the two agencies with little or no cross-utilization of information also points up this lack. 

To explore the possibilities of integrating some of the parallel activities and data and thus save to the State some money now used for administration; and more importantly to survey the possibilities of achieving more precise reporting and accounting procedures, to the end of achieving reconciliation of the reports of the agencies dealing with the gas industry, steps might well be taken to study the feasibility of use of automatic punch-card equipment and other procedures. 

In Conclusion

The natural gas tax, like other taxes or natural resources, finds itself today in an environment of complicated technology, and marketing techniques which are based on national, not State, considerations. The tax administration, in large part borrowed from the oil production tax, is archaic in relation to the industry to which it applies. Practical application of the tax is difficult. Certainty of tax coverage is not assured. 

In view of the tremendous potential development of the industry, a reconsideration of the tax now might be well warranted. A recognition of the complexities of the industrial processes to which the tax applies is essential. Such recognition has not been evident in the past. 

Significant, too, is the consideration of the tax in regard to its effect on conservation of natural resources. Only slight attention has been evidenced in the past to the role of taxation in conservation. Considerable feeling exists that the two activities should not be joined. On the other hand, some thought has been expressed that a closer coordination should be effected. 220

Chapter IX MOTOR FUEL TAX SECTION 1--HISTORICAL AND LEGAL DEVELOPMENT 

For proper perspective the motor fuel tax in Texas must be viewed against the backdrop of the development of the automotive industry in the United States. Without such an approach, the tax stands only as a selective sales tax productive of considerable revenue, applied against a particular group of taxpayers already taxed by the Federal Government. The significance of the tax,even when viewed out of its historical perspective, is greatby virtue of its dominant position in the tax structure of the State. However, its basis and its limited scope can be understood only through a knowledge of the motivations which brought about the tax and an understanding of the developments which provided a setting for such a tax law. 

Rapid Increase of Cars and Highways

These developments were the phenomenal growth of the automotive industry and the concomitant spectacular spread of hardsurfaced roads across the country, and most particularly their early growth and spread. 

Highways and motor vehicles have been probably the most significant factors in the progress and development of the country in the past 50 years. 

They have completely changed our way of life; they form essential links in production of all kinds; and they have made possible great changes in our system of education and in our social life. Yet this tremendous impact was scarcely discernible prior to World War I. 

Before then, highways were virtually of local significance only, State highway departments, of which there were but 22 in existence in 1910, spent their time on construction of short sections of roads. Less than one-half million motor vehicles were in use in the United States in 1910, and these 1 were operated largely in cities.

By 1915, however, two and one-half million motor vehicles were in use in the United States. Their owners were vigorous supporters of the movement for better roads, particularly for the improvement of roads between cities. As more and more cars came from the factories, the clamor 1 

Fifty Years of Accomplishment on State Highways, T. H. MacDonald, U. S. 

Commissioner of Public Roads, in State Government, June, 1950. 

221

for more and better roads grew. By 1920 there were more than nine million motor vehicles in use. In Texas alone, the 40 thousand vehicles of 1915 had multiplied to about a half-million in 1920.2 

To accommodate this tremendous outpouring of cars from the automobile factories, road and highway development was required at a rate inconceivable even a decade before. The demand for money in large amounts was urgent. 

To meet the need, the Federal Government initiated the program of federal aid for highways in 1916, requiring each State, in order to be eligible for such aid, to have an adequate highway department and to match the federal funds granted. 

By the end of 1917, every State had organized a highway department. The Texas department was organized in 1917, thus permitting it to take advantage of Federal aid.

Oregon Enacts First Gasoline Tax 

State revenues from registration fees soared with the increase of motor vehicles, but these alone did not meet the demands for road building and improvement. States began to search for new sources of revenue to fulfill the needs for roads and highways. In 1919 Oregon placed a tax on gasoline to provide funds for highways and was quickly followed the same year by Colorado, New Mexico, and North Dakota. By 1929, every State was collecting a gasoline tax. A federal motor fuel tax was enacted in 1932.

To quote from a survey recently conducted in New York: "The spread of the gasoline tax over the entire country in only a ten-year period was unique. 

Enactment of the tax with such relative speed seems to have been due to the attitude of the groups most directly concerned. While proposals to impose new taxes generally create vigorous opposition from the groups to be affected, the gasoline tax encountered little opposition from the motor vehicle owners, gasoline producers, automobile makers, and others . . 113 

New Tax Unique in Its Popularity 

The motor fuel tax has proved to be a relatively popular tax. It is virtually alone among major levies as a "benefit" tax; i. e., one based on the benefit theory of taxation. This theory rests on the premise that the recipient of special benefits should pay for them. A motorist who pays the tax can see a more direct relationship between the tax he pays and the service he receives than is the case with other taxes. As the tax is paid on a per gallon basis, it is paid roughly in proportion to the use to which the purchaser puts the highways. 2 3 

Highway Statistics, Summary to 1945, Public Roads Admin., 1947, p. 26. 

Highway Finance and Taxation in New York, Citizens Public Expenditure 

Survey, Inc., February, 1950. 222

The convenience of tax payment has been another reason for its general acceptance. It is so unobtrusively a part of the price per gallon in many cases that the motorist pays little attention to its imposition. No special interests have opposed the tax, since it has in large part been used for the betterment of roads, and the automobile industry and most other industrial and commercial groups have had a strong interest in road betterment. 

Its simple and relatively inexpensive method of collection and the fact that it bears some relation to all of the more important criteria of road destructiveness have also brought it considerable support. 4 

Thus, the gasoline tax, conceived as it was in a period of specific financial need, was almost universally accepted as an ideal solution for a difficult problem and an equitable fulfillment of an ever-increasing need. 

The motor vehicles of the early twenties disclosed no prophecy of their counterparts of a quarter-century later. Then, the range of weight and load between the lightest and the heaviest was not so great but that the different deteriorating effects of each upon the highways could be considered as being approximately proportionate to the difference in gasoline consumption. 

At that time, too, the widely ramified influence of highway motor transport upon all phases of life which later grew up could not be foreseen. Indirect benefits were not easily apparent, if any existed, and there was little question that the highways were built for the almost sole benefit of those who drove their own vehicles over them. In the earlier era, too, mechanized farming, public aviation, and many of the present day uses of motor fuel were virtually unknown. 

It is no wonder that there was such quick and general acceptance of the new tax. 

And, more significantly, it can be understood why, after such a popular introduction to the new tax, the States and the public were loath to change from the initial statutory patterns, even after the development of the automotive industry and other forms of internal combustion engines had almost completely changed the picture which existed in the early twenties. 

First Texas Gasoline Tax 

Texas was one of the 35 States which quickly followed the lead of Oregon and by 1923 had similar taxes on their books. The first Texas gasoline tax, destined for a number of years to be the leading tax revenue producer in the State, became effective April 1, 1923. Stating that "public free schools and highways require additional funds, " the Legislature imposed "an occupation tax equal to one cent upon the gallon. . upon what is commercially known. as gasoline . . . 115 wording of this law. The influence of the Texas Constitution is clear in the 

Proceedings, National Tax Association, 1930. 

5 Tex. Laws, 1923, 38th Leg., ch. 134. 223

The Question of a General Taxing Power 

6 In the Texas Constitution four types of taxes are designated as taxes the Legislature can levy: property taxes, poll taxes, occupation taxes, and income taxes (Art. VIII, Sec. 1). This enumeration has led to what has been described as an "occupation tax complex" of the Legislature; i. e., the labeling of many taxes as occupation taxes even though they might not be ordinarily so considered. (Texas Taxes by M. M. Mahany). Although a subsequent section of the Constitution (Art. VIII, Sec. 17) reads "The speci fication of the objects and subjects of taxation shall not deprive the Legislature of the power to require other subjects or objects to be taxed . . ., "' there has been no clear-cut decision as to whether or not the four types of taxes are the only kinds the Legislature may impose. Although recent years have brought a legislative swing away from the occupation tax complex and there has been a wide acceptance of the notion of a general legislative taxing power, the issue has not been completely settled and the effect of the seemingly limiting provisions of the Constitution still influence tax legislation. The questions of what is or is not an occupation tax and whether sales or excise taxes can be levied without being designated and intended as occupation taxes are complicated by another section of the Constitution which provides that "one-fourth of the revenue from State occupation taxes and poll taxes shall be set apart annually for the benefit of the public free schools. ..." (Art. VII, Sec. 3). Thus, the question of occupation taxes is one not merely of constitutional power to tax but one which vitally affects the constitutional power of the Legislature to control the disposition of funds. 

Designated an Occupation Tax

The initial gasoline tax was levied on the sale of gasoline by wholesale dealers. Payment was made directly to the Treasurer and was based on monthly reports submitted by these dealers to the Comptroller. No refunds or exemptions were provided. And, following the requirements of the State Constitution in regard to occupation taxes, one-fourth of all revenues from the tax were earmarked for the public school fund. The remaining three-fourths of the revenue were earmarked for construction and maintenance of the public highways of the State. Thus, as a result of this apparently innocuous expression of the "occupation-tax complex," the stage was set by this initial act to plunge this tax law, a few years later (1929), into the middle of one of the most controversial problems connected with the taxation of gasoline--that of diversion of highway-user revenues for purposes other than highway work, Texas Constitution of 1876. 224

Later the same year (1923), the new tax law was amended twice. Controls for enforcement purposes, in the form of required reports from wholesale dealers, were added. Collection of the tax was given to the Comptroller, instead of the Treasurer, as before. 7 In cases where suit was to be brought to recover unpaid taxes, etc., the Attorney General was charged with the duty to do so; the earlier acts had split this responsibility among the Attorney General, district attorney, or any county attorney. This latter change eliminated a fee system which the earlier acts had written into the enforcement provisions of the law. 

It is interesting to note that the Legislature still carefully described this tax as an occupation tax and ostensibly left the assessment of it at the wholesale level. Again, no exemptions or refunds were mentioned, and no provisions were enacted to permit or restrict the passing of the cost of the tax on to the consumer. "revenues now available to 

First Change of Rate 

Apparently the new tax law proved satisfactory for several years. At any rate, no changes were made until 1927.9 At that time, for the reason that public free schools are insufficient to provide for an efficient public school system for the ensuing scholastic year . "' the 

Legislature made the first rate change in the history of the tax. The rate was boosted from the previous one cent per gallon to three cents until September 1, 1928, and thereafter two cents. However, though ostensibly the reason for the increase was the need for money for the schools, the distribution of revenues was kept the same--one-fourth to the school fund, three-fourths for roads. No other changes were made in the law.

In view of the long period following 1923 which had brought forth no changes in the gasoline tax law, the action of the Legislature in 1929 was somewhat surprising. However, several events had preceded this action. 

One was a court decision in 1927, 10 which though upholding the validity of the 1923 acts, pointed up the looseness and vagueness of several of the provisions. Specifically, it brought out, among other things, the broadness of definition of "wholesale dealer, " stating that the terms of the acts suggested no line to be drawn between the popular meaning of wholesale and retail, and actually included every transaction in gasoline at wholesale or retail. The court stated that "An apparent overabundance of caution, lest something escape, is to be found in the declaration that a producer, refiner, manufacturer, or compounder who sells at retail direct or through an agent to the consumer shall be regarded as a 'wholesale dealer. " 

Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 2d C. S., 1923, ch. 55. 

Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 3rd C. S., 1923, ch. 5. 

Tex. Laws, 40th Leg., 1927, ch. 93. 10 

State v. Pioneer Oil & Refining Company, 292 SW 869, (Tex. Comm. App. 1927). 225

Another was a later court decision (1928) which held the gasoline tax to be an occupational excise tax and not a tax on the gas itself. 11 In this case the court stated that "' ... while nomenclature cannot give character in beliance of fact, naught is exhibited in the statute, or in the effect to warrant any thought that use of gallonage as a mere measure of the exaction was not done in good faith rather than in a covert effort to impose a burden on the gasoline itself, the proceeds of the sales thereof, or on the purchaser." 11 Still another event which appears to have had considerable influence on the 1929 action of the Legislature was the submission by the Comptroller of his annual report12 to the Governor November 1, 1928, which read in part: 

Especially is it urgent that our gasoline tax law be strengthened in a number of respects, especially (a) to more clearly define 'liquid fuels'; (b) to impose the tax uppn the product for both sale and use; (c) to fix an arbitrary allowance for evaporation, loss, waste, etc.; (d) requiring permits to be obtained by distributors, importers, and consumers before engaging in business; (e) requiring adequate surety bonds from such distributors, importers, and consumers; (f) providing for the proper labeling of tank cars, automobile trucks, tank wagons, and other containers and receptacles for the transportation of liquid fuels; (g) increasing the rate of tax; (h) providing for the keeping of permanent records of transactions and access to same by the Comptroller; (i) authorizing the Comptroller to arbitrarily fix the amount of any account where the records are not adequate or available, and (j) prescribing penalties." 

Major Changes in 1929

The effect of the court decisions and the suggestions of the Comptroller are interesting to observe in the 1929 law. Small heed was paid to the pointed language of the court in regard to the broad definition of wholesale dealer, as virtually a verbatim copy of the provisions of the 1923 law was included in the 1929 act, plus the addition of new paragraphs which put users of gasoline under the taxable category. How this inclusion of gasoline users in the taxable occupation group was reconciled with the 1928 court ruling that the tax was an occupational excise, and with the implication of that ruling that if the tax imposed a burden on the purchaser it might not be valid, is difficult to see. 

However, in spite of the apparent disregard for the rulings of the courts, the 1929 law did provide for virtually every suggestion of the Comptroller, ins cluding a boost in the rate to four cents a gallon and in addition provided exemptions for the first time. 13 These allowed refund's for taxes paid on purchases of gasoline for non-highway use. This action brought up one of the most controversial problems related to the gasoline tax. 

Grayburg Oil Company v. State, 3SW 2d 427, (Tex. Comm. App. 1928).

Annual Report of the Comptroller of Public Accounts, 1928. 

11 12 13 

Tex. Laws, 41st Leg., 2d C. S., 1929, ch. 88, sec. 17. 226

Basis of the Benefit Theory 

It will be remembered that the search by the States for money for roads and highways to accommodate the rapidly growing number of motor vehicles motivated the enactment of gasoline tax laws. Taxes on gasoline when such were first enacted were essentially taxes on highway-users, as other uses of gasoline at that time were relatively insignificant. Thus, from the beginning the benefit aspects of the tax were predominant in most States. As stated in a recent publication of the Council of State Governments ". . . the suitability of user imposts for the financing of roads arises from the relationship which exists between such roads and the use of motor vehicles. "14 ..

As a benefit tax to pay for the privilege of using roads and highways, it is understandable that the gasoline tax not be applied to non-highway users. 

Hence, in most States during the nineteen-twenties, exemptions were written into the law providing refunds to those taxpayers who used the gasoline for other than highway purposes. These exemptions were occasioned particularly by the rapid spread of use of gasoline-powered farm equipment, which in the early days of the gasoline tax was not significant. Thus, the benefit features of the tax were upheld--gasoline taxes were exacted in order to build and maintain roads, and only road-users were to pay the tax. 

The Diversion Problem 

However, by virtue of the occupation-tax label on the Texas gasoline levy, and evidently by intent of the Legislature (particularly in 1927 when the rate was increased for the ostensible reason of providing more funds for public schools), at no time since the initial enactment of the tax in 1923 has the gasoline tax in this State been a true benefit tax. This fact results from the "diversion" of gasoline tax money for other than highway purposes. Until 

1929, this diversion could not technically be quarreled with as taking money which rightfully should have gone for roads and using it elsewhere, because nowhere in the earlier statutes was it stated nor implied that the tax was imposed for the privilege of using the highways of the State. Such implication could be derived only from a review of the general situation existing at the time the statutes were enacted. However, it could just as easily be maintained that the intent behind the gasoline tax was simply to pass another tax law to provide for revenues to be expended wherever the Legislature felt the funds were needed.

But, with the passage of the 1929 law, providing non-highway-user exemptions, such was no longer the case. It was obvious that the intent was to levy the tax for the privilege of using the roads and highways. Yet as an 14 Federal Grants - In-Aid, The Council of State Governments, p. 228, 1949.

227

occupation tax, one-fourth of the revenues collected were still earmarked for the Available School Fund. Obviously, the tax was not a true benefit tax. 

Either the tax rates had been fixed so high that they produced more revenue than was required to maintain the road plant in satisfactory condition, or the plant was being permitted to deteriorate in quality or extent because of the diversion of road funds to other purposes. It would seem that in any case an element of inequity was involved. It would seem also that taxing highway users of gasoline for support of public schools while exempting non-highway users of gasoline from such tax for the support of schools raises a question of discrimination.

However, the problem of diversion is not unique to Texas. As a matter of fact, it grew to significant proportions throughout the country in the 1930's. 

Only three per cent of all State motor-user taxes were diverted by the States in 1931, but by 1941 more than 15 per cent were diverted. The trend was changed, however, during the war years and afterward. 

Federal Action on Diversion 

15

The seriousness of the diversion problem can be gauged from the action of the National Congress, which twice passed legislation to discourage diversion of highway revenues in the States. The first enactment in 1934 attempted to penalize such diversion by limiting federal aid in cases where a State diverted more highway money than it had prior to 1934. The second enactment in 1936 gave a bonus to States not diverting highway moneys.

As can be seen, the two major problems of the gasoline tax are those of diversion of the taxes collected to non-highway uses, and refunds of taxes (essentially exemptions) to non-highway users of gasoline. The two are inseparable.

Both problems actually came into being as such in Texas with the enactment of the 1929 law. And underlying both is a vital question of policy--does a levy on highway users alone take sufficiently into consideration the generally acknowledged fact that virtually everyone and every facet of our social and economic life benefits to some degree from our highway system. Some States have answered this by permitting only partial refunds to non-highway users of gasoline.16 

As might have been expected after such a major revision of the tax law as occurred in 1929, several amendments were forthcoming soon afterward. 

The all-inclusive term "wholesale, " utilized in every act from the very first, was continued in the 1931 amendments, clarified somewhat and definitely broadened to include "the first distribution, sale, or use" in the State. The four-cent rate was continued, and refunds were extended to cover losses by fire or other- 17 wise. 15 16 Federal Grants-In-Aid, The Council of State Governments, 1949, p. 228.

State Tax Guide, Summaries By States, Commerce Clearing House, 1950. 

17 Tex. Laws, 42d Leg., 1931, ch. 98. 228

Loss Allowance Adopted

A one per cent deduction from the taxable gallonage was written into the law, too, in 1931 to cover loss by evaporation. The 1929 law, though permitting such deduction, did not specify the amount. And, for the first time, it was provided that the tax was to be posted separately from the price of the gasoline "so that the tax will be paid by the ultimate consumer to the distributor." 

In 1932 the State assumed the highway bonded indebtedness of counties and road districts incurred for construction of highways constituting part of the system of State Highways. 18 A provision changing the distribution of gasoline tax revenues was included in this legislation. One-fourth of the revenue was still to go to the Available Free School Fund (after refunds); one-fourth to the newly created County and Road District Highway Fund; and the remainder to the State Highway Fund.

This was followed, in 1933, by a complete revision of the gasoline tax 19 law. Essentially, this revision simply put the 1929 act and the 1931 amendments into one act and added certain provisions designed to facilitate the administration and enforcement of the tax. Of these new provisions, one of the most significant was the establishment of a fund for the Comptroller to cover administration and enforcement of the tax. This fund was to be two-thirds of one per cent of the gross collections. The act also created a Motor Fuel Tax 

Division in the Comptroller's Office. 

Of interest is the evidently deliberate omission, for the first time, of the term "wholesale." This tax was no longer an occupation tax on wholesale dealers in gasoline. Though long delayed, this act gave recognition to the 

1927 opinions of the court in State v. Pioneer Oil and Refining Company in regard to the term "wholesale," but still continued to postulate the existence of a tax on occupation. The usual pattern of amendments following a major revision of the law occurred after the 1933 enactment. Because this act "'contains certain defects and omissions which seriously hinder and impair the effective administration and enforcement . . "the Legislature passed an act in 1935 designed to facilitate administration and enforcement. 20 Additional records were required of distributors and dealers and their form and content was prescribed in some detail, wording of certain provisions of the tax law was made more clear, and in general the tax law was tightened up." In addition, the administrationenforcement fund of the Comptroller was raised to one per cent of the gross revenues. 18 19 

Tex. Laws, 42d Leg., 3rd C. S., 1932, ch. 13, sec. 6. 

Tex. Laws, 43rd Leg., 1933, ch. 44. 20 

Tex. Laws, 44th Leg., 1935, ch. 240. 229

Refund Dealers' Permit System 

Since 1929 the administration of the gasoline tax had been confronted by the difficult problem of refunds. In 1939, however, the first major change 21 in the procedure of granting fefunds was written into the law. This called for a system of permits for dealers or distributors of gasoline sold subject to refund, thereby appreciably reducing the number of sellers of refund gasoline and aiding materially the enforcement of the law. It also made specific the requirement that invoices of exemption be signed by the purchaser of refund gasoline. Another control was added by a provision for use of certain prescribed and serially-numbered invoice forms. But the provisions for allocating funds to the Available School Fund were continued. 

This retention of the concept of the gasoline tax as an occupation tax and the resultant diversion of its re venues to the schools is particularly interesting during the period of the 1930's in view of the federal actions taken to discourage such diversion, as discussed above. Although for the country as a whole, the diversion of 15 per cent of gasoline tax revenues by the States to other than highway purposes was the peak of such diversion, Texas, throughout the history of the tax, has never diverted less than 25 per cent. 

The year 1941 saw a complete revision of the gasoline tax law. The rewritten act included all of the amendments since the last revision in 1933, and is substantially the law today. 22 The most significant addition was the levy of a tax on the use of "liquefied gases and other liquid fuels" which particularly included diesel fuel.

The tax, in less than a score of years, had evolved from an occupation tax on wholesale dealers in gasoline to an excise tax on motor fuels used to propel motor vehicles upon the public highways of this State. Yet, in spite of this radical evolution, the word "occupational" was still used in the 1941 law to describe the tax, and the distribution of revenues still included one-fourth to the schools.

Other Fuels Made Taxable 

The new portions of the law relating to liquefied gases and other liquid fuels set rates of four cents per gallon on the first category and eight cents per gallon on the latter category. This tax is, interestingly enough, not described as an occupation tax but simply as an excise tax on all users of these commodities. The distribution of revenues, however, is the same as for revenues procured under the gasoline tax. Permits for users were provided 21 22 

Tex. Laws, 46th Leg., 1939, ch. 3.

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, ch. 184, Art. XVII. 

230

for, and in general the tax administration was similar to that for gasoline, except that the new tax was applied on the user instead of the dealer or distributor, save for certain stated types of instances. A fee system to compensate District or County Attorneys when used in seizure cases was reinstated; this, however, was a flat-fee system instead of the percentage type written into the early 1923 Acts. 

Pipeline operators were included as distributors and required to have a permit by virtue of a 1943 amendment to the law. The war-time act also made more liberal the refund provisions of the 1941 act in regard to sales to the United States Government and lowered the rate on "other liquid fuels" (diesel fuel) to six cents per gallon.

By 1951, the Texas motor fuel tax law has developed to perhaps full maturity, along with the kindred tax laws of the other States of the nation. 

It holds an established spot in the tax structure of the State as one of the leading revenue producers. It has grown through the period of administrative development and has arrived at a reasonably stable and workable administration. It has kept pace with developments in other States and has, at least in general, achieved its original purpose of providing substantial revenues for the ever-growing highway and road system of the State. 

Perhaps at this stage, the motor fuel tax will pass into a new phase of its existence, a phase of mature development rather than youthful expansion and growth. Such a phase could be characterized by refinement of procedures, by readjustment of application to better conform to contemporary economic and social requirements, and by re-evaluation of its basis and its motivating principles. 231

The original gasoline tax act of 1923 made no effort to provide for a unified or coordinated administrative organization to levy, collect, and enforce the motor fuel tax. 23 It simply gave to the State Treasurer the duty of receiving tax payments submitted; to the Comptroller the duty of prescribing the form for, and receiving from dealers, the reports of gasoline sales; to the Attorney General, district attorney or county attorney the duty of bringing suit in cases of default in reporting or payment of taxes. Apparently it was initially intended to divide the functions of assessment, collection and enforcement among the several agencies. However, the difficulties inherent in such an uncoordinated organization, and the glaring lack of any specification of responsibility brought early changes. 

Early Administrative Structure Simple 

The second revision of the initial act the same year spelled out more clearly the intent of the Legislature to vest in the Comptroller the primary responsibility for administration of the tax. 24 He was not only to receive the reports of sales but was to receive the collections of the tax based on the se reports, and by implication was held responsible for the enforcement of the law, aided by the Attorney General in cases where suits were to be brought. 

Thus, in this tax, the organizational form of the administration of the law was made quite simple soon after its inception. During the intervening years, as more and more provisions were added to the law, complexities of administrative organization crept in. Fortunately, however, none of these fundamentally altered the basically simple organization established in 1923. 

Under the present law the Comptroller is still vested with the primary responsibility for administration of the tax. It is his duty, according to the law, "to collect, supervise and enforce the collection of all taxes, penalties, costs, and interest due . . . under the provisions of this Article, and to that end the Comptroller is hereby vested with all of the power and authority conferred by this Article. "125

He is also authorized to promulgate rules and regulations for the enforcement of the provisions of the law. 26 Thus in its essentials, a centralized administration of the tax law has been effected. 23 

Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 1923, ch. 134. 

24 Tex. Laws, 38th Leg., 3rd C. S., 1923, ch. 5. 

25 TEX. REV. CIV. STAT. ANN. (Vernon, Supp. 1949) Art. 7065b-23. 

26 

Tex. Laws, 47th Leg., 1941, ch. 184, Art. XVII. 

232

Many Agencies Now Administer Law Some

However, several other agencies are given duties and responsibilities by the law in connection with the collection and enforcement of the tax. of these play only indirect roles, but several are given by the law positive and direct responsibilities and authorities. The enumeration of other agencies and public offices who by the tax statute participate to some degree in the administration of the motor fuel tax includes the State Treasurer, the Attorney General, the Division of Weights and Measures of the Department of Agriculture, the Railroad Commission, highway patrolmen, officers of the Department of Public Safety, Sheriffs, Constables, other peace officers, and District and County Attorneys.

It would be difficult, indeed, to imagine a situation involving so many public agencies remaining simple in operation. Yet, essentially such has been the case in this tax law. The explanation for it lies, as in other laws, in the fact that administrative practices have cut through the complexities of the law and have found satisfactory and workable procedures which cannot be regarded as contravening the law, but which essentially ignore many of the confusing complexities written into the statute.

The Attorney General shares certain enforcement responsibilities with the Comptroller. He is authorized to inspect records of distributors and dealers, he has the duty of filing forfeiture proceedings upon request of the Comptroller, and the duty of prosecuting suits in cases of non-payment of taxes. He also is responsible for preparation of the bond form required in conjunction with user permits, for approval of the bond from prescribed by the Comptroller for distributor's bonds, for approval of the amount of bond in cases of injunction bonds, and for custody of seizure bonds in cases arising from such action. 27 

Treasurer Collects Some Taxes Directly

The State Treasurer is made responsible by the law for the custody of injunction bonds, for holding of cash and securities deposited in lieu of bonds in certain types of situations, and for direct collection of taxes in certain forfeiture cases. Sheriffs and other peace officers are included in the enforcement phases of the tax by the statutes. Such officers are specifically empowered to stop motor vehicles for investigation purposes, and given certain seizure powers. 

District and County Attorneys are required to file forfeiture proceedings when requested to do so by the Comptroller. 27 

Tex. Laws, 45th Leg., 1937, ch. 310. 233

The Division of Weights and Measures and the Railroad Commission are involved to the extent that the statutes permit certificates issued by them to be accepted in place of inspections by the Comptroller as proof of conformity to the law, thus placing on these agencies a part of the responsibility for enforcing the motor fuel tax law. It is apparent that in such a situation effective coordination is essential, and yet extremely difficult to achieve. 

Complexity of Bonding Procedures

This multiplicity of agencies has apparently developed less from intent of the Legislature than as the result of the confusion which almost inevitably results from a series of amendments to the law. A uniform bonding procedure would greatly reduce the present confusion and simplify the organizational form of the tax administration. At the present time the law specifies that the Comptroller, Treasurer and Attorney General all participate in the bonding procedure, and that each type of bond be processed differently.

In the matter of collections, full responsibility is placed on the Comptroller, and all tax payments are made to him, except in cases where the 

Comptroller waives proceedings for forfeiture in which case the offending taxpayer pays directly to the Treasurer an amount equal to twice the tax due. 

Why this one type of collection is made an exception to the general practice is not readily apparent.

Inspection authority is not clear in the statutes. Both the Comptroller and Attorney General are specifically mentioned in some sections of the law as having the authority to inspect records of various kinds. In other sections only the Comptroller is specifically given this authority. In practice, apparently, no record inspections are made by the Attorney General for purposes of administration and enforcement. If such authority is not inherent in his office in cases where suits are being instituted, a general authority to permit him to get necessary information for any legal actions would simplify to a great extent the present law which does not reflect the actual practice and which puts the Attorney General into the actual administration of the tax law. 

Question of Limits of Police Authority 

Since the tax law provides that a violation of practically any section of the Act may constitute a misdemeanor or a felony, it is not entirely clear why specific authorization is necessary to be given in two sections of the law to police officers to stop motor vehicles for the purposes of investigation. 

These specific authorizations tend to give an impression that the right to stop vehicles by police officers is exclusive of any other authority to enforce provisions of this Act.

Thus, it can be seen that the basically simple organizational structure has been complicated in the law by a lack of uniform bonding procedures, by division of the function of collection, by a complex authorization of police powers to several agencies, and by indirection in the delegation of enforcement functions. 234

SECTION 3--ASSESSMENT 

The motor fuel tax in Texas includes a tax on gasoline, and a more recently enacted tax on liquefied gases and other liquid fuels (principally diesel fuel). This discussion concerns itself largely with the tax on gasoline, which is by far the larger revenue producer. 

The tax on gasoline is a flat-rate tax of four cents per gallon based on the volume of the commodity sold, distributed or used. It is imposed at the time of first sale, distribution or use; however, it is expressly provided the ultimate consumer should actually bear the burden, inasmuch as the tax is permitted to be added to the sales price of the gasoline by the initial distributor and any subsequent seller. The tax is limited only to gasoline used for "propulsion of any motor vehicle upon the highways of this State. "28 

Levy of the tax is made on the distributor, who is the refiner or manufacturer of the gasoline. In this way the number of taxpayers whose reports and records have to be processed is substantially reduced from the number who would be involved if the tax were applied at a later stage of distribution. Direct payment by a user is required in those cases where the user receives directly gasoline from another State.

Contrasted to the procedure of assessment in the gasoline tax is that used in the tax on liquefied gases and other liquid fuels. Here the six cent per gallon tax is levied directly on the user, who is required to be licensed and to submit reports and tax payments monthly. 

Exemptions and Deductions 

29 The distributor is compensated for his part in the administration of the tax by being authorized to deduct from his total tax payment an amount equal to the tax on one per cent of the taxable gallonage which he distributes. 

This is for the purpose of covering losses and expenses of collecting the tax. 

However, since evaporation losses actually occur after the first sale the trade practice sometimes is for the distributor to share a part of his deduction allowance with his purchasers through his sales agreements. In addition to this deduction there are included in the law the usual exemptions for situations where payment of the tax would amount to a direct burden upon interstate commerce, or be a violation of constitutional provisions. Also exempt from the tax is all motor fuel sold "to the United States Government, for the exclusive use of said government. "130

If such a sale occurs after the tax on motor fuel has already 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's 1948), art 7065b-2(a). 

29 Ibid. 28 

30Ibid. art. 7065-13(d). 235

been paid, a credit in the amount of such taxes is obtainable. 

It is worthy of mention that the phrase "for the exclusive use of said government" has been given an extremely broad interpretation by the Comptroller General of the United States. As a result virtually all motor fuel sold to the U. S. 

Government is tax exempt. In 1930 the United States Comptroller General delivered an opinion to the Secretary of the Interior which stated that motor fuel sold to the United States Government and later legally resold to government employees is not subject to tax. 31 The above opinion notwithstanding the Attorney General of Texas has written opinions in 1944 and 194532 which state in essence that the motor fuel tax is collectible from army post exchanges and naval stores, which are agencies of the Federal Government, on all motor fuel except that portion which is actually used by the Federal Government. Refunds

The principal exemption in the tax law, however, exists in the form of a refund. As mentioned above, the tax applies only to gasoline used to propel vehicles on the highways. In order to accomplish this exemption the non-highway user of gasoline must first pay the tax at the time of his purchase of gasoline, and then submit a claim for refund. Among the principal uses of gasoline for non-highway purposes are: Uses in stationary engines or tractors for agricultural or industrial purposes, motor boats, aircraft, and dry cleaning.

These refunds constitute a significant portion of the gross tax collected, amounting to a total of $16, 322,164. 48 of a gross collection of $92, 207, 598.25 in 1949, or almost 18 per cent. 33 Because of the volume of the refund claims, the processing of the claims is one of the chief administrative problems of the tax administrator. During the fiscal year 1950 there were more than 317,000 separate claims for refund of motor fuel taxes paid by the Comptroller. 

By virtue of this refund procedure, too, the tax has posed a problern as to its underlying theory. As was discussed in Section 1, since 1929 when the non-highway user exemptions were written into the tax bill, this tax has generally been considered in the category of a benefit tax, i. e., a tax levied on highway users for the privilege of using State highways. However, only a part of the revenues are allocated for the maintenance and building of roads and highways. Thus, it has been maintained that the tax is not a true benefit tax, but because of the "diversion" of funds resulting from the Constitutional provisions in regard to occupation taxes, it is a selective sales and use tax levied on only some purchasers and users of gasoline. 31, Comptroller General of the U. S. to the Sec'y of the Interior, Aug. 28, 1930. 

32Opinion of the Attorney General, Nos. 0-5995, June 2, 1944 and 0-6341, April 13, 1945. 

33 Annual Report of the Comptroller of Public Accounts of the State of Texas, 

1949, Table 16, p. 38. 236

There has been in recent years a great amount of speculation concerning evasion of tax payment via the refund procedure. Some have said that from eight to ten million dollars of the refunds made annually could be eliminated if a closer scrutiny of refund requests could be made. On the other hand, others maintain that only a very small amount of the refunds made are unwarranted. 

Reports of the Motor Fuel Tax Refund Division indicate that concurrent with tremendous increases in the total motor fuel taxes collected amounts of refunds have increased, but not out of proportion to the increase in gross tax receipts. 

Refunds have remained nearly constant, in the neighborhood of 17 per cent of total collections. 

The refunding of motor fuel taxes to non-highway users is not unique to Texas. As a matter of fact most States make some provision for such refund. However, in some cases the refund does not amount to a return of all taxes paid, but only a part. One State refunds half the taxes paid on gasoline used in aircraft. Another State, Nebraska, has instituted a detailed reporting system covering actual gallonage used for non-highway purposes. In others, only agricultural use of gasoline is exempted from the tax. 

Before leaving the matter of refunds, note should be taken of another refund provision in the law. Under this, any person who exports, or loses by fire or other accident any motor fuel in quantities of 100 gallons or more, 34 may make application for refund of any taxes already paid on such fuel.

34 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's 1948) art. 7065b-13(d). 

237

SECTION 4--COLLECTION AND ENFORCEMENT 

Collection

Distributors of motor fuel act as collectors of the motor fuel tax and remit monthly tax payments upon the basis of reports submitted to the Comp- 35 troller. To cover evaporation losses and the expenses of such collection, distributors are authorized a deduction from amounts due the Comptroller equal to the tax on one per cent of the taxable gallonage. Users of liquefied gases and other liquid fuels make payments and reports in the same manner as is required of distributors.

Distributors' Reports. The nature of the information to be included in distributors' and users' monthly reports is specified in detail in the tax law. Some seventeen items of information are enumerated for inclusion in distributors' reports. Persons "selling motor fuel in export or interstate commerce only" and persons selling motor fuel upon which no tax is due must make the same reports required of distributors. If a distributor makes no sale, distribution, or use of motor fuel within a given month, the Comptroller must be appraised of that fact. The Comptroller may authorize producers, pipe line carriers, or licensed distributors to make sales, resales, or distribution without collecting the tax when such sales, resales, or distribution are made to licensed distributors for the purposes of "exportation, further treating, blending or compounding." The purchaser of such non-taxed motor fuel is liable for the tax when it is subsequently sold, distributed, or used. 36 The manifest issued upon such sales is required to carry a notation as to the purpose for which such fuel is to be used. . Carriers Reports. In addition, "all persons operating trucks, pipelines, and other conveyances. in the transportation of motor fuel. exclusive of railroads" must render reports to the Comptroller each month. Such reports must include origin and destination, consignor, consignee, quantity, and kind of all motor fuel transported.

Railroads operating as common carriers must also furnish similar reports, on request of the Comptroller, including information relative to changes in route from interstate to intrastate or the reverse of all shipments of motor fuel.

Pipeline Operators. Operators of pipelines for the purpose of conveying natural gas are required either to collect and conserve for sale, distribution or 35 36 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's 1948), art. 7065b-3(a). 

Ibid., art. 7065b-5 238

use "the liquid residuent commonly known as 'drip gasoline'" or, if such gasoline has no practical value, the operator must neutralize, burn, or otherwise destroy this product to prevent its use as motor fuel. 37 If the drip gasoline is sold, distributed, or used for motor fuel, the pipeline operator must make reports and keep the records required of distributors. Enforcement

Enforcement of the motor fuel tax depends upon two procedures, the issuance of permits to distributors and refund dealers and the maintenance of records by such distributors and refund dealers. 

Distributors Permits. The issuance of permits by the Comptroller is conditioned upon two requirements--an application and the posting of bond. 

Distributors engaging in the sale, distribution, or use of motor fuels must 38 file application with the Comptroller on forms prescribed by him. The permits are non-assignable and consecutively numbered. Renewal is required annually on or before December 31 and may be accomplished by a renewal certificate.

All producers and pipeline operators are required to obtain a distributor's permit before selling listed commodities to other than licensed and qualified distributors. They may be authorized by the Comptroller, however, to make sale or distribution without collecting the tax to distributors holding valid permits.

Distributors are required to post bond or cash in lieu of bond to assure their faithful performance of duties specified in the tax statute. The surety bond must be in the amount of not less than $1,000 nor more than $25,000 payable to the State of Texas, the amount, within these limits, to be established by the Comptroller. He may also require additional bond at any time. Six months from the effective date of the bond, distributors may demand reduction of their bond to not more than three times the highest tax collected by such distributor during the preceding six months provided that the bond is within the minimum and maximum permitted. 

In lieu of bond, distributors may deposit cash or securities in the Suspense Account of the State Treasury to the amount of the required bond. 

Securities so deposited must be of the same classes as those permissible for investment of University of Texas funds. Should the Comptroller desire additional cash or securities and a distributor fails to supply them the distributor's permit may be cancelled. 37 

TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's 1948), art. 7065b-12. 

38 Ibid., art. 7065b-6. 239

Refund Dealers' Permit. In order to sell gasoline upon which a refund is authorized a distributor is required to get a permit or license for this purpose from the Comptroller. 39 A separate application, and oath to faithfully perform and comply with the tax statute, is required for each place of business from which refund motor fuel is to be sold. These permits are required to be renewed annually, and are transferable upon consent of the Comptroller. 

User Permits. 

These are issued annually by the Comptroller upon 40 the basis of applications submitted. At the time of initial application, the user of liquefied gases and other liquid fuels must also post a $100 bond, application for which must be made on a form prescribed by the Attorney General. Cash or securities, in lieu of bond, may be deposited with the Comptroller. User permits are not transferable.

Records 

Distributors are required to maintain in Texas for periods of two years records which must be open to inspection by the Comptroller and the Attorney General or their representatives. Information in such records must include: a. Amounts of all crude oil and other products used to refine or blend any motor fuel sold or used by the distributor. b. Amounts of all motor fuel, casinghead gasoline, natural or drip gasoline and other derivatives or condensates or crude petroleum or natural gas purchased, or received and inventoried on first of each month. 

C. Record of each and every sale, distribution, or use of motor fuel, crude oil, kerosene, .naptha, distillate, casinghead gasoline, drip gasoline, and other derivatives or condensates of crude oil or natural gas, including fuel and other liquid residues, "regardless of whether or not a tax is due upon said products. "41 Each sale, distribution, or use must be recorded on a manifest, the form of which is prescribed or approved by the Comptroller. Such manifests must be numbered consecutively and issued in duplicate. 

Detailed information to be shown concerning both distribution and purchase, the quantity sold, means of transportation and so forth is prescribed by law. 

39 TEX. CIV. STAT. (Vernon's 1948), art. 7065b-13(b).
