"If any person affected by this Article (a) shall fail to pay tax due and owing under the provisions of this Article . . he shall forfeit any 11 as a penalty the sum of not less than $25 nor more than $500. Each day's violation shall constitute a separate offense. 

In addition, ". any distributor. does not make remittance for any taxes collected. within the time prescribed by law, said distributor... shall forfeit two per cent of the amount due"; if not paid within 20 days, he shall forfeit an additional eight per cent penalty. In addition, "a felony conviction for any of the above-named offenses!' shall cause the convicted person to "forfeit the right . . . to obtain a permit as a distributor . . . for two years. 1,47 Some forty-five or fifty violations are enumerated within the tax statute and, similarly, penalties are provided for each of them, either singly or in combination. Probably no other tax statute in Texas contains such a plethora of penalties. No system or priority is provided for their application, and they are sometimes made individually applicable to distributors, dealers, refund dealers, or carriers or collectively applicable to any person subject co the Act.

Measurement of Containers 

For the purposes of enforcement, the Comptroller or his authorized representatives has the "authority to measure, calibrate, and determine the capacity in gallons of any vehicle tank or other container in which motor fuel, lending materials, liquefied gases and liquid fuels are transported. 1148 Deailed statutory instructions are given for the marking of each such vehicle -5 -6

If a distributor markets his products through his own service station. records may be kept at such stations. 

Users of liquefied gases and other liquid fuels must maintain records similar to those required of distributors. Non-resident users are required to exhibit records in Texas upon request by the Comptroller. 

Dealers are required to maintain records in Texas for two years sub ject to inspection by the Comptroller and Attorney General and including all manifests furnished by sellers and complete information on all motor fuels, naptha, kerosene, and similar products, purchased or received and inventoried on the first of each month. Refund dealers must keep the same records required of both distribu tors and dealers, subject to the same inspection. In addition, when a refund dealer issues an exemption invoice, he must make the invoice in duplicate, the duplicate to go to the user and the original to be kept by the refund dealer for a period of two years to be available for inspection by the Comptroller and the Attorney General.

Penalties 

Many varied and overlapping provisions are contained in the motor fuel tax statutes It is difficult to determine, from a reading of the law, the penalties applicable for any specific offense. The following summary of the penalties applicable to distributors for delinquency or failure to pay taxes is illustrative. They are presented in the order in which they appear in the statute. Emphasis is used to indicate specific points of duplication. "If any distributor shall fail to remit proper taxes" he shall bear the 42 expenses incurred in making audits to ascertain the correct amount due.

11 "If any such distributor . . . or any person, shall wilfully fail or refuse to pay. such tax funds" he "shall be guilty of a felony, any punishable by confinement in the State penitentiary for not more than ten years or in the county jail for not less than one month nor more than one year or by a fine ranging from $500 to $10,000 or by both fine and imprison- 43 ment. "The Comptroller . . . is hereby authorized to cancel" any license or permit issued to a distributor for any violations of this act including "(a)failu or refusal to remit . any tax levies herein.. ..1144 42 

Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Legislature, RS, Ch. 184, art. XVII, sec. 3 (c). 

43 Ibid., sec. 4 (b). 

44 Ibid., sec. 16. 241

tank and container, and any vehicle tank or container must "be of such design and construction that they do not facilitate the perpetration of fraud." The 

Comptroller may, however, at his discretion, accept any weights and measures certificate issued by the Division of Weights and Measure of the Department of Agriculture. The measurement certificate must be carried with the vehicle tank or container for which it is issued. 

All authorized representatives of the Comptroller have "the power and authority to inspect, test, measure or remeasure" vehicle tanks and "to correct, condemn or mark 'out of order' any such vehicle tanks . . ." They may also hold any truck up to 72 hours until measurement certificate is produced.

In addition, any highway patrolman, sheriff, constable, and their deputies, and all other peace officers are empowered to stop any motor vehicle transporting "motor fuel or other derivatives of crude petroleum or natural gas, or their products" for examination of the manifest and the commodity being transported; to take samples of the cargo; or to make any other investigations necessary to determine the nature of the cargo and that the motor fuel tax has been paid thereon. If the manifest and proof that the tax has been paid are not produced within 72 hours, the motor fuel and truck must be delivered to the Comptroller or his representatives. 

Failure to comply with these provisions, alteration of any measurement certificate, failure to produce vehicle tank for inspection and measurement, and transportation of any products in a vehicle tank condemned or tagged "out of order" is considered a misdemeanor, punishable, upon conviction, by a fine of not less than $25 nor more than $200. 

Seizure and Forfeiture

If any motor fuel upon which tax is due is found in the possession, custody, or control or any person for the purpose of being used, sold, or distributed in fraud of the Motor Fuel Tax Law or if any motor fuel is removed, deposited, stored, or concealed with intent to avoid payment of taxes, all vehicles, or other tangible personal property incidental to and used for such purpose may be seized by the Comptroller and forfeited to the State. 

Proceedings may be instituted by the Attorney General, or District or County Attorney at the request of the Comptroller to validate such seizure and forfeiture. If seizure is maintained and forfeiture perfected, the sheriff in the county of seizure may、sell at public auction to the highest bidder all property so forfeited. Proceeds of the sale are to go to the State Treasurer to be allocated as motor fuel tax revenue. District or county attorneys prosecuting such suits shall be paid a fee of $15. 243

In lieu of the above procedure, the Comptroller may sell motor fuel and property so seized if the appraised value is $500 or less. The procedure for such sale is outlined in the statute. 49 

The Comptroller may also authorize waiver of forfeiture proceedings if the offender pays a penalty equal to twice the amount due on the motor fuel "plus all other costs in connection with such seizure." 

Police Powers

Extensive police powers are granted the Comptroller to determine the amount of tax collected and payable. He or his authorized representatives may inspect any premises where motor fuel, crude oil or natural gas or their condensates or derivatives are "produced, made, prepared, stored, transported, sold or offered for sale or exchange. "They may also examine books and records kept in the conduct of the business. In addition, they may "examine and gauge or measure the contents of all storage tanks, containers and other property or equipment . . and . . . take samples of any and all products stored therein." For these purposes, authorized officers may remain on the premises any length of time necessary. . "The Comptroller, or any duly authorized representative under direction of the Comptroller, shall . . . have power to issue subpoenas, compel the attendance of witnesses, administer oaths, certify to official acts, take depositions within or without the State of Texas, as now provided by law, and compel the production of pertinent books, accounts, records, and documents. " 

All taxes, penalties, interest, and costs due the State constitute "a preferred lien, first and prior to any and all other existing liens. . . upon all property of any distributor." 

The Comptroller, his authorized agents, "any highway patrolman, officer of the Department of Public Safety, Sheriff, Constable, or their Deputies, may halt any motor vehicle 'which might appear to be using liquefied gas or products described herein" for investigation. The Comptroller or his authorized representative may cancel, refuse issuance, extension or reinstatement of a distributor's permit,users permit, or refund dealers license for some ten offenses specified in the Act. Before such action is taken, a hearing must be held in Austin according to rules prescribed by the Comptroller and five days notice of hearing must be given the licensee or applicant. Appeal from action of the Comptroller may be made to the District Court of Travis County. 49 

Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Legislature, RS, Ch. 184, art. 7065b-20(c). 244

By means of this long array of enforcement powers accumulated over a period of years, the tax administrator of this tax law is perhaps better armed with authority to act under almost any situation than is the administrator of any other Texas tax. 

Refunding Procedure

The Comptroller issues to refund dealers books of blank invoices, serially numbered and prepared in duplicate according to detailed instructions contained in the tax statute. A record is kept by him of all such refund invoice books, and each refund dealer must account for the invoices issued him. Their transfer is permissible when approved by the Comptroller. 

Each purchaser receives the duplicate of the exemption invoice and the dealer is required to keep the original, signed by the purchaser, for a period of two years.

When making application for refund of taxes paid, the user of exempt motor fuel must submit, within six months of date of sale, the duplicate invoice and an affidavit showing the name and location of the refund dealer from whom motor fuel was purchased and stating that it was not used to propel a motor vehicle upon the highways of the State. The Comptroller may require additional affidavits and, when satisfied that the refund claim is valid, may 50 issue a warrant for its payment.

Such warrants are for the amount of tax paid, less the one per cent deduction permitted the dealer and a fifty cent filing fee. This fee is designed for the use of the Comptroller in the administration and enforcement of the motor fuel tax law and to defray the expense of furnishing refund invoices to dealers.

Penalties for violation of any of the refund provisions provide for forfeiture of the right to obtain a refund for a period of one year, and for the dealer in instances of his violation, forfeiture of the right to sell motor fuel for refund purposes. 

50 Tex. Laws, 1941, 47th Legislature, RS, Ch. 184, art. 7065b-13 245

SECTION ANALYSIS OF OPERATION 

Rates 

Since the first enactment of a motor fuel tax in Texas in 1923, the rate in this State has consistently been at or near the average of all States. In the face of a steady and uninterrupted upward climb of gasoline tax rates throughout the country, the Texas rate has remained static for over 20 years and at no time has it been among the high rates of the country. 

During the year 1949 alone, 13 states effected an increase in motor fuel tax rates, in most cases boosting the rate by one cent per gallon. At present, with a four cent per gallon rate, Texas is below the national average of 5. 3 cents.

As of July 1, 1950 there were 30 States with higher rates than Texas, 12 others with the same rate as Texas, and five with a lesser rate. 

The range of rates throughout the country is from two cents in Missouri to nine cents in Louisiana. Of the rates higher than the Texas rate, six cents and seven cents per gallon are the most prevalent. 

Texas retained its initial rate of one cent per gallon from 1923 until 1927, then temporarily went to a three cent rate until 1928, when the rate was lowered to two cents. However, the 1929 revision of the law set a four cent rate which has remained to the present time. Among the other States the prevalent rate until 1923 was one cent. Seventeen of the 19 States with gasoline taxes in 1922 had a one cent rate. However, thirteen States increased their rates in 1923 and by 1924 there were 35 states levying motor fuel taxes and only seven of this group levied a one cent rate. Texas and Rhode Island were the last States to change from the one cent rate, both boosting the tax in 1927.

The general trend toward higher rates in all States can be seen in Table MF-1, which shows the number of States having each rate of tax for several selected years: 246

Some conception of the relative cost of the motor fuel tax can be gathered from the Table MF-2. The statute provides that a 50 cent fee be deducted from each refund to cover the administrative cost of such refund. 

The full amount so deducted has not been authorized for payment of administrative costs, however. It has actually far exceeded the appropriations for this purpose in the past ten years. The same is true of the provision that one per cent of the revenue received from the motor fuel tax be devoted to the cost of its administration. Thus, only a portion of the amount collected for administrative purposes is devoted to that purpose; the remainder goes into the General Revenue Fund. In the same ten-year period, the combined appropriations to the Motor Fuel Tax Refund and Motor Fuel Tax Divisions accounted for more than one-third of the total appropriations to the Comptroller's Department. In

Of total taxes collected by the Comptroller in 1949--$269.million-the Motor Fuel Tax Division collected $92, 907, 598 from the motor fuel tax before refunds or approximately one-third of total tax collections by the Comptroller. However, the Gross Receipts Division which accounted for some 40 per cent of total taxes collected by the Comptroller received only 11 per cent of total appropriations to the Comptroller for the same year. relation to other Texas taxes, then, the cost of collecting the motor fuel tax is relatively high. On the other hand, an analysis of this comparison might indicate that allocations to the Gross Receipts Division are insufficient rather than that those for motor fuel tax administration are high. It should be kept in mind, too, as has been pointed out in regard to other taxes, that the enforcement problem of each tax varies considerably, thus preventing any valid comparison on a simple per-dollar-of-revenue basis. When the administrative cost of this tax is compared to its net revenue however, the cost does not appear out of proportion. In 1949, appropriations to the Motor Fuel Tax and Motor Fuel Tax Refund Divisions represented only. 

eight-tenths of one per cent of net collections. The cost/revenue ratio is, in this light, relatively small.

Upon the basis of appropriations to the Motor Fuel Tax Refund Division, the administrator had 45 cents per refund claim paid in 1942 to defray costs. 

In 1947, he had 35 cents and in 1948 39 cents. Although general costs have probably increased, appropriations per item processed have decreased. 

Analysis of Revenues

The present tax rate of four cents per gallon was placed in effect in 1929, and the year 1930 was the first complete year in which that tax was collected. Since that time the net revenue gained from it has increased with considerable regularity (See Table MF-4). In 1930 the State retained slightly 248

more than 32 million dollars in motor fuel taxes. In 1949, more than double this amount, over 76 million dollars, was paid into the State Treasury by this tax. It is of some significance that in spite of this tremendous increase in revenue, the percentage of total State tax revenues borne by the motor fuel tax has decreased quite consistently from 40.9 per cent in 1930 to 24.4 per cent in 1949.

In order to picture the status of the State of Texas relative to other states in the nation Table MF-3 was constructed. The data included are for the year 1946, the most recent year for which complete information was available, and more recent information indicates that the relations displayed have not changed significantly. Total use of motor fuel was selected as the basic item, and the seven states which reported over one billion gallons used were selected for comparison. Since tax rates and refund provisions are major areas of interest, information concerning these matters has been included. Rankings of the leading users have been made with respect to several items. Note that the material is presented for the calendar year 

1946 which will not correspond exactly with the same information for the fiscal year. 

Collection and Evaporation Allowances

As of January 1948, a total of 36 States permitted the tax collecting agent either a deduction, or a refund, as a compensation for evaporation, for loss, or for expenses. In the reamining 12 states, only fuel sold or used was taxable so that evaporation losses were not, in effect, taxed. Only eight 

States include the expenses of collection among the permitted purposes for allowances, while six additional States provide a deduction solely for the cost of collection. Allowances range from 1/2 of one per cent of gallonage in Pennsylvania to four per cent in South Dakota. Fifteen States permit a deduction of less than two per cent, 11 between two and three per cent, nine three per cent, and one four per cent. 51

The diversity of percentages allowed illustrates the problems connected with such allowances. The Texas allowance of one per cent is for the purposes of collection and losses. 

Use of Revenues for Non-highway Purposes

If the motor fuel tax is considered a benefit tax, then the use of the revenues, beyond defraying administration costs, for purposes other than highways, local streets and the like could be considered a diversion of the Nation-wide data is available for 1946 on the basis of use made of all highway user revenues, including revenue from the motor fuel tax, revenues. 51 

Federation of Tax Administrators, R M-271, (RB-68 Revised) April, 1948. 249

motor vehicle license fees and special imposts on motor carriers. In 1946, twenty-two states devoted all such revenues, less administrative costs, to' highways, local streets and roads. In the remaining twenty-six states, from two-tenths of one per cent (Montana and Virginia) to 45.5 per cent (Rhode Island) of the highway user taxes were allocated to non-highway or non-motorist purposes. Texas ranked fourth highest, devoting 20.5 per cent of its highway user taxes to non-highway uses. 

The average allocation by these twenty-six states of such tax revenues to non-highway purposes was 5.9 per cent. 52 

Users of Liquefied Gases and Other Liquid Fuels 

The increasing use of vehicles utilizing "liquefied gases" (propane and butane) and "other fuels" (diesel) has produced an administrative problem of some magnitude. Since the tax on these fuels is paid by the users, taxpayers in this category now number approximately six times the number of distributors collecting the gasoline tax of four cents. As a consequence, "considerably more man-hours are required in the Austin office to perform the clerical work of the users of these special fuels paying around $644, 000 per annum than is required to handle the clerical work of less than 300 motor fuel distributors who will collect and pay over to the State more than one hundred million dollars in motor fuel tax during the present fiscal year. 153 Year

Evasions

A determination as to the validity of all refunds for non-highway uses of motor fuel would be difficult if not impossible. The Motor Fuel Tax Refund 

Division audits all claims, but the immensity of the problem is apparent when it is remembered that a total of 317,036 claims were processed in fiscal 1950 or approximately 1200 claims per working day. Of the total received, 1, 236 or about four-tenths of one per cent were rejected. 

Refunds for agricultural uses are those generally considered most susceptible to misuse. The Comptroller has presented the following comparison of average consumption per tractor. Year

SECTION -  

Although in operation the administration of the motor fuel tax exhibits a high degree of efficiency, and the administrator is armed with possibly more enforcement powers than is the case in any other Texas tax, there are several areas of policy and practice which can well be described as problem areas. 

These include (1) Refunds for non-highway use of gasoline. (2) Utilization of Revenues for non-highway purposes. 

3) Possible evasions. (4) Collection allowances (5) Some instances of statutory inconsistency. 

The first two of these are clearly matters of tax policy; the others are to a large degree matters of administration. It may be decided that matters should be left as they now are; in that case the following discussion is largely irrelevant. 

However, if it is decided to consider any of the above matters with a view toward change, then the following material is intended to provide various possible alternate approaches that may be taken. The material is not intended to be exhaustive, but only illustrative. Refunds

The politically sensitive nature of the question of refunds makes any discussion and analysis of the matter difficult. However, laying aside the political considerations involved, several features of the refund provisions may be examined.

First, the total annual amount represented by refunds is sizable -- 16 or 17 million dollars. This is a significant amount whether compared with the total revenues from this tax or with that of a number of other taxes. This fact does not constitute an argument for or against the refund practice, although it may explain the interest in the subject. 

Secondly, it may be more instructive to examine each class of users entitled to a refund than to consider the question of refunds in general terms. The use of the refund provisions in the motor fuel tax laws of the various States seems to be an adoption of the benefit principle in this tax; that is, that the motor fuel tax should be paid by those who benefit from the use of the highways and the roads of the State. The central problem in applying this principle is to determine what is meant by benefit - Does benefit mean only direct and immediate, or both direct and substantial indirect benefits? Having defined the concept, the problem is to determine whether each particular class of user is obtaining the 252

requisite benefit. A brief look at a few of the groups of users exempt under present Texas laws illustrates the problem. 

Taxes paid on gasoline used in aircraft are refundable. Aircraft, obviously, do not use the highways. However, if a liberal definition of benefit were used, it could be argued that aircraft do obtain substantial indirect benefits from highways, streets and roads. These highways provide easy access to the adjacent cities from the airports; thus, highway and air travel combine to offer one transportation service. In addition, considerable amounts of public money have been expended for the building and maintenance of airports. 

Operators of motor boats on the rivers, lakes and ocean waters in and adjacent to Texas are entitled to refunds on gasoline used to power their craft. 

Again, the operator does not use the highway to operate his craft. However, if again a liberal interpretation of benefit is used, it could be argued that the highways and roads are necessary to the operator to get to and from his craft. 

Thus he obtains substantial indirect benefit from the highways of the State. 

Industrial use of gasoline is increasing year by year. Refunds are authorized for gasoline used for such purposes. Obviously, the stationary and other industrial motors do not use the highways. But again, unless a strict interpretation is placed upon benefit, it could be argued that most industries are heavily dependent upon the State network of highways and roads for the receipt of their supplies and the distribution of their goods and that therefore the industrial user of gasoline obtains substantial indirect benefits from the highways. Similarly, other users of tax-exempt gasoline, it is maintained, get substantial, though indirect, benefits from the highways of the State to an extent equal to or beyond the benefits received by the highway-user who pays the tax. In a sense, it could be said that all people obtain very real benefits from the State's road network.

As is apparent, and as was discussed in Section 1, much of this situation is the result of significant changes in the ways of American life during the past 20-odd years. When refunds were first established, air travel was relatively new and limited, mechanized farming was not a universal practice, and other-than-highway use of gasoline had not assumed the proportions it currently has. 253

A number of approaches have been tried and many more suggested to adjust the refund procedures to this changed situation. Some States have levied a gasoline tax without any refunds or exemptions, others have tried only partial refunds, still others have provided full or partial refunds for some of the non-highway users of gasoline.

Of course, there is a substantial school of thought which argues the point of view that the gasoline tax should be borne by only the users of the highways of the State. The contention is that this tax is a benefit tax for the privilege of using the highways, that the benefits are reasonably well measured by the amount of gasoline consumed on the highways, and that payment of the tax by non-highway users would change the true role of the tax. 

In recognition of this, but with the contention that highway-users are not the only consumers of gasoline who derive direct and measurable benefits from State expenditures made specifically for them, some States have applied a separate tax on gasoline used by aircraft. The proceeds from such levý are allocated for airport and related expenditures.

Thus, it is evident that a number of approaches are possible to the refund question if it is felt that the present method of total refund for all nonhighway use of gasoline warrants adjustment or modification: (a) Elimination of refunds for those non-highway users who benefit directly from expenditure of State funds; (b) Provision of partial refunds for those non-highway users of gasoline who derive substantial though indirect benefits from public expenditures; (c) Provision of full refunds for only those non-highway users of gasoline who derive no direct benefit nor substantial indirect benefit from State expenditures on projects having a bearing on the exempted activity; (d) And, of course, discontinuance of all refunds. 

In essence, every one of these approaches, including the present system, is a recognition of the gasoline tax as a benefit or privilege tax. The difference between them is one of degree and of amount of recognition of benefits other than use of highways. 254

Utilization of Revenues for Non-highway Purposes 

The question of the use of the revenues for non-highway purposes, or diversion, as it is commonly called, is tied inextricably to the problem of refunds. If the gasoline tax is to be borne by the highway-users only, it is in effect a benefit or privilege tax on the use of State highways. As such, it is argued, the revenues from the tax should properly go to building and maintenance of public highways and roads, Any use of such money for other purposes is thus considered a diversion. As previously mentioned, the 

Federal Government and many other groups and individuals have long attempted to minimize such diversion. 

The effect of the Constitution in Texas apparently has resulted in a regular allocation of a goodly portion of gasoline tax revenues to the schools of the State. In effect, it is contended this means that highway-users of gasoline pay not only for the privilege of using the highways, but in addition pay for a part of the cost of the schools which are also used by the non-highway consumers of gas.

Suggestions for change of the present method are several. Two of those frequently mentioned are: (a) Discontinuance of the allocation of gasoline revenues to other than road and highway programs. This assumes that the Constitutional provision is not applicable to this tax on the ground that it is not in reality an occupation tax. (b) Provision that refunds do not include the one-fourth of the tax allocated for the use of the schools; in other words, that only partial refunds be made. Evasions

As discussed earlier, much has been said in regard to the problem of evasion of the gasoline tax. There have been many and vociferous complaints that a very large amount of gasoline on which refunds have been paid has eventually found its way into the tanks of cars and trucks using the highways. 

An accurate determination of the situation is manifestly difficult. 255

However, since almost everyone is agreed that some evasion does exist, although wide disagreement prevails as to the extent, a number of approaches to the problem have been suggested. Among the most frequent are: (a) Use of colored gasoline for the purpose of identifying that on which a refund has been paid. Several States have tried this method. As a deterrent upon improper use of gas, it has many proponents. (b) Provision for complete records and reports to be maintained by the user of refund gasoline. 

Such reports and records would permit the tax administrator to make much more certain that refund gasoline was properly used. This method has been tried in at least one St ate and has been strongly advocated by some adminis- 'trators. (c) Provision for only partial refunds coupled with one or both of the preceding suggestions. This, it is contended, would take much of the gain out of the practice of evasion, but does involve a change in the present concept concerning non-highway use. 

Collection Allowances

As with deductions permitted in other taxes, two questions arise in regard to the deduction authorized in the gasoline tax for losses and for cost of collection. They are first, the question as to whether such deduction does any more than lower the tax rate for the distributor, and second, the question of the reasonableness of the amount of deduction. 

In this tax, the present deduction appears to have been generally acceptable to those affected. However, since the problem of evaporation does not actually enter into consideration until after the first sale or distribution, the allowable deduction authorized the distributor, essentially becomes an allowance to compensate the distributor for the bookkeeping and report-making required by the tax law. Such allowance is not common among other taxes, and might be considered as compensating for a service whose cost is absorbed in the general administrative costs of the taxpayer for most other taxes. The determination of such cost is of course difficult and varies considerably from distributor to distributor. 256

Since administrative costs of collection of this tax vary also from time to time depending upon the general economie situation, as well as between distributors, it is of course possible that such an allowance, set years ago, may not be adequate today. At any rate, because of its purpose · and the amount of money it represents, scrutiny of both the theory on which it rests and its practical application might well be warranted. 

Administration and the Law

As discussed in earlier sections, a plethora of provisions dealing with certain aspects of the tax have been added to the law over the years. 

As a consequence, although reasonably clear in intent, the law is somewhat confusing in practical application in certain matters. Particularly is this true in regard to bonding procedures, enforcement authority and penalties. 

Clarification through revision of certain provisions could be achieved without change of intent or effect, by consolidation and simplification of some of the statutory provisions.

In the matter of bonds, serious consideration might well be warranted of a uniform bonding procedure which could be used for the administration of this tax, and possibly might be worthy of general application. 

In Conclusion

As a tax paid directly by perhaps more people than any other State tax, and as one of the leading revenue produqers in the State's tax structure, the gasoline tax in Texas is a subject of utmost importance in any consideration of State tax policy. For these reasons it can be hoped that its future application will be on a firmly expressed and clearly understood basis. And it can be hoped, too, that requirements of procedure and application will continue to be made to carry along a development that has thus far been noteworthy in Texas tax administration. 257
